Carbohydrates & Sugars

Early man was a carnivore who ate insects, grubs,
worms, small frogs, snakes, lizards, turtles, rats, birds, fish and
meat he could grab from a carcass when a large carnivore had
had their fill and left their kill to the two legged scavengers
- many early humans were cannibals.

According to anthropologists, man only began to
eat grains, fruits and vegetables in a serious and consistent

===== Screenshot (39) =====

fashion some 10,000 years ago when agriculture as we know
it began in the “Fertile Crescent” in what is now Iraq.

Contrary to popular belief, carbohydrates and sugars
are not essential nutrients by anybody’s standards, as we
can make all of the carbohydrates and sugars we need for
energy and maintenance and repair of structural components
by breaking down and reconfiguring fats and proteins at the
cellular level.

Carbohydrates and sugars have become part of the
human diet, not because of necessity, but rather because of
convenience and ease of production and storage.

Historically grains were able to sustain humans in
between annual fish runs, bird and large mammal migrations.
Stored grains were also used as a buffer against catastrophic
starvation during famine and draught.

During times of bumper harvests and plenty, human
cultures learned to create a large variety of breads, cakes, pies,
pastas, granolas and cereal dishes. Cook books sprang up in
every culture, teaching and encouraging the use of grains and
sugars until human societies all over the world came to think
of bread as the “staff of life.”

Plants are able to store

carbohydrates as their chief
source of reserve energy. Water, mine rals and nitrogen in the
soil are taken up by the plant roots, stem (trunk), branches
and leaves. The plant leaves absorb carbon dioxide (CO,)
from the air. The energy from sunlight acting on water (H,O)
and carbon dioxide in the presence of chlorophyll produces
a chemical reaction known as “photosynthesis,” a process in
which the leaves manufacture sugar by breaking the carbon off
of the CO, and dumping the oxygen (O,) into the atmosphere
and then making carbon chains from the remaining carbon.

CO2 + H2O —(sunlight, chlorophyll)→ carbohydrates (CH2O) + O2

The sugar and carbohydrates manufactured in the
leaves will be used for energy to support the physiological
and biochemical activities and the growth of the plant or tree
- excess production above the plants immediate needs will be
stored as energy or “solar income” in the plants leaves, stems,
roots, seeds, pods and fruits - ultimately the sun provides
the energy to support all living systems on earth. To draw
on this “solar currency” stored in the plant, the carbohydrate
or sugar is “burned” in the human body producing chemical
energy and the byproducts of carbon dioxide and water.

Glucose and complex carbohydrates can provide
fuel for energy that is required for human movement,
mechanical work and life itself. Grains furnish a major source
of carbohydrates for humans and have the highest yield of
stored solar energy per acre of land when compared with
other foods.

The level of carbohydrate consumption varies
significantly between countries and regions within countries.
In America, approximately 45% of the diet is carbohydrates
(in poorer section of big cities the percent of carbohydrates
in the diet rises to 65% or more); in Asia, a high rice diet,
carbohydrates can make up 65% or more; in the tropics as
much as 90% of dietary energy comes from carbohydrates.

Carbohydrates are classified as monosaccharides,
disaccharides, oligosaccharides and polysaccharides. The
monosaccharides or simple sugars cannot be hydrolyzed
to a simpler form. Disaccharides can be hydrolyzed to give
two molecules of the same or different monosaccharides.
Oligosaccharides yield three to ten monosaccharides and
polysaccharides yield more than ten simple sugars, up to
10,000 or more.

Carbohydrates ofall kinds (sugars and starches) provide
a protein-sparing effect. If little or no sugar or carbohydrate is
in a meal, the body, will breakdown protein into glucose via a
process known as gluconeogenesis.

===== Screenshot (40) =====

Monosaccharides

Glucose: (dextrose, grape sugar) is found in abundance
in fruit, sweet corn, corn syrup, sugar beets and honey.
Glucose is the principal sugar produced by the breakdown of
the more complex carbohydrates in the process of digestion.
Glucose is the “blood sugar” found in the blood stream (10
gm in an average size adult).

Glucose is burned in the cells as fuel to produce energy
and is stored in the liver and muscles as glycogen, a complex
carbohydrate called “animal starch.”

Approximately 110 gm of glycogen is stored in the
liver and 225 gm is stored in the skeletal muscles. Typically
the brain uses glucose as its primary energy fuel.

Fructose: (levulose, fruit sugar) is found together with
glucose and sucrose in honey and fruit. It is the sweetest of the
natural sugars. Sweeteners containing glucose and levulose
can be made from grains (corn) as well as from the sugar beet
and sugar cane to make high-fructose corn syrup.

Sorbitol: a hexahydric alcohol derived from glucose,
it has the sweetening power similar to glucose. Sorbitol is
absorbed slowly and it serves to keep blood sugar levels high
following a meal. It is commonly used in weight loss products
as an aid to delay the onset of hunger pangs. Sorbitol has the
same energy value as glucose and is naturally found in many
fruits, vegetables and diet aids.

Galactose: is not found by itself in nature but is part
of the lactose or milk sugar molecule. It is found naturally in

nerve tissue.

Mannitol: is a hexahydric alcohol derived from


 

mannose and is found in a wide variety of foods. It is poorly
digested and thus yields about half as many calories per gram
as glucose. Dried pineapple, olives, asparagus, sweet potatoes,
carrots and “sugarless” gum often contain mannitol.

Xylitol: a sugar alcohol of xylose with the sweetness of
sucrose, it is absorbed at only one fifth the rate of glucose; for
this reason xylitol is commonly used in sweeteners and foods
for diabetics; it is also used in “sugar free” chewing gum and
high quality weightloss chocolates. Xylitol is found naturally
in fruit and vegetables.

Disaccharides

Disaccharides, or double sugars, include sucrose (cane
or beet sugar), maltose (malt sugar) and lactose (milk sugar):

Sucrose = glucose + Fructose

Maltose = glucose + glucose
Lactose = glucose + galactose

Disaccharides are broken apart into the single
monosaccharides before being absorbed through the intestine.

Polysaccharides

The primary polysaccharides commonly found in
human nutrition including starch, dextrin, cellulose and
glycogen are simply chains of glucose molecules.

===== Screenshot (41) =====

Dietarv fiber is different than simple crude fiber.
In addition to lignin and cellulose, dietary fiber contains
hemicellulose, gums, pectin, and other carbohydrates that are
indigestible by humans.

Mucopolysaccha rides occur mixed with protein in body
secretions and physical structural units and are necessary for
the natural viscosity of body mucus secretions.

Mucopolysaccharidesa refound insignificant quantities
in food. Some of the more common mucopolysaccharides
include hyaluronic acids (HA) found in intercellular tissues
including cartilage; chondroitin sulfate, typically found in the
cornea of the eye, cartilage, tendons, ligaments, intervertebral
discs, skin, aorta and heart valves; heparin, a naturally
occurring anticoagulant found in blood; keratosulfates witch
are associated with blood groups; and dermatan sulfate found

in skin.
Proteins

Protein, is derived from the Greek, “ of first importance”
was isolated almost 200 years ago. Proteins, the key organic
compounds of all living tissue and organisms are nitrogen
containing carbon compounds that will yield amino acids
when broken down into the basic building blocks.

Proteins are the fundamental structural compounds
in the cell; they are essential components of the nucleus and
protoplasm of every cell. Proteins are the most abundant of
the organic compounds in the body.

Most of the protein in the human body is found in
skeletal muscle; the balance is distributed in soft tissue,
internal organs, bones, teeth, blood and other body fluids.

Hormones and enzymes are proteins that do work.
As proteins are required for essential functions in the body,


 

and since many indispensable protein components can’t be
manufactured by the body and must be obtained solely through
dietary sources, it is obvious that the amounts and quality of
the protein sources are matters of considerable importance.

Composition and functions of Protein

Proteins, like fats and carbohydrates, contain carbon,
hydrogen and oxygen, however, they also contain 16%
nitrogen along with sulfur, and can contain phosphorus, iron
and cobalt. The building blocks of proteins are the amino
acids. They are found in chains in a number of geometric and
chemical combinations to form unique proteins, all of which
are very large and complex molecules, each having its own
physiological function.

Even though they are structurally complex, proteins
are easily broken apart by enzymatic action or boiling in acid
or alkaline solutions.

Plants obtain their nitrogen from the nitrates and
ammonia in the soil, and with them are able to synthesize
protein. Animals and humans obtain their nitrogen by eating
plants and other animals. The byproduct of animal metabolism
(feces, urine), excretion and death returns the nitrogen to the
soil - this continuing cycle is termed the “nitrogen cycle.”

Essential Amino Acids

There are 12 amino acids that are required by the body
as protein building blocks, and because the body is unable to
manufacture them, they are termed “essential amino acids.”
If any of these “essential” amino acids are missing from the

===== Screenshot (42) =====
diet for any length of time, a specific, sometimes reversible
disease will occur.

Valine methionine’
Tyrosine histidine
Tryptophan Phenylalanine
Lysine arginine
Isoleucine Taurine
Threonine Leucine

Proteins can be broken down and used as a source of
heat and energy. Proteins supply four kcalories per gm, the
same as using carbohydrate for fuel; however, the process is
more biologically “ expensive” than burning carbohydrates for
fuel (five percent of calories required to burn carbohydrates
and fat versus 30% calories to burn an equal amount of
protein).

* Selenium researcher, Dr. Gerhard Schrauzer, considers selenomethionine,
the selenium analog of methionine the 13th essential amino acid.

Lipids

Lipids (fats) are a large heterogeneous group of
compounds made up of fatty acids. This group of organic
compounds includes ordinary fats and oils, waxes and related
compounds. They all have the common features of being
insoluble in water; soluble in organic solvents such as ether
and chloroform; and they are utilizable and required by all
living organisms.

The majority (98%) of the fats found in the human
body, are triglycerides, and the greater majority of these are
long chain triglycerides. The balance of lipids found in the
body, are comprised of monoglycerides and diglycerides, free
fatty acids, phospholipids and sterols.


 

Fatty acids are described by three characteristics:

1) chain length; 2) degree of “saturation” with hydrogen;
and 3) the location of the first “double bond” on the carbon
chain.

The length of a fatty acid is determined by the number
of carbon atoms in the chain - C16 refers to 16 carbons in the
chain. The chains can be further described by “short” (six or
fewer carbons); “medium” (seven to 11 carbons); and “long”
(12 or more carbons).

The degree of hydrogen “saturation” is defined by the
number of double bonds between the carbon atoms in the
chains. A chain may contain all of the hydrogen it can hold
and have no double bonds, in this instance it is referred to as
a “saturated fatty acid.” The chain may contain one double
bond and be called a monounsaturated fatty acid, or it can
be a polyunsaturated fatty acid and contain several double
bonds. The location of the first double bond as counted from
the tail of the fatty acid is the omega number (i.e. - omega-3,
omega-4, etc.).

Fats supply a concentrated source of energy. Each gram
of fat supplies 9 kcalories, whichis more than twice the amount
of energy supplied by each gram of carbohydrate. Because of
the high energy density and low solubility of fats, they are
used as a storage form of energy. Up to two thirds of the total
energy of the cells may be supplied by triglycerides rather
than carbohydrate. Fat spares protein for tissue synthesis.

Fats aid in the transport and absorption of the fat-soluble vitamins A, D, E and K. Fats spare thiamin since they
can be metabolized for energy without the need for thiamin,
whereas, the metabolism of carbohydrate requires thiamine.

Triglycerides: (the primary component of fats and
oils) are similar to carbohydrates in that they are composed
of carbon, hydrogen and oxygen. One hundred grams of fat
from triglycerides will contain 95 grams of fatty acids.

===== Screenshot (43) =====

Cholesterol: Cholesterol is a member of a large group
of compounds known as sterols. They all have the same
complex ring structure. The “ol” ending of the cholesterol
indicates that it is an organic alcohol.

Cholesterol is an essential nutrient and an essential
component of the structural membranes of all cells, all steroid
hormones and is a major component of brain (myelin) and
nerve cells.

Cholesterol is found only in animal tissues, however,
some similar sterols are found in plants. Ergosterol, a yeast
sterol, is converted to vitamin D2 when exposed to ultraviolet
light. Cholesterol is converted by the intestinal mucosa
to 7-dehydrocholesterol, the provitamin of vitamin D3,
cholecalciferol. This process is also carried out by the skin and
other tissues, when exposed to ultraviolet light and sunlight.

Essential Fatty Acids (EFA): Three polyunsaturated
fatty acids, linoleic, linolenic and arachidonic acids, have
essential fatty acid activity. Only linoleic and linolenic
fatty acids have been designated as essential fatty acids, as
arachidonic acid can be synthesized from linoleic acid.

The essential fatty acids have important roles in fat
transport and metabolism and in maintaining the function
and integrity of cellular membranes.

The essential fatty acids are the building blocks and
raw materials for a group of hormone-like compounds
including prostaglandins, thromboxanes and prostacyclins,
that participate in the regulation of blood pressure, heart rate,
vascular dilation, blood clotting, lipolysis, the central nervous
system and airway dilation.

The daily requirement for the essential fatty acids is
two to three percent of the total calories consumed. A daily
diet containing 1,500 calories would dictate an essential fatty
acid requirement of 45 calories or five grams of EFA.


 

The EFA Health Claim Story

Ithad been known for more than 65 years that there were
in fact essential fatty acids that were required in the human diet
for optimal health, disease prevention and longevity, however,
because the FDA ruled that the information was “inconclusive”
it was deemed illegal to inform people that they could reduce
their risk of stroke and coronary thrombosis sudden death by
supplementing with omega-3 essential fatty acids.

To make a long story short, Wellness Lifestyles,
Inc (Wallach) filed a petition for an Amended Health
Claim: Omega-3 Fatty Acids and Coronary Heart Disease
(Co-Petitioner); Docket No. 03Q-0401; filed on 6/23/2003;
qualified claim allowed by FDA on 9/8/2004. Claim approval
from the FDA was hard fought and won so that the world
would have access to the benefits of supplementing with
omega-3 essential fatty acids to reduce their risks of stroke
and sudden death from heart attacks.

Vitamins

Vitamins are an essential group of unrelated organic
carbon compounds needed only in minute quantities in the
diet. They are essential for metabolic processes within the
cell and necessary for normal growth, maintenance and
longevity. Deficiencies of many vitamins during pregnancy
can result in catastrophic birth defects (neural tube defects,
mental retardation, muscular dystrophy, cystic fibrosis, cleft
lip, cleft palate, heart defects, etc.).

Many vitamins act as coenzymes or a prosthetic group
of enzymes responsible for facilitating essential physiological
reactions.

===== Screenshot (44) =====

Vitamins are often called “accessory food factors”
in view of the fact that they do not in of themselves supply
calories or contribute to building body mass.

Vitamins regulate metabolism, participate in the citric
acid cycle and converting fat, sugar, carbohydrates and
proteins into energy.

There are three categories of vitamins:

1) Water soluble vitamins: B complex and vitamin C

The B complex consists of 10 separate water soluble
vitamins. In general they function as essential nutrients in the
metabolic processes of all living cells by acting as cofactors
in multiple enzyme systems that drive the oxidation or
“burning” of food and the production of energy.

Thiamine or B, is essential throughout life for tissue
respiration and energy production. Thiamine combines with
phosphorus to form the coenzyme thiamine pyrophosphate
(TPP), which functions as a cocarboxylase enzyme. TPP is
required for the oxidative decarboxylation of pyruvate to form
active acetate and acetyl coenzyme A, the central compound
of the Krebs cycle.

Clinical thiamine
primarily involves the nervous system and cardiovascular

signs of deficiency (beri-beri),
systems.

; Mild deficiencies produce mental confusion, muscular
weakness, peripheral paralysis, calf muscle tenderness,
muscle fatigue, emotional instability, loss of appetite, loss of
interest in daily tasks and general lethargy.

Chronic deficiencies of thiamine result in a polyneuritis
and congestive heart failure with edema and life-threatening
pulmonary edema.

Central nervous manifestations include
stiffness while walking and Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome,
an encephalopathy that is expressed as a loss of short term

system


 

memory (looks like early Alzheimer’s disease), disorientation,
nystagmus (rhythmic side to side movements of the eyes) and
ataxia (a type of staggering gait).

Simple consumption of thiamine and elimination of
sugar from the diet oftentimes will produce a rapid cure.

Riboflavin or vitamin B, was recognized in 1879 as a
yellow-green fluorescent pigment in milk whey. The biological
significance of this yellow pigment was not understood
until 1932 when a team of German investigators isolated
“Warburg’s yellow enzyme” from yeast and demonstrated
that the substance was necessary for the functioning of an
intracellular enzyme. The substance was synthesized in 1935
by Kuhn and named riboflavin.

Riboflavin is present in small amounts in milk, cheddar
and cottage cheese, organ meats (liver, heart, kidney, etc.),
eggs and green leafy vegetables.

Deficiency symptoms begin with soreness and burning
of the lips, mouth and tongue (glossitis); photophobia;
lacrimation; burning and itching of the eyes; and loss of visual
acuity.

Chronic deficiency of riboflavin results in the
development of cheilosis (fissuring of the lips) and angular
stomatitis (cracks in the skin at the corner of the mouth).

Niacin (nicotinic acid) or vitamin B,, its deficiency or
pellagra has been recognized for centuries where people used
corn as a staple diet - it was described by Casal of Spain in the
early 18th century and in Italy soon afterwards.

Pellagra was first described in the United States in the
early 1900s. It was initially thought to be an infectious disease
and the federal government built “retardation centers” across
the old Confederate states and treated pellagra patients in
much the same manner as tuberculosis patients. The United
States Bureau of Public Health eventually identified the

===== Screenshot (45) =====

root cause of pellagra to malnutrition and a corn-based diet
resulting in a niacin deficiency.

Niacin functions in the body as a component of the
coenzymes nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide (NAD) and
nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADP),
known as the pyridine nucleotides. The coenzymes are
necessary for the function of subcellular respiratory enzymes.
NAD is also used in glycogen synthesis.

The early stages of niacin deficiency show up as
muscular weakness, anorexia, indigestion and skin eruptions.
Severe deficiency of niacin produces classic pellagra which is
characterized by the “3 D’s” of pellagra (dermatitis, dementia
and diarrhea), tremors and sore tongue or “ beef tongue.”

The role of corn based diets in the precipitation of
pellagra is well known. There is very little niacin in corn
and the tryptophan in the corn is unavailable and can’t be
absorbed from the intestine, as a result there is no available
tryptophan to be converted into niacin.

In successful corn consuming
Mexico, Central America and South
soaked in lye and wood ashes overnight before it is consumed
- the alkali in the lye and wood ashes allows the tryptophan
to be absorbed and pellagra is rare; in India where sorghum
or millet is a common food, high levels of leucine in the

societies such as in
America the corn is

grains produce an increased requirement for vitamin B, and
several of the enzymes in the tryptophan-niacin pathway are
B, dependent, therefore a diet high in sorghum or millet can
produce pellagra.

Pantothenic acid or vitamin B. is part of coenzyme A,
which functions in two-carbon unit metabolism. Pantothenic
acid is required for the metabolism of carbohydrates, lipids
and proteins.

Deficiencies of pantothenic acid appear as dermatitis,
burning sensations of the feet, pain in the arms and legs, loss of
appetite, nausea and indigestion -many become quarrelsome,


 

sullen and depressed, fainting attacks and tachycardia (rapid
pulse rate) are common.

Pyridoxine or vitamin B, was identified in 1938 as a
fraction or separate entity of the vitamin “B-complex.” Vitamin
B, is a functional complex of three closely related chemical
compounds - pyridoxine, pyridoxamine and pyridoxal.

Deficiency of B, produces depression, nausea, vomiting,
seborrheic dermatitis, mucus membrane lesions, peripheral
neuritis, ataxia, hyperacusis, hyperirritability, altered mobility
and alertness, abnormal head movements and convulsions.

Treatment of tuberculosis patients with Isoniazid
produces an antagonistic reaction against pyridoxine function
which then results in the classical symptoms of deficiency.

“Morning sickness” commonly observed in the first
trimester of pregnancy is thought to be due to pyridoxine
deficiency.

Fifteen to 20 percent of women taking oral contraceptives
show the symptoms of pyridoxine deficiency manifested by
malaise, depression and glucose intolerance.

A deficiency syndrome for pyridoxine has been
identified in mentally retarded children that also have
convulsions due to an inborn error of B, metabolism. It is
thought that these children are unable to synthesize GABA;
correction of this syndrome requires daily ingestion of large
amounts of B, to prevent permanent retardation.

Children with cryptogenic epilepsy that appears at
several years of age will require large oral doses of vitamin B,
to improve the EEG and control the seizures.

Folacin or folic acid was identified as one of many
growth factors in bacteria and laboratory animals and in
the prevention and treatment of anemia. It was synthesized
in 1946 and quickly established as an essential nutrient for
humans.

Folic acid deficiencies may be the most common

===== Screenshot (46) =====

vitamin deficiency in humans. The deficiency of folic acid
produces poor growth, megablastic anemia, multiple blood
disorders, glossitis and gastrointestinal tract disturbances.

Protein malnutrition can impair the utilization and
function of folicin and conditions in which the requirements
are unusually great, such as pregnancy, hemolytic anemia,
leukemia, I lodgkin’s disease and drugs (methotrexate).

In pernicious anemia (B,, deficiency), folic acid will
produce a marked improvement in the anemia, however,
the gastrointestinal symptoms and neurologic lesions will
continue to progress - this is referred to as the “masking
effect” of folic acid.

Untreatedandunmanaged gluten-sensitive enteropathy
(celiac disease) can result in folic acid deficiency.

Neural tube defects (hyd roencephaloceole, spina bifida,
etc.) are extremely common in babies born to women who have
folic acid deficiency during the first trimester of pregnancy.

Cobalamin or vitamin B,, was isolated from a liver
extract in 1948 and shown to prevent and reverse pernicious
anemia. The vitamin B,, structure contains the heavy metal
cobalt, chelated in a large tetrapyrrole ring very similar in
structure to the porphyrin ring structure of hemoglobin and
chlorophyll.

The form of vitamin B,, originally isolated contained
cyanide (cyanocobalamin). Several cobalamin compounds
have vitamin B,, activity including cyanocobalamin and
hydroxycobalamin.

Cobalamin is poorly absorbed from the intestinal tract
unless the “intrinsic factor” (a mucoprotein enzyme called
“Castle's intrinsic factor”) is present in the gastric secretions.

The presence of hydrochloric acid is necessary to
split vitamin B,, from its peptide bonds. The intrinsic factor
combines with vitamin B,, in the food and in the bound form
becomes adsorbed to a receptor in the membranes of the


 

ileum, through which it is actively transported to the cells in
pinocytic vesicles and calcium is a required cofactor for this
transfer to take place.

There is an enterohepatic circulation of B,, that recycles
the vitamin from the bile and other intestinal secretions - this
conservation pathway is the reason why vitamin B,, deficiency
can take so long to develop in vegans. 7 ‘

Vitamin B,, is present in significant levels in animal
proteins. Vitamin B,, is found in liver, kidney, fresh whole milk,
eggs, cheese and muscle meats are rich sources. Pasteurized
and evaporated milk have lost 40 to 90% of vitamin B,,. While
a small amount of bacterial synthesis of vitamin B,, occurs in
the colon, distal to the terminal ileum, it can’t be absorbed.

Deficiencies of vitamin B,, can occur when the stomach
pH (acid level) rises toward neutral or alkaline levels and/or
when insufficient amounts of intrinsic factor are produced.
Surgical resection of or the by-passing of the intrinsic factor-secreting portions of the stomach (fundus and cardia) or of
the ileum (celiac disease, Crohn’s disease, etc.) can produce
vitamin B,, deficiency.

Vegans (vegetarians who consume only grains, fruit,
vegetables and nuts to the complete exclusion of animal products
and supplements) have an extremely low intake of vitamin B,,
and are very likely to develop a vitamin B,, deficiency and a
classic megaloblastic anemia and a classic and progressive
demyelinating neuropathy unless they supplement.

Biotin or vitamin H (coenzyme R) was first isolated in
1936 and synthesized in 1943. Originally biotin deficiency was
observed in chicks and rats that were fed raw egg whites, which
it was learned later, contained an “egg-white-injury” factor.

Avidin, a material found in egg white, combines with
biotin in the intestine making it unavailable for absorption.
It takes 24 raw egg whites to contribute enough avidin to
create a biotin deficiency in humans. Avidin is denatured and

===== Screenshot (47) =====

rendered harmless by normal cooking temperatures.
Symptoms of biotin deficiency in humans include skin
lesions over the nose and mouth, depression, hyperesthesia
and _ paresthesia particularly over the extremities, muscle
pain, conjunctivitis, blepharitis and a progressive loss of hair

and hair color.

Choline has been known for more than 100 years as
being essential and it has a vitamin-like coenzyme function.
Man can synthesize choline from the amino acids methionine
or serine in the presence of vitamin B,, and folacin. The rate at
which choline can be synthesized is insufficient to meet daily
requirements and for this reason it is considered an essential
nutrient.

The function of choline in humans is part of the
metabolism of and in the transport of fat from the liver. A
deficiency of choline can result ina fatty liver. Asa component
of phospholipids, choline functions in triglyceride transport
and in cell membrane structure. In the liver, choline forms
lecithin (phosphatidyl choline). During this process, fatty
acids are removed from the glycerides of the liver, thus
reducing the triglyceride level.

Choline is also a component of another phospholipid,
sphingomyelin, which is an important component of the
brain and peripheral nerves. As a precursor for acetylcholine,
choline is involved in the transmission of nerve impulses.

Deficiencies of choline result in a “fatty liver,” which
ina severe, form can land you ona liver transplant list. Large
doses of choline have proved to be beneficial for managing
and alleviating the symptoms of tardive dyskinesia and
Huntington’s disease in humans.

Inositol was identified as a vitamin in 1940. It
occurs abundantly in diets containing whole grains and
raw vegetables as inositol phospholipids and as phytic acid
(inositol hexaphosphate or “phytates”).


 

Phytic acid interferes with the absorption of calcium,
iron, zinc and other nutrients - on one hand a required nutrient
and on the other a negative in the diet.

Myoinositol, “muscle sugar,” has a chemical structure
that resembles glucose and is concentrated in the brain,
cerebrospinal fluid, skeletal muscle, heart muscle and other
tissues. Free inositol is found in especially high levels in the
organs of the male reproductive tract, particularly in semen.

Inositol has been shown to have a therapeutic role in
diabetic neuropathy.

Deficiencies of inositol will produce pathological fatty
liver disease.

Ascorbic acid or vitamin C is known to prevent and
cure scurvy. Scurvy was first described during the Crusades
and was common among soldiers and sailors until the Dutch
discovered that the consumption of citrus fruit and fresh
vegetables could prevent and cure the disease. British sailors
were oftentimes referred to as “limeys” because they carried
lemons (not limes) on their ships to prevent and cure scurvy.

The identification of ascorbic acid as the specific agent
that prevented and cured scurvy did not occur until the 20th
century. Pure ascorbic acid was isolated in 1928 by Albert
Szent-Gyorgyi, who discovered it in adrenal tissue, orange
and cabbage and identified it as hexuronic acid. It was not
until 1932 that C. Glenn King at the University of Pittsburgh
reisolated ascorbic acid from lemons that it was identified as
vitamin C and had the properties of preventing and curing
scurvy in guinea pigs.

After much fanfare, vitamin C supplementation has
been shown to reduce the rate, severity and duration of a cold
on the average by 20%.

A deficiency of vitamin C is characterized by general
weakness, poor appetite and growth, anemia, tenderness,
swollen and inflamed gums, bleeding gums, loose teeth,
osteoporosis, swollen wrist and ankle joints, shortness of

===== Screenshot (48) =====

breath, small pin-head hemorrhages (petechial), fractures,
wounds heal slowly, scars break open, secondary infections
are common, depression and neurotic disturbances including
hysteria.

Cigarette smoking increases the need for ascorbic acid
to about twice the normal requirement.

2) Fat soluble vitamins: A, D, E,and K

Fat soluble vitamins are related primarily as a result of
their being soluble in lipids and insoluble in water and they
are stored primarily in the bodies fat stores - this simple fact
is where their similarities end.

The fat soluble vitamins are absorbed along with other
common and essential dietary lipids and any health challenge
or health practice that interferes with the absorption of fats
(antibiotic use, gall bladder disease, celiac disease, dietary
fat deficiencies, high fiber diets, mineral oil
consumption during meals and overt deficiencies) interferes
with fat soluble vitamin absorption and function.

Retinol or vitamin A was the first fat soluble vitamin
be recognized. McCollum and Davis at the University
f Wisconsin and Osborne and Mendel at Yale University
re te ohte sd their discoveries almost simultaneously in 1913. They
observed that young weanling animals became unhealthy,
failed to thrive and grow when fed fat free diets.

Investigators also noted that those animals that lived
longer on fat free diets developed inflammation and infections
of the eyes - these conditions were quickly rever sed when fats
from butter or cod liver oil were added to the diet.

In 1924, Bloch, showed that
xerophthalmia in children was the same disease described in
1913 in laboratory rats, and that the disease could be prevented
or cured by adding liver, butter or cod liver oil to their diet.

Calciferol or vitamin D was recognized, in the Middle
Ages, as a prevention and cure for rickets, however, it was not
until WW I that the cause and effect of vitamin D deficiency
was recognized as the spec ific cause of rickets.

Vitamin D was separated from vitamin A in
by McCullum. In 1924 Steenbock and Hess discovered that

irradiationwithultravioletlightproducedantirachitic properties
to certain foods. In 1936, Windaus demonstrated that the natural

1922

prehormone found in the skin which converts to calciferol
when irradiated by UV light was 7-dehydrocholesterol.

In 1968, Blunt, De Luca and Schnoes discovered that
the metabolically active form of vitamin D was not calciferol
but rather 25-hydroxycholecalciferol (vitamin D,), which was
synthesized in the body from calciferol when the skin was
exposed to ultraviolet light or sun light.

In 2006 Dr. Michael Holick, a professor of medicine,
physiology and biophysics at Boston University said, “The
bottom line is, we now recognize that vitamin D, is important
for health for both children and adults and may help prevent
many serious chronic diseases.”
Holick’s studies have shown that deficiencies of
vitamin D, in adults results in an increase in risk of heart

attacks, increased mortality in breast and colon cancer


 

victims, increased risk of mortality in dialysis patients and an
increased risk of developing type II diabetes in children.

A large study reported in the June 8, 2007 issue of the
American Journal of Clinical Nutrition, is the first study in which
researchers significantly boosted - and measured - blood levels
of vitamin D, and then followed identical groups of patients
from start to finish.

“The findings, whichshow areductionin the risk of several
types of cancer by 60%, are a breakthrough of great medical and
public health importance,” said Cedric Garland, a prominent
vitamin D researcher at the University of California, San Diego.
“No other method to prevent cancer has been identified that has
such a powerful impact,” Garland continued.

The danger of vitamin D, deficiency is very high in the
21st century as people have been directed by their doctor and
the government to avoid the sun, spend little or no time in the
sun and when they do they are instructed cover themselves
with sun blockers, long sleeves, gloves and hats.

Tocopherol or vitamin E was first identified by Evans
and Bishop in 1922, when they observed infertility in rats
could be reversed when they were fed a substance isolated
from vegetable oils. Vitamin E was chemically identified in
1938 and named tocopherol after tokos from the Greek which
translates to “offspring.”

Deficiency of vitamin E can produce a hemolytic
anemia (rupture of red blood cells), especially when diets are
too high in unsaturated fatty acids and iron. Deficiency also
produces muscle weakness, accumulation of ceroid lipofuscin
in smooth muscle, liver spots in the skin and creatinuria as
seen in muscular dystrophy and patients with celiac disease,
cystic fibrosis, sprue, etc.

Additional symptoms of vitamin E deficiency include
miscarriage, fetal reabsorption, testicular atrophy, birth
defects, muscular dystrophy, hemolytic anemia, myocardial
degeneration, encephalomalacia and liver necrosis.

Vitamin E Function:

Antioxidant activity which prevents the formation of

peroxides.

Prevents oxidation damage of polyunsaturated fatty

acids.

Enhances the activity of vitamin A by preventing its
oxidation.

Protects cellular and mitochondrial membranes from
oxidation.


 

The ability of vitamin E to protect membrane lipids from
peroxidation has been related to its anti-aging properties.

A relationship between vitamin E, selenium,
glutathione peroxidase, cystine and polyunsaturated fats in
the cell membrane has been clearly demonstrated.

Selenium, which activates glutathione peroxidase, destroys
peroxides after they are produced but before they can cause
cell membrane damage. f

Sulfur-containing amino acids, such as _ cystine,

maintain optimal tissue glutathione levels. ;

Phylloquinone or vitamin K is the naturally occurring
form of the synthetic fat soluble vitamin menadione (K).
In 1935, Dam in Copenhagen observed a fatal hemorrhagic
disease in newly hatched chickens fed a laboratory diet that
contained all of the known essential nutrients. When hog liver
fat extract or alfalfa was added to the diet normal clotting time
was restored.

In the liver, vitamin K is required as an essential cofactor
for an enzyme (carboxylase) that converts specific glutamic
acid residues of certain proteins to a new amino acid, alpha-carboxyglutamic acid (Gla) in the completed proteins. These
proteins include the vitamin K-dependent blood clotting
factors prothrombin (factor II) and factors VII, IX and X.

Several Gla-containing proteins are found as well.
The vitamin K-dependent Gla-containing protein called
“osteocalcin” is now known to modulate calcium deposition
into bone matrix - vitamin K is as essential to healthy bone
maintenance as vitamin D. It is this calcium-binding action of
Gla that gives prothrombin its unique place in coagulation.

The coumarin anticoagulant drugs (warfarin and
dicumarol) act to prevent coagulation by antagonizing the
action of vitamin K.

The deficiency of vitamin K is uncommon except when
an individuals diet is low in green leafy vegetables and the

===== Screenshot (51) =====

growth of intestinal flora has been inhibited by the use of
antibiotics or there is a fat absorption problem.

3) Flavanols (Bioflavonoids): Epicatechins and polyphenols.

Flavonoids or vitamin P commonly found in cocoa,
tea and wine are now considered to be the third category of
vitamins and are officially added to the water soluble and fat
soluble groups by Harvard Medical School.

Norman Hollenberg from Harvard Medical School
and Brigham and Women’s Hospital reports, “that the island-dwelling Kuna people who drink the flavanol-rich cocoa,
have a 10% lower risk of four of the five most common killer
diseases: stroke, heart failure, cancer and diabetes.”

“If these observations predict the future, then we can
say without blushing that they are among the most important
observations in the history of medicine,” said Hollenberg.

Flavonoids were first used clinically in 1936 by Szent-Gyorgyi to treat patients with diseases characterized by
increased permeability and fragility of the capillary wall that
resulted in hemorrhages.

The flavonoids found in “dark chocolate have been
found to lower blood pressure,” says Dr. Dirk Taubert from
the University of Cologne, Germany - an extensive report was
published in the August 27, 2003 issue of The Journal of the
American Medical Association.

Minerals

Minerals refer to elements in their simple inorganic
form. Unlike the carbon-based essential nutrients, which
plants can manufacture, plants can not manufacture elemental
and mineral nutrients and therefore man and animals must


either serendipitously or consciously consume them.

The Hunzas are legendary for long healthful lives. Their
health and longevity has been attributed to several things
including the minerals found in the “glacial milk” produced
by the Ultar Glacier grinding up rock and the resultant rock
silt being placed in their terraces through irrigation and the
compost (wood ashes, manure, kitchen wastes, etc.) regularly
used to fertilize their gardens.

After much consideration we realized that the people of
Hunza had a very unique combination of sources of minerals
and that only 0.00001% of humans on earth live near glaciers
and only 0.0001% of humans on earth live on flood plains that
are inundated with flood water every year with the resultant
silt (mineral dust) deposits revitalizing the soil - these facts
begged the question, “Where and how were the greater masses
of humans on earth getting their daily source of minerals.”

A determined search revealed that humans universally
used wood ashes (plant minerals - not carbon), peat moss ash,
rice straw ash, manure ash, kelp ash and kelp ash and sea salt
mixes as their basic and daily source of minerals. World wide,
humans used plant minerals from various and oftentimes
multiple sources (Hunzas - glacial milk and wood ashes;
Okinawa - wood ashes, rice straw ashes, kelp ash; etc.) for
fertilizing their gardens, thereby getting minerals indirectly that
were taken up by the fruits, vegetables and grains and adding
plant minerals directly into their food as “culinary ashes.”

There are three major categories of minerals known in
human and animal nutrition:

Major minerals
Trace minerals

Rare earths

Chapter 5

Minerals - The Staff of Life

Minerals were used proactively as nutritional
supplements some 50,000 to 200,000 years ago. Originally the
source of minerals were the minerals in plant and animal food
sources and then at the start of “the Great Leap Forward”

humans began to proactively put plant minerals derived from
wood, grass, kelp and manure ashes onto and into their food
(“culinary ashes”).

The ancient civilizations of China, India and Egypt
learned to use pure elements and ground up jewel dust
(minerals) as medicines 6,000 to 10,000 B.C. to treat and cure
specific deficiency diseases.

Nutritional minerals never occurred in a uniform
blanket over the crust of the earth. These essential elements
occurred in veins like chocolate in chocolate ripple ice cream
or veins of gold and silver. Neighboring farms will often
have different varieties and amounts of minerals in their soil
- therefore the farmers and their families on one property
containing many minerals in large quantities could have few
diseases and live to the age of 103 years, while their neighbor
might have few minerals on their property with the result
they would be sickly and only lived to be 48 years of age - not
genetics, but rather a mineral deficient microenvironment.

As a result of the wide regional variation in mineral


content of the soil, diseases of deficiency appeared in
regional patterns. Iodine deficiency (goiter) occurred in
communities living at a distance from the sea; Keshan disease
(cardiomyopathy) caused by a selenium deficiency occurred
in Keshan Province, China; phosphate deficiency (pica) was
common in South Africa; copper deficiency (enzootic ataxia)
was common in Australia, etc.

Dr. Willhelm Heinrich Schuessler (1821 - 1898) of
Oldenburg, Germany, cremated the remains of indigent
patients in his hospital. He performed an analysis of the
resulting ashes using the limited techniques of the day. He
was able to identify 12 mineral elements which he called
“Tissue Salts”(“cell salts”) in the human ashes, and by
relating the mineral content of each body to the disease the
patient had suffered and died from he identified and created
12 “Biochemic Tissue Salts” or “cell salts” with which he then
successfully treated live patients who suffered from the same
diseases.

Schuessler published his findings in his classic book,
Shortened Therapeutics, in 1873.

Only when one looks at a USDA data base that
compares the level of vitamins and minerals in certain common
foods between 1975 and 2004, do we truly understand how
depleted our foods have become since we gave up the back
yard gardens and wood ashes (plant minerals).

A simple barometer of the nutritional value of our diet
is to look at the health of immigrants. We can look at what
happens to people who leave their third world birth place
and come to the U.S. for a new life. In a study published in
the Journal of the American Medical Association, December 15,
2004, it was reported that, “The longer you live in America,
the more likely you are to be obese.” (Remember - obesity is a
mineral deficiency disease - not a disease of excess)
The study examined the obesity rates among

===== Screenshot (54) =====

immigrants compared with native born Americans. It found
that immigrants, regardless of race or country of origin, living
in the USA for more than 15 years resulted in an average of
a 10 pound weight gain. These events are the result of being
placed in the mineral deficient “Feedlot Syndrome” fostered
by the American “four food group” mentality, combined with
the loss of their historical “culinary” and garden ashes (plant
minerals) without any conscious effort to take responsibility
and supplement with plant minerals to replace the loss.

The most significant development in the trace element
field of study in the last 50 years of the 20th century was the
recognition that trace mineral elements have a fundamental
role in health maintenance for humans and that optimal intakes
from food sources can no longer be taken for granted.

Linus Pauling, two time Nobel laureate, and author of the
great work, The Nature of the Chemical Bond, relegated minerals
to secondary importance to vitamins, recommending only eight
to eleven minerals (calcium, magnesium, phosphorous, iron,
zinc, copper, manganese, chromium, molybdenum, iodine and
selenium) and then only at minimal doses - an example was
selenium for which he insisted the upper safe limit was 15 mcg
(today the recommended safe limit for selenium is 200 to 1,000
mcg for an adult). Pauling died of prostate cancer at age 94 - a
common result of a selenium deficiency.

The essential role of trace elements in the support and
maintenance of the human immune system in its fight against
bacteria, viruses, fungi and cancer, degenerative diseases and
birth defects are well documented. The mode of action and
the tissue concentrations of trace elements vary considerably.
The only common denominator of trace minerals is that they
are all found in exceedingly low levels in healthy tissue.
They are usually present in levels of parts per million (ppm);
micrograms/ gram (u/g); or as low as parts per billion (ppb)
or nanograms per gram (ng/g).

The concentration of trace elements in tissue or
requirement levels does not represent their relative importance


as an essential nutrient. The concentration of trace elements
and their functional concentration must be continuously
maintained within narrow physiological limits if the functional
and structural fabric of the tissues and organs of the animal
and human body are to be maintained for optimal fertility,
development, growth, health, energy and longevity.

Higher species such as birds, mammals, and humans
have a “homeostatic” mechanism that maintains tissue
concentrations of trace minerals at optimal levels at their
physiological sites at the cost of trace mineral reservoirs.

Almost all trace elements function as catalysts for a
cellular or sub-cellular enzyme system. Enzymes are simply
proteins that do work and in their role as catalysts their function
ranges from that of a weak ionic (pH affects) modulator to
highly specific metalloenzyme (“metallic finger”) functions
for DNA and RNA.

In metalloenzymes, the metal atom cannot normally
be substituted by any other trace element. Although Vallee
demonstrated that cobalt and cadmium can replace zinc in
certain enzymes with some change in substrate specificity but
the enzyme is not deactivated!

Because of the frequent wide disparity between
the absolute and the dietary requirements (results from
different intestinal absorption efficiencies for individuals and
individual elements) the most efficient form of trace mineral
supplement, such as plant derived colloidal minerals should
be consciously and proactively consumed.

For the metallic form of iodine the ratio of absorption
is almost 1:1; for iron its 1:10 and for chromium its 1:100.

The RDA (Recommended Daily Allowance) and
RDI (Recommended Daily Intake) and other dietary
recommendations for trace elements are conceived by the
National Science Foundation to “meet the needs of practically
all healthy people” - they are in reality not applicable for people
with increased energy requirements, “health challenges” or
full blown disease states.

===== Screenshot (55) =====
 


More than 79 elements and minerals have been detected
in animal and human tissue (i.e. - blood, liver, muscle, brain,
bones, connective tissue, etc.) in the 20th and 21st centuries. Their
presence in human tissue fulfills the first criteria for designating
an element or mineral an “essential nutrient.” Literally thousands
of additional studies provide support for designating 60 of these
79 elemental nutrients to be essential nutrients.

These studies were performed on animals at every stage
of life: pregnant, newborn, suckling, weanling, mature and
aged. These studies were carried out on animals of virtually
every description: laboratory mice, rats, rabbits, hamsters,
guinea pigs, dogs cats, pigs, sheep, cattle, horses, primates,
chickens, turkeys, ducks, hundreds of species of captive wild
animals, etc.

Minerals are required by humans for building and
maintaining structural components and enzymatic and
biochemical cofactors that facilitate the physiological workings
of the human cell, tissue, organ, system and body - nothing in
the body can function without mineral cofactors.

On the average there are ten deficiency diseases that
can occur in the absence of each essential mineral, others, like
calcium can produce as many as 147 different diseases when
the body is deficient.

Initial Depletion Phase

Initial depletion of trace elements in animals and
humans is characterized by changes only in the metabolism
of the element itself in response to a suboptimal intake, the
individual target tissue compensating so that no disturbance
of biological structure or function are detectable by means of
a test. However, the individual usually complains of “lack of

energy.”


Increased intestinal absorption efficiency, and/or
reduction in excretion; and gradual diminishment of body
reserves occur. Pica, or the “munchies” appear in a mild form
in this early stage of deficiency. This phase of trace mineral
deficiency returns to normal (including the elimination of
the “munchies”) quickly following the return of an optimal
intake.

Compensated Metabolic Phase

The compensated metabolic phase is characterized by
a reduction of certain specific biochemical functions, such
as trace mineral dependent enzyme reactions or receptor
sites. The “homeostatic” mechanism becomes ineffective
in maintaining the normal trace mineral concentrations at
receptor sites causing a reduction in biochemical function
(i.e. - low blood sugar, irregular heart beat, loss of hair color,
weight gain, mild obesity, etc.). The “munchies” become
consciously evident in the individuals everyday life - they
become “chocoholics” or “cola addicts.” Supplementation
with minerals will, quickly reverse the symptoms, behavior
changes, including pica, and the more extreme “munchies.”

Decompensated Metabolic Phase

The decompensated phase of trace element deficiency is
characterized by the appearance of serious clinical symptoms
or defects in the metabolic (i.e. - pre-diabetes, significant weight
gain, obesity), cognitive (i.e. - learning disabilities, ADD,
ADHD, autism, etc.), emotional (i.e. - DR. Jekyll/Mr. Hyde,
“Bad Seeds,” depression, manic depression, bipolar, anger,

===== Screenshot (56) =====
 


rage, anxiety attacks, panic attacks, etc.), developmental (i.e.
- dyslexia, gay orientation, spina bifida, neural tube defects,
cleft lip, cleft palate, heart defects, cerebral palsy, Downs
Syndrome, etc.), structural (i.e. - muscular dystrophy, morbid
obesity, etc.), fatigue and loss of work stamina and reduction
of the longevity of a species (i.e. - average lifespan of an M.D.
in the U.S. is 56 years of age).

Obsessive pica and the “munchies” are obvious, even
to the casual observer - the affected individual is aware that
they can not help themselves in curbing their cravings and
appetites by means of conscious will alone.

Clinical Phase

The clinical phase of trace mineral deficiency is
characterized by the onset of full blown disease states and
even death (i.e. - cardiomyopathy, diabetes, aneurysm,
arthritis, periodontal disease, degenerative disc disease,
cataracts, goiter, morbid obesity, etc.). For most trace
mineral deficiencies in the clinical phase, correct diagnosis
by the “orthodox” physician is almost nonexistent; the cure,
however, is accomplished simply by the replacement of the
specific trace element.

A correct diagnosis can lead to a remarkably rapid
positive clinical response ranging from 48 hours to 30 days for
recognizable results; including the loss of one pound of excess
weight per day on a proper mineral supplement program;
others do not reach full return to normal after permanent
biochemical (i.e. - gout), chromosomal (Downs Syndrome) or
gross physical damage (i.e. - cataracts) has occurred.


Metallic Minerals

Metallic minerals include egg shell, oyster shell,
calcium carbonate, limestone, coral calcium, dolomite,
bentinite, inorganic mineral salts, sea water, Great Salt Lake
minerals, mineral oxides (i.e. - iron oxide, copper oxide, zinc
oxide, etc.), vortex waters, sea bed minerals, “soils” (which
are usually clay), sea bed clay, clay, “rock flour,” and various
antacids such as Rollaids and Tums.

Typically, metallic minerals are found in tablets,
powders, capsules and a few are found as liquids (i.e. - calcium
gluconate solutions, zinc gluconate solutions, etc.), lactates,
sulphates, carbonates, oxides, sea water, etc.

Metallic minerals, despite wild claims to the contrary
are only 8 to 12% biologically available to younger animals
and humans; after attaining the age of 35 to 40 years, the
availability of metallic minerals for humans plummets to 3 to
5%.

A typical metallic mineral supplement, either alone or
part of a multiple is calcium carbonate. Calcium carbonate can
be purchased in 1,000 mg tablets or capsules, which breaks
down to 400 mg of metallic calcium and 600 mg of carbonate.
Two 1,000 mg calcium carbonate capsules or tablets do not
give you 2,000 mg of calcium - they only supply 800 mg of
elemental calcium and at a 10% availability rate you will net
only 80 mg of available calcium. Therefore, to get 2,000 mg of
usable calcium from a 1,000 mg capsule or tablet of calcium
carbonate, you would have to take 25 capsules each day! A
100 capsule bottle of calcium carbonate will therefore only
last 4 days.
