Chelated Minerals

Chelated (Key-lay-ted) minerals were an invention of
the livestock industry. In the 1940s, the livestock industry, to
ensure maximum availability of dietary and supplemental
minerals fed to livestock being fattened for market, created a
new form of minerals.

The original chelating agent use was calcium EDTA, a
man-made amino acid that was created by the Germans just
prior to WWI to antidote arsenic and lead exposure in chemical
warfare attacks (calcium EDTA is used today for intravenous
chelation therapy to open up obstructed arteries).

The term chelated, from the Latin, literally means “claw,”
but in more modern times it is used to describe the process by
which an amino acid, protein or enzyme (enzymes are proteins
that do work) is wrapped around the mineral molecule which
enhances the bioavailability of the metallic mineral.

Chelated minerals are approximately 40 to 65%
bioavailable, a 400% increase over the availability of metallic
minerals - certainly, a significant improvement over the


amounts elemental minerals that are absorbed.

Chelated minerals are easily recognized on the label
of a multiple vitamin/ mineral supplements or free standing
single supplement such as selenomethionine, chromium
picolinate, or sometimes as an “amino acid chelate.”

Colloidal Minerals

Colloidal (Koal-oid-ul) chemistry, while not a new
concept, is not widely known about or understood by the
general public. Simply s said, a colloid refers to a substance
that exists as ultra fine particles (Angstrom unit) that are
suspended in a medium of different matter. The colloidal
state is the state of a solute (mineral or other substance such
as a paint pigment) in a solution when its molecules do not
separate into atoms as with a true solution (sodium chloride
or salt separates into separate sodium and chloride atoms
while in solution), but rather the colloidal particle remains
grouped together to form solute particles.

The presence of these inorganic colloidal particles,
which are approximately one hundred-thousandth to one ten-millionth of a centimeter in diameter (about 400 thousandths
to four millionths of an inch), can often be detected by means
of an electron microscope. As a result of the grouping of the
molecules, a solute in the colloidal state cannot passively pass
through a suitable semi-permeable membrane and gives rise
to a negligible osmotic pressure (they will pass through filter
paper), depression of freezing point and elevation of boiling
point effects.

These ultra-fine particles of the colloid are just barely
larger than most molecules and are so small that they cannot
be seen with the naked eye-about one billion of these colloid
particles would fit into 0.01 cubic inch.

===== Screenshot (58) =====
 


The “solution” part of a colloid provides a solid, gas or
liquid medium in which the colloid particles are suspended.
The suspended particles in a colloid can also be a solid, gas or
liquid.
In 1925,

categories:

H. Freudlich classified solutions into three

1. True solutions



2. Colloidal solutions

3. Emulsions and suspensions

The four part method of classifying solutions is as
follows:

1. Identify particle size
2. Determine the presence of Brownian movement
(random movement of particles suspended in liquids or
gases resulting from the impact of molecules of the fluid
surrounding the particles).

3. Ability to pass through filter paper
4. Level of solubility

In 1975, S.S. Voyutsky (a Russian) wrote the classic
text on colloidal chemistry. Voyutsky referred to solutions as
“molecular dispersion systems” and “heterogeneous highly
dispersed colloid systems.” pn

The exact point between molecular and the colloidal
degrees of dispersion cannot be established because the
transition from molecularly dispersed systems to coarsely
dispersed systems is a continuum.


 

A colloidal system must have three basic characteristics:

1. It must be heterogeneous (consists of dissimilar
ingredients or constituents).

2. The system must be multi-phasic (i.e. - solid/liquid,
gas/ liquid, etc.)

3. The particles must be insoluble (do not dissolve in
the solution).

Each one of these classifications interacts with the
others to give colloids their unique qualities. The interesting
thing about colloids is that they remain heterogeneous, multiphasic and insoluble at different concentrations as long as a
larger number if not all of the particles are within a range of
sizes of colloids (1nm to 100nm) known as “nano-technology.”

The molecular groups or particles of the colloid solute
carry a resultant electrical charge, generally the same sign
(negative) for all of the particles. A small percentage of these
inorganic colloids will pass through the intestine of a living
animal or human because a natural chelating process takes
place in the gut in the presence of protein-containing food.

Inorganic colloidal material which readily passes
through filter paper may be separated from dissolved
substances, such as starch, sugar or salt, by placing the
mixture of mineral colloid and non-colloid in a parchment
shell surrounded by distilled water. The inorganic colloids are
“too large” to pass through the membrane, but the molecules
of salt, starch and sugar or any other dissolved substances
pass readily through the semi-permeable membrane (they
separate into individual atoms or very small molecules). This
kind of separation process is referred to as “dialysis.”

In the process of digestion, the inorganic minerals in
food or supplements soon become inorganic colloids and as an
inorganic colloid they cannot penetrate the intestinal wall to

===== Screenshot (59) =====
 


enter the blood stream. In the presence of amino acids, a small
percentage of the inorganic colloids form chelated minerals
and organic colloids which are able to be dialyzed through the
mucus membranes of the intestinal walls into the blood stream
- this bio-available mineral state is known as a “crystalloid.”

Crystalloids or organic colloids readily pass
through cell walls, while inorganic colloids are “too large.”
Additionally, we must remember that in the living organism
there are other physiological forces at work, notably “active
transport,” which interferes with or modifies the expected
osmotic phenomenon.

Active-transport, refers to the physical act of the
intestinal mucosal cell reaching out with a pseudopodium,
engulfing and transporting the particle into itself and then
transported into the adjacent blood stream. In fact almost all
absorbed food and nutrients are absorbed into the human
body by active transport rather than by a passive trip through
a semi-permeable membrane.

Colloidal mineral supplements and commercial colloids are found in four different forms:

1. Unprotected colloids are made of bare “rock flour,”
this is the form of inorganic colloids found in the sea bed
minerals, clays, “soils,” and “glacial milk.” This form of
inorganic colloid is in fact a metallic mineral and is only
efficiently available to plants when there is a healthy soil
population of bacteria and fungi.

2. The second type of mineral colloid is found in living
systems of bacteria, fungi, green plants (food crops), animals
and humans and is coated with a water-loving (hydrophilic)
substance such as gelatin, albuminoids or collagen. This
coating protects the now “organic mineral colloid” and allows
it to be a crystalloid for absorption, storage and physiological
uses and thus maximizing its bioavailability up to 98%.

3. The third type of organic mineral colloids has a protective coating of carbon with a molecular chain length
of 10 to 12 carbon atoms. This type of colloid is also found
in bacteria, fungi, plants (including some forms of petrified
wood), animals and humans and is thought to be the most
stable form of natural organic mineral colloid.

4. The fourth type of mineral colloid is not found in
nature, but rather is manufactured industrially by coating the
metallic colloid with sulfated castor oil (lipophilic or fat or
oil loving) to form commercial detergents.

The following list of elements and minerals is presented
in alphabetical order according to the chemical symbols of
each:

Ac- Actinium originates in igneous rocks and is usually
found at extremely low concentrations of 5.5 x 10% ppm.
Actinium is readily absorbed by plant roots, however, very
little is transported to the stem, leaves, and shoots of the plant.

Actinium is a metal and like radium it glows in the
dark. Its name is taken from the Greek word aktinos, which
translates to “ray” or “beam.”

There are 26 known isotopes of actinium and all are
radioactive to some degree. Its chemical properties resemble
those of the rare earth element lanthanum.

Actinium accumulates in and presumably has metabolic
functions in the bones and liver.

Ag-Silver originates from igneous rocks and
sedimentary rocks. It’s found at the rate of 0.07 ppm in rocks,
0.01 ppm in soils, 0.00013 ppm in fresh water and 0.0003
ppm in sea water. It is present in marine algae at 0.25 ppm,
land plants from 0.06 ppm to 1.4 ppm in accumulator plants
growing near silver ore. Epiogonum ovalifolium is a silver
accumulator and silver indicator plant.

===== Screenshot (60) =====
 


Silver is found at 3.0 to 11.0 ppm in marine animals,
0.05 to 0.7 ppm in terrestrial mammals and at 0.16 to 0.8 ppm
in muscle tissue. Even tortoise shell contains silver at 0.05 to
0.7 ppm.

Silver is one of the few metals found in its free state
in nature and was known in ancient times and derives its
chemical symbol, Ag, from the Latin word argentums, which
translates to silver.

Silver has been used for money since biblical times and
so it is often referred to as a “coinage” metal.

Silver has been employed in human health care and the
search for immortality, since the time of the Chinese alchemist
8,000 B.C. Many feel that silver is in fact an essential element,
not because it is required by an enzyme system, but rather
because it serves as a systemic disinfectant and immune
system stimulator.

Sir Malcom Morris reported in the British Medical Journal
(May 12, 1917) that colloidal silver is free from the drawbacks of
other preparations of silver, viz. pain caused and discoloration
of the skin. Instead of producing irritation, it has a distinctly
soothing effect. It rapidly subdues inflammation and promotes
healing of the lesions. It can be used with remarkable results
in enlarged prostate with irritation of the bladder, in pruritus
ani and perineal eczema, and in hemorrhoids.

J. Mark Hovell reported in the British Medical Journal
(December 15, 1917) that, “colloidal silver has been found to
be beneficial for permanently restoring the patentcy of the
Eustachian tubes and for reducing nasopharyngeal catarrh.
Colloidal silver has also been used successfully in septic
conditions of the mouth (including pyorrhea alveolysis -
Rigg’s disease), throat (including tonsillitis and quincies),
ear (including Menier’s symptoms and closure to Valsalva’s
inflation), and generalized septicemia, leucorrhea, cystitis,
whooping-cough and shingles.”

Taken internally, the particles of colloidal silver are
resistant to the action of dilute acids and alkalis of the stomach


 

and intestine. Consequently they continue their catalytic
action and pass into the intestine unchanged.

T.H. Anderson Wells reported in Lancet (February
16, 1918) that a preparation of colloidal silver was “used
intravenously in a case of puerperal septicemia without any
irritation of the kidneys and with no pigmentation of the
skin.”

Silver sulfadiazine (Silvadene, Marion Laboratories) is
used in 70% of the burn centers in America. Discovered by
D. Charles Fox, Columbia University, silver sulfadiazine has
been used successfully to treat syphilis, cholera, and malaria;
it also stops the Herpes simplex virus responsible for cold sores
and fever blisters.

Silver is an anti-bacterial, anti-viral, anti-fungal, anti-metabolite that disables specific enzymes that microorganisms
use for respiration. Silver is such an efficient bactericide that
our great-grandmothers put silver dollars into fresh milk to
keep bacterial counts down and to keep it from spoiling at
room temperature.

One of the most common uses of silver in orthodox
medicine is in “silver fillings” in dentistry. A typical “filling”
is a mix of silver powder and mercury. The silver dissolves in
the mercury to form an “amalgam.”

The mercury portion of the amalgam has been the
subjects of many law suites because of its known toxic effects
on the brain (selenium is the specific antidote for mercury
poisoning).

Humans can consume 400 mcg of silver per day. A
silver “deficiency” results in an impaired immune system. In
The Body Electric, Dr. Robert Becker identified a relationship
between low levels of tissue and dietary silver and the rate
of illness. He stated, “Silver deficiency was responsible for
the improper functioning of the immune system” and “silver
does more than just kill disease causing organisms, It was
also causing major growth stimulation (another criteria for
essentiality) of injured tissue.”

===== Screenshot (61) =====
 


Human fibroblast (stem) cells were able to multiply at a
greater rate, producing large numbers of primitive, embryonic
cells in wounds. These stem cells are able to differentiate into
whatever cell types that are necessary to heal the wound or
replace tissue mass.

Dr. Bjorn Nordstrom of the Carolinska Institute, Sweden
has used silver in his alternative cancer therapy programs.
According to Science Digest (Silver: Our Mightiest Germ
Fighter. March, 1978) silver is an antibiotic. Silver kills over 650
disease-causing organisms. Resistant strains fail to develop as
they do with prescribed antibiotics. Silver is absolutely non-toxic to humans at standard rates of consumption. CAUTION:
chronic overdoses can cause a whole body tattoo that produces
a permanent dusky blue discoloration of the skin.

Al-Aluminum is found in igneous rocks at 5,000 ppm,
82,000 ppm in shales, 25,000 ppm in sandstone, 4,200 ppm in
limestone, and 71,000 ppm in clay. Aluminum represents 12%
of the earth’s crust and is the third most abundant element in the
earth’s crust and the most abundant metal in the earth’s crust.
Aluminum is nontoxic and is found in high concentrations in
all plants grown in the soil, including common organically
grown food crops such as beans, tomatoes, squash, apples,
bananas, tea, wine, buckwheat and wheat.
Aluminum is found in large biological quantities in every
plant grown in the soil. You can’t eat any grain, vegetable,
fruit, or nut or drink any natural water source or juice without
taking in large quantities of aluminum.

The aluminum found in plants is organically bound
colloidal aluminum and appears not to have any negative or
toxic affect and in fact appears to meet all of the requirements
to be designated an essential nutrient.

rice, oats,

Acid soils yield the highest amounts of soil aluminum
to plants. Aluminum is found in marine plants at 60 ppm and
is especially high in plankton and red algae. Aluminum is


 

found in terrestrial plants at an average of 500 ppm, with a
range of 0.5 to 4,000 ppm. It is found in marine mammals at
19 to 50 ppm. In mammals, the highest levels are found in hair
and lungs.

Aluminum is found naturally in significant levels in
all healthy plant based foods grown in soil including grains,
vegetables, nuts and fruit. The amount of aluminum in infant
formulas averages 0.9 mg/kg of body weight per week for
milk based formulas and 1.1 mg/kg of body weight per
week for soy-based infant formulas. Some soy-based infant
formulas contain four times the average levels found in the
survey which was conducted and reported July 16, 2008 by
European scientists who were rerunning a 2007 report that
had been published by WHO.

Aluminum’s known biological function is to activate
the enzyme succinic dehydrogenase. Deficiencies of aluminum
created in laboratory diets results in congenital limb defects
(non-genetic birth defects) in laboratory animals and cirrhosis
of the liver in mature animals. Aluminum supplementation
increases the survival rates of newborns, and according to
professor Gerhard Schrauzer, former professor and chairman
of the department of chemistry at UCSD, is “probably an
essential mineral for human nutrition.”

A single faulty study over 35 years ago suggested a link
between chronic aluminum exposure and Alzheimer’s disease.
In a subsequent study that appeared November 5, 1992 in the
journal Nature, Frank Watt, et al (Oxford University) used a
highly accurate technique to quantify the levels of aluminum
in the brains of Alzheimer’s patients. To their surprise, they
found the same levels of aluminum in the brains of the non-Alzheimer’s controls as they did in their Alzheimer’s patients.
The June 1997 issue of the Berkley Newsletter reported that
there is no link between Alzheimer’s disease and aluminum.

In November 2007, the Federal Institute for Risk
Assessment issued an updated opinion on the risk of
developing Alzheimer’s through consuming food with

===== Screenshot (62) =====
 


aluminum in it, and they maintained that there is no scientific
proof of any risk of developing Alzheimer’s disease from the
consumption of aluminum found in consumer products.

Am-Americum is a radioactive isotope and has a half
life of 7,950 years. Americum is a transition element, one
of the elements that follows uranium in the periodic chart.
Like all of the transition elements, it is a man made element.
Americum was discovered in 1944 by Glenn T. Seaborg at
the Metallurgical Laboratory at the University of Chicago, a
laboratory that became the Argonne National Laboratory.

Americum-241 emits both alpha particles (helium
nuclei) and gamma rays (similar to X rays) and has a half-life
of 470 years. It is used in home smoke detectors, in which
alpha particles emitted by this isotope ionize the surrounding
air by stripping electrons from gas molecules. Ionized air is a
relatively good conductor of electricity; smoke particles in the
air reduce its electrical conductivity and generate a signal that
sets off the alarm.

Americum accumulates in mammalian bone.

Ar-Argon is found in igneous rocks at 3.0 to 5.0 ppm
and can be used to date ancient rocks using the potassium/
argon dating system. It is found in fresh water and sea water at
0.06 ppm and mammalian blood at 0.75 ppm and constitutes
about one percent of the earth’s atmosphere making it the
third most abundant gas in the air. Argon is a “noble” gas
that is chemically inert, is colorless and odorless.

In the year 2000, a group of Finnish researchers
demonstrated that in spite of its lack of chemical reactivity it
was possible to combine argon with fluorine and hydrogen
and form fluorohydride, or HArF.

Argon, discovered in 1894 by the English physicist
Lord Rayleigh and the Scottish chemist William Ramsay, was
the first noble gas to be identified. Rayleigh was awarded the
1904 Nobel Prize in physics for the discovery. They identified


 

the new element by means of the light it gave off when an
electrical discharge was passed through the gas. They called it
argon, translated from the Greek word argos, meaning “lazy”
or “inactive.”

Argon gas is used to fill incandescent bulbs to prevent
corrosion of the tungsten filament; it is used in the detector
probe of Geiger counters to detect radiation; and it is the
decay product of an important radioisotope used for dating
rock samples. The isotope is potassium-40, a naturally
occurring radioactive isotope with an extremely long half-life
of 1.25 billion years. Argon is formed as the product of the
disintegration of the radioactive potassium - the potassium-argon dating system. Because potassium is present in many
rocks, an analysis of the amount of argon present in the rock
will effectively reveal the date at which the rock solidified.

As-Arsenic is found in igneous rock at 0.0 to 8.0 ppm,
shale at 13.0 ppm, sandstone and limestone at 1.0 ppm, fresh
water at 0.0004 ppm, seawater at 0.003 ppm, and soils at 6.0
ppm. Argentina and New Zealand actually have toxic levels
of arsenic in some soil regions. Arsenic also accumulates in
marine plants at the rate of 30.0 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.02
ppm, marine animals at 0.005 to 0.3 ppmand terrestrial animals
at a rate of less than 0.2 ppm with the arsenic concentrating in
the hair and nails.

Arsenic is often thought of as a semimetal or metalloid.
Although a small quantity of arsenic does occur in nature in
its free form, most arsenic is found in a number of mineral
compounds such as realgar and orpiment - both compounds
are mixes of arsenic and sulfur. The history of arsenic dates
back to ancient China, the Greeks and Romans. Its name is
thought to have been derived from the Latin arsenicum for
orpiment, a common, widely used gold-like yellow pigment.

Arsenic was determined to be an essential nutrient
in 1947; the French Academy first identified arsenic in
dead human bodies in 1834. Arsenic normally appears in

===== Screenshot (63) =====
 


human female blood at 0.64 ppm. Levels rise to 0.93 ppm
during menstruation and 2.20 ppm during months five and
six of pregnancy. At 90 to 120 ppm, arsenic promotes and
enhances the growth rate of chicks. The rate of growth and
metamorphosis of tadpoles is enhanced by the presence of
arsenic as well.

Compound “606,” an arsenic containing compound
(arsphenamine) was developed in 1910 by Paul Ehrlich, the
German scientist, for the successful treatment and cure of
syphilis.

Arsenic in combination with the B-vitamin choline
prevents 100% of perosis (“slipped tendon”) in poultry. Arsenic
deficiency in humans results in a “carpal tunnel syndrome,”
“TMJ,” and other “repetitive motion” type degenerative joint
diseases and minute traces stimulate the production of red
blood cells.

According to the Memorial Sloan-Kettering Cancer
Center in New York, “infusions of arsenic works better than
any other drug for the treatment of certain types of leukemia.”
Their study involved 12 seriously ill patients suffering from
acute promyelocytic leukemia (APL).

In the November 5, 1998 issue of The New England Journal
of Medicine, Dr. Raymond Worrel Jr. stated, “We now know
that arsenic can safely bring patients with APL into remission,
which may ultimately give them a second chance at life.”

Eleven of the 12 patients went into remission within
12 to 39 days after starting the arsenic therapy - eight, of the
original APL test group, were still in “remission” ten years
after the therapy began. “ Based on highly sensitive test results,
treatment with arsenic appears to exceed the effectiveness of
any single drug to treat APL,” said Dr. Steven Soignet, lead
author of the study.

Arsenic metabolism is affected by tissue and blood
levels of zinc, selenium, arginine, choline, methionine,
taurine and quaniacetic acid, all of which affect methyl-group
metabolism and polyamine synthesis which is the site of


 

arsenic function in human physiology.

At-Astatine has 20 known radioactive isotopes with an
extremely short half-life of 7.2 to 8.0 hours. The isotope with
the shortest half-life is astatine-210 with a half-life of 8.3 hours.

Astatine was first made in 1940 by a team of
radiochemists from the University of California at Berkeley.
They produced astatine by bombarding bismuth with alpha
particles. They named the element from the Greek word
astatos, which translates to “unstable.”

Astatine is a member of the halogen family so that it
is not surprising that its chemical character is similar to that
of iodine; its physical characteristics are more metallic than
those of iodine.

Small quantities of astatine are produced naturally as
the decay products of isotopes of uranium and thorium. The
total amount of astatine estimated to be present in the earth’s
crust at any one time is estimated to be one ounce.

Astatine is accumulated by the mammalian and human
thyroid following ingestion, however, little is retained and it
is rapidly excreted.

Au-Gold is found in igneous and sedimentary rocks at
0.004 ppm, fresh water at 0.00006 ppm, sea water at 0.000011
ppm, marine plants at 0.012 ppm, terrestrial plants 0.005 to
0.002 ppm, marine animals at 0.0003 to 0.008 ppm, terrestrial
animals at 0.00023 ppm, and in mammalian liver gold forms a
colloid.

Gold is considered the most precious of all metals and
was used in conjunction with silver and mercury by the ancient
Chinese alchemist 8,000 B.C. to provide “immortality”. In its
pure state, gold is one of the most beautiful of elements. With
its bright yellow color and its resistance to corrosion, it has
been highly prized since ancient times. Its chemical symbol,
Au, comes from the Latin word aurum, which translates to
“shining dawn.” It has even older origins, coming from the
Sanskrit word jval and the Anglo-Saxon word gold, both

===== Screenshot (64) =====
 


 

meaning “gold.”

In nature, gold is usually found as a pure metal, most
often in the form of nuggets, flakes or “dust.” It also occurs in
veins of pure gold or in deposits of a class of minerals called
tellurides. It is widely distributed around the world and is
always found near deposits of quartz and pyrite.

Gold dissolves in mercury to form an amalgam, or
alloy of mercury and gold - this gold-mercury amalgam is
used by dentist to fill teeth. A radioactive isotope of gold
with a short half-life of 2.7 days is used to treat some forms of
cancer. Known as gold-198, it is easily produced by exposing
natural gold to a beam of neutrons in a nuclear reactor.

Gold will dissolve very slowly in the solution of
hydrochloric and nitric acids that was called “aqua regia”
by the alchemists because of its ability to dissolve the king of
metals.

Gold concentrates in the horsetail plant (Equisetum
arvense). Gold compounds (gold sodium thiomalate, gold
thioglucose also known as aurothioglucose) are frequently
administered by orthodox physicians as an add-on therapy
with salicylates (aspirin) for arthritis when added pain relief
is required. Gold has been reported only to be effective
against active joint inflammation and is not usually helpful
for treating advanced destructive rheumatoid arthritis.

B-Boron is found in igneous rocks at 10 ppm, shale at
100 ppm, sandstone at 35 ppm, limestone at 20 ppm, fresh
water at 0.013 ppm, seawater at 4.0 to 6.0 ppm, soil from 2.0
to 100 ppm (highest levels are found in saline and alkaline
soils), marine plants 120 ppm (highest levels found in brown
algae), terrestrial plants at 150 ppm (chenopodiaceae and
plumboginaceae are indicator plant families), marine animals
at 20 to 50 ppm, and terrestrial animals at 0.5 ppm.

Boron is a scarce element, making up only 0.0003% of
the earth’s crust. In its pure state, it is a brownish crystal that is
almostas hard a diamond. Boron was first isolated in 1808 by the


 

English chemist Sir Humphry Davy and two French chemists,
Joseph Louis Gay-Lussac and Louis-Jacques Thenard.

Boron is never found in its pure form in nature. Boron
is commonly found in combination with oxygen, water and
sodium in a compound called borax. Boric acid is another
important boron compound. It is made by heating borax with
either hydrochloric or sulfuric acid. Boric acid is a weak acid
that has an antiseptic property and is commonly used as a
wound disinfectant and an eye wash.

Boron is essential for bone metabolism including the
efficient use of calcium and magnesium and proper function
of endocrine glands (i.e. - ovaries, testes and adrenals).

Pure boron is hard and gray and melts at over 4,000
degrees F. Boron is a non-metallic mineral, occurs as a
combination in nature (i.e. - borax, boric acid or sassolite,
ulexite, colemanite, boracite, and tourmaline).

Prior to 1981, boron was not considered an essential
nutrient. Boron was first shown to be an essential mineral for
growing chicks. It was not until 1990 that boron was accepted
as an essential nutrient for humans.

Boron is required for the maintenance of bone density
and normal blood levels of estrogen and testosterone. Within
eight days of supplementing boron, women lost 40% less
calcium, 33% less magnesium, and less phosphorus through
their urine. Women receiving boron supplements had blood
levels of estradiol 17B doubled to “levels found in women
on estrogen replacement therapy.” Levels of testosterone
also double in both men and women following boron
supplementation.

Industrially, boron is used to make a special heat-resistant type of glass, known as borosilicate glass, which
usually carries the commercial name of Pyrex™. The most
common use of Pyrex™ is in the kitchen, where Pyrex™ glass
baking dishes and measuring cups are used because they can
withstand rapid changes in temperature without cracking.

===== Screenshot (65) =====
 


Ba-Barium is found in igneous rocks at 425 ppm, shale
at 580 ppm, sandstone at 50 ppm, limestone at 120 ppm, fresh
water at 0.054 ppm, sea water at 0.03 ppm, soil at 500 ppm,
marine plants at 30 ppm (highest levels in brown algae),
terrestrial plants at 14 ppm (the fruit of Bertholletia excelea can
have up to 4,000 ppm), marine animals 0.2 to 3.0 pm (highest
levels in hard tissues such as bone and shell), and terrestrial
animals at up to 75 ppm (highest levels found in bone, lung
and eyes).

Barium is an abundant element, ranking sixth in
abundance of the elements making up the earth’s crust. It is
found in the minerals witherite, a barium carbonate (BaCO.,),
and barite, a barium sulfate (BaSO,). Barium was discovered
by Sir Humphry Davy, the English chemist in 1808.

Radiologists use barium sulfate to facilitate the
examination of a patient’s gastrointestinal tract with X-rays.
Barium sulfate is a very dense salt that is opaque to X-rays.
When a patient drinks a suspension of barium sulfate in
water or is given a barium enema, consisting of a similar
suspension, the barium sulfate particles temporarily fill all of
the hollow organs of the gastrointestinal tract. As the barium
sulfate absorbs X-rays and blocks their transmission, the
gastrointestinal organs will be clearly outlined in white in an
X-ray film.

Barium was considered essential for mammals in 1949.

Be-Beryllium is found in igneous rocks at 2.0 to 8.0 ppm,
in shale at 3.0 ppm, sandstone and limestone at < 0.1 ppm, fresh
water at 0.001 ppm, sea water at 0.0000006 ppm, soil at 0.001
ppm, marine plants (highest levels in brown algae), terrestrial
plants at < 0.1 ppm (highest in plants grown in volcanic soils),
and terrestrial animals at 0.0003 to 0.002 ppm in soft tissue.

Beryllium heads the group of elements known as the
“alkaline-earth” metals. The most abundant elements in this
group are calcium and magnesium, but they all share common
chemical and physical properties. Even though several forms


 

of beryllium are found in the earth’s crust, it is a relatively
rare element, ranking only 32nd in order of the abundance of
the elements.

Beryllium in its pure state is a fairly hard, gray-white
metal. It is one of the lightest of the metals, however, it is hard
enough to scratch glass. As with all the alkaline-earth group,
beryllium is much too chemically reactive to be found in its free
state. It readily reacts with oxygen and many other elements.

The mineral known as beryl is actually a complex
compound of beryllium, silicon, aluminum and oxygen. Its
scientific name is beryllium aluminum silicate. Beryllium
was discovered in 1798 by the French chemist Louis-Nicolas
Vauquelin, while comparing the structures of beryl and
emerald

The mineral beryl is the most common source of
beryllium. Beryllium was formerly known as “glucinium”
because beryllium, like many of its compounds, has a sweet
taste.

Crystals of this valuable mineral have been collected
and used for jewelry for thousands of years. Both emerald
(green) and aquamarine (blue) gemstones are naturally
occurring forms of beryl. Gemstones, including emeralds,
have been used as sources of exotic minerals in Ayurvedic
and Egyptian medicine for thousands of years.

Inhalation of beryllium oxide dust (mining and
industrial contaminants) can lead to “berylliosis,” a painful
and sometimes fatal lung disease.

Bi-Bismuth is found in igneous rocks at 0.17 ppm, shale
at 1.0 ppm, sea water at 0.000017 ppm, terrestrial plants at
0.06 ppm, marine animals at 0.09 ppm, and terrestrial animals
at 0.004 ppm.

Bismuth is a white, heavy, brittle metal that has a slight
yellow cast. Bismuth has been known in the West since the
early 15th century; it was often confused with tin or lead.

===== Screenshot (66) =====
 


 

Claude Geoffroy the Younger, a French Nobleman is credited
with its identification in 1753.

Bismuth has been used by the Chinese alchemist to
treat gastric ulcers and gastroenteritis for more than 8,000
years; today it is used for over-the-counter GI remedies (Pepto
Bismol™) and as a filler, “glitter” and “shimmer” in many
cosmetics.

Stress has historically been blamed as the boogy-man
as the cause of gastric, peptic, and duodenal ulcers. Animal
and human studies have in fact demonstrated that the true
cause of ulcers is an infection with a bacterium known as
Helicobacter pylori.

Australian gastroenterologist Barry Marshall, M.D.
and pathologist J. Robin Warren proposed their theory for the
bacterial cause of gastric ulcers in 1983. The bacterial cause
(H. pylori) for gastric ulcers was known in pigs in 1952.

The treatment of choice for gastric ulcers is a course of
tetracycline antibiotics, antacids, and bismuth subsalicylate for
two to four weeks. You've heard of bismuth used for treating
stomach and intestinal disorders if you have ever heard of a
pink liquid with the name of Pepto Bismol™.

Br-Bromine is a “halogen” related to iodine. It is found
in igneous rocks at 3.0 to 5.0 ppm, shale at 4.0 ppm, sandstone
at 1.0 ppm, limestone at 0.2 ppm, fresh water at 0.2 ppm, sea
water at 65 ppm, soil at 5.0 ppm, marine plants at 740 ppm
(highest levels in brown algae), terrestrial plants at 15 ppm,
marine animals at 60 to 1,000 ppm, and terrestrial animals at
6.0 ppm.

Bromine functions in its active form as brominated
amino acids. In its pure form, bromine is a diatomic molecule
(two bromine atoms) that is a reddish liquid. Bromine and
mercury are the only two elements that are liquids at room
temperature.

Bromine was discovered by a young French student,
Antoine-Jerome Balard, in 1826. He first isolated the element


 

by adding chlorinated water to the brackish water found in
the salt marshes near Montpellier, a city in southern France.
The addition of the chlorine turned the water brownish. The
cause of the color change was found to be a new element that
the French Academy named bromine, taken from the Greek
word bromos, which translates to “stench.”

Sodium bromide, a salt of bromine, is a mild sedative
that was commonly used in the late 19th and early 20th
centuries. People still speak of taking a “bromide” to calm
down and ease stress and tension.

There is strong evidence for Bromine’s essentiality in
mammals and humans.

C-Carbon is found in igneous rocks at 200 ppm, shale
at 15,300 ppm, sandstone at 13,800 ppm, limestone at 113,500
ppm, fresh water at 11 ppm, sea water at 28 ppm, soils at
20,000 ppm (up to 90% of the carbon in soil is bound in the
humus), marine plants at 345,000 ppm, terrestrial plants at
454,000 ppm, marine animals at 400,000 ppm, and in terrestrial
animals at 465,000 (280,000 ppm in bone).

Carbon comprises only 0.09% of the earth’s crust by
mass, however, it is the element most essential for life on our
planet. When we examine the molecules that make up the cells,
tissues, organs and bodies of animals and tissues of plants
we see that almost all contain carbon. Organic chemistry is
the chemistry of organic compounds. Carbon is the center
of the “organic” world because of its ability link with other
carbon atoms to form long chains that are straight and linear
or branched. These carbon chains act as a spine to which other
elements are attached, producing an almost endless number
of possible carbon-containing molecules.

An example of a well known long-chain carbon
molecule is deoxyribonucleic acid, or DNA, found in the
genetic material of all living organisms, plant and animal.
DNA stores information for the construction of an immense

===== Screenshot (67) =====
 


 

number of proteins manufactured by plant and animal cells,
and it can replicate or copy itself. Found in the nuclei of all
plant and animal cells, DNA is essential for the reproduction
of living organisms.

In addition to its occurrence in organic compounds,
carbon also exists in its free state in nature, where it is found
in several different physical forms. When an element exists
in several different physical forms it is referred to as an
allotrope.

In the case of carbon allotropes, it is found as diamond
- the hardest of all materials. Carbon is also found as graphite
and coal. The atoms of carbon in graphite are arranged in
layers, like the pages of a book. Because the bonds between
these layers are weak, the layers easily slide over one another,
causing graphite to be soft and slippery which makes graphite
useful as a lubricant and as a marking material in pencils.

In 1985, a new allotrope form of carbon was created
in the laboratory of Harold W. Kroto at the University of
Sussex in Brighton, England, and Richard E. Smalley at Rice
University in Houston, Texas, using laser beams to vaporize
graphite. They named the new allotrope carbon molecule
buckminsterfullerene after the Americanarchitect Buckminster
Fuller, who became famous for designing geodesic dome
structures that resembled soccer balls (“buckyballs”).

Coal, primarily a mixture of carbon and more than 77
minerals, contains carbon ina noncrystalline form. It is a major
source of “solar income” energy for industry and domestic
use. Like oil and natural gas, coal is considered a “fossil fuel”
because it is the product of the decay of plants and animals
over hundreds of millions of years. Hard or anthracite coal is
the older form of coal and is thought to be approximately 250
million years old - it contains 80% carbon. Bituminous or soft
coal contains only about 40 to 50% carbon, and is the younger
form of coal.

The 1991 data from the United States Department of
Energy shows that 23.2% of the energy used in the United

States comes from coal, approximately 40% from petroleum,
and 24% from natural gas. Fossil fuels provide 90% of the total
energy consumed in the U.S.

Industrial, automotive and home use of carbon fuels and
respiration of vertebrates and many microorganisms produce
large amounts of carbon dioxide (CO,) as a byproduct.

Carbon dioxide has, since the beginning of time,
influenced the temperature of the earth by a process called
the “greenhouse effect.” Like the glass roof of the green house
carbon dioxide allows the “solar income” to pass through the
atmosphere and reach the earth’s surface - this is essentially a
one way event that traps heat in the earth’s crust.

While many individuals and organizations, want to
blame man’s activities and pitifully small biomass for “global
warming,” we must look to the past for the cycles of Ice Ages and
“global warming” that have taken place over and over through
the millennia to understand the cyclic nature of these events.
The proven method of delaying and/or reducing the “global
warming” effects of our times is to simply plant more trees and
let the trees convert the CO, into oxygen (O,) and water (H,O)!

Carbon functions as an essential structural element for
all living organisms and organic molecules (i.e.- carbohydrates,
fats, amino acids, vitamins, enzymes, etc.).

Carbohydrates & Sugars

In modern times, carbohydrates commonly furnish
most of the energy required to move, perform work and
for all of the basic biochemical functions of life itself. While
carbohydrates and sugars are convenient sources of fuel for
energy, they are not essential nutrients as all living organisms
can make them by reconfiguring proteins and fats.

The chief sources of dietary carbohydrates include

===== Screenshot (68) =====
 


 

grains, vegetables, fruits and sugars (i.e. - honey, molasses).
Carbohydrates in their simplest form have the formula CH,O.

Hy droge n and oxygen are present in the same ratio that is
found in water with one carbon for each molecule of water.

By using the sun’s light energy, the carbon dioxide
from the air and the chlorophyll dependent process known as
photosynthesis, plants are able to manufacture carbohydrates
(sugar and starch), vitamins, amino acids, and fatty acids.

To manufacture carbohydrates, plants take CO, and
H,O and subject them to a photochemical process known as
photosynthesis. In the presence of chlorophyll, a magnesium/
carbon ring structure, carbohydrate is produced using the
energy from sunlight - O, is released to the atmosphere as a
by-product of the reaction.

Lipids & Fats

Lipids or fats, like carbohydrates, are composed of
carbon, hydrogenand oxygen. Fats have the common property
of being insoluble in water, soluble in organic solvents such as
ether and chloroform, and can be utilized as energy by living
organisms.

Fats, as a group of carbon compounds, include ordinary
fats, oils, waxes, and unrelated compounds. The primary food
sources of lipids are butter, flax seed oil, olive oil, animal fat,
nuts, seeds, whole grains, olives, avocados, egg yolks, dairy,
etc. Fats serve as a source of immediate or stored energy. A


gram of fat contains nine calories per gram compared with 4.5
calories per gram of carbohydrate or protein.

Triglycerides (the primary component of fats and oils)
are composed of carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen. Structurally,
they are esters of a trihydric alcohol (glycerol) and fatty acids.
The fatty acids can have from four to 30 carbon atoms and
constitute the bulk of the triglyceride mass. One hundred
grams of fat or oil will contain 95 grams of fatty acids.

A fatty acid or hydrocarbon chain is described with
regard to three characteristics: chain length, degree of
“saturation” with hydrogen, and location of the first “double
bond.”

The length is a reference to the number of carbon
atoms in the chain (i.e. - C:16 has 16 carbons in the chain). The
terms “short chain” (less than 6 carbons), “medium chain”
(7 to 11 carbons), and “long chain” (12 or more carbons) are
used to describe the chains of fatty acids in the structure of
triglycerides.

The degree of hydrogen “saturation” in fatty acids is
defined by the number of “double bonds” between carbon
atoms in the fatty acid chains. A fatty acid chain can ae
all the hydrogen it can hold and have no double bonds, i
which case, it is referred to as a “saturated” fatty acid. It can
contain one double bond (monounsaturated fatty acid) or it
may contain several double bonds (polyunsaturated fatty
acids).

The location of the first double bond as counted from
the “tail” or the methyl end of the fatty acid is referred to as
the “omega” number (i.e. - omega-3, omega-6, etc.).

Three polyunsaturated fatty acids (linoleic, linolenic,
and arachidonic acids) are essential fatty acids (EFA). Three
percent of the total daily calorie intake must be from the EFA

- as essential fatty acids, they are required for life but cannot
be manufactured. However, only two of the EFA (linoleic and
linolenic) are truly regarded as essential because arachidonic
acid can be synthesized by humans from linolenic acid when

===== Screenshot (69) =====
 


 

it is present in optimal amounts.

Essential Fatty Acids have essential vitamin-like
functions in fat transport and metabolism, and in maintaining
the integrity of cell walls (bilipid layer membranes). They
are also part of the fatty acids of cholesterol esters and
phospholipids in plasma lipoproteins and mitochondrial
lipoproteins.

Serum cholesterol can be lowered by the consumption
of EFA. EFAs are also the raw material for the human body to
manufacture prostaglandins that help regulate blood pressure,
heart rate, vascular dilation, blood clotting, bronchial dilation,
and central nervous system (brain and spinal cord) function.
EFA deficiency in human infants results in a poor growth rate,
lowered IQ, seizures, learning disabilities, eczema, asthma,
and a lowered resistance to infectious diseases.

Cholesterol is a member of a large group of fats known
as sterols. They all have a complex carbon ring structure.
Cholesterol is an essential part of the structure of cell walls,
brain and spinal cord (myelin), the raw material for the
production of vitamin D in the human body, bile acids, adrenal
cortical hormones, estrogens (a cholesterol deficiency makes
menopause a living hell), progesterone, and testosterone (a
cholesterol deficiency will turn hubby into a TV watching
steer who is totally disinterested in sex).

Proteins

Proteins, like carbohydrates and fats, contain carbon,
hydrogen and oxygen. In addition they also contain 16%
nitrogen (the amine group) sometimes in conjunction with
other elements such as phosphorus, iron, sulfur and cobalt.
The basic structural unit of a protein is the amino acid. Amino
acids are united by “peptide bonds” into long chains of various


 

geometric structures to form specific protein. Digestion of
proteins breaks the peptide bonds to release individual amino
acids.

Classically there are nine essential amino acids that are
required in the daily diet as they cannot be manufactured by
the human body. Forty-three percent of the dietary protein
for human infants must contain the essential amino acids.
Growing children require 36% essential amino acids and
adults require 19% for maintenance.

To the classic list of essential amino acids, we would
add arginine, taurine, and tyrosine. Over the long haul, these
three amino acids help prevent certain specific diseases,
respectively, these diseases are cancer and PAD, macular
degeneration and goiter.

phosphate

===== Screenshot (70) =====
 


 

Use of protein for fuel to provide energy in place of
carbohydrate provides 4.5 calories per gram.

An individual consuming protein at even 300 gm per
day (almost 3/4 of a pound of meat a day) will have no adverse
effects as long as they do not have kidney or liver disease and
supplement properly with vitamins and minerals.

Proteins are the fundamental structural components
of the living cell (cytoplasm). They are essential parts of the
cell nucleus and protoplasm. Proteins are the most abundant
of all carbon containing organic compounds in the human
body. The greatest mass of body protein is found in the
skeletal muscle, the remainder is found in other organs (liver,
kidney, hear, stomach, etc.), bones, teeth, blood, and other
body fluids (lymph). Enzymes are proteins that do work to
facilitate chemical reactions in the body (energy production
and utilization, hormone production, nerve impulses, etc.)

Vitamins

Vitamins are a major carbon group of unrelated
essential organic carbon compounds needed by the human
body in minute quantities each day. They are essential for
specific metabolic reactions of the cell and are required for
normal growth, development, maintenance, health, longevity
and life itself.

Vitamins function as coenzymes or activating side
groups for essential subcellular enzyme systems. Vitamins
regulate metabolism, facilitate the conversion of carbohydrate,
fat and protein to energy, and are required for the formation
and repair of tissues in embryos, children, teens, adults, and
seniors. Vitamins are essential nutrients in that, they can not
be manufactured by humans; they do not supply calories or
contribute to body mass.


 

There are three classifications of vitamins:

1) Fat soluble - A, D, E, and K - Fat soluble vitamins,
as the name implies, function in fat (lipid) metabolism in
human cells. Fat soluble vitamins can not be absorbed from
the intestine when we eat low fat diets. People who have
their gall bladders removed should supplement with ox bile
to increase the availability of bile salts which are required
for the efficient absorption of essential fatty acids, good fats
(triglycerides, cholesterol, etc.) and fat soluble vitamins.

2) Water soluble - B-complex, biotin, choline and
vitamin C - Water soluble vitamins consists of 10 separate
water soluble B vitamins and vitamin C. In general they
function as essential nutrients in the metabolic processes of all
living cells by acting as cofactors in multiple enzyme systems
that drive the oxidation of food and production of energy.

3) Flavanols - Epicatechins and polyphenols - Flavanols
and bioflavonoids (polyphenols/epicatechins) are part of a
large family of compounds that are generally regarded as a
third category of vitamins - fat soluble vitamins, water soluble
vitamins and flavanol vitamins.

Flavanols found in dark chocolate, participate in
maintaining the strength of capillaries, lower blood pressure,
improve blood flow via increased production of nitric oxide
(a molecule used by the vascular endothelium to signal
the surrounding muscle to relax) and have vitamin C-like
functions.

Taking optimal levels of essential vitamins goes a long
way to preventing birth defects and solving the problems
of reduced physical effectiveness and many debilitating
degenerative diseases.

Ca-Calcium is found in igneous rocks at 41,500 ppm,
shale at 22,100 ppm, sandstone at 39,100, limestone at 302,000
ppm, fresh water at 15 ppm, sea water at 400 ppm, soils at
7,000 to 500,000 ppm (lowest in acid soils and highest in
limestone or alkaline soils), marine plants at 10,000 to 300,000
ppm (highest in calcarious tissue, - red, blue-green and green
algae and diatoms), terrestrial plants at 18,000 ppm, marine
animals at 1,500 to 20,000 ppm (350,000 ppm in calcarious
tissue - sponges, coral, mollusks and echinoderms), terrestrial
animals at 200 to 85,000 ppm (260,000 ppm in mammalian
bone, 200 to 500 ppm in soft tissue and 5 ppm or less in red
blood cells). The metal, calcium, was isolated in 1808 by Sir
Humphry Davy.

Calcium is an extremely active element and is not found
in a pure form in nature. It reacts with water to form calcium
hydroxide (Ca(OH),) and with the atmospheric oxygen to
form calcium oxide (CaO). Pure calcium metal is hard and has
the characteristic silvery-white color of all the alkaline-earth
metals.

In the 1st century, the Romans used a compound of
calcium they called calx (lime) in Latin.

Calcium is essential for all living organisms and is
found in the cell walls of plants, in all calcareous tissues, and
mammalian bones and teeth. Calcium also helps to regulate
electrochemical functions in cells and is a required cofactor
necessary to activate several enzymes.

Calcium is the fifth most abundant mineral element in
the earth’s crust and biosphere. There is evidence that clearly
shows humans are designed to consume and use high calcium
diets. The late Paleolithic period of 35,000 to 10,000 years ago
was the most recent time that our human forebears lived
in the environment for which they had been biochemically
designed.

The agricultural revolution occurred 10,000 years ago
and it reduced the wide variety of wild foods in the human
food chain while increasing food energy (calories). These


 

dietary changes universally and forever decreased man’s
dietary intake of minerals, trace minerals and rare earths. The
uncultivated food plants and wild game commonly available
to Stone Age humans would supply 1600 mg of calcium at
basal energy intakes and between 2,000 and 3,000 mg of
calcium at the energy levels required to support hunting and
work.

During the 20th century, American adults had acalcium
intake of only one-fifth to one-third as much as did Stone
Age humans. The National Health & Nutrition Examination
Survey II reported a median calcium intake for American
women of between 300 and 508 mg per day and only 680 mg
for men.

Other nutrients that are commonly found in the
American diet can aggravate the national calcium deficiency
crisis. Diets rich with an overabundance of fiber, salt and
phosphates (soft drinks) result in an increased calcium “cost.”
In effect, these foods increase the dietary requirements for
calcium. Urinary calcium loss increased from 96 mg per day
to 148 mg per day when food was excessively salted. As


 

phosphate intake is doubled, the output of urinary calcium
increases by 50%.

There are no less than 147 deficiency diseases that can
be attributed to calcium deficiencies or imbalances. The most
recent clinical research clearly points out that the entire scope
of American diets are critically deficient in calcium. The only
practical way to obtain enough sufficient amounts of calcium
is through supplementation. Interestingly, the allopathic
physicians who did the study failed in their duty. Instead of
simply recommending effective calcium supplementation,
they recommended eating five cups of broccoli per day as a
valuable source of calcium. Try and get a kid to eat five cups
of broccoli each day.

The most common calcium deficiency diseases are
easy to recognize and range from poor clotting time of the
blood when you nick yourself shaving (calcium is a cofactor
in the clotting mechanism), arthritis (which physicians treat
with pain killers, anti-inflammatory drugs and surgery), to
the dozens of variations of osteoporosis.

Famous people who have suffered from an advanced
degree of calcium deficiency include Pope John Paul II
(fractured hip/ osteoporosis), Elizabeth Taylor (osteoporosis/
multiple hip replacement surgeries), “Bo” Jackson (fractured
hip/osteoporosis), Bill Walton, a vegan of professional
basketball fame (osteoporosis, arthritis, fractures), etc.

Calcium is the most abundant mineral in the human
body. The average male has 1,200 grams and the average
female has 1,000 grams. Calcium makes up two percent of the
adult body weight (water makes up 65 to 75%). Calcium also
composes up to 39% of the total mineral reserves (ash after
cremation). Ninety-nine percent of body calcium is found in
bones and teeth. The other one percent is found in the blood,
extracellular fluids, and within cells where it is a cofactor and
activator for numerous enzyme systems.

Calcium, inbones, is found in the form of hydroxyapatite
salts composed of calcium phosphate and calcium carbonate

===== Screenshot (73) =====
 


in a classic crystal structure bound to a protein framework
called “bone matrix.”

To extract the calcium from a bone and reveal the
protein matrix, put a chicken “drumstick” bone in a quart of
vinegar for two weeks and the calcium will be leached out of
the bone leaving the protein matrix.

Hydroxyapatite crystals are found in the enamel and
dentine of teeth; however, little calcium is readily available
from teeth to contribute to rapidly available Ca to maintain
blood levels.

In addition to being a major structural mineral, Ca is also
required as a cofactor for the release of energy from ATP for
muscular contraction, and blood clotting. In the blood clotting
process, ionized Ca stimulates the release of thromboplastin
from platelets and converts prothrombin to thrombin. Thrombin
helps to convert fibrinogen to fibrin, and fibrin is the protein
web that traps red blood cells to create blood clots.

Calcium mediates the transport function of cell
and organelle membranes. Calcium affects the release of
neurotransmitters at synaptic junctions. It also mediates the
synthesis, secretion, and metabolic effects of hormones and
enzymes. Calcium helps to regulate the rate and strength of
the heart rate, muscle tone and muscle receptivity to nerve
stimulation.

Calcium is primarily absorbed in the duodenum, where
the environment is still acid. Once the food in the intestine
converts to an alkaline pH, the absorption rate of calcium will
decline. Calcium is absorbed from the small intestine by active
cellular transport and by simple diffusion. Metallic calcium
absorption may be limited to one to three percent or less and
can be affected by many substances in the gut. Under ideal
circumstances calcium is highly absorbable, up to 98% in the
organically bound plant derived colloidal mineral and water
soluble chelated form.

Causes of a calcium deficiency can include, a
deficiency or lack of vitamin D; the lack of stomach acid
or hypochlorhydria can result from a restricted NaCl (salt)
intake; lactose intolerance; celiac disease; high fat diet; low
protein diet; and a high fiber and phytate consumption.

Phytic acid is a phosphorus containing acid compound
found in the bran of grains, seeds, as well as the stems and
roots of many plants. Oatmeal and whole wheat especially
contain phytic acid. When phytic acid combines with calcium
it forms an insoluble product called calcium phytate which
cannot be absorbed by the body.

Oxalic acid in rhubarb, spinach, chard, and mustard
greens combines with calcium to form an insoluble calcium
oxalate, which is not absorbed. Fiber itself, besides the phytate
content, prevents calcium absorption. Alkaline intestinal
environment, gut hypermobility (too rapid transit time
brought on by too much fiber, fruit, etc.), and pharmaceuticals
(anti-seizure drugs, diuretics, etc.) result in decreased
absorption and retention. Excesses of sugar, carbonation and
caffeine from coffee, tea, colas, etc. will reduce the efficiency
of absorption and will leach stored calcium from the bones.

Under optimal conditions, parathormone secreted by
the parathyroid gland and calcitonin secreted by the thyroid
gland, maintain a serum calcium of 8.5 to 10.5 mg% by drawing
on calcium reserves from the bones. The parathormone can
also affect the kidney so that it retains more calcium. The same
hormone can cause the gut to be more efficient in absorption.
When blood calcium begins to rise above 10.5 mg% from too
much parathyroid activity, the calcitonin produced by the
thyroid gland reduces the availability of calcium from the
bones.

In 1980, McCarron et al theorized that chronic calcium
deficiency probably led to hypertension. More than 30
subsequent studies supported the original theory of calcium
deficiency as the cause of hypertension. In addition, recent
studies have shown that serum ionized calcium is consistently
lower in humans with untreated hypertension. In a recent
review article, Sower and others noted that the association of

===== Screenshot (74) =====
 


calcium intake and high blood pressure is most clear in people
with daily calcium intakes of less than 500 mg per day.

The phenomenon of salt sensitive hypertension consists
of a rise in blood pressure and a sustained increase in urinary
loss of calcium in response to excess salt consumption and a
coexisting calcium deficiency. Among black and elderly whites
with essential hypertension, restricted intakes and deficiencies of
calcium and potassium, rather than elevated salt consumption is

responsible for “salt sensitivity.” In a four-year study of 58,218
nurses, hypertension was more likely to develop in females who
took in less than 800 mg of calcium per day.

Up to 75% of ingested elemental calcium is lost in the
feces. Two percent is lost in the urine and sweat (15 mg per
day is lost in normal sweating - this can double or triple in
active athletes or individuals engaged in physical labor). In
cases of excess urine loss of calcium (osteoporosis, hyperactive
parathyroid glands, excess dietary phosphorus and phytates,
etc.), kidney stones, bone spurs and calcium deposits will
develop.

Bone and heel spurs and calcium deposits always
develop at the sites of insertions of tendons and ligaments
during a raging
and progressive osteoporosis. Bone spurs,
heel spurs, calcium deposits and the elevated blood calcium
of hyperparathyroidism can be reversed and eliminated by
supplementing with significant amounts of chelated and
colloidal chelates of calcium.

Nutritional secondary hyperparathyroidism, resulting
from a raging calcium deficiency, produces a significant
elevation in blood calcium levels. The parathyroid glands
will attempt to compensate and will enlarge in an attempt
to absorb more calcium from the gut. This scenario will
almost always result in the surgeon calling the enlarged or
hypertrophied parathyroid glands a “tumor” and they will
recommend surgery. Supplementation with the appropriate
amounts of usable calcium will result in a rapid and complete
resolution of the problem - DO NOT GET SURGERY!


 

Not only are most of our soils and foods deficient in
calcium, additionally the diet of the industrialized world is
rich in phosphorous, which is found in just about everything
we eat. Ideally, the calcium to phosphorous (Ca:P) ratio in
our daily diet should be 2:1. The ideal ratio is not possible
by simply eating food. You would have to eat 25 pounds of
broccoli every time you ate a 16 oz. T-bone steak! The only
possible way to approach the 2:1 ideal is to avoid as much as
possible the food items containing high levels of phosphorous
(i.e. - raw vegetables (phytates), colas, etc.) and supplement
with plant derived colloidal and chelated calcium.

Cd-Cadmium is found in igneous rocks at 0.2 ppm,
shale at 0.3 ppm, sandstone at 0.05 ppm, limestone at 0.035
ppm, fresh water at 0.08 ppm, sea water at 0.00011 ppm, soils
at 0.06 ppm, marine plants at 0.4 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.6
ppm, marine animals at 0.15 to 3.0 ppm and in land animals
at 0.5 ppm (accumulates in kidney tissue).

Cadmium is a silver-colored metal that is soft enough
to cut with a knife. Its chemical properties are similar to those
of zinc. It is a rare metal with an abundance level of only one-thousandth of that of zinc in the earth’s crust. It is generally
collected as a byproduct of zinc refining.

Cadmium was isolated and recognized as a separate
element in 1817 by Friedrich Strohmeyer, a pharmaceutical
chemist who was investigating the compound zinc oxide,
known as calamine.

A cadmium alloy, Wood's metal, consists of bismuth
with 12.5% cadmium. It has an extremely low boiling point of
70 degrees C and is used to seal overhead sprinkler systems.
A fire will melt the Wood’s metal seal before it becomes too
hot, releasing water to snuff out the fire.

Cadmium is used to make nickel-cadmium, or “nicad,”
batteries. The “nicad” battery can be recharged an infinite
number of times.

Tobacco accumulates cadmium, as

a result, both

===== Screenshot (75) =====
 

smokers and victims of second hand smoke inhale dangerous
cadmium ashes.

Cadmium functions in nature by stimulating the
hatching of nematode (round worm) cysts. Cadmium-bound
proteins have been isolated from molluscs and horse kidneys
- cadmium is known for its ability to replace zinc in zinc
dependent enzyme reactions.

Ce-Cerium, a rare earth, is found in igneous rock at
60 ppm, shale at 59 ppm, sandstone at 92 ppm, limestone at
12 ppm, sea water at 0.0004 ppm, soil at 50 ppm, terrestrial
plants accumulates cerium at 320 ppm, and terrestrial animals
at 0.003 ppm (accumulates in bone).

Cerium is the most abundant of the rare earth minerals.
It was simultaneously isolated and identified as a new element
in 1803 by the Swedish chemist Jons Jakob Berzelius and the
German chemists Wilhelm Hisinger and Martin Klaproth, as
an impurity in the mineral bastnasite. It was named for the
asteroid Ceres, which was discovered in 1801.

Cerium is found in the mineral ores called monazite
and bastnasite. Monazite sand contains all of the rare earth
elements and is found in Florida beach sand, and in river sand
from India and Brazil. Large deposits of bastnasite are found
in Southern California.

Cerium nitrate is used as a topical disinfectant for
severe 4th degree burn patients.

Cl-Chlorine is found in igneous rocks at 130 ppm,
shale at 180 ppm, sandstone at 10 ppm, limestone at 150 ppm,
fresh water at 7-8 ppm, sea water at 19,000 ppm, soil at 100
ppm (higher in alkaline soils, near the sea and in deserts -
a major exchangeable anion in many soils), marine plants at
4,700 ppm, terrestrial plants at 2,000 ppm, marine animals
at 5,000 to 90,000 ppm (highest in soft coelenterates), and
in land animals at 2,800 ppm. In land animals, Cl’s highest
concentration is found in mammalian hair and skin. Chlorine


 

is essential for all living species’ electrochemical and catalytic
functions, activates numerous enzymes, and is the basic
raw material for our stomachs to make stomach acid (HCl)
required to activate protein digestion by the enzyme pepsin,
B12 absorption (intrinsic factor activation), and for absorption
of minerals. Salt (NaCl) is the universal source of chloride ions
for all living things.

Cm-Curium is found in igneous rocks at 0.0001 ppm.
All isotopes are radioactive with a 2.5 x 108 years half-life.
Curium is a man-made element.

It was isolated in 1944 by Glenn T. Seaborg, Ralph A.
James and Albert Ghiorso, when they bombarded plutonium-
239 with alpha particles. They named the new element after
Pierre and Marie Curie, who themselves discovered radium
and polonium. Curium exists in some molybdenites.

The radiation these isotopes emit are easily converted
into electricity with compact units that are useful for powering
pacemakers, remote navigational buoys, and instruments
employed in space missions.

The element curium accumulates in mammalian bone.

Co-Cobalt is found in igneous rock at 25 ppm, shale
at 19 ppm, sandstone at 0.3 ppm, limestone at 0.1 ppm, fresh
water at 0.0009 ppm, sea water at 0.00027 ppm, soils at 8 ppm
(higher in soils derived from basalt or serpentine; vast areas
of the earth are known to be totally devoid of cobalt), marine
mammals at 0.5 ppm and terrestrial animals at 0.03 (greatest
concentrations in bone and liver).

Cobalt is a rare element, making up only 0.003 percent
of the earth’s crust. Cobalite is one of its common ores, a
compound made up of cobalt, arsenic and sulfur. The pure
metal, which has a characteristic bright bluish-white color,
is obtained by roasting this ore in air. Cobalt metal was first
isolated by the Swedish chemist Georg Brandt in 1739.

Many thousands of years ago the ancient Egyptians

===== Screenshot (76) =====
 


 

manufactured a form of blue glass that was made with traces
of cobalt. The name cobalt was derived in the 18th century
from the German word “kobald” that refers to an evil gnome
or spirit. Cobalt miners commonly blamed mining accidents
on the evil “kobald” or cobalt.

Cobalt is well known for its ability to be magnetized. It
is easily magnetized and retains its magnetism at temperatures
far higher than those that demagnetize iron. Twenty five
percent of all of the cobalt produced in the world is used to
make a super magnetic substance called “alnico.” Alnico is an
alloy of aluminum, nickel and cobalt and is used for making
industrial magnets.

Cobalt is essential for all forms of life including blue-green algae, some bacteria and fungi, some plants, insects,
birds, reptiles, amphibians and mammals including man.
Cobalt functions as a cofactor and an activator for enzymes,
“fixes” nitrogen during amino acid production, and a single
cobalt atom is the central metal component of vitamin B12,
which itself is a cofactor and activator (cobamide coenzymes)
for several essential enzymes.

B12 cobalt is chelated in a large tetrapyrrole ring
similar to the porphyrin ring found in hemoglobin (iron) and
chlorophyll (magnesium). The original B12 molecule isolated
in the laboratory contained a cyanide group, thus the name
cyanocobalamine. There are several different cobalamine
compounds that have B12 activity, with cyanocobalamine and
hydroxycobalamine being the most active.

Vitamin B12 is a red crystalline substance that is water
soluble. The red color is due to the cobalt atom in the molecule.
Vitamin B12 is slowly deactivated by exposure to acid, alkali,
light, and oxidizing or reducing substances. About 30% of
B12 activity is lost during cooking (electric, gas, microwave,
wood, charcoal, etc.).

In 1948 B12 was isolated from liver extracts and
demonstrated anti-pernicious anemia activity. The essentiality
of cobalt is unusual in that the requirement is for a cobalt

complex known as cyanocobalamine or vitamin B12. A pure
cobalt requirement is only found in some bacteria and algae
and the need for B12 cobalt is thought by some to represent a
symbiotic relationship between microbes which generate and
manufacture B12 from elemental cobalt and vertebrates that
require B12.

Ruminants (i.e. cows, sheep, goats, deer, antelope,
buffalo, giraffe, etc.) can use elemental cobalt because the
microbes fermenting and digesting plant material in their first
stomach (rumen) convert elemental cobalt into vitamin B12,
which the animal then uses.

Carnivores can get their B12 from their ruminant prey
by consuming stomach contents, liver, bone, and muscle from
their kills.

Poultry, lagomorphs (rabbits and hares), and rodents
actively eat feces during the night (coprophagy) and in the
process obtain vitamin B12 that has been manufactured by
intestinal microorganisms.

Metallic cobalt itself is absorbed at the rate of 20 to
26.2% by mice and in humans if intrinsic factor is present
in the stomach and the stomach pH is 2.0 or less. Intrinsic
factor is a mucoprotein enzyme known as Castle’s intrinsic
factor and is part of normal stomach secretions. If a person
has hypochlorhydria (low stomach acid- usually a NaCl
deficiency) the intrinsic factor will not work and B12 cobalt is
not absorbed - this is why doctors frequently give B12 shots
to older people on salt restricted diets. Sublingual (under the
tongue) and oral spray B12 is available. Plant derived cobalt is
very bioavailable; however, because of low salt diets and cobalt
depleted soils, vegetarians frequently have B12 deficiencies.

The B12 intrinsic factor complex is primarily absorbed in
the terminal small intestine or ileum. Calcium is required for
the B12 to cross from the intestine into the blood stream as well
as an active participation (active transport) by intestinal cells.
Simple diffusion can account for only one to three percent of
the vitamin’s absorption. There is an enterohepatic (intestine

===== Screenshot (77) =====
 

direct to the liver) circulation of vitamin B12 that recycles B12
from bile and other intestinal secretions which explains why
B12 deficiency in vegans may not appear for five to ten years
after giving up meat.

The maximum storage level of B12 is 2 mg which is slowly
released to the bone marrow as needed. Excess intake of B12
above the body’s storage capacity is shed in the urine (expensive
urine). Vitamin B12/cobalt joins with folic acid, choline, and
the amino acid methionine to transfer single carbon groups
(methyl groups) in the synthesis of the raw material to make
RNA and the synthesis of DNA from RNA. DNA and RNA
are directly involved in gene function; remember the concept
of preconception nutrition to prevent birth defects. Growth,
myelin formation (converts cholesterol into the insulating
material myelin found around nerves in the brain, spinal
cord, and large nerve trunks), and red blood cell synthesis are
dependent on B12. Cobalt is also required as a necessary cofactor
for the production of the thyroid hormone thyroxin.

The discovery of the essentiality of cobalt came from
observing a fatal disease (“bush sickness”) in cattle and
sheep from Australia and New Zealand. It was observed that
“bush sickness” could be successfully treated and prevented
by cobalt supplements. Bush sickness was characterized by
emaciation (unsupplemented vegans), dull stare, listless
and starved look, pale mucus membranes, anorexia, and
pernicious anemia (macrocytic/hypochromic).

In humans, a failure to absorb B12/cobalt results in a
deficiency disease. This can result from a surgical removal
of parts of the stomach (eliminates zones of intrinsic factor
production), or surgical removal of the ileum portion of
the small bowel, small intestinal diverticulae, parasites
(tapeworm), celiac disease (allergies to wheat gluten, barley,
rye, oats and cows milk albumen), and other malabsorption
diseases. Pernicious anemia and demyelination of the spinal
cord and large nerve trunks are classic diseases that are the
result of B12/cobalt deficiency.


 

Less than 0.07 ppm Co in the soil results in cobalt
deficiency in animals and people who eat crops grown from
those soils; 0.11 ppm Co in the soil prevents and cures Co
deficiency.

The human RDA for B12/cobalt is 3 to 4 mcg per day,
however 250 to 400 mcg gives a respectable safety margin.
Pregnant and nursing mothers should especially take care
to supplement with the optimum levels of B12. A baby being
nursed by a deficient mother has their congenital deficiency
extended over a longer period of time, which can result in a
serious and permanent form of nerve damage.

Cobalt excess in man (20 to 30 mg/day) may create an
accelerated erythropoiesis (RBC -red blood cell- production)
by stimulating an increased production of the kidney hormone
erythropoietin.

Cr-Chromium is found in igneous rocks at 100 ppm,
shale at 90 ppm, sandstone at 35 ppm, limestone at 11 ppm,
fresh water at 0.00018 ppm, sea water at 0.00005 ppm, soils
at 5 to 3,000 ppm (highest in soils derived from basalt and
serpentine), marine plants at 1 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.23
ppm, marine animals at 0.2 to 1.0 ppmand in terrestrial animals
at 0.075 ppm, where it accumulates in RNA and insulin.

Chromium was discovered by French chemist Louis-Nicolas Vauquelin in 1797 and named from the Greek word
chroma, which translates to “color.”

This word chromium was chosen because all of the
chromium compounds display a color; however, the pure
metal is a silver-white color. Although brittle, chromium is
quite hard.

Chromium metal is commonly extracted from chromite,
an oxide of chromium. Chromium oxide (Cr,O,) is among the
10 most abundant compounds in the crust of the earth. When
exposed to air, chromium forms an almost invisible oxide
that makes it extremely resistant to corrosion and very useful
for decorative and protective coatings over other metals

===== Screenshot (78) =====
 



including brass (bathroom fixtures), bronze (jewelry) and
steel (car bumpers). Chromium added to steel at the rate of
18% produces “stainless steel.”

The beautiful and radiant colors, of many precious gems,
are also due to the presence of trace amounts of chromium.
The red color of rubies is produced by small amounts of
chromium in the form of corundum. The characteristic green
color of emeralds is produced by the presence of the chromium
bearing mineral beryl.

One of the more exotic precious stones that contain
chromium is alexandrite, which contains a small amount
of chromium in the base mineral chrysoberyl. Alexandrite
changes color depending on the light source - when seen in
firelight it is a bright red, when seen in daylight it is a brilliant
blue.

Chromium activates phosphoglucomutase and other
enzymes and is closely associated with glucose tolerance factor
(GTF). Glucose tolerance factor is a combination of chromium
III, dinicotinic acid and glutathione. The reported plasma
levels of chromium in humans over the past 20 years has
ranged from 0.075 to 13 ng/ml. Concentrations of chromium
in human hair are ten times greater than in blood, making
hair analysis a much more accurate view of chromium tissue
stores and function in the human. There is an average of 1.5
mg of chromium in the human body.

Very little inorganic chromium is stored in the body.
Once inorganic chromium is absorbed, it is almost entirely
excreted in the urine (therefore urine chromium levels can
be used to estimate dietary chromium status). Dietary sugar
loads (i.e. colas, apple juice, grape juice, honey, candy, table
sugar, fructose, etc.) increase the natural rate of urinary Cr loss
by 300% for 12 hours. The average intake of 50 to 100 ug of
inorganic chromium from food and water supplies only 0.25
to 5 ug of usable and retainable chromium. By contrast, 25%
of chelated chromium is absorbed and up to 98% of colloidal


 

chromium is absorbed. The chromium RDA for humans is a
range of 50 to 200 ug of usable chromium per day for adults.

The concentration of chromium found in newborn
animals and humans is higher per pound of body weight
than it is in older individuals. In fact, the chromium levels of
unsupplemented human tissue, steadily decreases throughout
life. Of greater concern has been the steady decline in the
average American serum chromium since 1948.

The fasting level of plasma chromium is lower in
pregnant women than in non-pregnant women. Increasing
impairment of glucose tolerance in “normal” pregnancy is
well documented and reflects a chromium deficiency that
oftentimes results in pregnancy onset diabetes. One study
demonstrated abnormal glucose tolerance in 77% of clinically
“normal” adults over the age of 70. According to Richard
Anderson of the USDA, “Ninety percent of all Americans are
deficient in chromium.”

===== Screenshot (79) =====
 



Gary Evans, Bemidji State University, Minnesota, very
clearly showed an increased lifespan in laboratory animals
by 33.3%, when they were supplemented, with chromium.
Prior to this study, gerontologist, led by Dr. Roy Walford,
felt a Calorie Restricted diet was the only way to extend life
past the expected average age. Deficiencies of chromium in
humans are characterized by a wide variety of clinical diseases
as well as a shortened life expectancy. The clinical diseases of
chromium deficiency are aggravated by concurrent vanadium
deficiencies and increased sugar or alcohol intakes.

Cs-Cesium is found in igneous rocks at 1 ppm, shale at 5
ppm, sandstone and limestone at 0.5 ppm, fresh water at 0.0002
ppm, sea water at 0.00005 ppm, soils at 0.3 to 25 ppm, marine
plants at 0.07 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.2 ppm, terrestrial
animals at 0.064 ppm (highest concentrations in muscle).

As an alkaline mineral, cesium behaves chemically in a
similar manner as sodium, potassium and rubidium. Cesium
and potassium enter into a solute complex, which participates
in ion antagonism, osmosis, permeability regulation, and
maintenance of the colloidal state in the living cell. The increase
in supplemental potassium increases the rate of excretion or
loss of cesium.

Cesium was discovered in 1860 by the German chemists
Robert Bunsen and Gustav Kirchhoff. They isolated cesium by
observing colors or spectral emission lines, that cesium emits
when it is heated. The color lines were bright blue, which
prompted them to name the element cesium after the Latin
coesius (“sky blue”).

Cesium chloride is used as part of alternative cancer
therapy programs. Cesium provides “high pH therapy” for
cancer by entering the cancer cell and producing an alkaline
environment. It has been recommended for all cancers including
sarcomas, bronchiogenic carcinoma and colon cancer.
