Cu-Copper is found in igneous rocks at 55 ppm, shale
at 45 ppm, sandstone at 5 ppm, limestone at 4 ppm, fresh water
at 0.01 ppm, sea water at 0.003 ppm, soils at 2 to 100 ppm
(copper is strongly bound and retained by humus, but there
are known areas in the world with extreme copper deficiency),
marine plants at 11 ppm, terrestrial plants at 14 ppm, marine
animals at 4 to 50 ppm (accumulates in the blood of annelid
worms, crustaceans and mollusks, especially cephalopods),
and in terrestrial animals at 2 to 4 ppm with highest levels
being found in the liver.

Despite the common use of copper, it makes up only
0.007% of the earth’s crust. Copper gets its name from the
Latin word, cuprum, which translates to “from Cyprus,” a
reference to the fact that the Romans mined their copper from
the island of Cyprus.

Copper is easily refined from many different ores and
was known and employed by many ancient civilizations,
copper jewelry dating back to 9000 B.C. was discovered in
Iraq. Copper is soft and had to be mixed with tin (bronze),
zinc (brass) or nickel (monel) to make harder alloys for tools
and weapons.

Copper is an essential nutrient for all living organisms
and is a universally important cofactor for many hundreds
of metalloenzymes. Copper deficiency is widespread and
common and copper deficiency diseases have an important
impact on human populations.

Copper deficiency Symptoms and Diseases
White, grey and “silver” hair

Vitiligo (loss of skin pigment)

Dry brittle hair (“steely wool” in sheep)

Ptosis (sagging tissue - eye lids, “crows feet,” skin, arm


 

flappers, breasts, belly)

Hernias (congenital and acquired)

Varicose veins (including hemorrhoids)

Spider veins

Aneurysms (cerebral artery, coronary artery, aorta and large
artery “blowouts”)

Kawasaki disease (congenital aneurysms with streptococcal
infections)

Anemia (especially common in high milk and vegan diets)

Hypo and hyperthyroid dysfunction

Arthritis (especially where immature bone growth plates are
involved)

Disc disease (ruptured, bulging, degenerative)

Liver cirrhosis

Violent behavior, blind rage, explosive outbursts and
“criminal” behavior

Learning disabilities (ADD, ADHD, some forms of autism)

Cerebral palsy, aplasia and hypoplasia of the cerebellum

Enzootic ataxia in sheep

Mad Cow Disease in cattle (same disease as enzootic
ataxia-sheep)

Creutzfeldt-Jakob Disease (Humans-madcow disease)

Elevated blood cholesterol (above 350)

Iron storage disease (hemosiderosis)

Reduced carbohydrate tolerance

Neutropenia (low neutrophil count)

===== Screenshot (81) =====
 


Copper is required for many subcellular and
physiological functions: RNA, DNA, Lysyl oxidase (elastic
fiber cofactor), melanin production (skin and hair), electron
transport for subcellular respiration, tensile strength of elastic
fibers in skin, hair, wool (“steely wool”), cartilage, tendons,
ligaments, vertebral discs, blood vessels, etc.

Neonatal “enzootic ataxia” (sway back, lamkruis) was
recognized as a clinical and disease entity in 1937 as a copper
deficiency in pregnant sheep. Copper supplements prevented
the syndrome, which was characterized by demyelination of
the cerebellum and spinal cord.

“Mad Cow Disease” and Creutzfeldt-Jakob Disease in
humans, while thought by many, to be an infectious disease of
cattle and humans that is transmitted by “prions,” a needle-like
protein crystalloid that is neither a bacteria, fungus or virus,
has not been experimentally transmitted to any laboratory
animal, livestock or humans.

Efforts to transmit Mad Cow Disease have been a failure.
The brain lesions of Bovine Spongiform Encephalopathy
(BSE) appear to be similar to those of Enzootic Ataxia in sheep
- a copper deficiency.

Cavitation or gelatinous lesions of the cerebral white
matter, chromatolysis, nerve cell death and myelin aplasia
(failure to form during embryonic development) were also
identified as a copper deficiency disease in sheep - these
congenital lesions are identical to those of the classical changes
of human cerebral palsy.

Famous people affected or dying of an obvious copper
deficiency include: Albert Einstein (ruptured aortic aneurysm),
Franklin D. Roosevelt (ruptured cerebral aneurysm), Paavo
Airola (ruptured cerebral aneurysm), Conway Twitty
(ruptured abdominal aortic aneurysm), John Ritter (ruptured
abdominal aortic aneurysm), George and Barbara Bush
(thyroid disease and white hair). Four to six out of every 100
Americans autopsied have died of a ruptured aneurysm; an
additional 40% of Americans autopsied have aneurysms that


 

have not ruptured.

The average well-nourished adult human body contains
between 80 to 120 mg of copper. Concentrations are higher
in the brain, liver, heart and kidneys. Bone and muscle have
lower percentages of copper but contain 50% of the body’s total
copper reserves because of their mass. It is of interest that the
greatest concentrations of copper are found in the newborn.

The daily copper requirement for the newborn is 0.08
mg/kg; toddlers require 0.04 mg/kg; and adults only 0.03
mg/kg.

The average plasma copper levels of women ranges
from 87 to 153 mg/dl; for men the range is 89 to 137 mg/dl.
About 90% of the plasma copper is bound in a protein called
ceruloplasmin.

Copper functions as a cofactor and activator of numerous
cupro-enzymes that are involved with the development and
maintenance of the cardiovascular system. Deficiency of
copper in the pregnant female results in congenital defects of
the heart and vascular system (Kawasaki disease) and brain
(cerebral palsy, atresia and hypoplasia of the cerebellum).
Deficiency of copper also results in reduced lysyl oxidase
activity causing a reduction in conversion of proelastin to
elastin causing a decrease in the tensile strength of venous
and arterial walls (spider veins, varicose veins, hemorrhoids
and aneurysms).

Deficiency of copper also results in a reduced skeletal,
connective tissue and muscular integrity which can produce
a specific type of arthritis in children that forms bone spurs in
the bone growth plates.

Lack of copper can result in myelin defects, anemia,
poor hair keratinization and loss of hair color. Neutropenia
(reduced numbers of neutrophilic WBCs or white blood cells)
and leucopenia (reduced total WBC count) are the earliest
indications of a copper deficiency in an infant. Infants whose
diets are primarily cow’s milk frequently develop copper
deficiency anemia and/or iron storage disease.

===== Screenshot (82) =====
 

ee ————————————E————

Menkes’ Kinky Hair Syndrome is thought to be a sex-linked recessive genetic defect of copper absorption. The affected
infants exhibit retarded growth, defective keratin formation
of the hair, loss of hair pigment, low body temperature (low
thyroid function, anemia), degeneration and fractures of aortic
elastin (aneurysms), arthritis in the growth plates of long bones,
and a progressive mental deterioration. Mental deterioration
results from brain tissue being totally devoid of the essential
copper dependent enzyme cytochrome oxidase.

Because of the absorption problems of elemental copper,
injections of copper and liver extracts and the employment of
a diet program designed for celiac patients will be useful for
children with the Menkes’ Kinky Hair Syndrome.

Serum and plasma copper increases 100% in pregnant
women and women using oral contraceptives. Serum copper
levels are also elevated during acute infections, liver disease,
and pellagra (niacin deficiency).

Accumulations of copper in the cornea form Kayser-Fleischer Rings.

Dy-Dysprosium, a rare earth, is found in igneous rocks
at 3.0 ppm, in shale at 4 to 6 ppm, sandstone at 7.2 ppm and
limestone at 0.9 ppm. and in terrestrial animals at 0.01 ppm
(accumulates in the bones).

Of the rare earths, dysprosium is a metal that ranks
ninth in abundance. It was discovered in 1886 by the French
chemist Paul-Emile Lecoq de Boisbaudran who was working
with a sample of erbium oxide. It was named for the Greek
word dysprositos, which translates to “hard to get at.”

Dysprosium is a metal that is soft enough to be cut
with a knife; it is a shiny, silvery element that is fairly stable
in air. Dysprosium forms alloys whose magnetic features are
employed to help compact discs function efficiently.

Er-Erbium, a rare earth, is found in igneous rock at 2.8
ppm, shale at 1.9 ppm, sandstone at 1 ppm, limestone at 0.36


 

ppm, terrestrial plants accumulate erbium (up to 46 ppm in
Carya spp.), marine animals at 0.02 to 0.04 ppm and terrestrial
animals primarily in bone.

Erbium is a scarce rare earth metal that was discovered
in 1843 by Carl Gustaf Mosander. The main sources of erbium
are the minerals xenotime and euxerite. Erbium, along with
other rare earths is considered an impurity in these ores.

Erbium is employed in special alloys with metals such
as vanadium to improve their workability characteristics.
Erbium is frequently added to glass and enamel to tint them
pink, this glass is commonly used for sunglasses and costume
jewelry.

Eu-Europium is a “light” rare earth found in igneous
rocks at 1 to 2 ppm, shale at 1.1 ppm, sandstone at 0.55
ppm, limestone at 0.2 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.021 ppm
(accumulates up to 16 ppm in Carya spp.), marine animals at
0.01 to 0.06 ppm, and terrestrial animals at 0.00012 ppm in
soft tissue and 0.2 ppm in bone.

Europium is one of the most-rare of the rare earth
elements. It was isolated in 1901 by the French chemist
Eugene-Anatole Demarcay, who named the new element for
the continent of Europe.

Europium is found mixed in ores containing monazite
and bastnasite. Monazite contains all of the rare earths along
with thorium and calcium. Monazite is found in river sands
from India and Brazil and in beach sand in Florida. Large
deposits of bastnasite are located in Southern California.

Europium is the most soft of all of the rare earths and
will ignite spontaneously in air at temperatures above 150
degrees C.

Europium supplementation has extended the life
expectancy of laboratory species over their expected lifespan
by 100%. Europium is found in higher concentrations in breast
milk from women in third world countries than in American
women.

F-Fluorine is found in igneous rocks at 625 ppm, in
shale at 740 ppm, sandstone at 270 ppm, limestone at 330 ppm,
fresh water at 0.09 ppm, sea water at 1.3 ppm, and soils at
200 ppm (fluoride can be “fixed” or tightly bonded in several
types of clay).

Fluorine is a member of the halogen family or “salt
formers.” The other members are chlorine, bromine, iodine
and astatine. All halogen atoms react chemically by accepting
electrons from other atoms and easily combine with metals
to form compounds known as salts. Fluorine is the smallest,
lightest and most reactive member of the halogen family.

Fluorine is a pale yellow gas whose molecules contain
two atoms. Fluorine is far too reactive to be found in nature
in its diatomic form. Fairly large quantities of fluorine occur
in the minerals fluorite or calcium fluoride (oftentimes called
fluorspar).

The French chemist Henri Moissan won the Nobel
Prize in 1906 for being the first to produce fluorine in its pure
elemental form. The name fluorine is derived from the Latin
word fluere, which translates to “to flow.” It took 75 years of
continuous effort on the part of many chemists before fluorine
was finally isolated by Moissan in 1886.

Certain types of fluorine rich soils in Madras, Spain
South America are toxic to grazing livestock. Fluorine is found
in marine plants at 4.5 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.5 to 40.0
ppm (accumulates in Dichapetolum cymosum), marine animals
at 2.0 ppm (accumulates in fish bones), and terrestrial animals
at 150 to 500 ppm in mammalian soft tissue and 1,500 ppm in
teeth and bones.

Teeth are protected by a hard “enamel” that is composed
of a mineral called hydroxyapatite. This mineral is not soluble
under normal circumstances, however, in the presence of
weak acids that oral bacteria produce from high sugar foods
and drinks, some dissolving of the hydroxyapatite structure
occurs.

The resulting loss of the hydroxyapatite enamel


promotes the initiation of tooth decay or “caries.” One of the
body’s defenses against the breakdown of hydroxyapatite is
to generate new enamel continuously (remineralization).

In the presence of sodium fluoride, the remineralization
process manufactures a form of enamel referred to as
fluorapatite. Fluorapatite is considerably more resistant to
damage from acid than the natural enamel. It is this process
that has motivated the producers of toothpaste to add sodium
fluoride to their products.

Prior to 1972, fluoride was considered essential in
animals because of its apparent benefit for tooth enamel in
warding off dental caries (“cavities”). In 1972, Schwarz proved
that fluoride was in fact an essential mineral for animals and
humans.

The skeletal reserves of fluoride in an adult man can
reach 2.6 gm (2,600 mg); the average daily intake by Americans
is 4.4 mg from combined sources of food, water, toothpaste
and nutritional supplements.

The universal fluoridation of drinking water is still
highly controversial. Some studies show that fluoridated
water helps reduce fractures from osteoporosis, while other
studies showed an increase in hip fractures. Clinical toxicity
is observed as dental fluorosis at fluoride concentrations of 2
to 7 ppm and osteosclerosis at 8 to 20 ppm. Chronic systemic
toxicity appears when the daily fluoride intake levels are
above 20 to 80 ppm over several years.

Approximately 10,000 American towns and cities
serving a total of 100 million people have added fluoride to
their municipal drinking water at the rate of 1 mg/L, which
has reportedly reduced dental caries by 60 to 70%. In certain
western states in the U.S., there is an excess of fluoride,
reaching levels of 10 to 45 ppm with a resultant mottling of
teeth in children.

As a result of epidemiological studies by Yiamouyiannis
and Burk in 1977, full scale congressional hearings were held
to examine the charge that 10,000 excess cancer deaths were

===== Screenshot (84) =====
 


peer. Uae eye
caused by fluoridation of certain public water systems. As
a result of those hearings, the committee mandated that the
U.S. Public Health Service conduct animal studies to confirm
or refute the theory that fluoridated water increased the rate
of cancer deaths. The studies were carried out by the National
Toxicology Program under the supervision of the U.S. National
Public Health Service with a special focus on oral, liver and
skeletal cancers.

In 1990, the results of the fluoride study showed an
increase in rat precancerous lesions in mucus membrane cells.
There was an increase in cancers of the oral mucus membranes
(squamous cell carcinoma). A rare form of osteosarcoma
appeared at double the rate in males as females, and there was
an increase in thyroid follicular cell tumors and liver cancer
(hepatocholangiocarcinoma).

Fe-Iron is found in igneous rocks at 56,000 ppm, shale
at 47,200 ppm, sandstone at 9,800 ppm, limestone at 3,800
ppm, fresh water at 0.67 ppm, sea water at 0.01 ppm, and
soils at 38,000 ppm (iron content is responsible for most soil
color). Iron is most available in acid soil and availability is
greatly determined by bacterial activity in the soil. It’s found
in marine plants at 700 ppm (very high in plankton), terrestrial
plants at 140 ppm, marine animals at 400 ppm (high in the
blood of annelid worms, echinoderms, fish, and in the eggs of
cephalopod molluscs).

Iron is the fourth most abundant element in the earth’s
crust and is second only to aluminum as the most abundant
metal. The “core” or interior of the earth is theorized to be
made up of magnetized, molten iron.

Iron is rarely found in its pure form in nature, most iron
ores are iron mixed with oxygen; two of the most important
iron-containing minerals are hematite and magnetite.

Iron was identified, as a useful metal, thousands of years
ago. The ability to extract iron from the red-brown ores was
a milestone in human development - known as the Iron Age


 

(1100 B.C.). The discovery of iron and man’s understanding
of it allowed the production of tools and weapons that were
harder and more durable than those of the Stone Age and
the Bronze Age (3000 B.C.). Today, iron makes up 90% of all
metals that are extracted from ore.

Today, iron is one of the most commonly used metals in
all human cultures. Iron and its alloys (steel) play a universal
role in every aspect of daily life. Whenever we use a tool or
appliance, vehicle (car, truck, train, boat, airplane, etc.) or build
something (frame, nails, screws, bolts, pipe, etc.), the importance
and utility of iron as a structural material is obvious.

Iron is an essential nutrient for all terrestrial species
of animals. It is the four iron atoms in each hemoglobin
molecule that are central to its structure and physiological
function. Oxygen actually bonds to the iron atoms in the lung
capillaries, and then are transported via the circulation to
distant cells.

Carbon monoxide, a byproduct of imperfect
combustion, is bound to the hemoglobin iron 200 times more
tightly than oxygen - this phenomenon can lead to “carbon
monoxide poisoning!”

In the 1860's, Boussingault, was the first to regard iron
as an essential nutrient for animals. During the 1920s, feeding
rats on an exclusive milk diet created an animal model for
iron deficiency research.

In a healthy adult human there is three to five grams
of iron, the newborn infant has nearly double the amount of
iron per kg than adults. Sixty to 70% of tissue iron is used as
essential or functional iron, and 30 to 40% as storage iron.

Essential iron is found as an integral part of hemoglobin,
myoglobin (muscle oxygen storing pigments - particularly
rich in deep diving animals such as whales, dolphins, walrus,
seals, etc.), and subcellular respiratory enzymes involved
with oxidation and electron transfer processes.

Functions of iron include cofactor and activator of
enzymes and metallo enzymes, respiratory pigments (iron

===== Screenshot (85) =====
 


is to hemoglobin what magnesium is to chlorophyll), and
electron transfer for the subcellular utilization of oxygen.

Iron is stored in bone marrow and liver (i.e. hemosiderin
and ferritin). Heme iron from meat is 10% available for
absorption, while iron from fresh plant sources are only one
percent available because of the endogenous phytates in the
plant. Iron absorption takes place primarily in the duodenum
where the intestinal environment is still acid.

Experimental evidence shows very clearly that
“pica” is a specific sign of iron deficiency. The pica behavior
can drive children and adults to eat ice (pagophagia), dirt
(geophagia), lead paint, excess food (“munchies,” obesity),
alcohol (alcoholism) and sugar (sweet tooth).

Iron deficiency can be the result of unsupplemented
pregnancy, dysmenorrhea, chronic infections, hypochlorhydria
(low stomach acid from a salt-restricted diet), chronic diarrhea,
chronic bleeding (i.e. cancer, ulcers, parasites, blood thinners,
nutritional deficiencies, etc.) and impaired absorption (i.e.
high fat diets, high fiber diets, celiac disease, etc.).

Symptoms of iron deficiency include listlessness,
fatigue, heart palpitations on exertion, reduced cognition,
memory deficits, sore tongue, angular stomatitis, dysphagia
(poor appetite, pica, etc.), obesity and hypochromic microcytic
anemia.

Stomach hydrochloric acid is required for optimal
absorption of iron. Ascorbic acid increases the absorption of
iron; clays and dietary fiber (phytates) decrease the absorption
of iron. The RDA of 18 mg per day as metallic iron is too low
for those individuals consuming high fiber, high phytate diets
(vegans, vegetarians, etc.).

“Excesses of iron can result in cirrhosis of the liver,
fibrosis of the pancreas, diabetes and heart failure. These
diseases are not the direct result of toxic effects of iron, but
rather, the increased iron consumption produces an increased
need for selenium, chromium, vanadium, copper, zinc, etc.


Fr-Francium is found only as radioactive isotopes. The
longest lived of its isotopes has a half life of 22 minutes. Of its
30 known isotopes, only francium-223 exists in nature. It is
estimated that the earth’s crust contains less than one ounce
of francium.

The element was discovered in 1939 by Marguerite
Perey, when she worked at the Curie Institute in Paris.
Francium was named for France where it was discovered.

Ga-Gallium is found in igneous rocks at 15 ppm, shale
at 19 ppm, sandstone at 12 ppm, limestone at 4.0 ppm, fresh
water at 0.001 ppm, sea water at 0.00003 ppm, soils at 0.4 to
30.0 ppm, marine plants at 0.5 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.06
ppm, marine animals at 0.5 ppm, and in terrestrial animals at
0.006 ppm.

Gallium belongs to the same family of elements as
aluminum and shares many of the same chemical properties.
It is an extremely soft metal that can easily be cut with a knife.
It also has an extremely low melting point of 29.8 degrees C,
allowing gallium to liquefy when it is held in the hand. Gallium
has an extremely high boiling point of 2,403 degrees C, allowing
gallium to be in its liquid form in a range greater than that of
any known metal.

The history of gallium is unique as it is one of the
elements that was predicted to exist by Mendeleyev (creator of
the periodic chart) in 1871. Paul-Emile Lecoq de Boisbaudran
identified gallium in 1875 and named it for Gallia, the Latin
word for France.

Gallium was claimed to be an essential element in 1938
and again in 1958. Gallium has specific areas of metalloenzyme
activity in the human brain and has been reported to specifically
reduce the rate of brain cancer in laboratory animals. British
research has shown that diets, of pregnant women that are
supplemented with gallium, significantly reduces the rate of
brain cancer in children.

Gallium-67 was one of the first artificially produced

===== Screenshot (86) =====
 


radioactive isotopes to be used in medicine. Because of its
short half-life (78 hours), it rapidly loses its radioactivity and
its destructive properties. This isotope has the property of
accumulating in certain cancers (melanoma) and is used to
treat these cancers without damage to surrounding tissues.

Gd-Gadolinium, a rare earth, is found in igneous rocks
at 5.4 ppm, shale at 4.3 ppm, sandstone at 2.6 ppm, limestone
at 0.7 ppm, terrestrial plants (Carya spp.) can concentrate
gadolinium up to 70 ppm, and in marine animals at 0.06 ppm.
Terrestrial animals accumulate gadolinium in bone and liver
almost immediately after absorption.

Ge-Germanium is found in igneous rocks at 5.4 ppm,
shale at 1.6 ppm, sandstone at 0.8 ppm, limestone at 0.2 ppm,
sea water at 0.00007 ppm, soil at 1.0 ppm in humus in alkaline
soils and in marine animals at 0.3 ppm.

The existence of the “metalloid” element germanium or
eka-silicon, had been predicted by Mendeleyev in his periodic
table in 1871, however, it was not until 1886 that Clemens
Winkler, a German scientist, isolated the silicon-like element
germanium. Germanium is a poor conductor of electricity
and therefore is relegated to a semi-conductor status.

The addition of small amounts of other elements such
as arsenic, gallium, or antimony is called “doping” - this
process greatly increases its ability to conduct electricity.
Doped germanium is used to manufacture transistors, which
essentially become micro-computers.

Symptoms of a typical germanium deficiency include
reduced immune status, arthritis, osteoporosis, low energy,
fatigue and cancer.

Twenty to 30 mg per day is the recommended
maintenance dose for germanium. Fifty to 100 mg per day
doses are commonly used when an individual has a serious
germanium deficiency.

Radio-do-it-yourself kits from the 40s and 50s utilized


the germanium diode crystal to attract the radio signal to the
radio. The germanium atom is structured so it accepts and
transmits electrons effectively acting as a semiconductor. It is
not too surprising that germanium is closely related to silica
and carbon. Biologically, germanium is a highly effective
electrical impulse initiator intracellularly and acts as a metallic
cofactor for oxygen utilization.

In 1950, Dr. Kazuhiko Asai, a Japanese chemist, found
traces of germanium in fossilized plantlife. Russian researchers
quickly attributed anti-cancer activity to germanium.

Dr. Asai was able to connect the healing properties of
certain herbs to relatively high levels of germanium. Many of
these herbs are germanium accumulator plants. Germanium
is known to enhance the effectiveness of the immune
system by stimulating the production of natural killer cells,
lymphokines, such as IFN(γ) interferon, macrophages, and T-suppressor cells.

Asai synthesized Ge-132, carboxyethyl germanium
sesquioxide, in 1967 by a hydrolysis reaction. This organic
germanium structure forms a cubic structure with three
negative oxygen ions at the base of a cubic triangle. As an
organic or chelated form of germanium, Ge-132 is absorbed
at the rate of 30% efficiency and the total intake is completely
excreted in one week.

Food plants and animals contain small amounts of
germanium (i.e. beans - 4.67 ppm, tuna - 2.3 ppm). Healing
herbs such as garlic, aloe, comfrey, chlorella, ginseng,
watercress, Shiitake mushroom, pearl barley, sanzukon,
sushi, waternut, boxthorn seed and wisteria knob contain
germanium in amounts ranging from 100 to 2,000 ppm.

The “Holy Waters” at Lourdes, France, known world
wide for their healing properties, contains large amounts of
germanium and lithium.

H-Hydrogen is found in igneous rocks at 1,000 ppm,
shale at 5,600 ppm, sandstone at 1,800 ppm, limestone at 860
ppm, fresh water at 111,000 ppm, sea water at 108,000 ppm,

===== Screenshot (87) =====
 



soil at 600 to 24,000 ppm (in very acid soils it can become the
major exchangeable cation), marine plants at 41,000 ppm,
terrestrial plants at 55,000 ppm, marine animals at 52,000
ppm, and in terrestrial animals at 70,000 ppm.

Hydrogen is the most simple of all atoms, consisting
of a single proton, its nucleus, which is circled by a single
electron. Its simple and basic structure partially explains
why hydrogen is the most abundant element in the universe.
Enormous quantities of hydrogen gas is found in interstellar
space, and is by far the most dominant element found in the
stars. Hydrogen atoms make up a whopping 93% of all the
atoms in the universe.

It is surprising, with the great and diverse role played
by hydrogen in the universe, that there is only a small quantity
of hydrogen gas in the earth’s atmosphere. Out of 100 million
liters of atmospheric air, hydrogen would only make up 5
liters!

Hydrogen gas is typically a diatomic molecule, meaning
that a hydrogen molecule is made up of two hydrogen atoms
(H2). Hydrogen gas is colorless, odorless and tasteless;
however, hydrogen is extremely flammable.

When hydrogen burns in air, it reacts violently with
oxygen to form large amounts of heat and water. It is this
reaction with oxygen that gave hydrogen its name, which
is translated from the Greek words hydro (water) and genes
(creator).

The identification of hydrogen as an element is credited
to the English chemist Henry Cavendish (1731-1810) after
whom the Cambridge University’s Cavendish Laboratory
was named.

Cavendish, a frugal, shy, stuttering and eccentric
scientist, was the son of an extremely wealthy English
aristocrat. Cavendish never spoke directly to women; he gave
instructions to his female servants by handwritten letters,
and would summarily fire them if they came into his line of
sight as he walked through his laboratory or house. He spent


little of his great fortune and spent his entire life buried in his
laboratory “in the pursuit of knowledge.”

In 1766, Cavendish was the first to produce hydrogen
gas in the laboratory by placing a piece of zinc in hydrochloric
acid. He deduced that the “inflammable air,” was a distinct
and separate substance and in fact a separate element.

Cavendish accurately calculated the mathematical
value for the gravitational force (G) and the mass of the
earth.

Hydrogen in its free form is a very light gas, weighing
so little, that the gravitational pull of the earth, was not strong
enough to keep the hydrogen gas from escaping into outer
space. Most of the hydrogen gas found on earth is bound in
the water of oceans and lakes.

By contrast, larger planets such as Saturn and Jupiter
have a greater gravitational force and their atmosphere has a
much higher percentage of hydrogen gas.

Hydrogen is widely known for being a gas that is
lighter than atmospheric “air.” A balloon of any size that
is filled with hydrogen gas will quickly rise, float and drift
away. The low density of hydrogen gives it its enormous
lifting power. Hydrogen was once the main lifting power for
manned balloons and “blimps.” A discharge spark from a
grounded docking line set the hydrogen containing German
blimp, Hindenburg, on fire in 1937, causing the “lighter than
air” airship industry to abandon the gas and switch to the
non-flammable helium.

Hydrogen makes up a small portion of the gaseous
atmosphere; hydrogen functions as a major constituent of
water and all organic molecules. Seventy percent of the human
body is water. The regulation of the acid-base balance in the
human body is in fact the regulation of the hydrogen ion (H+)
levels of cellular and extracellular fluids.

The acidity of the body is critically regulated within
a narrow range by numerous and complex homeostatic
mechanisms. The pH of healthy blood ranges from 7.36 to 7.44.

===== Screenshot (88) =====
 




When the pH falls below 7.30, the patient has acidosis; when
the blood pH rises above 7.44, the individual has alkalosis.

Blood pH below 6.8 and above 7.8 is rapidly fatal.
Intracellular pH ranges between 6.0 and 7.4. Rapid metabolism
(hyperthyroid, Graves' disease) or decreased blood flow
(heart attack) increases the carbon dioxide levels and therefore
decreases pH or acidifies the blood. In contrast to the internal
body, the pH of secretions and excretions can be more variable
and range from 1.0 (stomach acid) to 8.2 (pancreatic juice) and
alkaline urine in vegans.

Hydrogen ions circulate in the body in two forms,
volatile and non-volatile (metabolic hydrogen ions). Volatile
hydrogen ions are found as a weak acid (carbonic acid), which
must continuously be excreted from the lungs as carbon
dioxide and water.

Non-volatile (metabolic) hydrogen ions are produced
by the normal metabolic processes of the body or are taken in
as food. The largest amounts of hydrogen ions are produced
by normal and abnormal metabolism. Large amounts of
hydrogen ions may be generated and/or retained as part of a
disease activity (i.e. emphysema, diabetes, anxiety or loss of
chloride ions, NaCl [salt] deficiency, cystic fibrosis, Addison’s
Disease, etc.).

Hydrogen ion concentration (pH) is controlled by the
body by means of dilution, buffering respiratory control of
volatile hydrogen ion concentrations and kidney-control of
non-volatile hydrogen ions. Buffer systems react to hydrogen
ion concentrations in fractions of seconds, respiratory controls
react in minutes, and the kidneys may require as much as an
hour to several days to respond.

Metabolic hydrogen ions must be excreted by the
kidney in one of three forms: 60% as ammonium ions, 40% as
weak acids, or trace amounts as free hydrogen ions. It is the
amount of free hydrogen ions in the urine that determines the
urine’s pH.

Acidifying the urine with unsweetened cranberry juice


can often times control bladder infections (cystitis). Acidifying
the urine and saliva can also be accomplished by consuming
meat. Alkalinizing the urine and saliva can be accomplished
by consuming vegetables.

Hydrogen is found in an innumerable number of organic
or carbon-containing compounds, and biological compounds
found in living organisms. It is commonly combined directly
with carbon in organic molecules (hydrocarbons).

Hydrocarbons are long chain-like molecules that make
up natural gas and oil which are burned to produce the energy
that is used to drive industry, vehicles and provide raw
materials for the production of many commercial products
(i.e. plastics, pesticides, fertilizer, clothing, dyes, perfumes,
etc.).

Hydrogen is also a significant part of the molecules
that make up living organisms such as carbohydrate, sugar,
fats, proteins, enzymes, DNA, RNA, etc. In the commercial
preparation of “food,” enormous quantities of hydrogen are
used in the “hydrogenation” process and in the production of
“trans fatty acids.” This process is accomplished by adding
hydrogen gas to liquid vegetable oils (i.e. soy, corn, cotton
seed, rape seed, sunflower, sesame seed, olive, etc.) in order
to convert it to the solid “margarine.”

Hydrogenated and partially hydrogenated vegetable
oils, once touted by the medical trade as a safe substitute for
butter in the prevention of arteriosclerosis, atherosclerosis and
stroke, are now known to be the direct cause of these diseases.
The consumption of fried foods, super-heated carbohydrates
(breads, French fries, croutons, etc.), margarines, cooking
oils and trans fatty acids are now known to produce the
“inflammation” that damages the intima or arterial lining
that produces clogged and blocked arteries, blood clots,
thrombosis and stroke.

He-Helium is found in igneous rocks at 0.008 ppm and
sea water at 0.0000069 ppm. One only has to add a second
proton and two neutrons to the hydrogen nucleus to produce

===== Screenshot (89) =====
 


 

the helium molecule. Helium is found in the periodic table at
the head of the “noble gas” column - these “noble gases” are
inert and gave the “impression that they were keeping aloof
from the other elements.”

Helium was discovered on the sun before it was
discovered on earth. In 1868, the French astronomer Pierre
Janssen (1824-1907) observed an unusual and unexpected
spectral line during his analysis of the spectral emission of
the sun. Janssen reasoned quite correctly that the new spectral
line was the emission of an unknown element. He named the
new element helium, translated from the Greek word helios,
meaning sun. In 1895 the Scottish chemist William Ramsay
discovered helium gas in a sample of uranium ore.

Spectral emissions refer to the wavelengths of light
given off by each element when it is heated. Each element
gives off colors that are unique to its self. Scientists use these
color emissions to identify unknown elements much in the
same manner that finger prints are used to identify people.

Helium is colorless, odorless and is so inert that it will
not even react or combine with itself. It is so inert that it is
one of those few elements that are found in nature in its pure
elemental form. No stable compound of helium has ever been
manufactured.

Helium is second only to hydrogen as the most abundant
element in the universe. Helium makes up seven percent of
all the existing atoms. Together, helium and hydrogen make
up 99.9% of elements in the universe. Helium ranks sixth
amongst the gasses found in the earth’s atmosphere.

Almost all of the helium gas on earth is found in
“natural gas,” produced by the decay of uranium and radon
radioactive isotopes. The more common name for these
helium nuclei is “alpha particles,” named by their discoverer,
Sir Ernest Rutherford, a professor of physics at the Cavendish
Laboratory at Cambridge University.

Divers use helium/ oxygen “air” when working at great
depths; helium being less soluble than nitrogen dissolves


at a low percentage in blood, therefore reducing the risk of
“bends.”

The “bends” is a condition that is produced by a diver
coming to the surface too quickly, the lower pressure at the
water's surface results in the dissolved gasses to bubble out of
solution. The effect is like fizzing of an opened bottle or can of
carbonated soft drinks. In the human body these gas bubbles
become trapped in the joints, causing severe pain and causing
the diver to “bend” in a fetal position.

Inhaling pure helium produces a dramatic change in
the pitch and speed of vocalizations, producing a “Donald
Duck” speech.

A mixture of helium and neon gases was used to
produce the first gas “laser.” The term laser is an acronym for
“Light Amplification by Stimulated Emission of Radiation.”
A common application of the helium-neon laser is found in
retail outlets, where it is used to read bar codes on price tags
at the checkout line.

Hf-Hafnium is found in igneous rocks at 3 ppm, shale
at 2.8 ppm, sandstone at 3.4 ppm, limestone at 0.3 ppm, sea
water at 0.000008 ppm, soil at 3.0 ppm, marine plants at 0.4
ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.01 ppm and terrestrial animals at
0.04 ppm.

Hafnium is a common and abundant metal, however,
it was not discovered until 1923. Numerous investigators,
including the creator of the periodic table Mendeleyev,
predicted the existence of “element 72,” however, the ever
present diamond-like twin zirconium overshadowed the
existence of hafnium. Ultimately, Dirk Coster, a Dutch
physicist and George Karl von Hevesy, a Hungarian physicist
found the element. To honor Bohr, Coster and de Hevesy
named their new element for his native city, Copenhagen,
using the Latin translation, Hafnia.

Hafnium is a bright, silvery-white metal, its elemental
properties and history are joined with the element zirconium.

===== Screenshot (90) =====
 


 

Hafnium, is more dense than zirconium, however, the two
metals are chemically similar and it is almost impossible to
work with one without working with the other.

Hg-Mercury is found in igneous rocks at 0.08 ppm,
shale at 0.4 ppm, sandstone at 0.03 ppm, limestone at 0.04
ppm, fresh water at 0.00008 ppm, sea water at 0.00003 ppm,
soil at 0.03 to 0.8 ppm (lowest in the surface layers of the soil
because it leaches to the subsoil and it also volatilizes), marine
plants at 0.03 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.015 ppm (Arenaria
setacia is an accumulator plant), terrestrial animals at 0.046
ppm (accumulates in the brain, kidney, liver and bone) and
marine animals at 0.0009 to 0.09 ppm. Mercury is the only
metal that is liquid at room temperatures. It is an extremely
dense metal, and therefore a heavy metal with a silvery-white
color that has resulted in the common name of “quicksilver.”

Mercury is rarely found as a pure metal in nature,
rather it is found bound in a bright red ore called cinnabar
or vermillion. Cinnabar is primarily mercury sulfide and is
found in large deposits in Spain and Italy. Spain has mercury
mines that have been operating continuously for more than
2,000 years. Metallic mercury is produced from cinnabar by
heating the ore and condensing the mercury vapor.

Like many heavy metals, mercury is an accumulative
poison that binds with enzymes in the body, producing
a reduction or loss of function as a catalyst of biological
functions. It is easily absorbed into the body through the
gastrointestinal tract and can enter the body via trans-dermal
routes. The vapor is considerably more dangerous than the
elemental metal. It has been estimated that the vapors from
the volume of mercury as small as a teaspoon can saturate a
large room within a week and make it unsafe to work in.

Mercury occurs universally in the bios and has long
been known as a potentially toxic element, even though the
early Chinese alchemists insisted that the regular consumption
of mercury or “potable gold” was the path to immortality.


Mercury was well known to the ancient Chinese and
Egyptians, samples of the metal have been found in tombs
dating back to 1500 B.C. Mercury takes its name from the
planet Mercury and chemical symbol, Hg, from the Latin
word hydragyrum, which translates to “liquid silver.”

The first Chinese Emperor (210 B.C.), Jing Shi Huang,
in a failed attempt to attain immortality, inadvertently killed
himself by drinking several doses of a mercury spiked elixir
over an extended period of time.

Prior to his death, Jing became paranoid (“mad as a
hatter”), had many of his loyal inner circle, advisors, scholars
and academics executed and had hundreds of life sized terra
cotta likenesses of his loyal palace guard constructed, painted
and dressed to be with him throughout a thousand year reign
(he knew that the average man only lived to be 45 to 60).

Mercury is concentrated in the environment by
industry, mining operations, agriculture, dental repairs
(amalgams), and microorganisms that methylate mercury
in the sediments at the bottoms of fresh water or salt water
rivers, lakes, oceans and seas. Mercury has been detected in all
tissues of accident victims, with no known mercury exposure
except dental mercury amalgam fillings.

Historically, the property of being able to dissolve
other metals and form alloys (amalgams) was used by dentists
to create silver-mercury and gold-mercury amalgams to fill
caries (cavities in teeth).

Mercury in fish is present as methyl mercury. People
who rarely eat fish have low levels of mercury (2-5ug/kg);
moderate fish consumers have 10 ug/kg; high fish consumers
(especially if they eat shark, tuna, or swordfish) have higher
values ranging up to 400 ug/kg.

Mercury mineworkers accumulate mercury, which can
reach tissue levels that produce clinical disease. The biological
half life of methyl mercury in humans is 70 days and only
four days for inorganic mercury. The placenta acts as a barrier
against the passage of inorganic mercury but not for methyl

===== Screenshot (91) =====
 


 

mercury. Methyl mercury transfers very easily to the fetus
(“congenital” Minamata Disease in Japanese infants).

The main industrial source of mercury is the chloralkali
industry. Additional sources of mercury that are associated
with human activity include the manufacture of electrical
appliances, paint, dental amalgams, pharmaceuticals, vaccines,
slimicides and algaecides (paper and pulp industries), seed
treatments as agricultural fungicides - especially dangerous
as methyl mercury, and burning of fossil fuels.

The metabolic antagonism between mercury and
selenium, results in the protection from selenium poisoning
by mercury and protection against mercury poisoning by
selenium supplements. Because a mutual antagonism between
Hg and Se exists, Se protects the human kidney from necrosis
(tissue death) by mercury poisoning and the placental transfer
of mercury. Mercury vapor off-gassing from dental amalgam
has been shown to increase the percent of antibiotic resistant
bacteria in the gut from nine percent to 70% in monkeys
given dental mercury fillings. The drug resistant bacterial
population dropped to 12% when the fillings were removed.

Mercury poisoning from inhalation of mercury vapors
was reported during the Victorian Age in “hatters” who used
mercuric nitrate paste to prevent molds from growing on felt
hats and as a result, commonly developed serious problems,
including hair loss, loss of teeth, loss of memory, bizarre
behavior and various forms of insanity, hence the expression

“mad as a hatter” from Alice in Wonderland. Goldsmiths and
mirror workers could also suffer from inhalation poisoning.

In modern times dentists and dental patients have
developed high rates of several diseases thought to be related
to mercury exposure including multiple sclerosis, ALS (Lou
Gehrig’s Disease) and Parkinson’s Disease depending on
what part of the brain was most severely affected. Annette
Funicello contracted multiple sclerosis, which is believed to
be caused by vapors from dental mercury amalgams.

The manifestations of direct mercury poisoning are


 

primarily neurological (i.e. tremors, vertigo, irritability,
moodiness (suicidal, depression, etc.), salivation, inflammation
of the mouth, stomatitis and diarrhea.

Poisoning with inorganic mercury targets the liver and
kidneys. Poisoning with the more toxic alkyl mercury results
in progressive lack of coordination, loss of vision, heart
palpitations, loss of hearing, and mental deterioration caused
by a toxic neuroencephalopathy in which the neuronal cells of
the cerebral and cerebellar cortex are selectively affected.

In 1962, in the small fishing village of Minamata, Japan,
mercury contaminated factory effluent (waste water) was
dumped directly into the bay, which in turn contaminated
aquatic plants (kelp and algae) and bacteria, which were then
eaten by fish. Subsequently it was learned that certain species
of bacteria and plants can metabolize mercury safely.

The contaminated fish were eaten by the bay
residents with disastrous results. The Minamata disaster was
characterized by a high incidence of “congenital” (damage
to the fetus) mental retardation, cerebral palsy, neural tube
defects and high rates of infant mortality.

The Minamata disaster was the eco-disaster event
that resulted in the creation of The Center for the Biology of
Natural Systems.

The cause of autism is thought by some to have a
relationship to the mercury preservatives in some vaccines.

It is of interest to note that none of the other countries (Third
World or Industrialized) using the same vaccines and vaccine
manufacturers have registered the same complaints, level
of diagnosis or law suites regarding a relationship between
autism and vaccinations.

The rate of autism in America in 1980 was 1/150,000 and
2008 it is claimed to be 1/150. A few autistic babies (10%) are
born with the disease, 90% are born normal and then develop
it somewhere between the ages of two and six years of age.

Since 2006 much of the mercury preservatives have
been removed from childhood vaccines because of universal

In Iraq, a large scale methyl mercury poisoning was
reported when large numbers of people were fed bread made
with mercurial fungicide treated seed grain and meat (liver
and kidneys) from animals fed the treated grain. The result of
consuming the mercury contaminated grains was thousands
of babies born retarded and having a high incidence of
congenital brain defects including neural tube defects and
cerebral palsy.

Ho-Holmium, a rare earth, is found in igneous rocks at
1.2 ppm, shale at 0.6 ppm, sandstone at 0.51 ppm, limestone
at 0.17 ppm, terrestrial plants at 16 ppm (Carya spp.), marine
animals at 0.005 to 0.01 ppm and terrestrial animals at 0.5 ppm
in bone.

In 1878, two Swiss chemists noticed holmium’s
characteristic spectral lines, but could not identify them, so
they referred to the unknown element as “element X.” In 1879,
a Swedish chemist, Per Teodor Cleve isolated and identified
the element and called it Holmia, Latin for his home town of
Stockholm.

I-Iodine is found in igneous rocks at 0.5 ppm, shale at
2.3 ppm, sandstone at 1.7 ppm, limestone at 1.2 ppm, fresh
water at 0.002 ppm, sea water at 0.06 ppm, soil at 5 ppm,
(strongly bound in humus- large areas of earth are known to be
devoid of iodine), marine plants at 30 to 1,500 ppm; terrestrial
plants at 0.42 ppm, marine animals at 1.0 to 150 ppm, and in
terrestrial animals at 0.43 ppm (concentrates in the thyroid
gland and hair).

Iodine is a violet-black solid that vaporizes to give a
violet gas. It is found in sea weed and brine wells, in the sea,
in the form of inorganic salts and organic iodides. It is the
heaviest element in the halogen family and is highly reactive.
Iodine was discovered in 1811 by Bernard Courtois, who
noticed a purple vapor emanating from a sample of kelp


to which he had added sulfuric acid. The color of the gas
determined its name, translated from the Greek word iodes,
meaning “violet-colored.” A drop of iodine will turn starch a
deep blue (potato, flour, etc.).

Commercial quantities of iodine are extracted from
sodium iodate, which is found in “saltpeter” or potassium
nitrate, mined in Chile. At one time Chile was one of the
largest producers of iodine. Historically iodine was extracted
from the ashes of burnt seaweed. The ocean contains large
quantities of iodine which concentrates in various kinds of
seaweed, including the giant kelp that grows off the coast of
California. Iodine can also be recovered from the natural brines
found in underground wells in Arkansas and Oklahoma.

Historically, one of the most common uses of iodine
was in the form of an alcohol tincture, which was used as a
wound antiseptic for treating minor lacerations and scrapes
of the skin.

Because iodine kills bacteria, the military, campers and
scouts use iodine tablets to “purify” water.

Iodine-131 is used by the medical trade to treat thyroid
cancer and hyperthyroidism and iodine-125 along with an
isotope of palladium is used to treat prostate cancer.

Iodine is known to be essential to red and brown
algae and all vertebrates. In combination with the amino acid
tyrosine, iodine is manufactured into the thyroid hormone
thyroxin. Iodine intake is typically low to begin with, but since
Americans have been restricting their salt intake at the advice
of their doctors, hypothyroidism and goiter have become
epidemic diseases.

The average American takes in 170-250 mcg/day of
elemental iodine. Humans lose considerable amounts of
iodine in their sweat - a loss, of as much as 146 mcg/day, with
only moderate exercise. Metallic iodine is not toxic with long
term intakes of up to 2,000 mcg/day.

Goiter develops in Japanese living along the sea coast
despite high daily iodine consumption. Japanese subjects

being fed Chinese cabbage (bok choi), turnips, buckwheat,
noodles, 2.0 mcg iodine, soybean or sea weed developed
goiter in all groups except the sea weed fed group.

Northern parts of the Adriatic Islands had more clinical
goiter than the southern areas while the southwest was goiter-free. Forty-six percent of the population of Pisila, 40% of the
population of Polje, and only three percent of the population of
Milahnici were affected. There is an identical iodine content of
the soil in all three locations. A severe copper deficiency in the
soils of the north and south cause the deficiency state because
copper is a cofactor required for the efficient utilization of
iodine.

Some one million Americans have either a hypothyroid
(low, under active) or a hyperthyroid (overactive) condition.
Thyroid hormones control and regulate digestion, heart rate,
metabolism, body temperature, sweat gland activity, nervous
and reproductive systems and body weight.

Many foods and food additives are known

“goitrogens” because they interfere with the normal thyroid

metabolism and produce thyroid disease. These substances

include nitrates and nitrites (i.e. ham, bacon, sausage, bologna,
salami, pastrami, pepperoni, jerky, deli-meats, etc.) and
cruciferous vegetables (i.e. broccoli, Brussel sprouts, cabbage,
cauliflower, etc.).

In-Indium is found in igneous rocks at 0.05 to 1.0 ppm,

terrestrial plants at 0.62 ppm, and terrestrial animals at 0.016

ppm. Indium is a rare, soft, bluish-white metal that is corrosion
resistant. When it is vigorously rubbed against other metals it
will leave small residues of itself; when bent rapidly, indium
emits a bizarre “shrieking” noise. Commercial sources of
indium are produced as a byproduct of refining zinc.

Indium was discovered in 1863 by the German chemist
Ferdinand Reich while studying zinc ore. It was identified by
the bright violet light that it gave off during spectroscopic
analysis. Its name is taken from the Latin word indicum, which
translates to “violet” or “indigo.”

Ir-Iridium is found in igneous rocks at 0.001 ppm,
terrestrial plants at 0.62 ppm and terrestrial animals at 0.00002
ppm. Iridium is one of the hardest and most corrosion resistant
metals known. It is considered a precious metal similar to
platinum.

The English chemist Smithson Tennant discovered
iridium in 1803, using the same process that led him to the
discovery of osmium; he isolated iridium from the residues of
platinum ores he treated with aqua regia (a mixture of nitric
and hydrochloric acids).

Tennant named the new metal iridium after the Latin
word, iris, which translates to “rainbow” because of the highly
colored salts.

K-Potassium is found in igneous rocks at 20,000 ppm,
shale at 26,000 ppm, sandstone at 10,700 ppm, limestone at
2,700 ppm, fresh water at 2.3 ppm, sea water at 380 ppm, soil
at 14,000 ppm (a major exchangeable cation in all soils, but
in highest amounts in alkaline soils), marine plants at 52,000
ppm, terrestrial plants at 14,000 ppm, marine animals at 5,000
to 30,000 ppm, and terrestrial animals at 7,400 ppm (highest
levels are found in soft tissue).

Potassium is a silver-white metal with a characteristic
putty or wax-like nature that is so soft that it can easily be cut
with a knife. It is a member of the family of alkali metals along
with the elements lithium and sodium, and like them is never
found as a pure metal in nature.

A naturally occurring radioactive isotope of potassium
is potassium-40, which has a half-life of 1.25 billion years.
Potassium-40 is used to date rocks. When potassium-40
decays, it becomes the noble gas argon. To determine the age
of a rock, it is necessary to know how much argon is present
in the rock. The oldest rocks on earth have been dated by this
method as being 3.8 billion years old.

The human body contains approximately 140 grams of
potassium and since the natural level of potassium-40 is 0.012
percent, we all contain small amounts of this isotope - it is a
natural and life-long contributor to our background radiation
load.

Potassium was first isolated by Sir Humphrey Davy
in 1807 using electrolysis as a means of separating elemental
potassium from its compounds.

Sodium and potassium are chemically similar and are
found in equal abundance in the crust of the earth. Although
both metals occur in silicate minerals and sea water, however,
the large bodies of salt water (oceans, seas and lakes) contain
considerably more sodium than potassium. Potassium is
essential for plant growth, while sodium is not - plants
therefore preferentially take up potassium allowing dissolved
sodium to flow to the oceans.

===== Screenshot (95) =====
 


 

Almost all of the potassium chloride that is mined is
used as plant fertilizer. In fact, plants (crops and trees) were
the early source of essential minerals including potassium for
humans. Wood and other plants were burned in pots or under
pots to produce a potassium-rich ash (“potash”).

The original “chemical fertilizer,” first produced in the
1700s by the German chemist Liebig, was made up of NPK
(nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium) which he formulated
after the ash he produced by burning bread and then analyzing
the mineral content of the ash.

The name, potassium, has its origin from the term
potash, from the early source of the element - wood ashes, the
byproduct of daily living, that was produced by heating and
cooking with wood.

Potassium nitrate, KNO3, is an important compound
that has been used by many cultures for centuries. It is
commonly called “saltpeter” or “rocksalt.” The name is taken
from the Greek word petra, which translates to rock. It looks
like table salt and when dissolved in water, it tastes like salt.

Saltpeter is commonly used as a preservative and
fertilizer. Gunpowder is a mixture of saltpeter, charcoal and
sulfur.

Potassium is essential to all organisms and is the major
cation in cell cytoplasm with a wide variety of electrochemical
and catalytic functions for enzyme systems. Potassium
constitutes five percent of the total mineral content of the
body. It is the major cation of the intracellular fluid and there
is a small amount in the extracellular fluid. With sodium, the
other “electrolyte,” potassium participates in the maintenance
of normal water balance, osmotic equilibrium and acid-base
balance. Potassium participates with calcium in the regulation
of neuromuscular activity.

Potassium is easily absorbed. Ninety percent of ingested
potassium is excreted through the urine. Essentially there is
no storage of potassium in the human body, thus requiring a
significant daily intake of 5,000 mg.


 

Muscular weakness and mental apathy are features
of a potassium deficiency. Hypokalemic cardiac failure is
the most serious potassium deficiency event. Diuretics (both
natural and prescribed), water toxicity (over drinking water),
sweating, colds and flu, vomiting and diarrhea increase
the rate of loss of all minerals, including potassium, when
compared to the normal expected excretion rate.

Kr-Krypton is found in igneous rock at 0.0001 ppm
and sea water at 0.0025 ppm. Krypton is legendary as the
home planet of “Superman” and the source of the mineral
“kryptonite” which had a crippling effect on “Superman.”
In fact krypton in its natural elemental form is a noble gas,
totally harmless to humans and may in fact be an essential
element for all vertebrates.

As none of the Noble gases had been discovered when
Mendeleyev first constructed the periodic table in 1869, he
made no provision for them in the original table. It was only
the discovery of argon in 1894 that produced the need for a new
column in the table. In 1898, the English chemist Sir William
Ramsay isolated and identified krypton as a new element.
He named it for the Greek word kryptos, which translates to
“hidden.”

The chemical inertness of krypton was challenged by
the celebrated American chemist and two time Nobel Prize
winner, Linus Pauling, who in 1933 used quantum mechanics
to demonstrate that a molecule containing krypton and
fluorine should be stable enough to exist. In 1966, Neil Bartlett
of the University of British Columbia produced krypton
fluoride (KrF2) as Pauling had predicted.

La-Lanthanum is a “light” rare earth and is found in
igneous rocks at 30 ppm, shale at 20 ppm, sandstone at 7.5
ppm, limestone at 6.2 ppm, sea water at 0.000012 ppm, soil
at 30 ppm, marine plants at 10 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.085
ppm, marine mammals at 0.1 ppm, and in terrestrial animals

at 0.0001 ppm in soft tissue and 0.27 in bone.

Notably the yeast Candida albicans accumulates
lanthanum at the rate of 370 ppm/day. It is theorized that
this is how C. albicans produces a debilitating, energy sapping
“chronic fatigue” disease, by “stealing” lanthanum from the
victim.

The growth of the protozoa Blepherisma and Tetrahymena
pyriformis is stimulated and their life span doubled by the
presence of the rare earth lanthanum at concentrations of 0.32
ppm.

Li-Lithium is found in igneous rocks at 20 ppm, shale
at 66 ppm, sandstone at 15 ppm, limestone at 5 ppm, fresh
water at 0.0011 ppm, sea water at 0.18 ppm, soil at 30 ppm
(lithium is freely mobile in the soil), marine plants at 5 ppm,
terrestrial plants at 0.1 ppm, marine animals at 1.0 ppm, and
terrestrial animals at 0.02 ppm. Lithium’s density is so low
that a small piece can actually float on water.

Lithium was discovered in 1817 by the Swedish chemist
Johan August Arfvedson during a routine chemical assay of
ore from a mine in Sweden. It was named for the Greek word
lithos, which translates to “stone.”

Lithiumisanextremely reactive metal. Itreacts vigorously
with water, to produce hydrogen gas, and with oxygen to form
lithium oxide (Li,O). To prevent these reactions from occurring,
free lithium is usually stored in oil or kerosene.

Since 1915, the risk of clinical depression nearly doubles
with each succeeding generation. Myrna M. Weissman, a
psychiatrist at Columbia University, New York City, says
that, “Depression is a world wide phenomenon happening at
younger and younger ages.”

In 1935, the age of the early onset of depression was
in the early 20s, by 1955, the age of early onset of clinical
depression dropped to between 15 and 20 years of age.

One in four women and one in ten men will develop
depression. Prozac™, America’s

leading antidepressant


 

pharmaceutical, was introduced to the public in 1987. Sales
soared to $350 million in 1989, more than was spent on all
antidepressants just two years earlier. Prosac™ sales topped $1
billion in 1995 as a result of 650,000 prescriptions per month!

While the professional psychiatrist says that depression
and manic depression are due to feelings that “we are out of
control of our lives,” negative thinking, and self recrimination
(‘I'm a loser”), they treat depression successfully with the
trace mineral lithium. Depression and manic depression with
all that implies, are simply a lithium deficiency aggravated by
a high consumption of sugar.

Lithium carbonate is regularly used to treat patients
with the severe form of bipolar disorder. More than 3 million
prescriptions for this trace mineral are filled yearly. The role of
lithium is thought to be related to the role the brain cells play
and respond to certain hormones and neurotransmitters.

Animal studies show that a deficiency of lithium
results in reproductive failure, infertility, reduced growth
rate, shortened life expectancy, and behavioral problems.

In humans, manic depression, depression, “bi-polar”
disease, rage, road rage, “going postal,” Dr. Jekyll/Mr. Hyde
behavior, hyperactivity, ADD, ADHD, certain forms of autism
and “bad seeds” are all hallmarks of a lithium deficiency
aggravated by a high sugar or alcohol intake.

Chelated lithium supplemented at 1,000 to 2,000 ug/day causes a dose related increase in hair lithium levels. Hair
lithium levels increased after four weeks of supplementation
and leveled off and became stationary after three months.
When the lithium supplementation was stopped, hair lithium
levels dropped to presupplement values in two months. This
scenario is not seen with the supplementation of metallic
lithium carbonate. A comparison of 2,648 subjects showed
that 65% had hair lithium values ranging between 0.04 and
0.14 ug/G; 16% had hair lithium values of more than 0.14
ug/G; and 18.4% had less than 0.04 ug/G. The highest levels
of lithium were found in university students from Tijuana,

Mexico. The lowest levels found in hair analysis were found!
in Munich, Germany.

Normal controls showed almost 400 times more hair
lithium than do the violent criminals from California, Florida,

ingested lithium is metallic and not necessarily biologically
available.

Lu-Lutetium, a rare earth, is found in igneous rocks att
0.5 ppm, shale at 0.33 ppm, sandstone at 0.096 ppm, limestone?
at 0.067 ppm, terrestrial plants at up to 4.5 ppm (Carya spp.),,
marine animals at 0.003 ppm, terrestrial animals at 0.003 ppm,,
and terrestrial animals at 0.00012 ppm in soft tissue and 0.08}
in bone. As with most of the other rare earth metals, lutetium
is silvery-white and corrosion-resistant. As the last lanthanide
in the periodic table, it is the hardest and heaviest rare earth
element.

Lutetium is one of the least abundant of the rare
earth elements. It was discovered in 1907 by the Austrian
mineralogist Baron Carl Auer von Welsbach and the French
scientist Georges Urbain as an impurity in a mineral sample
thought to contain ytterbium.

Urbain created the name for the new element from
Lutetia, the ancient name for the city of Paris. Welsbach
had chosen the name cassiopium, a name derived from the
constellation Cassiopia. Many German scientists still refer to
lutetium by the name cassiopium.

Though he never published his findings, the American
chemist Charles James, known to his colleagues and students
as “King James,” is now believed to have independently
identified lutetium in 1907. |

Mg-Magnesium is found in igneous rocks at 23,300
ppm, in shale at 15,000 ppm, sandstone at 10,700 ppm,
limestone at 2,700 ppm, fresh water at 4.1 ppm, seawater at


 

1,350 ppm, and soil at 5,000 ppm (highest in soil derived from
basalt, serpentine, or dolomite). Magnesium is the second
most common exchangeable cation in most soils. Magnesium
is found in marine plants at 5,200 ppm, land plants at 3,200
ppm, marine animals at 5,000 ppm, and in land animals at
1,000 ppm where it accumulates in mammalian bone.

Magnesium is a white silvery metal that is very light
weight, its name is derived from the ore magnesite (dolomite)
which is primarily magnesium carbonate. Magnesite is
believed to have been originally mined in Magnesia, a district
in ancient Greece.

Magnesium was identified as a separate element by the
British chemist Sir Humphry Davy in 1808, using electrolysis
to extract it from magnesium oxide.

Magnesium is the seventh most abundant element in
the earth’s crust; it is present in such large quantities in sea
water that the earth’s oceans constitute an unlimited supply
of the dissolved metal.

Milk of magnesia is a suspension of magnesium
hydroxide, Mg(OH),, in water, it is used as an everyday OTC
laxative. Another OTC remedy containing magnesium is
Epsom salts. These salts are a hydrated form of magnesium
sulfate; it was first discovered in well water from Epsom,
England in the mid 17th century. Epsom salts are still used
today as a wound disinfectant and for reducing the swelling
and inflammation of bruises, strains and sprains.

Magnesium oxideisanimportantcommercial compound
of magnesium and is extracted directly from magnesite. It is
used as a source of metallic magnesium for animal and human
dietary supplements. It is also the metallic atom found in
chlorophyll of all green plants, which plays an essential role
in photosynthesis, the photochemical process that converts the
sun’s solar energy into a stored “solar currency” for a source
of chemical energy for all plants and all animals and humans.
Magnesium, the source of the deep green color of plants, is the
ultimate source of all biological energy!

===== Screenshot (98) =====
 


Magnesium, as a mineral is essential to all living
organisms and has cofactor, electro-chemical, catalytic, and
structural functions. It activates numerous enzymes and is a
constituent of all chlorophylls.

The adult human contains 20 to 28 grams of total body
Mg. Approximately 60% is found in bone. Twenty-six percent
is associated with skeletal muscle and the balance is distributed
between various organs and body fluids. Serum levels of Mg
range from 1.5 to 2.1 mEq/L (a measure of electrolytes) and is
second to potassium as an intracellular cation. Half of the Mg,
including most that is bound in bone, is not exchangeable.

Magnesium is required for the production and transfer
of energy for protein synthesis, for contractility of muscle and
excitability of nerves, and as a cofactor in a myriad of enzyme
systems. AN EXCESS OF MAGNESIUM WILL CAUSE
DIARRHEA AND INHIBIT BONE CALCIFICATION.

Calcium and magnesium have antagonistic roles in
normal muscle contraction, calcium acting as the stimulator
and magnesium as the “relaxer.” An excessive amount of
calcium can induce signs of magnesium deficiency.

Perhaps the most important manifestation of
magnesium deficiency in modern times is “malignant
calcification.” Malignant calcification appears as calcium
deposits in soft tissue, especially the media or middle layer of
arterial walls. Magnesium deficiency appears to be the basic
root cause of arteriosclerotic calcium deposits, not elevated
blood cholesterol or triglycerides!

The rate of absorption of magnesium ranges from 24
to 85%. The lesser absorption rate is for metallic sources of
Mg, the higher rates of absorption are associated with plant
derived colloidal mineral sources. Vitamin D has no effect
on Mg absorption. The presence of dietary fat, phytates, and
calcium reduces the efficiency of absorption. High performance
athletes lose a considerable amount of Mg in sweat.

The RDA for Mg is 350 mg/day for adult males, 300
mg/day for adult females, and 450 mg/day for pregnant and
lactating females. If the kidneys are healthy, there is no evidence
of toxicity at supplemental doses of up to 6,000 mg/day.

Mn-Manganese is found in igneous rocks at 950 ppm,
shale at 850 ppm, sandstone at 50 ppm, limestone at 1,100
ppm, fresh water at 0.012 ppm, sea water at 0.002 ppm, soil
at 850 ppm (can be a major exchangeable cation in very acid
soil), marine animals at 1.0 to 6.0 ppm (lowest in fish), and in
terrestrial animals at 0.2 ppm with the highest concentration
in mammalian liver and kidney.

Manganeseisahard, gray-white metal thathas chemical

===== Screenshot (99) =====
 


LI, Se SPY FN eee
and physical properties that are similar to iron. Manganese
does not occur as a pure mineral in nature, it is usually found
combined with oxygen in pyrolusite, a mineral that is made
up chiefly of manganese dioxide (MnO,).

Manganese was identified in 1774 by Carl Wilhelm
Scheele who named it from the Latin word magnes, which
translates to “magnet,” referring to the magnetic properties
of pyrolusite.

The United States has no deposits of manganese of
any commercial quantities. Rich ores containing up to 40%
manganese are found in South Africa and India. A unique
source of almost pure manganese is found on the floor of
the world’s seas as “manganese nodules.” These nodules,
spherical in shape, are made up primarily of manganese
and iron oxides, and are thought to be the byproduct of
microorganisms that have the ability to extract manganese
from sea water.

One of the most common compounds of manganese
found in chemical laboratories is potassium permanganate
(KMn0O,), which is employed as an indicator for acid solutions.
When dissolved in water, it produces a deep purple color. If
an acid is added to the solution, its color instantly turns to a
pale pink. It is also used to purify community water supplies
and to absorb toxic gases.

The total body content of manganese in humans
is only 10 to 20 mg. Manganese is known to be essential
for all living organisms. Manganese activates numerous
enzyme systems including those involved with glucose
metabolism, energy production and antioxidant properties
of superoxide dismutase (SOD). It is a major constituent of
several metalloenzymes, hormones, and proteins essential to
all vertebrates and humans.

Manganese is an essential part of the developmental
process and the physical structure of the three fragile ear
bones and joint cartilage.

Excessive levels of certain

manganese found in


 

community water supplies and in some industrial processes
can produce a Parkinsonian syndrome or a_ psychiatric
disorder (locura manganica) resembling schizophrenia.

Deficiency diseases of manganese are very striking,
ranging from severe birth defects (congenital ataxia, cerebral
palsy, deafness, chondrodystrophy), asthma, convulsions,
retarded growth, skeletal defects, disruption of fat and
carbohydrate metabolism to joint problems in children and
adults (i.e. TMJ, Repetitive Motion Syndrome, Carpal Tunnel
Syndrome, etc.).

Repetitive stress injury or repetitive motion syndrome
now costs America $20 billion per year and accounts for
56% of the 331,600 gradual onset work related illnesses and
disabilities.

In 1991, orthopedic surgeons performed 100,000 carpal
tunnel operations (at $4,000 per surgery) with lost work,
wages and medical costs of over $29,000 per case.

At risk for the repetitive motion syndrome are those
working in the world of computers: journalism, airline
reservations, directory assistance, law research, data entry,
graphic design and securities brokerage. Chief among blue
collar victims are the auto assembly workers, chicken pluckers,
meat cutters, postal employees, dock workers, etc.

Repetitive motion syndrome was observed three
centuries ago in monks who were scribes and was described in
1717 by Bernardo Ramazzini, an Italian physician (considered
the father of occupational medicine).

Repetitive motion syndrome victims have reached
such numbers that federal legislation has been passed in the
form of OSHA and Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA)
to ensure work place safety. Large numbers of ergonomically
correct keyboards and furniture have been developed. We
now see literally millions of people in the work place with
Velcro wrist, neck, elbow, finger, knee, back and hip supports
- all for simple manganese deficiencies!!

Mo-Molybdenum is found inigneous rocks at 1.5 ppm,
shale at 2.6 ppm, sandstone at 0.02 ppm, limestone at 0.4 ppm,
fresh water at 0.00035 ppm, sea water at 0.01 ppm, soil at 2.0
ppm (strongly concentrated by humus, especially in alkaline
soils; a few soils worldwide are rich enough in molybdenum
to cause molybdenum poisoning in animals consuming
the plants). Numerous soils are known to be molybdenum
deficient. Molybdenum is found in marine plants at 0.45 ppm,
terrestrial plants at 0.9 ppm, marine animals at 0.6 to 2.5 ppm,
and terrestrial animals at 0.2 ppm with the highest levels


 

being found in the liver and kidneys.

Molybdenum is a hard, silvery metal that is extracted
from an ore called molybdenite (MoS,). There are large
deposits of “Moly” molybdenite in Colorado.

In 1778, molybdenum was originally extracted from
molybdenite and identified as a new element by the Swedish
chemist Carl Eilhelm Scheele who identified it as a new
element.

Molybdenum is essential for all organisms as a
constituent of numerous metalloenzymes. Molybdenum is
known to be an integral part of no less than three essential
enzymes: Xanthine oxidase, Aldehyde oxidase and Sulfite
oxidase.

The average American daily intake of Mo in food
ranges from 76 to 1,109 meg per day. The RDA for Mo is 250
meg per day. Toxicity occurs at 10 mg per day; clinical signs
of toxicity appear as a gout-like disease and interference with
copper metabolism.

N-Nitrogen is found in igneous rock at 20 ppm, fresh
water at 0.23 ppm, sea water at 0.5 ppm, soils at 1,000 ppm
(99% present as non-basic N bound in humus), marine plants
at 15,000 ppm, terrestrial plants at 30,000 ppm, marine animals
at 75,000 ppm, and in terrestrial animals at 100,000 ppm.

Nitrogen in its free state is a gas, and as a gas nitrogen
is relatively inert and is colorless, tasteless and odorless.
Nitrogen makes up 78% of the earth’s atmosphere by volume.
Although a relatively non-reactive gas, nitrogen is part of
hundreds of thousands of compounds that are essential for life,
agriculture and industry. Even though nitrogen is relatively
stable, some of its compounds are extremely unstable and
explosive.

Nitrogen was discovered in 1772 by David Rutherford
(1749-1819), an English chemist and physician. Rutherford
used a bell jar to limit a sample of atmospheric air, he then
removed all of the oxygen from the trapped air by consuming

===== Screenshot (101) =====
 


um

it by burning a substance in it; he then placed a mouse in
the oxygen free jar, which rapidly asphyxiated; he then
demonstrated that “nitrogen” could not sustain life.

Many compounds found in nature and contain nitrogen
are crucial for plant and animal life. Included in the plethera
of biological molecules that have nitrogen as a significant
functional component are proteins and nucleic acids. Despite
the lack of reactivity of nitrogen, there are several natural
mechanisms for converting nitrogen from the atmosphere
into usable nitrogen compounds called nitrogen “ fixing.”

During storms, the extremely high
temperatures of the lightening supply enough energy to break
apart the diatomic nitrogen molecule (N,). The free nitrogen
atoms that are formed can then combine with oxygen to form
nitrogen oxide (NO), and nitrogen dioxide (NO).

Most of the nitrogen dioxide dissolves in rainwater and
then falls to the earth’s surface, where nitrogen-fixing bacteria
in the soil called cyanobacteria use the nitrogen dioxide to
build nutrients such as proteins and amino acids. These
nutrients are then taken up by the roots of plants, which are
in turn eaten by animals, in whose tissues the nitrogen is used
to create animal proteins. Bacteria in the soil then convert the
inevitable waste products of nitrogen metabolism, such as
urea, into amino acids and ammonia. These same denitrifying

electrical

bacteria also breakdown these compounds to form gaseous
nitrogen, which is then returned into the atmosphere.

Nitric acid (HNO,), is of major commercial value in
the United States, where it is used to manufacture fertilizer
(ammonium nitrate), as well as for the manufacture of
explosives and plastics (nylon, polyurethane, etc.). Nitric acid
was known to early chemists as aqua fortis, which in Latin
translates to “strong water.”

More than 15 billion pounds of nitric acid were
produced in the US alone in 1992, chiefly by the Ostwald
process. This process was discovered in 1902 by the German
chemist Wilhelm Ostwald (1853-1932), who received the


 

Nobel Prize in 1909 for his research into the importance and
value of catalysts in chemical reactions.

In the Ostwald process, high temperatures and
platinum catalysts convert ammonia into nitric acid which
can then be used to make explosives.

When nitric acid combines with glycerol, a syrup-like alcohol, it produces nitroglycerine, which is extremely
dangerous to handle, because it can explode with the smallest
of shocks.

Dynamite, a useful and much safer derivative of
nitroglycerine was invented in 1867 by Alfred Nobel (1833 -
1896). He showed that when nitroglycerine is mixed with clay,
itformsa relatively safe, shock-resistant explosive-TNT. Nobel
eventually established the Nobel Prize with an investment of
$900,000 with some of the fortune he accumulated from the
sale and royalties from TNT.

When nitric acid is neutralized with bases including
sodium and potassium hydroxide, it forms compounds called
nitrates. Nitrates and nitrites are added to canned foods and
preserved meats to help prevent the growth of bacteria and to
color meat red.

Nitrates and nitrites are converted to nitrosamines in
the acid environment of the stomach; these compounds can
be converted to cancer causing chemicals (carcinogens) and
can suppress thyroid function.

Nitrogen functions as a structural atom in protein,
nucleic acids (RNA, DNA), and in a wide variety of organic
molecules. Dietary nitrogen (as protein) furnishes the amino
acids for the synthesis of tissue protein and other special
metabolic functions:

1. Proteins are used to repair worn out body tissue

2. Proteins are used to build new tissue (muscle, infant
growth, childhood development, teenager pu berty, pregnancy
(embryonic growth), maintenance and repair).

===== Screenshot (102) =====
 


 

io} Dy 1 - 5

3. Proteins can be an emergency source of heat and
energy (albeit more “expensive” in biological terms than
energy derived from fat or carbohydrate).

4. Proteins make up a major percentage of essential
body secretions and fluids (i. e. enzymes, hormones, mucus,
milk, semen, etc.).

5. Blood plasma proteins maintain osmotic fluid
balance (hypoproteinemia results in edema and acites).

6. Proteins help to maintain acid-base balance of blood
and tissue.

7. Proteins aid in the blood transport of other essential
substances (i.e. minerals, fats, vitamins, etc.).

8. Proteins
(antibodies).

make up basic immunoglobulins

9. Proteins provide a nitrogen pool for the synthesis of
amino acids and new proteins.

Classic protein deficiency results in infertility, poor
growth, lowered immune status, edema, and Kwashiorkor
(potbellied, thin children of Third World countries). The
availability and usability of nitrogen from various foods are
quite different and must be considered when choosing N
sources.
