Na-Sodium is found in igneous rocks at 23,600 ppm,
shale at 9,600 ppm, sandstone at 3,300 ppm, limestone at 400
ppm, fresh water at 6.3 ppm, sea water at 10,500 ppm, soil at
6,300 ppm (it is a major exchangeable cation in soils, especially
alkaline soil), marine plants at 33,000 ppm, terrestrial plants
at 1,200 ppm, marine animals at 4,000 ppm, and in terrestrial
animals at 4,000 ppm.

Sodium is a highly reactive, bright, silvery metal, light
enough to float on water and soft enough to cut witha knife. It
is the sixth most abundant element in the crust of the earth.

The English chemist, Sir Humphry Davy first isolated and
identified sodium as a new element in 1807 by separating it from
molten sodium hydroxide. The name, sodium is taken from the
English word soda, which itself is derived from the Latin word
sodanum, a common headache remedy of ancient Rome.

===== Screenshot (103) =====
 

ee Eee

Sodium chloride, the chemical name for common table
salt, is mined in large quantities from ancient salt mines on
every continent on earth. Sea water is another common source
of salt, every gallon of sea water contains a quarter pound of
salt.

Salt has been used and traded as a nutrient, preservative
and flavoring condiment since before Roman times. The word
salary, is derived from the Latin word sal, which translates to
“salt.” Roman soldiers were paid a “salary” or allowance in
the form of salt.

“Salt hunger” dates back to the beginning of time and
is one of the basic cravings of vertebrates. Carnivores (man or
beast) do not always show obsessive cravings for salt because
meat contains relatively large amounts of NaCl. By contrast
herbivorous animals and human vegetarians demand large
quantities of NaCl in grains, vegetables, nuts and fruit. The
average dietary intake of sodium per day in western cultures
is five to 12 grams per day, while the Japanese, who outlive
Americans by an average of four years, consume an average
of 28 grams of sodium per day!

Sodium, chlorine and potassium are three indispensable
“electrolytes” so intimately associated in the human body
that they can be presented together. Sodium makes up two
percent, potassium five percent and chlorine three percent of
the total mineral content of the human body. All three are
widely distributed throughout the body tissues and body
fluids, however, Na and Clare primarily extracellular (outside
the cell), while K is an intracellular (inside the cell) mineral.
Sodium, potassium and chlorine are involved in at least four
important physiological functions in the body:

1. Maintenance of
distribution.

normal balance and

water

2. Maintenance of normal osmotic equilibrium.


 

3. Maintenance of normal acid-base balance.
4. Maintenance of normal muscular irritability.

Hormonal control of Na, K and Cl balance is regulated
by the adrenal cortex hormones as well as by the anterior
pituitary gland. Addison’s Disease, a loss of function of the
adrenal cortex, results in the loss of Na and K retention with
clinical signs of general weakness, muscle cramps, weight loss,
and a marked “salt hunger.” The symptoms can be relieved
with the supplementation of NaCl or by administering adrenal
cortical hormones (steroids).

Deficiencies of NaCl occur primarily in hot weather (i.e.
the American heat wave of 1993), heavy work in a hot climate,
or exercise when large volumes of sweat are produced for body
cooling. “Water intoxication” occurs in infants fed low sodium
formulas (because of doctor’s insane paranoia with sodium)
and death occurs as a result of the resultant brain swelling. The
treatment for sodium deficiency is water and salt, either orally
(salt food to taste) or IV (saline administered at 0.9%).

Nb-Niobium is found in igneous rocks at 20 ppm,
shale at 11 ppm, sandstone at 0.05 ppm, limestone 0.3 ppm,
sea water at 0.00001 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.3 ppm, and
marine animals at 0.001 ppm.

Niobium is a soft, steel-gray metal that is primarily
found in the mineral columbite, an ore that also contains iron
and manganese. Niobium was discovered by the Englishman
Charles Hatchett in 1801, while he was analyzing a sample of
columbite that was owned by the British Museum in London.
The new element was named niobium for the mythological
goddess Niobe.

Nd-Neodymium, a rare earth, is found in igneous rock
at 28 ppm, shale at 16 ppm, sandstone at 11 ppm, limestone at
4.3 ppm, marine plants at5 ppm, terrestrial plants accumulates

===== Screenshot (104) =====
 


 

up to 460 ppm (Carya spp.), marine animals at 0.5 ppm, and
accumulates in the liver and bone of terrestrial animals.

Neodymium is a “light rare earth” proven to enhance
normal cell growth and double the lifespan of laboratory
species.

Neodymium was discovered in 1885 by an Austrian
amateur mineralogist, Baron Carl Auer von Welsbach. He
divided a mineral called didymium into two new and distinct
elemental salts, one of which contained neodymium. Welsbach
created the name for this new element from two Greek words,
neos, which translates to “new” and didymos, which translates
to “twin.”

Neodymium and its oxides, which tend to be rose
colored, are used to make colored glass for welder’s goggles
and the synthetic “ruby” for powerful lasers.

Neodymium is a magnetic substance and is used to
create the most powerful magnets on earth. Disc magnets
made from neodymium are powerful enough to recognize
the magnetic materials in the printing ink used to print paper
money and are used to detect counterfeit paper money.

Ne-Neon is found in igneous rocks at 0.005 ppm and
sea water at 0.00014 ppm. Neon is a “noble” gas, inert and
unreactive, it does not form any compound with any other
element. It is a colorless and odorless gas and is the fifth most
common element in the earth’s atmosphere. Its concentration
in air is 0.002%, or about one-tenth that of carbon dioxide.

Neon was discovered in 1898 by the English chemist
Sir William Ramsy during his experiments with the fractional
distillation of liquid air, he named it neon after the Greek
word neos, which translates to “new.”

The “neon signs” we see in store and restaurant
windows are glass tubes that contain neon gas that glows
when it is energized by electricity. The term “neon sign” is
now used generically for all such glowing tubes, many of
which contain gases other than neon. Different gasses produce


 

signs of different colors; every gas, when electrically excited,
radiates its own characteristic color, argon produces a purple
light, xenon produces a blue-green light.

Ni-Nickel is found in igneous rocks at 75 ppm, shale
at 68 ppm, sandstone at 2.0 ppm, limestone at 20 ppm, fresh
water at 0.01 ppm, sea water at 0.0054 ppm, soils at 40.0 ppm
(higher in soils derived from serpentine), marine plants at 3
ppm, terrestrial plants at 3.0 ppm (accumulated by Alyssum
bertalonii), marine animals at 0.4 to 25 ppm, and terrestrial
animals at 0.8 ppm (primarily in RNA).

Nickel is a silvery metal found in an ore called
millerite, which is a compound of nickel and sulfur. Nickel is
considered a rare element because only one one-hundredth of
one percent of the earth’s crust is nickel. The molten core of
the earth is thought to be primarily iron and nickel. Nickel is
a common element found in meteorites.

While nickel compounds were known since ancient
times, the pure metal was only identified in 1751 by a Swedish
chemist named Axel Fredrik Cronstedt. Nickel is a German
word for Satan, and the element is thought to be named for the
German Kupfernickel, or “Satan's copper.” Like cobalt, nickel
was known in the ancient world as a method for coloring glass
- nickel turns glass green.

The “nickel” or the five cent U.S. coin is made of copper
and nickel, with nickel being 25% of the alloy.

Like iron, nickel can be made magnetic; the alloy
named alnico, formed from aluminum, nickel and cobalt, is
used to produce some of the most powerful magnets.

An important use of nickel is the nickel-cadmium (Ni-Cad) battery.

Less than 10% of ingested metallic nickel is absorbed.
Nickel deficiency was first reported in 1970. Nickel functions as
a cofactor for metalloenzymes and facilitates gastrointestinal
absorption of iron and zinc.

Optimal tissue levels of vitamin B,, are necessary
for the optimal biological function of nickel. Vitamin B,,
deficiency results in an increased need for nickel in animals
and humans.

Np-Neptunium is a highly reactive silvery metal,
known only as radioactive isotopes. The half-life of neptunium
is 2.2 x 10. Neptunium accumulates in mammalian bone
after ingestion. Neptunium has been found in fresh water
organisms in the Hanford River (USA).

Neptunium was the first manmade transuranium
element. Neptunium derives its name from the planet
Neptune, because that is the planet beyond Uranus (the planet
from which the name uranium was derived from).


 

Currently there are 18 known isotopes of neptunium,
the most important of which is neptunium-237, with a half-life of 2.1 million years.

O-Oxygen is found in igneous rocks at 464,000 ppm,
shale at 483,000 ppm, sandstone at 492,000 ppm, limestone
at 497,000 ppm, fresh water at 889,000 ppm, sea water at
857,000 ppm, soils at 490,000 ppm, marine plants at 470,000
ppm, terrestrial plants at 410,000 ppm (except for anaerobic
organisms), marine animals at 400,000 ppm, and terrestrial
animals at 186,000 ppm. Terrestrial oxygen consists of 99.76%
O° with a half-life of less than two minutes.

The English chemist, Joseph Priestly (1733 - 1804) is
credited with the discovery of oxygen. In his most famous
experiment, Priestly heated an oxide of mercury, and noted
that the gas it gave off “caused a candle to burn with a
remarkably brilliant flame.”

Credit for naming oxygen is given to the French chemist
Antoine Laurent Lavoisier (1743 - 1794) who was beheaded
during the French Revolution. The word oxygen is derived
from the Greek oxys, which translates to acid and gens, which
translates to creator or former.

Oxygen is one of the most important and abundant
elements on earth. It exists in the atmosphere as a gas, in water
as part of the water molecule, and in rocks and minerals in
the earth’s crust. In its most common elemental form, oxygen
is found in the atmosphere as diatomic oxygen - O,. Oxygen
is colorless and odorless; many natural chemical reactions
consume oxygen, including combustion and decay of organic
matter.

Oxygen is constantly being “produced” through the
solar-chemical process unique to green plants - photosynthesis.
Photosynthesis is a chlorophyll dependant process. The green
plant will use the suns solar light energy to cut CO, into carbon
atoms and O,. The oxygen is pumped into the environment,
thus replenishing the oxygen supply.

===== Screenshot (106) =====
 


Thecarbonatoms thatarea byproduct of photosynthesis
are used to construct carbon chains which are then in turn
used to produce, wood (cellulose), complex carbohydrates,
sugars, amino acids, fatty acids, oils and vitamins.

The solar energy stored in wood, carbonaceous plant
materials, carbohydrates and sugars is referred to as “solar
income.

Almost 46% of the earth’s crust is oxygen, and it is by
far the most abundant element in the earth’s crust. Oxygen is
essential to life and is part of almost every biological molecule
in our body. The combustion of various fuels depends upon
oxygen and provides most of the energy for industry and for
heating.

Oxygenisa structural atom of water (inand out of living
systems) and all organic compounds of biological interest, O,
is required for respiration by all aerobic organisms. We can
live for 30 days without food, three to seven days without
water, under ideal circumstances, however, we can only
survive for four minutes without gaseous oxygen.

This critical requirement for oxygen makes it the most
important of all elements from the standpoint of immediate
survival and maintenance of human life.

Oxygen is transported across the alveolar (microscopic
air sacs) membranes in the lungs into capillaries and is
transported to the bodies trillions of cell attached to a large
complex protein molecule - hemoglobin. This molecule is
very large, consisting of 574 amino acids. Found only in the
red blood cell, hemoglobin chemically binds oxygen to itself
and then releases it to our cells. When loaded with oxygen,
hemoglobin is bright red and gives oxygenated blood its
characteristic color. When the oxygen is released, hemoglobin
turns color to a dark bluish red.

According to the 1980s US. Geological Survey,
our earth’s atmosphere had 50% oxygen at the time when
dinosaurs flourished. These oxygen levels were arrived at by
inserting microneedles into trapped air bubbles in polar ice


 

and determining the oxygen levels in the dated prehistoric ice.

Some paleontologists claim that the simultaneous and
universal demise of the dinosaurs followed the widespread
quieting of the earth’s volcanoes, which reduced the
atmospheric levels of CO,, which in turn reduced the oxygen
levels to 38%. It is theorized that the 12% drop in the earth’s
oxygen levels was sufficient to cause the apocalyptic end of
the dinosaurs.

The Geological survey also reported that the earth’s
atmosphere still contained 38% oxygen as late as 100 years
ago. During the 1950s the percentage of oxygen in our
atmosphere dropped to 21% and today oxygen is only 19%
of our gaseous atmosphere. The continued drop in oxygen
levels reflects an increase in oxygen consuming species
and fossil fuel combustion (i.e. vehicles, electric and power
generating plants). Combined with increased consumption is
lower oxygen production (i.e. decreasing acreage of the rain
forests and aquatic algae). The net result of this continued
drop in atmospheric oxygen levels is a relative “anaerobic
state” compared with the 38% of just 100 years ago and a very
dramatic shift to the “anaerobic state” compared with the 50%
oxygen levels of 75 million years ago.

Most pathogenic organisms (disease producing germs)
are by themselves anaerobic. They are “happier,” and flourish
and reproduce with more vigor in the absence of oxygen. You
know them as gangrene organisms, type A “flesh eating”
Streptococcus, MRSA, etc. They are able to survive and
grow in and around living cells weakened by low oxygen
environments, which are also conducive for the growth of
viruses, yeast, fungi, cancer, etc.

The question is, why have anaerobic
“suddenly” appeared in the 80s and 90s, diseases that we
have little or no human history or experience?

Regardless of the name, tuberculosis, consumption,
or scrofula can be found in five thousand-year-old mummies
from Egypt and China, one thousand-year-old corpses from

diseases

===== Screenshot (107) =====
 


Peru, and in ancient writings from the Greeks and Romans.
The “new” modern day anaerobic diseases have little or no
history with humans, nor will you find anything in biblical or
ancient writings describing HIV, EBV, CMV, Herpes II, Hanta
virus, SARS, H5N1 Avian Flu, Candida albicans, yeast, Toxic
Shock Syndrome, E. coli, MRSA, or type A “flesh eating”
Streptococcus Sp.

The most plausible theory is that the anaerobic disease
causing organisms laid around ina dormant state as long as the
atmospheric oxygen remained high (i.e. 50, 38, or even 21%)
and inhibited their activity and growth. With the precipitous
dip in atmospheric oxygen we are having an “oxygen counter
revolution” with a return to the anaerobic bios.

Dr. Otto Warburg of the Max Plank Institute,
Germany, was the recipient of two unshared Nobel Prizes.
Linus Pauling was the only other individual to be awarded
two unshared Nobel Prizes. One of Warburg's Nobel Prizes
was awarded for his discovery of the amino acid and for
describing the basic chemical composition of proteins and
the second for discovering that the metabolism of the cancer
cell is fermentative and anaerobic while the metabolism of
the normal noncancerous cell is aerobic. During the 1950s,
Warburg was able to demonstrate clearly that cancer cells
ferment sugar under anaerobic conditions and die in the

presence of oxygen.
Neutrophils, a type of white blood cell that help

===== Screenshot (108) =====
 


 

defend us by identifying, engulfing, and destroying invading
micro-organisms (i.e. viruses, bacteria, yeast, fungus, malaria,
etc.), parasites, and cancer cells, use hydrogen peroxide as
their “lethal weapon.” Neutrophils tend to be very sloppy,
dribbling their over-production of hydrogen peroxide freely
into the blood stream.

The potential danger of hydrogen peroxide free in the
blood stream could be a loose cannon, but fortunately, we
humans are blessed with a protective ubiquitous enzyme called
catalase. Catalase literally covers our red blood cells and coats
the inside walls of our blood vessels. The function of catalase
is to rapidly facilitate the decomposition of hydrogen peroxide
down to water (H,O) and a singlet oxygen atom (O).

There are concerns by the uninitiated regarding
the “free radical” status of singlet oxygen which has a free
electron. When singlet or atomic oxygen comes into direct
contact with tissue cells outside of the circulatory system
(i.e. cell culture, test tube, wounds, etc.) the cells will die. In
the whole animal or human, other factors come into play to
prevent “free radical” damage.

When ingested in proper dilution on an empty stomach
or administered intravenously under proper conditions, food
grade hydrogen peroxide is readily absorbed through the
stomach and duodenal walls directly into the blood stream
where it is immediately broken down into water and singlet
oxygen. The free electron of the singlet oxygen either combines
with a free electron of a carcinogenic free electron or with the
free electron of a second singlet oxygen, becoming O,,.

Carcinogenic free electrons frequently remain free
electrons under many circumstances. They are actually quite
happy with their free electron status. On the other hand, the
free electron of the singlet oxygen does not like to be a singlet
electron and if it doesn’t locate another free electron to attach
to, it will grab onto another singlet oxygen in nanoseconds
becoming atmospheric oxygen (O,) - the required stuff of
respiration.


 

catalase
@©zonei@gaa bl OF Os emma tO}

Oxygen in the form of hydrogen peroxide has been used
topically, intravenously and orally since the Civil War. It has been
used widely in Europe for over 50 years for alternative cancer
therapies, circulatory disease, arteriosclerosis, emphysema,
asthma, gangrene and more recently as a therapy for stroke
patients.

Stroke patients have inactive but living cells
surrounding the stroke site known as “sleeping beauty” cells
that can be reactivated or jump started when they are exposed
to several atmospheres of oxygen in hyperbaric chambers.
Athletic injuries can also be treated by putting the injured
athlete into a hyperbaric oxygen chamber. The healing time
is shortened significantly so the athlete can return to play in
weeks instead of months.

Os-Osmium is found in igneous rocks at 0.0015 ppm.
It oxidizes organic matter as OsO, and is reduced to Os.

Osmium is a hard, brittle, bluish-white metal with
an extremely high melting point (3,054 C). Osmium was
discovered in 1803 by the English chemist Smithson Tennant,
who found the new element while examining the residue
formed by treating platinum ores with aqua regia, a mixture
of nitric and hydrochloric acids. Tennant named his newly
discovered element for its odor, using the Greek word osme,
which translates to “smell.”

P-Phosphorus is found in igneous rocks at 1,050 ppm,
shale at 700 ppm, sandstone at 170 ppm, limestone at 400 ppm,
fresh water at 0.005 ppm, sea water at 0.07 ppm, and soil at 650
ppm, where it has been “fixed” by hydrous oxides of Al and
Fe in acid soil. Phosphorous is found in marine plants at 3,500
ppm, terrestrial plants at 2,300 ppm, marine animals at 4,000 to

===== Screenshot (109) =====
 


 

18,000 ppm, and terrestrial animals at 17,000 to 44,000 ppm.

Phosphorus is a nonmetal element that was discovered
by the German physician Hennig Brand in 1669. Brand carried
out experiments in the fashion of the ancient alchemists. He
distilled the residue from boiled-down urine and obtained a
new element that glowed in the dark and burst into flames
when exposed to warm air. He had discovered white
phosphorus, a waxy, white solid that melts at 44 C. The name
given to the new element was taken from the Greek word
phosphorous which translates to “bearer of light.”

In many ways, the chemistry of phosphorus is similar
to that of nitrogen. Phosphorus occurs in nature as phosphate
rocks, which consists mainly of calcium phosphate and
fluorapatite, a mineral containing calcium, fluorine, and
phosphate. Almost all of the elemental phosphorus produced
commercially is in the form of phosphoric acid. Phosphoric
acid is a solid that is most often sold as an 85% solution in
water. Its primary use is for the production of triple-phosphate
fertilizers - soil that is devoid of phosphorus is barren.

The original use of NPK (nitrogen, phosphorus,
potassium) as a fertilizer began in the 1700s was a creation
of the German chemist Liebig, who burned bread, grain and
vegetables, analyzed the ash and then produced a cheap
chemical fertilizer from these three elements.

Phosphorous is an extremely important essential
mineral, however, it gets little or no attention from nutritionists
because it is widely available in all foods. Phosphorous is
a major structural mineral for bones and teeth. It has more
functions in the human body than any other mineral, including
its role as a vital constituent of nucleic acids, activating
enzymes, and for several steps of the ATP energy cycle, and
for RBC metabolism.

A complete discussion of phosphorous would require
a discussion of every metabolic function in the human body.
Second in abundance only to calcium in the human body, it

comprises 22% of the body’s total mineral content. The human


 

body contains about 800 grams of phosphorous, just short of
two pounds, of which 700 grams are found in bones and teeth
as insoluble calcium phosphate.

The balance of phosphorous in the human body is
found as biologically active intra and extracellular colloidal
P in combination with carbohydrates, lipids, protein and a
wide variety of other biologically active organic compounds
including the blood’s major buffer system. B-complex vitamins
function as coenzymes to intracellular metabolic functions
only when combined with phosphorus.

Phosphorus is part of most proteins and as such
becomes problematic because elevated P intake increases Ca
requirements when “high protein diets” are consumed. Under
those circumstances P can aggravate osteoporosis, arthritis,
high blood pressure, loosen teeth, periodontal disease, etc.
Phosphorus is present as phytates in cereals and whole grain
flours, therefore, if bread is made from unleavened flours, the
phytic acid will complex with Ca, Fe, Zn, and other minerals
further lowering their absorption rate.

The average adult human dietary intake of P is 1,000 to
1,500 mg/day. In adults and older children, the absorption of
metallic P is limited to about three to five per cent and as high
as eight to 12% in infants. Mixed dietary sources of chelated P
may be absorbed at the rate of 40 to 50%. Optimal absorption
of metallic and chelated P occurs when the dietary Ca:P ratio
is 1:1. Organically bound colloidal P is absorbed up to 98%.

Deficiencies of P have long been recognized in
livestock. Symptoms of P deficiency include the behavior of
pica and cribbing and fractures. Phosphorus deficiency has
only recently been recognized in humans. The widespread,
universal, and ultimately fatal results of P deficiency are the
result of its widespread biological functions, significantly as
the result of a decrease in ATP synthesis (complete metabolic
energy failure) with associated neuromuscular, skeletal, blood
and kidney disease.

Clinical P depletion and resultant low blood P

===== Screenshot (110) =====
 


ji meeennee, ener eee
(hypophosphatemia) without P supplements, excessive use of
antacids, hyperparathyroidism (low calcium/high phosphate
diets are the cause of this disease).

Improper treatment of diabetic acidosis, use of diuretics,
sweating during exercise, and work and alcoholism with and
without liver disease can create a P deficiency.

Vegetarians and vegans who do supplement with
minerals rarely have P deficiency, however, because of their
high phytic acid intake, they tend to always have other mineral
deficiencies including Ca, Cu, Cr, V, Li, Fe, Co and zn.

Pa-Protactinium is found in igneous rocks at 1.4 x
10“ ppm and sea water at 2.4 x 10" ppm. All isotopes of
protactinium are radioactive with a half-life of 32,000 years.
Protactinium accumulates in mammalian bone after ingestion.

Protactinitum is one of the most rare and most
expensive of the naturally occurring elements. It is found in
pitchblende, where it is produced as one of the decay products
of uranium.

Protactinium was discovered by the German physicists
Kasimir Fajans and O. H. Gohring in 1913 during a study of
the elements produced by the decay of uranium ore.

The two Germans originally named their new
element “brevum,” however, the name was later changed
to protactinium in 1949, Its name is derived from the Greek
word protos, which translates to “first,” and actinium, because
the decay product of protactinium is actinium. There are
22 known isotopes of protactinitum. The pure metal was
eventually purified in 1934.

Pb-Lead is found in igneous rocks at 12.5 ppm, shale
at 20 ppm, sandstone at 7 ppm, limestone at 9 ppm, soil at
10 ppm (higher in limestone soils and humus), fresh water at
0.005 ppm, sea water at 0.00008 pm, marine plants at 8.4 ppm,
terrestrial plants at 2.7 ppm (many plant species are adapted
to grow in lead-rich soils and accumulate lead including


Amorpha canescens), marine animals at 0.5 ppm (highest in fish
bones), and in terrestrial animals at 2.0 ppm. The highest lead
levels are found in bone, liver and kidney. Lead is known to be
part of and required for the RNA/DNA duplicating system.

Lead has anancient history, lead coins and lead sculpture
have been found in Egyptian tombs dating back to 5,000 years
B.C. The alchemists associated lead with the planet Saturn and
not only believed lead to be the oldest metal but also believed
that all other metals eventually transmutated into lead.

The metal, lead, takes its name from the Anglo-Saxon
word lead, and its chemical symbol from the Latin word for
the element, plumbum. Lead is rarely found in nature as the
pure metal, the most common lead ore is galena, which is
made up of lead sulfide.

Mineral deficient animals and children with the
symptoms of pica and cribbing, a craving for non-food
items (i.e. paint, sand, dirt, etc.), are very susceptible to lead
poisoning (“plumbism”). Infants and children with pica
will will chew on their toys, cribs, windowsills, caulking,
furniture, and paint. A chip of lead paint the size of a penny
can contain as much as 50 to 100 ug of lead. Consuming this
much lead daily over a three month period will result in lead
poisoning. Like many heavy metals, lead disables enzymes
that are catalysts for subcellular biochemical reactions.

The “normal” background blood lead level is below
40 ug/dl. Children with blood lead levels above 60 to 80
ug/dl have symptoms of vomitting, irritability, weight loss,
muscular weakness, headaches, abdominal pain, insomnia
and anorexia.

Children with blood lead levels above 80 ug/dl show
anemia, kidney disease, peripheral neuritis, ataxia and muscular
incoordination, joint pain, and encephalopathy (brain damage,
learning disabilities, etc.) with eventual death.

The best approach to treating lead poisoning is to
supplement with all 90 essential nutrients to eliminate pica
and cribbing and further ingestion of lead, restoring fluid and

===== Screenshot (111) =====
 


 

electrolyte balance. In addition, the use of IV or IM chelation
therapy using EDTA (ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid) and
BAL (British Anti-lewisite) for a minimum of five days. It is
not unusual for as many as 25% of Pb poisoned individuals to
have residual loss of IQ, loss of coordination, hyperactivity,
learning disabilities and impulsiveness.

Pd-Palladium is found in igneous rocks at 0.01 ppm
and terrestrial animals at 0.002 ppm. Palladium accumulates
in mammalian liver and kidney. Palladium is relatively rare
and, like rhodium and ruthenium, is frequently found mixed
with platinum; it was discovered in 1803 by William Hyde
Wollaston, who named it after the asteroid Pallas.

Palladium is commonly used for the treatment of
malignant tumors. These Palladium compounds inhibit cell
division of the cancer, yet are free of negative side effects.
Radioactive palladium pellets, the size of a grain of rice, are
commonly used as a treatment for prostate cancer. With a
half-life of only 17 days, the palladium-103 isotope delivers
doses of radiation and then disappears. :

Oral compounds of thiamine, lipoic acid and palladium
began to be used as a non-prescription OTC (home remedy)
cancer treatment after the year 2000.

Pm-Promethium is a synthetic rare earth element
created innuclear accelerators and nuclear reactors; its isotopes
are radioactive with a half-life of 2.6 years. Promethium is an
important fission product that has now entered the biosphere.
Prior to man-made nuclear explosions, promethium did not
exist in nature.

Very little is known about the chemical or physical
properties of pure promethium although pure salts have been
produced; the radiation produced by pure promethium salts
causes the surrounding air to glow in the dark with a brilliant
blue light.

Promethium is named after the Greek god Prometheus


 

whostole fire from the heavens; promethium has been identified
in the spectrum of several stars in the Andromeda Galaxy.

The existence of promethium was predicted in the
early 20th century, however, it was only in 1947 that it was
isolated by J. A. Mirinsky, L. E. Glendenin and C. D. Coryell,
a team of scientists working at the National Laboratory at
Oak Ridge, Tennessee. They found promethium among the
plethora of different nuclei of intermediate size, known as
fission fragments. When neodymium is bombarded with
intense neutron radiation it is transmutted to promethium.

Twenty-eight isotopes of promethium have so far been
synthesized, all of which are radioactive. Promethium-145
is the longest-lived isotope with a half-life of 17.7 years. The
electrons given off by promethium-147 nucleus disintegration,
are used to produce light from phosphors to produce heat in
nuclear-powered batteries, to produce X-rays in portable X-
ray units and to measure the thickness of thin sheets and film
such as paper and metal sheets and foil.

Promethium accumulates in mammalian bone and
liver after ingestion.

Po-Polonium is found in igneous rocks at 2 x 10“
ppm. The discovery of polonium by Marie and Pierre Curie in
1898 was one of the “great moments in the history of science.”
Their discovery of this radioactive element (originally c called

“radium”) eventually produced the modern concept of the
atomic nucleus and a basic understanding of its structure.

The Curies were looking for the source of the
radioactivity in the uranium-bearing mineral pitchblende.
Their curiosity was aroused when they removed the uranium
from pitchblende, r radioactivity was still present. Their
investigations eventually led to the isolation of two new
elements, polonium and radium.

Polonium was named for Poland, the native country
of Marie Curie. It is a rare element in nature, being formed
by the decay of radioactive uranium and thorium. Polonium

===== Screenshot (112) =====
 


 

is found in uranium mines in concentrations as little as 100
millionths of a gram per ton of ore.

Polonium has 27 known isotopes, more than any other
element, and all are radioactive. The most common isotope
is polonium-210. It has a half-life of only 138 days, making it
5,000 times more radioactive than radium.

Polonium-210 is a silvery metalloid that is extremely
volatile; it will almost completely vaporize at temperatures
as low as 60 C. As it decays, polonium emits alpha particles,
each of which is a nucleus of a helium atom. When polonium-
210 ejects an alpha particle, it is transmutted into lead-206, a
very stable isotope of lead.

The Nuclear Regulatory Commission has established a
quantity of 0.8 million millionths of a gram as the maximum
allowable amount of polonium-210 that can be ingested; this
makes polonium-210 approximately 100,000 million times
more toxic than cyanide.
Pr-Praseodymium is a “light” rare earth found in
igneous rock at 8.2 ppm, shale at 6 ppm, sandstone at 2.8
ppm, limestone at 1.4 ppm, marine plants at 5 ppm, terrestrial
plants accumulate Pr at 46 ppm (Carya spp.), marine animals
at 0.5 ppm and terrestrial animals at 1.5 ppm (accumulating
in liver and bone after ingestion).

Praseodymium was isolated in 1885 by Carl Auer
von Welsbach, an Austrian baron who had a passion for
mineralogy. He separated a mineral called didymium into
two new and distinct elements, one of which contained
praseodymium; it was named from two Greek words, prasios,
which translates to “green,” and didymos, which translates to
“twin.” The name refers to the green color of the element's
oxide; the second element was neodymium. Praseodymium
was not produced in its pure elemental state until 1931. It is a
soft, silvery-white metal that tarnishes to form a green oxide.

Praseodymium is found primarily in monazite and
bastnasite ores. Monazite sands contain all of the “rare earth”


 

elements, often in concentrations greater than 50%. Monazite
is found in river sand in India and Brazil and in Florida beach
sand. Large deposits of bastnasite are found in Southern
California

Pt-Platinum is found in igneous rocks at 0.005 ppm
and land animals at 0.002 ppm. Platinum is a precious metal,
like gold, is highly prized for jewelry and is used as a medium
of exchange world wide.

Platinum is a durable, heavy, silver-white metal that is
resistant to corrosion, does not react with oxygen and is found
in nature as the pure element. Most of the world’s supply of
platinum comes from South Africa.

Platinum was discovered in 1741 by an Englishman,
Charles Wood, however, there is considerable evidence
that points to the wide spread use of platinum by the pre-Columbian Indians. Platinum is derived from the Spanish
word platina, which translates to “silver.” A bar of platinum-iridium alloy is stored in a vault in Paris where it serves as the
world’s standard for the kilogram.

Platinum is used in the medical treatment of ovarian
cancer and testicular cancer (cis-platinum) as it specifically
inhibits the growth of ovarian and testicular cancer cells.

Platinum is commonly used in the mechanism of
cardiac pacemakers.

Pu-Plutonium isotopes are all radioactive with a half-life of 24,000 years. Plutonium was released into the earth’s
atmosphere by nuclear explosions. Marine plants show 4,000
times the background level of sea water, terrestrial plants
record 0.4 to 2.2 disintegrations/sec/kg, and terrestrial
animals show 0.07 to 6.8 disintegrations/sec/kg. Plutonium
accumulates in mammalian bone after contact or ingestion.

Plutonium is the most important of all of the
transuranium elements, all of which are synthetically created.
Plutonium was the second transuranium element, following

===== Screenshot (113) =====
 


 

neptunium. In1941, the American chemist,GlennT.Seaborg, at
the University of California at Berkeley discovered plutonium,
however, because of security reasons, the announcement of
the discovery was not made until 1946. Plutonium was named
for Pluto, the planet that is found outside the orbit of Neptune
in our solar system.

Plutonium has 15 known isotopes, all of which are
radioactive. Plutonium-239 has a half-life of 24,000 years, is the
most important because it readily fissions when bombarded
by thermal neutrons.

Ra-Radium is found in igneous rocks at 9 x 10 ppm,
shale at 11 x 10” ppm, sandstone at 7 x 10 ppm, limestone
at 4x 10 ppm, fresh water at 3.9 x 10!"™, sea water at 6 x 107
ppm, soils at 8 x 10‘? ppm, marine plants at 9 x 10 ppm,
terrestrial plants at 10 ppm, marine animals at 0.7) x 10
ppm, and in terrestrial animals at 7 x 10) ppm. The highest
concentrations are found in mammalian bone; all isotopes of
radium are radioactive.

Radium was discovered in 1898 by the Polish-born
French chemist Marie Sklodowska Curie and her husband,
Pierre Curie. It is generally agreed that the discoveries of
radium, the electron and Einstein’s theory of relativity
“ushered in the beginning of the modern era in science.”

During their analysis of the radioactive properties of a
uranium-containing mineral (pitchblende), the Curies realized
when they extracted all of the uranium from the pitchblende,
the remainder was still radioactive. Sifting through tons of
ore, they were able to isolate a radioactive mix that was mostly
barium. ;

The barium was employed as a precipitating reagent
for polonium and other elements in the ore. When they
heated the precipitate in a flame to create spectral lines, the
Curies observed an unexpected red color - a new element.
The Curies named their new element (radium) from the Latin

word radius, which translates to “ray.” They also discovered


 

polonium in the same ore. It takes seven tons of pitchblende
to produce one gram of radium.

It took four more years for the Curies to produce a
pure sample of radium, which they isolated from a solution
of radium chloride by electrolysis. In 1911, Marie Curie was
awarded the Nobel Prize in chemistry for her discovery of
these new elements. It was her second Nobel Prize, as she had
shared her first with her husband and the French scientist,
Henri Becquerel in 1903 for the accidental discovery of
radioactivity.

Radium has 25 known isotopes, the most common and
the one discovered by the Curies, is radium-226, which has a
half-life of 1,630 years.

The international standard for the measurement of
radiation is called the Curie in honor of the Curies.

Rb-Rubidium is found in igneous rocks at 90 ppm,
shale at 140 ppm, sandstone at 60 ppm, limestone at 3.0 ppm,
fresh water at 0.0015 ppm, sea water at 0.12 ppm, soil at 100
ppm (“fixed” by clay bearing soils), marine plants at 7.4 ppm,
terrestrial plants at 20 ppm, marine animals at 20 ppm, and
terrestrial animals at 17 ppm (the highest levels are found
concentrated in the liver and muscle, the lowest levels are
found in bone.

In 1861, the German chemists Robert Bunsen and
Gustav Kirchhoff discovered rubidium. They identified
it as an impurity mixed with the alkali metals they were
investigating; they identified rubidium as a new element from
the spectral lines it emitted, one of which is a deep red color.
The name rubidium is taken from the Latin word rubidus,
which translates to “deep red.”

It is documented that rubidium can replace the
electrolyte function of potassium in many species, including
bacteria, algae, fungi, and certain invertebrates (echinoderms
- starfish).

Re-Rhenium is found in igneous rocks at 0.005 ppm,
marine plants at 0.014 ppm, and marine animals at 0.0005 to
0.006 ppm, Terrestrial animals accumulate rhenium in the
thyroid.

Rhenium is one of the most-rare of all earthly elements.
It takes one million pounds of molybdenite ore to produce
one gram of rhenium, resulting in a price of $10,000 per gram
in 1928.

In 1925, the existence of rhenium was predicted by and
then discovered by the German chemists Ida Tacke, Walter
Nodack, and Otto Carl Berg. They named rhenium from the
Latin word Rhenus, which translates to the Rhine River.

Rh-Rhodium is found in igneous rock at 0.001 ppm.
Rhodium is very rare and is usually found mixed in platinum
ores. It was discovered by the Englishman William Hyde
Wollaston in 1803. He named it after the Greek word rhodon,
which translates to “rose” for its characteristic rose color.

The catalytic converter of carbon fuel vehicles are
filled with small catalytic beads that are made of platinum,
palladium and rhodium, that act to convert hot exhaust gases
that flow through them into harmless byproducts.

The carbon monoxide and unburned hydrocarbons
are converted into carbon dioxide and water and the nitrogen
dioxide is converted to nitrogen and oxygen.

Rn-Radon gas is found in igneous rocks at 4 x 10" ppm,
fresh water at 1.7 x 10°) ppm and sea water at 6 x 10 ppm.
All 20 known isotopes of radon are radioactive with a half-life
ranging from 54 seconds to 3.8 days. Radon is carcinogenic
and highly toxic when inhaled. Radon is a common household
pollutant and hazard; it is colorless and odorless. Detection
requires the use of OTC detection kits which are generally
available.

Radon is produced as one of the radioactive decay
products of uranium and thorium. This noble gas is the

heaviest known gas, being eight times heavier than air and is
chemically non-reactive..

Radon was originally discovered by the German
physicist Friedrich Ernst Dorn in 1900 while analyzing the
decay products of radium; he named the new gaseous element
“radium emanation.” William Ramsay and R. W. Whytlaw,
two early radon investigators, renamed the gas “niton,” from
the Latin word nitens, which translates to “shining.” In 1923
the name of this gas was changed again to radon.

Radon-222 is the longest-lived isotope of radon, witha
half-life of 3.82 days. Radon-222 is found naturally in the soil
in large concentrations as the result of trace levels of uranium
that are found throughout the earth’s crust. The gas diffuses
through the soil and into the air. Radon can diffuse into family
dwellings and businesses through basement floors and walls.
This dangerous pollution risk is particularly high during the
winter months when doors and windows are kept closed.

As early as the 16th century, it was well known that
uranium miners in Bohemia often died from strange diseases
of the lungs. When radon-222 is inhaled, it decays and emits
alpha particles, which are the nuclei of helium atoms. This
decay product, lead-210 is also radioactive and settles into the
lower reaches of the lung.

Lead-210 has a radioactive half-life of 20.4 years; it is
this extended exposure of the lungs to radiation of lead-210
that causes cancer. Experts believe that 10% of all lung cancers
are caused by radon and it’s decay byproducts.

Smoking cigarettes poses a radiation risk as well as
the well known chemical risk. While it is growing, tobacco
is a victim of contamination by radon in the soil, and the
phosphate fertilizers commonly used by farmers are rich in
uranium. When the tobacco leaf is burned, the inhaled smoke
subjects the smoker to levels of radiation that are 1,000 times
higher those suffered by nuclear power plant employees.

Ru-Ruthenium is found in igneous rocks at 0.001 ppm,
terrestrial plants at 0.005 ppm, and in terrestrial animals at at
0.002 ppm. RuO, is very toxic.

Ruthenium is a rare element that is a byproduct of
refining platinum ores. Its discovery was noted by the Estonian
chemist Karl Karlovitch Klaus, who named it from the Latin
word Ruthenia, which translates to “Russia.”

A current use of ruthenium is as a catalyst for a reaction
that splits water into hydrogen gas and oxygen. This catalytic
method of splitting water to obtain hydrogen and oxygen
depends upon the ruthenium being “excited” or energized by
exposing it to solar radiation. This alternative source of fuel
does not require electricity to generate energy and therefore
ruthenium has an obvious importance in the 21st century as
part of a method that can produce cheap fuel at a low cost of
man generated energy and it is environmentally green.

S-Sulfur is found in igneous rocks at 260 ppm, shale
at 2,400 ppm, sandstone at 240 ppm, limestone at 1,200
ppm, freshwater at 3.7 ppm, sea water at 885 ppm, soils at
700 ppm, marine animals at 5,000 to 19,000 ppm (highest
in coelenterates and molluscs) and in terrestrial animals at
5,000 ppm (highest levels in cartilage, tendons, keratin, skin,
intervertebral discs, hair and nails). Its lowest concentrations
are in bones. In soils, 90% of sulfur is bound tightly in humus,
SO, is a major exchange anion in many soils, and occurs in
soils near to volcanoes. In terrestrial plants sulfur occurs at
a rate of 3,400 ppm with the lowest concentrations found in
bryophytes and gymnosperms.

Sulfur is an important structural atom in most proteins as
sulfur amino acids (cystine, cysteine and methionine) and small
organic molecules. Glutathione, a tripeptide containing cysteine,
is essential to normal cellular reactions involving sulfur amino
acids in proteins. Sulfur is found in a reduced form (-SH) in
cysteine and in an oxidized form (-S-S-) as the double molecule
cystine. This “sulhydryl group” is important for the specific


configuration of some structural proteins and for the biological
activities of some enzymes (proteins that do work).

Sulfur-containing proteins work in indirect ways to
maintain life:

Hemoglobin

Hormones (insulin, adrenal cortical hormones)
Enzymes

Antibodies

Sulfur also occurs in carbohydrates such as heparin,
an anticoagulant that concentrates in the liver and to lesser
degrees other tissues, chondroitin sulfate (cartilage, ligaments,
tendon, discs, connective tissue, etc.), the vitamins thiamine
(B,) and biotin have sulfur bound in their molecule.

The toxic characteristics of arsenic are the result of its
ability to combine with sulfhydryl groups.

A deficiency of sulfur results in osteoarthritis,
osteoporosis and a degeneration of cartilage, weakened
tendons, weakened ligaments, erythematosis, sickle cell
anemia, lupus and other “collagen diseases.”

tartrate (tarter emetic) is still used today as the preferred
treatment for blood flukes (schistosomiasis or bilharziasis).

In its pure state, the metal antimony, is a hard, brittle,
grayish crystalline metal. The ore that produces the most
antimony element is stibnite (antimony sulfide), which has
been known for thousands of years. A black compound,

Sc-Scandium is found in igneous rock at 22 ppm,
shale at 13 ppm, sandstone and limestone at 1.0 ppm, and in
terrestrial animals at 0.00006 ppm, where it concentrates in
mammalian heart and bone.

Scandium is a rare element that is found in the crust of
the earth at 0.0025%, it is found in a higher rate in the sun and a
few other stars. Scandium, using the periodic chart in 1871, was
one of the elements that Mendeleyev predicted would exist. In

Se-Selenium is found inigneous rock at 0.05 ppm, shale
at 0.6 ppm, sandstone at 0.055 ppm, limestone at 0.08 ppm,
fresh water at 0.02 ppm, sea water at 0.00009, soils at 0.2 ppm,
marine plants at 0.8 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.2ppm, and
terrestrial animals at 1.7 ppm where the highest concentrations
are found in liver, kidneys, heart and skeletal muscle. Keep in
mind that selenium is not universally distributed.

Selenium is a metalloid that can exist in two different
forms, red and gray, called allotrops. Red selenium is an
amorphous, glasslike solid, while the gray form of selenium
is a soft, bluish-gray metal.

Selenium was discovered by the Swedish chemist Jons


Jakob Berzelius in 1817. Selenium was named for the Greek
word selene, which translates to moon.

Vast areas earth are deficient or totally devoid of
selenium; where it occurs selenium is found in the humus of
alkaline soils.

Selenium is found in the humus of alkaline soils.
Selenium is the most efficient antioxidant; it is used at the
subcellular level in the glutathione peroxidase enzyme system
and metalloamino acids (selenomethionine, etc.).

Selenium prevents cellular and subcellular lipids and
fats from being peroxidized, which literally means it prevents
body fats from going rancid. “Rancid” body fats are seen
externally as “age spots” or “liver spots.” These golden brown
pigments of aging are technically known as cer oid lipofuscin.

Lipofuscin gradually fills cells as the individual ages
The medical theory is that these pigments eventually “fil
the cell and render them helpless and this “aging” process
eventually kills the organism.

In fact the accumulation of ceroid lipofuscin is
preventable and can be reversed.

Selenium also functions to protect cellular and organelle
bi-lipid layer membranes from oxidative damage. Highintakes
of vegetable oils, including salad dressing and cooking oils,
concurrent with a selenium deficiency is the quickest route
to a cardiomyopathy heart attack, arteriosclerosis (build up
of plaque in the arteries resulting from free radical damage
to the “intima” or lining of the Posty cataracts, macular
degeneration, dementias, eainiiple sclerosis, cancer, etc.

The polyunsaturated configuration of oils when heated
or treated with hydrogen (“trans fatty acids”) literally causes
the rancidity (“free radical” damage) of cellular fat.

The clinical diseases associated with a selenium
deficiency are diverse and to the uninformed shrouded in
mystery. Selenium deficiency is one of the more costly mineral
deficiency complexes affecting embryos, the newborn,
toddlers, teens, young adults, and seniors alike.

MS (Multiple sclerosis)

Alzheimer’s Disease

Cancer (deficiency increases risk significantly)

 

Selenium deficiency can result in infertility in both
men and women. Congenital selenium deficiency (during
pregnancy) can result in a wide variety of problems ranging
from failure to conceive, miscarriages, low birth weight, high
infant mortality, SIDS, muscular dystrophy, Cystic Fibrosis,
cardiomyopathy heart disease and liver cirrhosis. Selenium
deficiency in growing children can result in “crib death” or
SIDS (Sudden Infant Death Syndrome).

Sixty-five percent of SIDS death occurs in children being
fedcommercialinfant formulas low or devoid ofselenium.Slow
growth, small size (failure to reach genetic potential for size
and mass), Muscular Dystrophy, scoliosis, cardiomyopathy
(Keshan disease), anemia, liver cirrhosis, muscular weakness,
lowered immune capacity and neuromuscular diseases are
also linked to selenium deficiency.

In young adults, selenium deficiency appears as
anemia, chronic fatigue, Wilson’s Syndrome, muscular
weakness, myalgia, fibromyalgia, muscular tenderness,
pancreatitis, infertility, Muscular Dystrophy, scoliosis and
cardiomyopathy.

Cardiomyopathy is quite common in young athletes
such as basketball and football players at the high school,
college, university, and professional levels because of selenium
deficiency, as are multiple sclerosis and liver cirrhosis.

Selenium deficiency in adults appears as reduced
immune capacity, anemia, infertility, “age spots” or “liver

===== Screenshot (118) =====
 


 

spots,” myalgia, fibromyalgia, muscle weakness, MS, ALS,
Parkinson’s Alzheimer’s palpitations
(irregular heart beat), cardiomyopathy, liver cirrhosis and
increased risk for cancer (i.e. prostate, breast, colon, lung, etc.).
Selenium acts as an antagonist of mercury. Those at
risk for chronic mercury toxicity (dental amalgams, industrial
pollution, etc.) supplement with
selenium prior to, during and after detoxification activities.

preservatives, should

Ina review of the anti-cancer properties of selenium, Dr.
Gerhard N. Schrauzer (Head of the Department of Chemistry
& professor emeritus, UCSD) states:

Selenium is increasingly recognized as a versatile
anticarcinogenic agent. Its protective functions cannot be
solely attributed to the action of glutathione peroxidase.
Instead, selenium appears to operate by several mechanisms,
depending on dosage and the chemical form of selenium and
the nature of the carcinogenic stress. In a major protective
function, selenium is proposed to prevent the malignant
transformation of cells by acting as a “redox switch” in
the activation-inactivation of cellular growth factors and
other functional proteins through the catalysis of oxidation-reduction reactions of critical -SH groups or -S-S- bonds.

The growth-modulatory effects of selenium are
dependent on the levels of intracellular glutathione
peroxidase and the oxygen supply. In general, growth
inhibition is achieved by the Se-mediated stimulation of
cellular respiration (more oxygen/less cancer).

Selenium appears to inhibit the replication of tumor
viruses in animals and the activation of oncogenes by
similar mechanisms. However, it may also alter carcinogen
metabolism and protect DNA against carcinogen-induced
damage. In additional functions of relevance to its
anticarcinogenic activity, selenium acts as an acceptor of
biogenic methyl groups, and is involved in detoxification
of metals and certain xenobiotics. Selenium also has


immunopotentiating properties. It is required for optimal
macrophage and natural killer cell functions.

The school of pharmacy from the University of Georgia
released a report in August of 1994 that concluded “that a
human selenium deficiency is related to the clinical onset of
full blown AIDS inclinically infected HIV patients.” According
to their report, HIV requires large amounts of selenium for
replication. In selenium deficient patients, the virus competes
with the patient for the limited amount of available selenium.
The HIV patient actually dies of a chronic selenium deficiency
encephalopathy, liver cirrhosis, or cardiomyopathy. Long-term HIV patients (20 years or more) that never developed
full blown clinical AIDS had supplemented with large doses
of selenium.

Si-Silica is found in igneous rocks at 281,500 ppm,
shale at 73,000 ppm, sandstone at 368,000 ppm, limestone at
24,000 ppm, fresh water at 6.5 ppm, sea water at 3.0 ppm, soils
at 33,000 ppm (found as SiO,, the most abundant form of Si in
nature, in silicates and clays), marine plants at 1,500 to 20,000
ppm, marine animals at 70,000 ppm, and in terrestrial animals
at 120 to 6,000 ppm where it is mainly concentrated in hair,
lungs and bone.

Silicon is the second most abundant element in the crust
of the earth, exceeded only by oxygen. Compounds of silicon
bond chemically to oxygen make up most of the earth’s sand,
rock and soil.

Silicon was first produced in its pure state in 1824 by
the Swedish scientist Jons Jakob Berzelius (1779-1848).

The most important silicon compound is silica, or
silicon dioxide (SiO,). It exists in two familiar forms in nature:
a crystalline form called quartz, small chips of which are
found in sand, a a noncrystalline form called flint. Small gems
and semiprecious stones such as amethyst, opal, agate and
jasper are crystals of quartz with colored impurities.

===== Screenshot (119) =====
 


 

Flint has been known for thousands of years. When
struck with an acute-angle blow, it will flake in such a manner
as to produce a sharp cutting edge. Some of the earliest
prehistoric stone tools were made of flint.

Flint also allowed prehistoric man (Neanderthals) to
start fires anywhere and at will by striking flint with iron
pyrite or another piece of flint. Once they understood the fire
starting properties of flint, man was able to move without
carrying fire or burning embers with him - he could now start
fire anywhere in just a few moments with dry tinder, flint and
iron pyrite.

Silica as sand can be mixed with soda ash (sodium
carbonate) and limestone (calcium carbonate) and when the
mixture is melted it becomes glass - there are more than 1,000
forms of glass.

Plants accumulating the most silica are diatoms,
horsetail, ferns, Cyoeraceae, Graineae, and Juncaceae, and the
flowers of Pappophorum silicosum. Silicon supplementation
increases the collagen in growing bone by 100 per cent. Tissue
levels of silicon decrease with aging in unsupplemented
humans and laboratory species. Dry brittle finger and toenails,
poor skin quality, poor calcium utilization, and arterial disease
characterize silica deficieny. High fiber diets contain high
levels of silicon, which leads many investigators to believe
that silicon helps to lower cholesterol. The recommended
intake of silicon ranges from 200 to 500 mg/day.

Sm-Samarium is a “light” rare earth and is found
in igneous rocks at 6.0 ppm, shale at 5.6 ppm, sandstone at
2.7 ppm, limestone at 0.8 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.0055
ppm (accumulates up to 23 ppm), marine animals at 0.04
to 0.08 ppm, terrestrial animals at 0.01 ppm in heart muscle
and 0.0009 ppm in mammalian bone and liver. Samarium
enhances normal cell proliferation and doubles the life span
of laboratory species.

Samarium is the fifth most abundant of the rare earth

elements, it was discovered in 1879 by the French chemist
Paul-Emile Lecoq de Boisbaudran, who correctly identified
the element's spectral absorption lines in a mineral called
samarskite, which has been named for a Russian army officer,
Colonel Samarski. ;

The primary ores that samarium is extracted from are
monazite and bastnasite. Monazite contains all of the rare
earth elements; it is found in river sand in India and Brazil
and in Florida beach sand. Extensive deposits of bastnasite
are found in Southern California.

Sn-Tin is found in igneous rocks at 2.0 ppm, shale at 6.0
ppm, sandstone and limestone at 0.5 ppm, fresh water at 0.00004
ppm, sea water at 0.003 ppm, soils at 2.0 to 200 ppm (strongly
absorbed by humus), marine plants at 1.0 ppm, terrestrial
plants at 0.3 ppm (highest in bryophytes and lichens), marine
animals at 0.2 to 20.0 ppm, terrestrial animals at 0.15 ppm with
highest levels being found in lungs and intestines.

Originally the presence of tin in tissue was attributed
to environmental contamination; however, careful and
detailed studies by Schwarz demonstrated that tin produced
acceleration of growth in rats and further met the standards
for being an essential trace element. Tin has several chemical
and physical properties similar to those of carbon, silica,
germanium and lead.

Rats fed tin at 17.0 ng/gm show poor growth, reduced
feeding efficiency, hearing loss, and bilateral (male pattern)
hair loss, while rats fed 1.99 ug/gm were physiologically and
anatomically normal.

Schwarz demonstrated tin to be an essential element
in 1970. Tin has been shown to exert a strong induction effect
on the enzyme heme oxygenase, enhancing heme breakdown
in the kidney. There is also evidence for tin having cancer
prevention properties. A federal study released in November
1991, showed that men in recent generations have poorer
hearing at any given age than men in earlier generations. Men

===== Screenshot (120) =====
 

Sel gasses

over age 30 lose their hearing more than twice as women of
the same age. So much for having a “tin ear”!

Sr-Strontium is found in igneous rocks at 375 ppm,
shale at 300 ppm, sandstone at 20 ppm, limestone at 610 ppm,
fresh water at 0.08 ppm, sea water at 8.1 ppm, soils at 300
ppm, marine plants at 260 to 1,400 ppm, terrestrial plants at
26 ppm, marine animals at 20 to 500 ppm, and in terrestrial
animals at 14 ppm, where it is most highly concentrated in
mammalian bone.

Natural sources of strontium are the mineral ores
celestite, which is a form of strontium sulfate, and strontianite,
a strontium carbonate. Both strontium and the mineral
strontianite were named in 1789 by the English scientist
Dr. Adair Crawford, who first identified the element in the
Scottish village of Strontian.

Strontium can replace calcium in many organisms,
including man. There is considerable evidence for essentiality
in mammals including man. Deficiencies of strontium are
associated with certain types of calcium and boron resistant
osteoporosis and arthritis.

Strontium-90, the man-made radioactive isotope of
the fission atomic explosions and the greatest biohazard fear
during the “Cold War,” does not occur in nature and has a
half-life of 28.8 years.

Ta-Tantalum is found in igneous rocks at 2.0 ppm,
shale at 0.8 ppm, sandstone and limestone at 0.05 ppm, sea
water at 0.0000025 ppm, and in marine animals accumulates
up to 410 ppm.

Anders Gustav Ekeberg, aSwedish chemist, discovered
the new element in 1802 near the Swedish town of Ytterby,
which played an important role in the history of chemistry,
particularly as a source of minerals for the discovery of rare
earths.

Ekeberg named his new element for Tantalos, the father


of Niobe, who, in Greek mythology, was queen of the city of
Thebes. For almost 50 years after Ekeberg’s announcement
of his new element, many chemist thought he had confused
tantalum with niobium. Pure tantalum metal was not
produced until 1903.

The principal ore of tantalum is the mineral columbite,
which is widely distributed throughout the world, with large
deposits in Africa, Thailand, Portugal and Canada. Tantalum
is used for dental and joint prosthesis.

Tb-Terbium is found in igneous rock at 0.9 ppm,
shale at 0.58 ppm, sandstone at 0.41 ppm, limestone at 0.071
ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.0015 ppm, marine animals at 0.006
to 0.01 ppm and terrestrial animals at 0.0004 ppm where it
accumulates in bone.

Terbium is one of the least abundant of the rare earth
elements. Carl Gustaf Mosander discovered the element in
1843 by isolating its oxide from the mineral yttria.

Terbium is recovered commercially from the mineral
monazite, a mixture of rare earth elements combined with
thorium and calcium. It is found in river sands from India
and Brazil and in beach sand from Florida.

In its pure metallic form, terbium is silvery-white and
is soft enough to be cut with a knife.

Tc-Technetium is not found in nature and all isotopes
are radioactive. Technetium was the first new element to be
produced in a laboratory. Technetium gets its name from the
Greek word teknetos, which translates to “artificial.”

While it is not found in nature in the earth’s crust,
technetium has been discovered in some stars. Technetium
was discovered in 1937 by Emilio Segre and Carlo Perrier.
The discovery was made in Italy from a small sample sent to
them by the University of California at Berkeley. Technetium
is poorly absorbed by mammals.

===== Screenshot (121) =====
 


 

Te-Tellurium is found in igneous rocks at 0.001 ppm,
terrestrial plants at 2 to 25 ppm, and in terrestrial animals at 0.02
ppm. Tellurium is a rare, silvery-white metalloid belonging to
the family of elements that includes such nonmetallic elements
as oxygen and sulfur. Sulfur and oxygen can combine with
other metals to form sulfides; tellurium combines with gold
to form a telluride.

Tellurium is one of the few elements that combines
with gold. The compounds are gold telluride, and they are
commonly found in gold-bearing ores.

Tellurium was discovered in Germany in 1782 by an
inspector of mines, Franz Joseph von Reichenstein, an amateur
scientist, who was experimenting with an ore he picked up in
a gold mine in Transylvania. It was named in Berlin from the
Latin word tellus, which translates to earth.

Th-Thorium is found in igneous rocks at 9.6 ppm, shale
at 12 ppm, sandstone and limestone at 1.0 - 7.0 ppm, soils at
5 ppm, marine animals at 0.003 to 0.03 ppm and terrestrial
animals at 0.003 to 0.1 ppm. Thorium is a radioactive, silvery-white metal that is very soft. Its oxide has a melting point of
3,300 C, one of the highest of any oxide, and when shaved
into small pieces, it can be ignited in air to produce a brilliant
white light.

Thorium was discovered in 1828 by the Swedish
chemist Jons Jakob Berzelius, who named it for Thor, the
Scandinavian god of war. He was not aware of its radioactivity
as radioactivity was not known until it was discovered by
Henri Becquerel and the Curies in 1897.

Thorium-232, the radioactive isotope of thorium that
occurs naturally, is actually very weakly radioactive. Its half-life is a whopping 14 billion years, so that very little of it
decays in a short period of time.

Thorium lead and
approximately four times more abundant than uranium ores.
Monasite sand, which is common as beach sand in Florida,

ores are aS COMmmon as ore

can contain as much as 10% thorium, and this sand is used for
the commercial production of the element.

Ti-Titanium is found in igneous rocks at 5,700 ppm,
shale at 4,600 ppm, sandstone at 1,500 ppm, sea water at 0.001
ppm, soils at 5,000 ppm, marine plants at 12.0 to 80.0 ppm
(accumulates in plankton), terrestrial plants at 1.0 ppm, marine
animals at 0.2 to 20.0 ppm and terrestrial animals at 0.2 ppm.

Titanium is the ninth most abundant element in the
earth’s crust, and making up 0.6 per cent of the earth’s mass.
It was discovered in 1791 by an amateur English mineralogist,
the Reverend William Gregor, titanium was named for the
Titans of Greek mythology. The minerals rutile and ilemnite
are the common sources of titanium.

Each jet engine from a Boeing 747 contains 10,000

pounds of titanium and its compounds.
Tm-Thulium is a “heavy” rare earth and is extremely
rare; it is found in igneous rocks at 0.48 ppm, shale at 0.28 ppm,
sandstone at 0.3 ppm, limestone at 0.065 ppm, terrestrial plants
at 0.0015 ppm, and terrestrial animals at 0.00004 ppm. Thulium
supplementation enhances the growth of normal cells in cell
culture and has doubled the life span of laboratory species.

The Swedish chemist Per Teodor Cleve discovered the
element in 1879 by isolating its greenish oxide from the mineral
erbia. He named his new element for Thule, the ancient name
for Scandinavia.

The main source of thulium is the mineral monazite,
which consists of approximately seven thousandths of one
percent thulium. Monazite, which contains all of the rare earth
elements in addition to thorium and calcium, is found in the
river sands in India and Brazil and in beach sand in Florida.

Thulium is a bright silvery metal that is soft that it can
be cut with a knife.

U-Uranium is found in igneous rock at 2.7 ppm, shale
at 3.7 ppm, sandstone at 0.95 ppm, limestone at 2.2 ppm,
fresh water at 0.001 ppm, sea water at 0.003 ppm, soil at ppm
(absorbed by humus, especially in alkaline soils), terrestrial
plants at 0.038 ppm (Astragalus spp. is an accumulator plant),
marine animals at 0.004 to 3.2 ppm, and animals at 0.013 ppm.
All natural isotopes are alpha emitters and may also decay by
fission. Uranium is accumulated by mammalian kidney and
bone after ingestion.

Uranium is the last and heaviest of the natural elements.
The average person associates uranium with nuclear reactors
and the atomic bomb as a source of limitless energy. Once
considered rare, uranium is now known to be commonly
found in many minerals, including pitchblende, uranite,
“vellowcake,” and monazite sands. In the United States, the
ownership and sale of uranium are strictly controlled by the
U.S. Nuclear Regulatory Commission.

The use of uranium compounds to color glass and
ceramic glazes dates back for thousands of years. In 1789, the
German chemist Martin Klaproth, was the first investigator to
recognize that pitchblende contained a yet unknown element.
It was not until 1841 that the French chemist Eugene-Melchior
Peligot first isolated and identified uranium. It was named
after the planet Uranus. In 1896, the French physicist Henri
Becquerel discovered that uranium was radioactive.

A typical sample of uranium consists of two isotopes,
uranium-238 (99.2798 per cent) and uranium-235 (0.7171
percent). Uranium-238 is the dominant isotope of the element;
its half-life of 4.6 billion years makes it the longest-lived of the
three uranium isotopes.

V-Vanadium is found in igneous rocks at 1135 ppm,
shale at 130 ppm, sandstone at 20 ppm, limestone at 20 ppm,
fresh water at 0.001 ppm, sea water at 0.002 ppm, soils at
100 ppm (vanadium is absorbed by humus, especially in
alkaline soils), marine plants at 2.0 ppm, terrestrial plants
at 1.6 ppm (accumulated by the fungus Armanita muscaria),

a ear 5

marine animals at 0.14 to 2.0 ppm, and in terrestrial animals
at 0.15 ppm. Metallic vanadium (vanadyl sulfate) is absorbed
from the intestinal tract very poorly at only 0.1 to 1.0 percent,
vanadium chelates are absorbed at 40 percent, and plant
derived colloidal forms at up to 98%.

Vanadium makes up 0.02% of the earth’s crust, and is
present in trace amounts in more than 60 different ores. The
predominant source of vanadium is vanadinite. A Mexican
professor of mineralogy, Andres Manuel del Rio, discovered
vanadium in 1801. It was later named for the Scandinavian
goddess Vanadis because of its many beautifully colored
compounds. ‘ ”

Vanadium was proven to be essential in 1971. Vanadium
stimulates blood sugar (glucose) oxidation and transport in fat
cells and glycogen (animal starch) synthesis in liver and muscle,
and inhibits liver gluconeogenesis (production of glucose from
fat and protein) and absorption of glucose from the gut.

Vanadium enhances the stimulating effect of insulin
on DNA synthesis. Despite low serum insulin, the blood
glucose levels of diabetic rats fed vanadium was the same as
the glucose levels of normal controls. Vanadium appears to
function like insulin by altering cell membrane function for
ion transport. Therefore vanadium has a very beneficial effect
for humans with glucose and carbohydrate intolerance (i.e.
hypoglycemia, hyperinsulinemia, narcolepsy, prediabetes,
diabetes, depression, manic depression, bi-polar disease,
“chemical imbalance,” ADD, ADHD, violent behavior, autism,
etc.) by making the cell membrane insulin receptors more
sensitive to insulin.

Vanadium inhibits cholesterol synthesis in animals
and humans; this followed by decreased plasma levels of
cholesterol and reduced aortic cholesterol. Vanadium initiates
an increase in the contractile force of heart muscle known as
the “inotropic effect.”

Vanadium has known anticarcinogenic properties.
Feeding 25 ug of vanadium per gram of diet blocked

===== Screenshot (123) =====
 


ee

induction of mouse mammary tumor growth. The vanadium
supplement reduced tumor incidence, average tumor count
per animal, and prolonged median cancer-free time without
inhibiting overall growth or health of the animals.

W-Tungsten is found in igneous rocks at 1.5 ppm, shale

at 1.8 ppm, sandstone at 1.6 ppm, limestone at 0.6 ppm, sea

water at 0.0001 ppm, soils at 1.0 ppm, marine plants at 0.0035

ppm, marine animals at 0.0005 to 0.05 ppm, and in terrestrial

animals at 0.005 ppm. Tungsten accumulates in heart muscle
and teeth at 0.00025 ppm.

When all of its impurities are removed tungsten is soft
enough to be cut with a saw. It is believed that two Spanish
brothers, Juan Jose and Fausto del Elhuar discovered tungsten
in 1783, extracting it from the mineral known as wolframite.
There is evidence, however, that the element was known before
that time and was called wolfram. Many chemist, particularly
in Germany, still refer to tungsten as wolfram - this explains
the chemical symbol “W.” The more widely accepted name
for the element tungsten was taken from the Swedish words
tung stem, which translates to “heavy stone.”

The main ores of tungsten are wolframite and scheelite;
about 75% of the world’s tungsten deposits are thought to be
in China.

Xe-Xenon is found in ineous rocks at 0.00003 ppm and
in sea water at 0.000052 ppm. Xenon binds to mammalian
hemoglobin and myoglobin producing an anesthetic effect.

Xenon is a heavy “noble” gas with a density that is five
times heavier than air; like the other noble gases it exists as a
monoatomic molecule that has no color, odor or taste.

Xenon was discovered by the English chemist Sir
William Ramsay in 1898 and named by him. He derived the
name for this new element from the Greek word xenos, which
translates to “the strange one.”

In 1962, Neil Barlett, an English chemist working at
the University of British Columbia, made the first noble gas
compound. He combined xenon and platinum hexafluoride
which produced a solid, yellow-orange compound, which
consisted of xenon, platinum and fluorine.

Y-Yttrium is a “heavy” rare earth element found in
igneous rocks at 33 ppm, shale at 18 ppm, sandstone at 9.1
ppm, limestone at 4. 3 ppm, sea water at 0.0003 ppm, soils at
50 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.6 ppm (accumulates in ferns),
marine mammals at 0.01 to 0.2 ppm, and in terrestrial animals
at 0.04 ppm where it is found in mammalian bone, teeth and
liver.

Yttrium is found only in small quantities in the earth’s
crust. Rocks brought back from the moon by the Apollo
astronauts had a surprisingly high level of yttrium. Yttrium
was first identified in 1789 by Johan Gadolin; he named it for
the small Swedish village of Ytterby, near Stockholm. The
greatest quantity of yttrium is extracted from monazite sand.

Yttrium is a silver-gray metal that is reactive enough to
burn spontaneously in air w hen it is cut into tiny pieces.

Yttrium enhances normal cell growth and doubles the
life span of laboratory species. Exposure of pregnant mice to
yttrium leads to rapid placental transfer. Fourteen per cent of
yttrium ingested by the pregnant female can be detected in
the newborn mouse pups.

Yb-Ytterbium is a rare earth element found in igneous
rock at3 ppm, shale at 1.8 ppm, sandstone at 1.3 ppm, limestone
at 0.43 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.0015 ppm, marine animals
at 0.02 ppm, and in terrestrial animals at 0.00012 ppm, where
it accumulates at up to 0.3 ppm in bone, teeth and liver.

The French chemist Jean de Marignac discovered this
element in 1878 as a part of the mineral known as erbia and
was the first “rare earth” element discovered. It was named for
the Swedish village of Ytterby. The main source of ytterbium
is monazite sand which contains all of the rare earths, thorium
and calcium and is found in river sand from India and Brazil
and beach sand from Florida at three hundredths of one per
cent.

Alloys are used in dentistry and to manufacture certain
forms of stainless steel

Zn-Zinc is found in igneous rocks at 70 ppm, shale at
95 ppm, sandstone at 116 ppm, limestone at 20 ppm, fresh
water at 0.01 ppm, sea water at 0.01 ppm, soils at 50 ppm,
marine plants at 6 to 1,500 ppm, terrestrial plants at 100 ppm,
marine animals at 6 to 1,500 ppm, and in terrestrial animals at
160 ppm.

Zinc accumulates in mammalian kidney, prostate, and
eye. Zinc was known to be an essential growth factor for bread
mold more than 100 years ago, essential for rats more than 50
years ago, and essential for humans 20 years ago.

Zinc deficiency produces a wide range of diseases
including congenital birth defects and degenerative diseases
of all age groups.

Congenital Birth Defects Associated with Zinc Deficiency

Cleft lip and cleft palate

Agnathia (small lower jaw)

Polydactyly (extra limbs and digits)

etc.)

===== Screenshot (125) =====
 

Te ee

Hernias (hiatal, diaphragmatic, umbilical, inguinal, etc.)
Heart defects

Lung defects

Urogenital defects (horseshoe kidney, small kidney,
intersex malformations of the male and female genitals)

There are 1.4 to 2.3 grams of zinc in the adult human.
The liver, pancreas, kidney, bone and skeletal muscle have the
greatest reserves of zinc. Lesser amounts are found in the eye,
protate gland, semen, skin, hair, fingernails and toenails.

There are no less than 70 metalloenzymes that
require zinc as a functional cofactor. These metalloenzymes
include carbonic anhydrase, alkaline phosphatase, lactic
dehydrogenase, and carboxypeptidase. Zinc helps to
bind enzymes to substrates by maintaining spatial and
configurational relationships. Some enzymes bind zinc so
tightly that even during severe zinc depletion they can still
function.

Zinc participates in the metabolism of nucleic acids
and the synthesis of proteins. Zinc is also an integral part of
the RNA molecule itself where zinc provides the “metallic
fingers” and participates in cell division and synthesis of DNA.
The DNA-dependent RNA polymerase is a zinc-depndant
enzyme, as is thymidine kinase.

Excesses of dietary copper and iron and high phytate
diets (vegans) will reduce availability of dietary zinc. Heavy
losses of zine occurs in sweat, therefore, unsupplemented
athletes are particularly at high risk for zine deficiency with
the following symptoms such as anorexia nervosa, muscle
weakness, pica and cribbing, etc.

Zr-Zirconium is found in igneous rocks at 165 ppm,
shale at 160 ppm, sandstone at 220 ppm, limestone at 19 ppm,
fresh water at 0.0026 ppm, sea water at 0.000022 ppm, soils at
300 ppm, marine plants at 20 ppm, terrestrial plants at 0.64
ppm, marine animals at 0.1 to 1.0 ppm, and terrestrial animals
at 0.3 ppm.

Zirconium was discovered in 1787 by the German
chemist Martin Heinrich Klaproth. The best known compound
of zirconium is the mineral zircon. This mineral has been
known since ancient times and is even referred to in the Bible.
Zirconium derives its name from zargun, which is the Arabic
name for zircon and translates to “gold color.”

Zircon is a crystalline compound of zirconium silicate
(ZrSiO,). Itis found ina variety of colors, and when the crystal
is cut and polished, the stone is regarded as a semiprecious
gem. Zircon has a very high index of refraction, which means
that it has the property of bending light through large angles.
Because the colorless crystals of zircon have a high brilliance
they are oftentimes used as a diamond substitute in faux

jewelry.
