The Currency of Life

It doesn’t matter whether one is a devote of creationism
or a student of evolution - our immediate and mutual problem
is that the Earth, our planet, our environment is a limited finite
resource for the raw materials that are necessary for the basis
of all plant, animal and human life as we know it.

The sequence of events for the creation of all the
elements of the periodic chart and life on Earth is the same for
Biblical scholars, physicists, anthropologists, archeologists
and geologists - the only real difference in the thinking of
these groups is the time reference between events!

All living organisms procure their raw materials of
essential elements for reproduction, development, growth,
maintenance and longevity from a thin blanket of light, gas,
water and solid matter on or just above the Earth’s crust.

The Earth’s atmosphere, a standard mixture of inert
(nitrogen) and functional gases (oxygen), with the exception
of local or regional pollutants, is universally the same around
the Earth.

All water on Earth, our universal solvent and elixir
of life, with the exception of the diversity of minerals and
other elements in the aqueous solution or suspension, local or
regional pollutants, water itself is universally H,O.

Being found in the lowest crevices of the surface of the
earth, the largest bodies of water on earth are the seas and
oceans. The oceans are catch basins into which chemical and
mineral substances of every kind have been dumped by the
many moving forces in nature since the beginning of time.

Loose, transportable and soluble materials are either
moved directly and swiftly, or indirectly and by varying
degrees, downhill and downward into the sea. Particles
are lifted and transported by the irresistible forces of wind,

watercourse, and glaciers.


Highly soluble substances in soil are dissolved in
water and are transported to the sea where they accumulate.
In this way, “oceans have become a reservoir of accumulated
mineral wealth” which makes the mineral resources of the
land masses insignificant by comparison.

In contrast to the predictably and identical chemical
analysis of the atmosphere’s many gaseous elements and
the simple universal chemical analysis of water, the Earth’s
plethora of mineral elements found on the periodic chart,
are not distributed equally in the blanket over or in the
crust (soil or parent rock) of the Earth. Mineral elements are
found randomly in veins like chocolate in chocolate ripple
ice cream, or like gold and silver.

Theresult of this disproportionate distribution of mineral
elements in the crust of the Earth, there is in fact, a direct cause
and effect of what appears on the surface to the casual observer
to be a random pattern of the optimum location for plant (red
wood trees) and animal populations (kangaroos) on Earth just
as there is a disproportionate distribution of wealth.

Holy accounts of the formation of the Earth and the
organisms that populate the Earth that are accepted by
believers of the Jewish, the Kaleidoscope of Christian faiths
and the Muslim faiths are brief ,however, the details need not
be chronicled for the events to be in fact true any more than
the details not being required when grandma says, “I baked
you a dozen cookies” - you know she selected and ground the
organic whole wheat berries into flour, sifted the flour, added
filtered water, baking powder, salt, honey, yeast culture,
butter, chocolate chips and walnuts; she then dutifully blended
and mixed the basic elements into a cookie dough, she took
a spoonful of the now complete cookie dough and plopped it
onto a greased cookie sheet and baked them in the oven at 375
degrees F for 30 minutes into perfectly wonderful and chewy
chocolate chip-walnut holiday cookies - although grandma
never sat down and shared the details of her activities, we
inherently knew in fact she did them!

===== Screenshot (129) =====
 


The Biblical account of the Earth’s formation (Genesis)

goes like this:

“In the beginning God created the heaven and earth. And
the earth was without form, and void; and darkness was upon
the face of the deep. And the spirit of God moved upon the face
of the water. And God said, Let there be light, that it was good:
and God divided the light from the darkness. And God called the
light day, and the darkness He called night. And the evening
and the morning were the first day. And God said, Let there be a
firmament in the midst of the waters, and let it divide the waters
from the waters. And God made the firmament and divided the
‘waters which were under the firmament from the waters which
were above the firmament; and it was so. And God called the
firmament Heaven. And the evening and the morning were the
‘second day. And God said, Let the waters under the Heaven be
gathered together under one place, and let the dry land appear:
and it was so. And God called the dry land Earth; and the
gathering together of the waters called the seas: and God saw
it was good. And God said, Let the Earth bring forth grass, the
herb yielding seed, and the fruit tree yielding fruit after his kind,
whose seed is in itself, upon the Earth: and it was so.”

Like grandma’s cookies we don’t need to know the
intricate details of how it happened to appreciate the fact that
the Earth or the delicious steaming chocolate chip-walnut
cookies appeared miraculously before us to see, smell and
savor the result of the effort.

The only scientific chemical theory of the origin of
the Earth and its elements was crystallized in 1957 by E. M.
Burbridge. According to his theory, all of the Earth’s elements
can be built up electron by electron from hydrogen gas;
hydrogen atoms in space condense together by gravitational
attraction into clouds of gas, which ultimately condense into

stars and planets.
Burbridge’s theory of the origin of the stars and the


Earth, to say the least is complex, detailed and controversial;
some researchers support his theory of the condensation of
hot gases with the subsequent transmutation of one element
into another while others favor the theory of an accretion of
cold gases. :

The technique of radio-dating puts the Earth’s genesis
or “birth” at 4 to 5 billion years ago. Regardless of which
theory is believed (and it is not an all or nothing choice as the
biblical belief includes all elements of creation and formation),
biblical or the “big bang,” all must agree that the geochemical
fractionation of the Earth’s elements continues even today
although at an almost imperceptible snail’s pace and that the
Earth is still not in a geochemical equilibrium (i.e. - erosion,
earthquakes, tidal waves, volcanoes, etc.).

Radioactive dating is a powerful method of measuring
relative geologic time. It has been employed in the estimate
that the Earth is 4 to 5 billion years of age. However, even
with the radioactive-dating techniques the embryologic stages
of terrestrial development, including the accumulation of the
Earth’s great iron core and the genesis of the relatively light
and movable land masses, have remained obscure and elusive
because numerous events (i.e.- thermal and geochemical) that
can reset the “radioactive clock.”

As land masses float, migrate and relocate across the
Earth’s surface, the elements that reach the ocean’s floor
through erosion from land masses into creeks, streams and
rivers into the ocean floor sink and recycle into the hot interior
mantle and core; when land masses are forced into each other,
mountain ranges are born. Molten elements find their way
through the continental faults, frequently breaking through
the surface and reaching the crust’s surface as volcanic lava.

Three major segregations of the Earth’s elements can
easily be recognized; initially the primal Earth, heated by
the radioactive decay process separated into three onion-like
concentric layers:

We have limited indirect data on the Earth’s core and

===== Screenshot (130) =====
 

 


mantle, which in fact make up most of the Earth’s solid mass;
the Earth’s crust on the other hand has been systematically
and intimately probed, analyzed, gouged and extracted.

The Earth’s crust is cooled and solid, whereas the inner
layers of the mantle and core are in various states of molten
(magma) minerals which are on occasion ejaculated onto the
surface of the planet by volcanoes.

When the Earth’s crust was initially cooling it separated
into a series of minerals, crystallizing into igneous rocks in a
definite and systemic order, the by products being liquid water
and gaseous nitrogen which formed the great oceans and seas
and nitrogen that makes up the majority of our atmosphere.

Over time, wind and water erosion wear off the
mountain’s surface which produces a mineral-rich dust and
silt and sediment which are swept into valleys, forests, plains,
creeks, rivers, lakes and oceans where they eventually are
returned to the Earth’s mantle. Periodic floods historically
enriched the soils of flood plains and “bottom land” with life-giving mineral rich silt that accumulated over time to create
“soil.”

Soil, in its infinite number of forms is the last part
of the geological genesis puzzle - it is basically the product
of climate and the effects of living organisms on rocks; its
physical nature and chemical composition is determined by
the make up of the parent rock in a particular location and by
the natural forces attacking them.

As a rule of thumb, regions with similar climates have
like types of soils, but each type of soil has numerous variations
related to the underlying rock, topography or vegetation.

Soil structure is comprised of three distinct layers:

A) Humus or decomposed plant material - this is the
“organic” layer of the soil (“top soil”); usually the mineral
elements are rapidly lost by the leaching effects of water, rain,
irrigation, “mining” by plant growth (wild and cultivated)
and acid rain.


B) Sand and various accumulations of some of the
mineral elements leached from layer A.

C) The Subsoil, gravel and parent rock.

Soil is a dynamic “living” environment, an ecosystem
unto itself, the health of which we are dependent upon for our
own health and longevity. Frances Moore Lappe said it very
well, “If you eat, you're involved with agriculture.”

Coarse inorganic particles with a diameter greater
than 0.0002 cm contribute from 5 to 90% of the dry weight
of most soils and may be separated into gravel, sand and silt
depending upon particle size and character.

Colloidal inorganic particles have a diameter of less
than 0.0002 cm; they can be separated by mass or size in a
water column. Inorganic colloidal particles usually comprise
less than 10 to 80% of the dry weight of soils and are responsible
for a soils color, texture and ion-exchange properties.

The soil solution, or water held in soil capillaries, which
contains dissolved matter can be extracted by centrifugation
and may compromise as muchas 50% of healthy soil by weight.
The soil solution is important as a media for the propagation
of microorganisms and as an efficient medium for the rapid
growth of plant rootlets.

The soil atmosphere is comprised of various gasses
in equilibrium with the soil; it can be measured by use of a
vacuum pump and may constitute up to 50% of healthy soil
by volume.

The actual organic matter in soil is very difficult to
separate from its colloidal inorganic matter in the soil; it is
usually found as 1.0 to 50% of the dry weight of soil and rarely
up to 90%. Organic matter in the soil primarily originates
from plant debris and dead microorganisms (i.e.- bacteria and
fungi).

The living organisms in the soil are of infinitely greater
numbers and varieties than those species found on the surface

===== Screenshot (131) =====
 


of the soil. Soil organisms for the most part are unicellular,
however, worms can be important in excavating minerals
from sub soils and bring them to the Earth’s upper layers in
their manure or digestive “casts.” ;
Worms also carry plant debris (with their mineral
content) from the surface of the Earth into the humus layer
where microorganisms make up 0.1 to 0.2% of soil by weight.
Soil microorganisms, although small in percentage of
total weight of the soil are indispensable for soil formation,
soil health and for the regulation process of pH (acid balance),
nitrogen fixing (fertilization) and oxidation potentials.

From Dust to Dust

Our Earth is anemic! A potentially apocalyptic mixture
of mining, deforestation, intensive farming, irrigation and
acid rain has shifted, eroded or leached our life giving and
life-sustaining raw materials from the formerly mineral rich
crust of the Earth. ’

Unfortunately, we were given a finite amount of raw
materials (minerals) in the Earth’s crust and without daily
infusions of mineral rich meteors, volcanic eruptions (i.e. - Mt.
St. Helens, eastern Washington; Mt. Pinatubo, Philippines,
etc.), flooding with highly mineralized silt or “rock flour” or
“Glacial Milk”, dust (finely ground rock flour) or wood ash
(plant minerals) we are forced to make do with what we have
- a declining mineral reserve in the soil.

There are those who would propose to re-mineralize
mountain ranges, forests, farms and yes, whole continents to
get minerals into our crops and therefore into our human food
chain; but put into economic perspective these dreams and
proposals are not sustainable. To re-mineralize the Earth is
technologically possible, however, as a realistic economically


sustainable agricultural program, however, it is a non-starter.

To re-mineralize the Earth for one growing season we
would have to grind up the equivalent of Mauna Loa, the
colossal Hawaiian mountain, each year and redistributing the
resultant rock dust several times each year to coincide with
soil preparation and planting programs.

To comprehend the enormity of this task, one has to
appreciate that Mauna Loa is the most massive mountain
in the world and is also the largest volcano; Mauna Loa
rises 13,677 feet above the surface of the Pacific Ocean - an
additional 17,000 feet drops from the water’s surface to the
ocean floor making it technically taller (almost one mile taller)
than Mt. Everest.

Mauna Loa’s total mass is 100 times larger than that
of Mt. St. Helens in Washington State and the lava that forms
the massive dome could cover the land masses of the Earth
with four feet of additional mineral dust. The cost of such a
Herculean effort would result in your hamburger reaching an
eye-popping price of $275 per pound and corn $35 per ear - this
sticker shock would make $5 per gallon gasoline look cheap!

When 23 year old Charles Darwin sailed the H.M.S.
Beagle to the coast of South America in 1833, he recorded a
fine, red dust accumulating on his ship each day and correctly
deduced that the dust originated in West Africa.

This African dust in its westerly flight across the Pacific
Ocean travels all the way to the Amazon and the rainforests
where it is essential to annually replenish the rain leached
mineral poor soils of the rainforests.

Man has always realized the enormity of the great and
tireless power of the Earth’s winds for energy and travel, but
their importance to the biological vitality of the Earth and the
mineral value of our food is far beyond what we have ever
suspected!

There are many wind currents, east to west, that transport
great loads of mineral dust across predictable routes, “linking
ecosystems hundreds and even thousands of miles apart.”

===== Screenshot (132) =====
 


 

Billions of tons of mineral rich dust from the deserts
of Asia and Africa annually fertilize oceans and rainforests
halfway around the world.

Michael Garstang, professor of meteorology, at the
University of Virginia in Charlottesville says, “During the
violent Amazonian rainstorms, the particulates that originate
in African deserts are literally sucked out of the sky.”

Garstang and his colleagues calculate that 13,000,000
tons of African and dust enter the
Amazonian soils each season to enrich and fertilize the rain
leached and mineral depleted tropical soils!

The wind-borne African dust adds
critical minerals to help maintain and support the rainforest’s
survival and productivity.

“While the Amazon Basin teems with life the soil itself
lacks reserves of nutrients, especially phosphates, which spur
plant growth.

The historical record shows that the Amazon Rainforest
has periodically shrunk to a fraction of its present size, then
bounded back again, and the researchers now believe that it
expands and contracts as the supply of nourishing African dust
waxes and wanes with mirror changes in the Saharan Desert.”

essential and

The principal minerals found in the coarse inorganic
fraction of soil are directly related to those minerals present
in the local parent rock.

Minerals that are resistant to weathering (i.e.- quartz)
are especially abundant in soils while minerals that weather
quickly are rare or absent. It is therefore possible to calculate
the relative age or maturity of a soil covering igneous rocks
from its content of primary minerals (i.e. - feldspar, mica,
amphiboles and pyroxenes); these relatively soluble minerals
are common in young soil and less abundant in mature soils
and absent from senescent soils. Soils covering sandstone
often contain 90 to 99% quartz; those soils overlying limestone
are made up of as much as 80% calcium carbonate.

It is obvious that minerals are not equally distributed


ees?”

in soils. The scary part is that fields where our food crops are
grown do not contain a uniform blanket of minerals, if they
contain any nutritional minerals at all, they occur in veins
much like the chocolate swirls found in chocolate ripple ice
cream - at best depending solely on getting minerals from
food grown in soil is an extremely risky crap shoot!

Trace Elements

Trace elements are defined as those elements present
in soils in amounts of less than 0.1% by weight. Sixty percent
of all trace minerals are found in the coarse inorganic fraction

of the soil including Ba, Be, Co, Cr, Cu, Ga, La, Li, Mn, Mo, Ni,
Pb, Rb, Se, Sr, V, Y and Zr.

Rare Earths

The term, rare earths, refers to any of a large series of
very similar oxides of metals obtained from widely distributed
but relatively scarce minerals. The Rare Earth metals are
often divided into three groups, according to the method of
separating them:

1) the cerium metals

(lanthanum, cerium, praseodymium,
neodymium, illinium, samarium).

2) the terbium metals

(europium, gadolinium, terbium).

===== Screenshot (133) =====
3) the yttrium or ytterbium metals

(dysprosium, holmium, erbium, thulium,
yttrium, ytterbium, lutetium).

Bervllium, hafnium, scandium, thorium and zirconium
are also included as Rare Earths by some geochemists.

Rare Earths added to laboratory animal's diets as a
supplement to the well recognized major and trace minerals
have doubled the expected life span of several different
species. !
These Rare Earths, known to be so vital to animal
health and longevity (and most probably our human health
and longevity) are no longer found in our food supply in any
consistent or significantamounts - the only warranty toadequate
Rare Earth intake for we humans is daily supplementation!

Basic Soils

Basic soils tend to be rich in minerals whose ionic
radius is close to that of magnesium, i.e - Co, Cr, Cu, Fe, Mg,
Ni, Se, Ti, V and Zn.

Acid Soils

Acidic soils containing feldspar that have ionic radius’
close to silica, calcium or potassium, i.e. - Al, Ba, Ca, Cs, Hf,
K, La, Na, Nb, Pb, Rb, Sr, Ta, Tl, Y and Zr.

Limestone soils are rich in elements which form
insoluble carbonates, i.e. - Ba, Ca, Mg, Mn, Pb and Sr.

Colloidal inorganic particles are referred to as clay;

 


clay minerals are primarily aluminosilicates, many of which
swell in water and act as ion exchangers. The hydrous (water)
oxides of aluminum, iron and manganese are responsible for
the natural colors of most clays, i. e. - blue, gray, red, orange,
black; they also function as scavengers for phosphate and
some trace minerals and Rare Earths.

Manufactured Soil

A common denominator for all of the “old ones”
in the “Blue Zones” of the world is the fastidious attention
they devote to the soil that their vegetable gardens, orchards
and grains are grown in. In all instances this means the
daily injection of mineral nutrients (glacial milk, wood ash,
peat ash, rice straw ash, kelp ash, charcoal, etc.) and organic
soil conditioners (kitchen wastes, manure and agricultural
byproducts) into ancient growing sites that in some cases are
thousands of years old.

In many cases, the successful formula to their longevity,
supporting crops is a serendipitous mix of location, raw
materials available and political events.

Terra Mare & Terra Preta

Roman culture has been thoroughly investigated
and written about by cultural and anthropological scholars.
The enormous Roman contribution to European civilization
is obvious and agreed to by all who are familiar with the
languages that developed over almost half of the European
continent; the letters of the modern alphabet, the grand and
massive architecture of our governmental buildings, our

===== Screenshot (134) =====
 


practice and steadfast devotion to the letter of the law and
legal procedure, our republic style of governmental systems
of checks and balances and the legend of Rome as a world
state and the messenger and implementer of universal peace.

The Romans, themselves, first appear in history as
a people who were traders and farmers who settled on a
collection of low hills on the left bank of the Tiber River about
15 miles from its mouth.

The original settlement became a bustling trading
center, and by 753 B.C. it could be considered a small city-state governed by a king.

All of the romantic stories of Rome overshadowed the
fact that until well after Rome had ascended to the status of
a world class political power, Romans, were in fact simple
barbarians.

Even Pyrrhus, the Epirote, who encountered the
Romans almost 100 years after the time of Alexander the
Great, regarded them as barbarians and seemed surprised
that they employed what was an advanced form of tactical
thinking in regards to their military operations.

The Roman’s conquest of the Greek cities of southern Italy,
and their amazing and rapid expansion which established
their power over the eastern Mediterranean in the short space
of 50 years, surely was regarded by the “civilized populations”

of the region as a feat equal to a Gaulish invasion!

Culturally, Italy lagged behind the Aegean countries
throughout most of its history. The first Neolithic populations
of Italy came from the east, either in boats by sea or by
following and hugging the bountiful coastline, and were part
of the Mediterranean phalanx of the Neolithic colonization of
Europe.

The culture and practices of these original settlers
were relatively simple, and there were no significant cultural
advances after they set up house keeping in Italy.

The Neolithic period in Italy was also considerably
shorter than it was in northern Europe, as Italy provided a


considerable supply of mineral wealth, which attracted foreign
miners and salt traders. Merchants from the Aegean and Beaker
Folk from Spain had reached Italy by 1200 B.C., however, they
seemed to have had little influence on the local people. ;

By 1500 B.C., Bronze Age invaders occupied the Po
Valley. These people originated in central Europe (Hungary),
and are thought to have spoken an Indo-European language.
They constructed fortified villages by which they overpowered
and eventually absorbed the older Neolithic population. Their
villages were occupied for greater periods of time and were
characterized by mounds of a gray, greasy soil called terra
mare, which the Italian peasants constantly dug and spread
on their fields as fertilizer.

Terra mare was a mix of wood ash, charcoal and
organic compost consisting of manure, plant debris, kitchen
waste and the organic byproducts of daily living.

According to Peter Stahl, an anthropologist from
the State University of New York, Binghamton, many
investigators believe that, “what the ecoimagery would like
to picture as a pristine, untouched Urelt primeval world, in
fact has been managed by people for millennia.” The phrase
“built environment,” Erickson argued, “applies to most, if not
all, Neotropical landscapes.”

According to Wim Sombroek, author of Amazon Soils
(1966), “Every Ecology 101 student knows that Amazonian
soils are fragile and impoverished. If farmers cut down the
canopy of trees overhead to clear cropland, they expose the
earth to the pummeling rain and sun, which quickly wash
away its small store of minerals and nutrients and bake what
remains into something resembling brick - a ‘wet desert,’ as
these ruined areas are sometimes called.” The certainty of
destroying the land, environmentalists claim, makes large-scale corporate agriculture impossible in the rainforest. What
Sombroek observed was a big surprise - large patches of “terra
preta do indio (Indian black earth).

Amazonian Indians literally “created the ground

===== Screenshot (135) =====
 


beneath their feet.” According to Susana Hecht, a geographer
from the University of California, at Los Angeles, investigators
of upland Amazonia took most of their soil samples along
the regions modern highways, which indeed passed through
areas with lifeless soil. These original samplings gave a
skewed view of Amazonian soil nutritional values, however,
a few investigators found zones of something recognizably
different and considerably more fertile, “in part, because of
the original empty-Amazon model.”

Beginning a little more than 2,000 years ago, the central
and lower Amazon, were disrupted by violent cultural change.
Azawak-speaking peoples migrated into the area from the
south and west, sometimes apparently driving Tupi-speaking
groups north and east. Sedentary villages were established
and the production of terra preta appeared to begin in earnest
at the same time.

Terra preta exists in two forms: terra preta itself, a
black soil thick with fragments of pottery, and terra mulata,
a lighter dark brown soil with much less pottery. A number
of researchers believe that although Indians made both, they
deliberately created only the terra mulata.

Terra preta is blacker than the blackest coffee and the
man made soil is filled with fragmented and broken pre-Columbian pottery. According to Charles Mann, a National
Geographic reporter (2008), “It was as if the (Amazon) River's
firstinhabitants had throwna huge, rowdy fratparty, smashing
every plate in sight, (and) then buried the evidence.”

Terra preta was the soil created directly around homes
by charcoal (wood ash) kitchen fires and organic refuse of
various types. Just before the time of Christ (360 B.C.), the
central Amazon had numerous large settled villages when
and where terra preta manufacturing began.

Terra preta is rich in essential minerals, including
phosphorus, calcium, zine and manganese, whichare typically
missing in tropical soils.

Earlier inhabitants left no trace of terra preta; the new


ee ee
villages quickly developed thick deposits of “Indian black
earth.”

‘ Hecht, believed that, these fertile sites were originally
_Seen as anomalous and insignificant.” However, in the 1 990s,
investigators began studying these unusual sites of “terra preta
do Indio” - rich, fertile “Indian dark earth’ that anthropologists
are convinced were created by early humans. 3

Throughout Amazonia, farmer's prize terra preta for
its enormous power to stimulate productivity; some have
worked it for as many as 20 years with minimal need for
additional fertilizer. The ceramic fragments found in terra
preta indicate that the manufactured “soil” retained its
nutrients for a millennium.

Terra preta is sold in great quantities by merchants as
special growing “soil” for gardens. Woods has estimated that
manufactured terra preta might represent as much as 10% of
the total usable soil in the Amazon Basin, an area the size of
France! A recent, much more conservative, estimate is that
terra preta covers only 0.1 to 0.3% of the Amazon basin, a
few thousand square miles - a few thousand square miles of
manufactured farm land was sufficient to feed the millions of
people in the Mayan heartland!

Most large terra preta sites of manufactured soil are
found on the low bluffs at the edge of the floodplain. Typically,
they cover five to fifteen acres, however, some encom pass more
than seven hundred acres. The layer of manufactured black soil
is typically one to two feet deep, but can be as thick as six feet.

According to a study by Dirse Kern, of the Museu
Goeldi in Belem, terra preta is “not associated with a particular
soil type or environmental condition,” indicating clearly, that
it was not produced by natural events.

Another clue to the human origin of terra preta is
the broken ceramics with which it is usually mixed. “They
practiced agriculture here for centuries,” said Glaser. “But
instead of destroying the soil, they improved it, and that is
something we don’t know how to do today” in tropical soils.

===== Screenshot (136) =====
 


Typically, terra preta has more “plant-available”
phosphorus, calcium, sulfur, nitrogen and trace minerals than
is common in the rainforest; it also has much more organic
matter, better retains moisture and nutrients, and is not
rapidly exhausted by agricultural use when managed well.
[he key to terra preta’s long-term fertility, Glaser says, “is
charcoal (wood ash): terra preta contains up to 64 times more
of the wood ash than the surrounding red earth.”

Organic matter “sticks” or “adsorbs” to charcoal, rather
than being washed away or attaching to other non-available
compounds. “Over time, it partly oxidizes, which keeps
providing sites for nutrients to bind to.” But simply mixing
charcoal into the ground is not enough to create terra preta.

Glaser argues, “I ligh nutrient inputs - excrements and
waste such as turtle, fish and animal bones - are necessary.”
Special soil microorganisms are also likely to play a role in its
persistent fertility, in the view of Janice Thies, a soil ecologist,
who is part of a Cornell University team studying terra preta.
“There are indications that microbial biomass is higher in
terra preta than in other forest soils.”

Despite the presence of charcoal, terra preta is not
a byproduct of simple slash-and-burn agriculture. Slash-and-burn does not produce enough charcoal (wood ash) to
manufacture terra preta - the carbon mostly is released into
the air in the form of carbon dioxide, instead Indians appear
to have made terra preta by a process that Christoph Steiner,
a University of Bayreuh soil scientist, has dubbed “slash-and-char.” Instead of completely burning organic matter to ash,
ancient farmers burned it incompletely to make charcoal,

then, stirred the charcoal or wood ash back into the soil.

One must remember that pure“ woodash” isnot carbon,
but rather plant minerals as the carbon of the wood was burnt
or released as carbon dioxide into the atmostphere.

Indians, yet today, are still making terra preta in this
way, according to Hecht, the UCLA geographer. Hecht spent
years with the Kayapo’, in Central Amazonia, watching them


create “low biomass” fires “cool enough to walk through” of
pulled weeds, cooking wastes, crop debris, palm fronds and
termite mounds. Hecht wrote, “To live among the Kayapo, is
to live in a place where parts of the landscape smolder.” >

In an early test at recreating terra preta, Steiner
Wenceslau Teixeira of the Brazilian Agricultural Research
Enterprise, and Wolfgang Zech of the University of Bayreuh
applied a variety of treatments involving charcoal and
fertilizers for three years to rice and sorghum plots outside
Manaus. Inthe first year, there was littlerecognizable difference
amongst test and control plots (except that in the control plots
nothing grew). By the second year, Steiner declared, “the
charcoal really made a difference.” Plots with charcoal alone
grew little, but those treated with a combination of charcoal
and natural fertilizers yielded as much as an 880% increase in
yield than with fertilizer alone!

Human Aging: fire power

Fire is far older than man. In the beginning fire was
flung into the forests and grasslands by volcanic eruptions,
lightening strikes, spontaneous combustion ignited by the
friction of dry branches rubbing against each other and heat
generated by decomposing plant material.

These original untamed wildfires produced a mineral-rich plant ash on the crust of the earth that impregnated the
primitive “soil.” The process mixed and kneaded the ash, a
fine particle sized mineral rich nutrient mix, into the deep
accumulated organic mulch that had built up over the years,
decades, centuries and millennia.

The human is one of the most efficient converters of
the “solar income” found in food into mechanical work or
movement. Humans need only 50% of the calories required
by a horse to exert the same level of physical energy. Human
metabolism is very efficient!

Anthropologists theorize that our large brains are
the result of a constant stream of high protein, high fat food
supplies, “as we ate more meat our brain got larger and it
allowed us to figure out how to get more meat.”

Our larger and more functional brains allowed us to
develop other methods to tap into the seemingly endless
reserves of “solar income” trapped by plants - fire!

By burning plants that we couldn't eat, including trees
and underbrush, humans and their cultures quickly stretched
far beyond the creative limits and immediate needs of their
digestive system and their metabolic processes.

The ability to tame or kindle a fire at will catapulted
humans farther than our earthly competitors - humans controlled
fire, something that no other species would ever do!

Fire, as a tool, separated humans from other tool-using
animals - animals metabolically used the sun’s solar income

===== Screenshot (138) =====
 


by eating plants and other animals, they could use sticks,
rocks, fiber, mud and water - only man had fire! Man tamed
fire and employed it as a tool for many thousands of years
before he learned how to start and kindle fire at will.

Fire, the new technique of tapping into stored energy or
“solar income” protected us from the otherwise uninhabitable
frozen environment of the Ice Ages and allowed us to control
the temperature of our microenvironments or dwellings.
Humans were no longer controlled by the environment - we
had fire power!

Early human remains from nine individuals that were
discovered in an Ethiopian fossil cache were dated back to
43 million years ago. These prehistoric humans, Ardipithecus
ramidus, were surrounded by a collection of the oldest dated
stone tools. Plant and animal fossils identified at the same site
revealed that these very early humans lived and flourished in
a low lying woodland and grassland heavily populated with
monkeys, mole rats and wild cattle.

Anthropologist had long believed that primitive man
“tamed” fire some 500,000 years ago; however, a recent study
of burnt antelope bones from caves in Swartkrans, South
Africa, show that early man, Australopithicus robustus and
Homo erectus, had built camp fires and had cooked meat on
these fires about 1.5 million years ago - about one million
years earlier than had been previously thought.

The cooked antelope bones were discovered and
described in 1988 by Bob Brain, a South African paleontologist
from the University of Cape Town. Brain originally theorized
the bones had been cooked by humans because the degree of
charring indicated temperatures of more than 1,100 degrees
F - heat levels that could have only be achieved by a human
campfire as heat produced by a grass fire does not exceed 700
degrees F.

Many technologically ignorant humans, including
Homo erectus (Java Man some 250,000 years ago), found that
starting fire from raw cold fuel was a difficult and frustrating


job. Early on, when fire was originally “captured,” it was fed
and maintained for as long as possible, an ancient technique
which is still practiced in many areas of the world today.

Fire can easily be maintained and transported by the use
of wet wood, lichens, dry fennel stalks, etc. In some cultures
hearth fires are maintained for generation after generation
oftentimes for the life of the dwelling. ,
The first evidence we have for fire starting by humans
is associated with the early Paleolithic Period of Europe
approximately dated at 25,000 to 30,000 B.C. Flints in the
classic “strike-a-light” hand held shape and chunks of iron
pyrite were discovered together in charcoal fire beds.

Iron pyrite (fool’s gold) is only found in very few
places on earth, therefore most early fire starting methods fall
into the category of the fire drill (a bow used to spin an arrow
shaft that is inserted into a baseboard), fire saw and fire plow
(a pointed stick that is rapidly pushed back and forth in a
groove created in a log or board). Humans eventually learned
that they could start fire at will with less work by generating
sparks that were directed into fine dry grass “tinder.”

With fire, humans were able to smelt metal, make
tools, “fire” pottery, bake bricks, produce weapons, keep
themselves warm, cook and eat food that was otherwise
inedible (grains), keep predatory animals at bay, destroy
our enemies, develop specialties and unwittingly, we
humans, with the wood ash (plant mineral) by-product that
was generated by fire, produced the mineral fertilizer and
mineral supplements (“culinary” ashes), that would nurture
and sustain we humans for thousands of years to come!

Early humans who had not yet learned to put their
wood ashes from their heating and cooking fires into primitive
gardens lived a life that was short, hard, miserable, filthy,
fearful, brutish and violent. They were often on the verge of
starvation.

They subsisted primarily in family units or family
bands. During their waking day, they were driven by an

===== Screenshot (139) =====
 

N
Jt
Nm


eternal, internal, intrinsic and relentless salt and mineral
hunger (pica) as were all living creatures. The hunger for
salt and minerals didn’t begin with the appearance of potato
chips, these behaviors began with the appearance of the first
animals and the first humans.

One isolated group of tiny people, dubbed the “Hobbit,”
Homo floresiensis, existed on the Indonesian island of Flores,
370 miles east of Java. They were the size of a Homo sapiens
pygmy or midget only three feet tall with the body of a three
vear old, however, their brain was smaller than a grapefruit
~ a small human with a brain that was smaller than the brain
of a chimpanzee.

There is some evidence that these tiny, tribal and
carnivorous humans accepted gourds filled with food that the
modern human Floresians offered to them, until the 1500s,
when Dutch traders arrived.

The “Hobbit” were clever enough to employ fire, make
tools (points, blades, awls and tiny barbs) and hunt - cooking
fire charred bones of the Stegodon (dwarf elephant), Komodo
Dragon (dinosaur), a wide variety of rodents and other animal
species were collected from garbage dumps.

Early skeletons of the south Pacific “Hobbit” were
dated as far back as 95,000 years ago, the more recent ones
were dated at 13,000 - 18,000 years; however, the primitive
“Hobbit” crafted stone tools dating back to 800,000 B.C.

The island of Flores was populated by hoards of an
extinct dwarf elephant ((Stegodon), monster rats and giant
lizards (including the Komodo Dragon), all of which were the
primary food of the “I lobbit.”

There is no evidence that the “Hobbit” learned to use
the ashes from their heating and cooking fires to put in their
food, salt or gardens - they disappeared leaving only a small
fossil record. Only six skeletons have been found, suggesting
the Hobbit skeletons were minerally poor and their skeletons
simply didn’t survive the ravages of the millennia.

The small stature of the Hobbit demonstrates very


clearly that humans, like other life forms are not exempt from
natural laws, in this case the “island rule.” The island rule
states that “a combination of selective forces - geographic
and temporal isolation, scarce resources and the absence of
predators - will occasionally result in the development of giant
and dwarf species.” The Hobbit simply followed the same
historical course as the isolated desert Bushman of Southern
Africa.

‘ Humans were territorial, they hunted and gathered
in naturally productive and fruitful areas that had large
quantities of small game, edible insects, their favorite plants
and either salt or clay deposits for a source of minerals, a
virtual Garden of Eden. There were endless fights and mini-wars between competing family bands and tribes over access
to these flora and fauna rich Edens.

Their offspring had a very low survival rate, and those
that did survive were often deformed with congenital birth
defects (Down's Syndrome, heart defects, cleft palate, cleft
lip, spina bifida, muscular dystrophy, clubbed hands and feet,
etc.) caused by pervasive maternal nutritional deficiencies.

Injuries took their toll; fractures healed all askew or
didn’t heal at all, leaving the victim to remain deformed and
dependant upon the good graces and surpluses of the rest
of the band. If the injuries were catastrophic, the victim was
oftentimes eaten by his fellows. Revered relatives were often
eaten when they died so as to acquire and perpetuate their
spirit (mortuary cannibalism).

On the other hand, those humans who learned to put
wood, peat or kelp ashes from their heating and cooking fires
into their food and primitive gardens, were taller, their bones
stronger, they were more fertile, had lower rates of infant
mortality and they lived longer!

Harder mineralized skeletons resulted in their
bones surviving the ravages of rodents and scavengers,
decomposition and various destructive forces of the millennia

- full skeletons were now surviving into the 21st century.

===== Screenshot (140) =====
 


 

Between 200,000 and 40,000 years ago, Neanderthals
dominated the landscape of western Asia and Europe.
Archeologists and anthropologists have found over 10,000
complete skeletons of these ancient peoples. They were the
first humans to bury their dead and care for their sick and
infirm. Their stone tools, however, were still crude and lacked
the standard shapes that would indicate that they were
designed for a specific function.

African fossilized skeletons from 100,000 years ago
had more modern skeletal features (humans continued to put
the ash byproduct of heating and cooking fires into their food
and gardens) than did their Neanderthal counterparts - they
did, however, make the same crude stone tools.

The bone evidence of their food prey indicated that
their hunting skills were minimal and they only killed and
ate small safely killed animals, including rats and rabbits.
They had not yet reached the capability of killing pigs, buffalo
and larger more dangerous species such as the elephant
(mastodon, mammoth and the dwarf Stegodon). They could
not yet catch fish - their living sites on the sea coasts and lake
shores are devoid of fish hooks and fish bones.

Human developmental history (including physical
emotional, spiritual and creative) appears to have found
its footing, took hold and “gained traction” about 50,000
years ago marking a time that Dr. Jared Diamond calls the
“Great Leap Forward.” The first real signals of this human
leap forward include an increase in height and the universal
appearance of standardized tools and jewelry.

Our Food is Anemic

Marked human development occurred about 50,000
years ago in southwestern Europe, where numerous artifacts
are associated with the skeletons of Cro-Magnons. Soon
after, the garbage dumps unearthed at archeological sites
become more and more complex, pointing to biologically and
behaviorally modern humans.

Cro-Magnon garbage heaps yielded stone tools, bone
tools, and bone fish hooks. Tools were produced in diverse
and distinctive shapes so modern that their functions as
needles, awls and engraving tools are straight forward and
easily identified. Tools became more complex, moving beyond
simple hand held scrapers to multi-piece tools (stone axes)
and weapons such as harpoons, spear throwers and bows and
arrows.

These new weapons allowed the human hunter to
kill larger and more dangerous game from a safe distance.
The creation of ropes and thread for nets, twine and string
permitted the invention of nets, lines and snares which
allowed for the addition of small mammals, fish and birds
to our human diet, which then promoted the expansion of
human habitable territories.

Rubble fromhousesand scraps fromsewnclothing point
to an improved capacity to survive freezing environments.
Modern jewelry and tenderly buried skeletons showed a new
aesthetic and spiritual progression for humans - humans were
now talking to God!

Of all the Cro-Magnon creations that have been
preserved, the most remarkable is their artwork, including their
famous cave paintings, statues and musical instruments.

The impact of the life-sized painted bulls and horses
in the Lascaux Cave of southwestern France or the rhino, lion,
horses, mammoth, panther, owl, bear and buffalo paintings of

the limestone Chauvet grotto in southern France on a visitor
is dramatic - you realize these ancient people were modern,
not only in their skeletons but also in their abstract minds!

The last Chauvet artist produced a painting between
24,000 and 32,000 years ago. The artists work was “signed”
with a negative print of his hand, which was produced by
blowing red ochre onto a non-arthritic hand that was held
palm down against the cave wall. Other artists also signed
their work with a positive print of their hand by putting the
ochre on their hand and then placing their palm print on their
painting.

No human bones have been found in either of the
caves, however, the Chauvet grotto has been a treasure trove
of bones from 14 different animal species including rodents,
reptiles, birds and bears. The bear bones dominate - they have
recovered 3,700 of them.

It is important to note that there were no grains or
fruit seeds found in the caves, clearly indicating that the Cro-Magnon artists were primarily hunters and carnivores and
that hunting dominated their lives.

At 40,000 years ago, Cro-Magnons “moved into the
neighborhood” of Europe with their modern skeletons
(signaling the use of the ash by-product of their heating
and cooking fires as a supplement mixed with salt to extend
and cut the more valued salt and directly into their food as
“culinary ash”), tools and more clever weapons.

Classical anthropologists believe that the Cro-Magnons
used their advanced technology and superior weapons to
kill, infect or displace the Neanderthals - a theorized event
that left little or nothing behind in the form of evidence of
hybridization between the Neanderthals and Cro-Magnons
or a straight forward and ruthless genocide.

We don’t believe the Cro-Magnons fought, captured,
enslaved or slaughtered the Neanderthals, because they
would have eaten them and left their charred, cracked and
scraped bones in their garbage heaps with the rest of the

===== Screenshot (142) =====
 


 

debris of hunting, butchering and cooking. They would have
created art work that depicted the great heroic battles, heroes,
the slain Neanderthals and victories.

We believe the Neanderthals just failed to learn and
employ a basic surviv al technique that the Cro- Magnons had
learned, which caused them to be “edged out” in a similar
fashion as the prehistoric Bushmen allowed themselves to be

“edged out” of the isolated and idyllic Cape of South Africa
and chased into the Kalahari Desert by the more advanced
gun toting Dutch explorers from the Dute h East Indies Tea
Company in 1462; and in a similar fashion as the island of

Flores “Hobbit” allowed themselves to be “edged out” by the
16th century Dutch.
Neanderthals were primarily hunters and_ they

developed the nutritional deficiencies of pure carnivores who
had failed to develop the habit of using wood, peat or kelp ashes
to mix with salt and season food and/or failed to put the ashes
into their primitive or crude “gardens.” One bad hunting year
or a drought covering several years could have significantly
diminished their numbers or totally wiped them out.

The number of humans surviving to “old age” (at least
double the age of reproductive maturity) quadrupled at about
30,000 years ago - something equally as dramatic as the taming
of fire happe ned here which we will cover in due course.

Anthropologists looked at 750 fossilized dental and jaw
fragments of early humans spanning over millions of years,
from the Australopithecines to Neanderthals and early modern
humans. Old age was defined as at least double the age which
reproductive maturity was reached, which is also the point
when the third molars usually erupt in the teen years.

In calculating the old-to-young hominids,
Rachel Caspari of the University of Mic higan, at Ann Arbor
and Sang-Hee Lee of the University of California, at Riverside
found a trend of increased longevity across the human family.
With “modern” adults finally outnumbered
younger ones for the first time at the 30,000 year mark.

ratio of

humans, older


The leap in longevity is thought to have been critical to
the development of modern human culture, as elders passed
down knowledge and helped to weld together complex
societies.

This “hand-me-down’” knowledge, included, how to
cook and preserve meat, how to use wood ashes to extend
and cut what they perceived as the more valuable salt with
wood ashes and to employ the use of “culinary” ashes in the
preparation of certain foods and the value of putting wood
ash into their primitive gardens.

The results and benefits of incorporating wood
ashes (plant minerals) into their food, either directly as salt
extenders, or “culinary ashes” or indirectly as a garden
fertilizer produced stronger, taller, healthier, more fertile,
more creative and longer liver humans - we had not yet left
the main road and humans were in fact accumulating good
habits, becoming healthier, more fertile, living longer and
honing better survival skills.

Pica, Salt Hunger, and Basic Drive

The universal human drive to survive was overtly and
gainfully occupied by a search for salt and minerals. Killing
and eating small animals and insects almost daily and eating
the occasional and highly seasonal plant foods (grass seeds,
etc.) were just a means to the end.

“Food” as we think of it (meat, fish, birds, insects,
cheese, seeds, nuts, fruit, etc.) kept man alive while he was
searching for the more critical salt and minerals.

“Pica” is a term that describes a seeking, a craving
behavior dominated by licking and chewing frenzies, that
have their genesis in mineral deficiencies - interesting
enough neither vitamin deficiencies, protein deficiencies
or calorie deficiency initiates this “pica” behavior, nor will
supplementing vitamins or eating sugar, carbohydrate, fat or
protein quench it!

Historically, the consumption of salt to satisfy a pica
behavior was of value because salt was not processed and did
often times contain trace minerals and rare earths.

Today, salt consumption, although contrary to popular
belief, is notin and of itself harmful; it does present the problem
of allowing our bodies to think we are getting minerals (the
equivalent of the “empty calorie diets,” i.e. - processed food
calories without vitamins).

Farmers and husbandrymen use the salt hunger of
animals to ensure the consumption of trace minerals by
incorporating trace minerals into salt blocks containing 85%
salt - anything less than 85% sodium chloride in a salt block
will be ignored by the animals even if they have major mineral
deficiencies!

“Cribbing” is a name given to a particular form of
pica in domestic animals. A good husbandryman knows
when a horse or cow “cribs” (chews or gnaws on a corn

===== Screenshot (144) =====
 

ee

crib, wooden feed box, fence, stall rail, hitching post or barn
door), the animal really has a craving for minerals. Mineral
starved animals, when first presented with minerals, will at
first eat large amounts of supplemental minerals until they
are satisfied, then they will automatically reduce their level of
consumption to a maintenance level.

“Salt appetite” is very striking in both pregnant animals
and pregnant humans. Bizarre cravings are legendary in the
pregnant human. From antiquity, the description of cravings
or “pica” in humans records its major incidence to pregnant
women. The Hawaiian King Kamehameha’s mother, Queen
Kekuiapoiwa had cravings for eyeballs, althoughshe specifically
wanted chief's eyes, she was given the eyes of sharks to eat.

We have seen a hundred pregnant sheep in Montana
lined up along anembankment eating clay. This is a form of pica
known as “geophagia.” Earth eating, or geophagia, is a very
common behavior in minerally deficient pregnant women.

Pica, or perverted appetite or irresistible cravings with
the eating of bizarre or unsuitable nonfood substances (or
fattening nonfoods or snack foods) has been a curiosity for
philosophers, doctors and priests for thousands of years.

Pica as a symptom of mineral deficiencies is universal
in the world and has been reported in animals and humans of
all age groups. In modern times the easy availability of candy,
sugared-high calorie snacks, soft drinks and junk foods has
led to obesity when people feel the deep cravings of pica.

A doctoral thesis on pica by Agustus Fridericus
Mergiletus (1701) begins with the definition of pica: “The
malady pica is called “kitta” in Greek. Its derivation comes
from the name of the common magpie because the bird (“ pica”
is the Latin Name for “magpie” ) itself is believed to suffer from
some malady ....because it flits from tree to tree constantly
seeking food or because it enjoys all sorts of foods.”

A catalogue of bizarre instances of pica is found in
Mergiletus’ thesis. In men, he recorded one individual who
ate leather, wood, nestlings, live mice; a second consumed


 

woolen garments, leather, a live cat, and some mice; a third
ate cats’ tails and decomposed human bodies with maggots!

In Mergiletus’ female subjects, he recorded women
who ate human flesh including one particularly horrible lady
- “she lured children with sweets, killed them and pickled
them for her daily fare (sounds like a female version of Jeffrey
Dahmer). The murders were only discovered when the
women’s cat stole a pickled hand of a child and carried it over
to the neighbor's house.”

The most common description of pica by Mergiletus
were of women’s desire for mud and mortar scrapped from
the walls (just like modern children who eat caulking and lead
paint - we have often said that children who eat lead paint are
screaming for minerals for their mineral starved bodies - give
them minerals and they won’t eat lead paint); girls who ate
their own hair; girls who ate cotton, thread from their own
clothes, raw grain and lizards.

Mergiletus also described pica in animals including
cats that ate wood ash and pregnant hunting dogs that ate
unusual objects.

Cooper (1957), inher classic report on pica, refers to several
ancient and medieval writers who emphasized the occurrence
of pica in pregnant women, i.e. - Aetios noted pregnant women
to crave various odd foods, some salty and some acid; some
he said “crave for sand, oyster shells and wood ashes.” Aetios
recommended a diet including “fruits, green vegetables, pig’s
feet, fresh fish and old tawny fragrant wine.”

Boezo (1638) noted that pica occurred most often in
pregnant women. Boezo saw pica as a physiological problem,
and is the first to mention iron preparations as a treatment for
pica - he suggested, “one and one half scruples of iron dross taken
for many days as wonderfully beneficial for men and women.”

Boezo also noted, “The case of a virgin who was
accustomed to devour salt in great quantities from which
chronic behavior she developed diarrhea and wasting
(Addison’s disease?).”

===== Screenshot (145) =====
 


 

Christiani of Frankfurt (1691) reported a woman who
ate 1400 salt herrings during her pregnancy. LeConte (1846)
suggested that animals eating earth do so because of “ want of
inorganic elements.”

In modern times, the substances frequently reported as
eaten as a result of pica behavior in humans includes paper,
metallic gum wrappers, ice, dirt, coal, clay, corn starch, chalk,
baking powder, pebbles, wood, wood ashes, plaster, paint,
chimney soot, hair, human and animal feces (kitty litter box)
and cloth.

Because of social constraints on our public behavior,
most people under public scrutiny who display pica eat
sugar, salt, snack food, soft drinks when they crave minerals
- many minerally deficient individuals smoke, drink alcohol
or use street (or prescription) drugs in an attempt to satisfy a
nagging underlying craving.

Ice eating (pagophagia) is also common, especially for
obese iron deficient children and adults.

Worms have been reported as a cause of pica -
ankylostomiasis, ascariasis, oxyuriasis and others have been
blamed. Parasites of course can cause anemia and malnutrition
by direct blood sucking from the host or by competing for
food in the gut. The resulting anemia is in fact perceived by
the body as an iron deficiency resulting in pica.

Pica is more frequently seen in the poor and the obese;
however, David Livingston observed that “both slaves and
the gentry developed pica in Africa.” In Africa, pica is viewed
by tribal peoples as a magical superstitious behavior.

Henrock (1831) attributed pica to “paucity of good
blood and lack of proper nutrition.” Waller (1874) reported that
David Livingston observed many cases of clay and earth eating
(geophagia), a frequent form of pica in pregnant women.

Orr and Gilka (1931) and de Castro (1952) recognized
that “edible Earths might be rich in sodium, iron and
calcium.” Gelford (1945) reported that pica was common in
Kenya amongst African tribes (Kikuyu) living mainly on a


 

vegetarian diet; but absent in those people eating diets rich in
animal flesh, blood and bones (Massai).

Richter (1955) reported Kirkam and Birch’s analysis of
20 samples of edible clay from Africa:

Silica 47.76

Potassium oxide 2.88
Iron oxide 4.98
Manganese oxide 0.32
Calcium oxide 2.97
Magnesium oxide 1.08
Phosphorous pentoxide 0.13

Dickens and Ford reported that 25% of all children ate
earth. Cooper (1957) reported a 21% rate of pica in American
children referred to the Mother’s Advisory Service in
Baltimore.

Lanzkowsky (1959) reported that 12 children with pica
had a hemoglobin that ranged from 3 g% to 10.9 g% with a
mean of 7.89% =/- 2.64. The institution of iron (i.m. - iron
dextran) “resulted in a cure for pica in one to two weeks.”
Again, if children have sufficient nutritional minerals they will
not eat lead paint - they only eat lead paint because of pica.

McDonald and Marshall (1964) reported on 25 children
who ate sand. They divided the group in half; they gave one
group iron and the other group saline (salt solution). After
three to four months 11 of the 13 children given iron had lost
their pica behavior compared with only 3 of the 12 in the
saline group.

===== Screenshot (146) =====
 


Reynolds et al (1968) reported that 38 people with
anemia exhibited pagophagia (ice eating) as the most common
form of pica; twenty two of 25 had their symptoms of pica
disappear after correcting the iron depletion.

Woods and Weisinger (970) reproduced pagophagia
experimentally in rats by withdrawing blood. The pagophagia
in the anemic rats was cured when the anemia was cured. It
is of interest to note that pica or cribbing behaviors are not
produced by vitamin deficiencies!

Two thirds of the 153 pregnant women studied by
Taggart (1961) developed cravings. The most common craving
was for fruit, pickles, blood pudding, licorice, potato chips,
cheese and kippers; a craving for sweets, vegetables, nuts and
sweet pickles came in second place.

Charles Mann (2008) reported that to satisfy pica, a
traditional dietary supplement used by pregnant Haitian
women takes the form of gray clay pigeon-like “cakes” made
of clay, salt and lard.

; Phosphate appetite was described by LeVaillant (1796)
as the anxious search by cattle in phosphate deficient South
African pastures for discarded dog bones (osteophagia); they
also chewed on wood (pica) and each others horns.

Bone chewing (osteophagia, a form of pica) has been
reported in many wild species of herbivores including reindeer,
caribou, red deer, camels, giraffe, elephant and wildebeest.

We have seen elephant eating bones, limestone
roadbeds and large termite heaps as ready and available
mineral supplements. A search for calcium rich edible clays
and soils and territorial disputes over limited supplies led to
wars in tribal Africa.

It was demonstrated that calcium deficient weanling
rats will consume large amounts of a lead acetate solution
(even though the solution tastes bad) when compared with
calcium fed controls. The Oriental diet is extremely low in
calcium leading them to habitually and preferentially eat
bones (when Dr. Ma Lan and I were courting, she ate the bones


 

and gave me the fillets of the fish that we caught) softened
and partially leached of calcium with vinegar.

There is no evidence of a specific pica behavior unique
to a magnesium deficiency. Magnesium deficient animals will
crave and eat common salt. Magnesium tastes bad enough that
animals will die of magnesium deficiency initiated seizures
rather than consume a pure magnesium supplement. It has
been suggested that recovery from a magnesium deficiency
can be a very unpleasant process, enough so that the bad taste
of magnesium supplements normally required for recovery
exceeds the unpleasant features of the deficiency! :

A natural potassium deficiency is highly unlikely as
all grains, fruits and vegetables tend to be rich in potassium
- a potassium deficiency can, however, be common in those
individuals taking diuretics for high blood pressure or weight
loss.

Potassium deficient rats are hyper alert and _ lick
everything including their metal cage, lab equipment, each
other and urine puddles. It is of interest to note, that even
with a severe potassium deficiency, laboratory rats have a
strong craving for sodium chloride (salt)!

Chromium and vanadium deficiencies are manifested
by an intense thirst for liquid (high fructose corn syrup laden
soft drinks being the usual first choice and water the last for
minerally deficient humans) and hunger for carbohydrates
(i.e. - soft drinks, coffee and tea with added sugar, alcohol,
pasta, deserts, bread, etc.).

The “munchies,” cravings for alcohol and “candy
cravings” (especially chocolate) are sure signs of chromium
and vanadium deficiencies.

The preteen and teen age infatuation with snack foods,
drugs, tobacco and alcohol appear to be the result of pica
- because they are so minerally deficient they are seeking
something, anything to put in their mouth that will satisfy an
irresistible craving for minerals.
