Chapter 9

“To the Printer of the London
Chronicle”:

SIR, I have often heard it remarked, that our Colonies in North
America were unhealthy and unfavourable to long life; and
more particularly so upon their first settlement. In opposition
to this groundless notion, I here send you two paragraphs
taken from the Pennsylvania Gazette of July 16, and the New-York Gazette of August 27, giving an account of the deaths of
the first-born of the city of Philadelphia, and the province of

Pennsylvania:

PHILADELPHIA, July 16. “At Kennet, in Chester county, the
5th instant, died John Key, in the 85th year of his age, and
the next day was interred in the burial place belonging to the
people called Quakers, in that township, attended by a large
number of reputable people, his neighbours and acquaintance.
He was born in a cave, long afterwards known by the name
of Penny-Pot, near Race-street; and William Penn, our first
proprietor, gave him a lot of ground, as a compliment on his
being the first child born in this city. -- In the early part of
his life his conversation was very engaging, and his company
much sought after by those of his ownage; in his decline he was
much esteemed for his peaceful disposition. His constitution
was very healthy till about 80, when he was seized with the
palsy, and continued weakly till his death. --About six years
ago he walked on foot from Kennet to Philadelphia in one

ee

day, which is near 30 miles. --It has been said that people in
this province are short-lived; but when we consider how few
children were born here 75 years ago, and observe how many
old people there are still alive among us (who were born
here) we shall rather think no quarter of the world can shew
a greater number of aged persons than this province can, in
proportion to the children born.”

PHILADELPHIA, Aug 24. On the 10th instant, at Brandywine
Hundred, in New-Castle county, died Emanuel Grubb, in
the 86th year of his age, and the next day was interred in St.
Martin’s church-yard, at Lower-Cichester, in Chester county,
attended by a large number of his relations, neighbours, and
acquaintance. -- He was born in a cave, by the side of the
Delaware River, not far distant from where he always lived,
and died, and was the first child born of English parents in this
province. His constitution was remarkably healthy during his
whole life, having never been afflicted with any sickness till
a few days before his death. His strength and activity were
surprising ina man of his age; he could mount and ride a horse
with as much dexterity as a lad of 20. -- A few months ago, he
rode from his own house to this city, and back again, which is
upwards of 40 miles. -- His memory was equally surprising,
and not in the least impaired, til his death; he could remember
transactions of a late date equally well with those which
happened in his younger days. He was exceeding temperate
in his living, seldom making use of spirituous liquors; and for
his peaceable and friendly disposition, was highly esteemed
by all who knew him. This instance, among many, is a proof
of the longevity of people born here.

It is worthy of observation also, that Governor
Hutchinson, in his valuable History of the Massachusett’s
Bay, Vol. II. Pp. 148 and 216, says,

“July 20, 1704, died at Marshfield, Peregrine White, aged 83
years and eight months, the first-born in Plymouth colony.”

===== Screenshot (148) =====
 


 

“Tan. 14, 1716, died at Salem, Elizabeth Patch, the first-born
female in the old colony of Massachusett’s Bay, so that she
must have lived 86 or 87 years; and April 14, following, died
at Newport, in Rhode-Island, Mary Godfrey, aged 87, being
the first child born there. -- The longevity of the first-born in
each of the three colonies, is worth noting.”

As a well-wisher to mankind in general, I thought it worth
while to send this to your useful paper, in order to allay the
apprehensions of those, whose inclinations, business, or
necessities may induce them to settle in that part of the British
Empire. | must add at the same time, that I have myself lately
traveled over the greater part of that extensive continent; and
can with truth say, in spite of all that hath been boldly and
positively asserted to the contrary, that | could not discover
among the whole people, one grain of disaffection to their
brethren in Britain, nor did I meet with a single person who
had ever formed the most distant idea of throwing off their
allegiance to the mother country. This it is doing them but bare
justice to declare; and is all the return I can at present make
them for the kindness and hospitality with which, purely on
account of my being an Old England-Man, I was universally
treated. I am, Sir, your constant reader, F +S.

The London Chronicle, December 15, 1767

Benjamin Franklin


 

The Energy Crisis

It was a manmade ecological shift and the resultant
Industrial Revolution that interrupted the historically
successful renewable system for obtaining plant minerals
that had been developed and had met the needs of human
populations for millennia.

In 1268 Norman castles and great manors of noblemen
had chimneys to draw the wood and coal smoke up and out
of the fireplace, it would be another 200 years before this
inefficient luxury was installed in the humble houses of the
common people.

The heating and cooking fire of the commoner filled a
hole in the middle of the floor or sat on a raised stone hearth
in the middle of the main room to avoid setting the wooden
walls on fire. The fire produced smoke which filled the room
until it seeped through the gaps in the walls or roof - the dusty
ash byproduct of the fuel’s combustion were collected and
taken out each morning, mixed with salt (3:1 to 10:1) to cut
and conserve the precious salt, used as “culinary” ashes for
the baking of grain dishes and the balance was tossed outside
as kitchen waste, added to the compost pile or mixed directly
into the garden.

From an indoor air quality standpoint, the majority of
Englishmen had not advanced much beyond the conditions
suffered by Homo erectus, who, over hundreds of thousands of
years earlier, had huddled over open wood fires inside large
huts constructed of sticks and branches.

The 13th century Englishmen were still getting their
daily supply of minerals, either directly (wood ashes mixed
with salt or mixed directly in their food), or indirectly, by
placing the wood ashes into their gardens. Beginning in 1285,
during the reign of Edward I, various commissions were
established in London to address the problem of coal smoke,

===== Screenshot (149) =====
 


which early environmentalists complained, “had infected and
corrupted” the air.

During the summer of 1306, bishops, barons and
knights from all over England ventured from their country
estates and manors and traveled to London town. They arrived
to take part in a new political experiment called parliament.
When they arrived in London they were met with an awful
odor - fumes and smoke from burning coal. Blacksmiths and
other tradesmen had begun burning coal in place of wood,
filling London’s air with sooty smelly smoke.

By the summer of 1306 enough coal was being burned by
blacksmiths, brewers (20,000 wagon loads of wood were used
each year to distill beer and whisky), and other industries that
required large quantities of fuel, that there was an organized
resistance against the practice, which led to a royal ban of coal
use in the city limits of London and other cities.

Despite new laws threatening steep fines and the
destruction of furnaces, coal use continued to increase. Some
sources reported that one transgressor of the coal ban law
was, “hanged, tortured or decapitated.”

The reason for London’s rising coal use was obvious.
The population of London and of Great Britain generally, had
been increasing, and asa result the forests were ina severe state
of decline. Forests near London were consumed first simply
because of easy access; they were burned for firewood, cut for
building, or simply burned to clear land for crop production
or pasture for livestock.

Wood ash supplies for garden fertilizer, cutting salt
and culinary uses declined. The people did not like to use coal
ash or “clinkers” for fertilizing their gardens, culinary ashes
in food or cutting salt because they imparted an undesirable
burned petroleum smell and taste. The reduced supply of
wood ashes caused a general decline in the health and immune
defenses in the populations of Great Britain and Europe.

As supplies of energy from living forests declined
and became scarce, and more costly, the demand for energy


 

from coal rose significantly. The coal merchants, watching the
burgeoning cities and the shrinking forests surely thought
coal was nearing a boom in sales.

As luck would have it, the energy crisis was solved,
not by coal, but rather by a plague that devastated Europe’s
populations.

In October 1347, the crew of a trading ship that docked
in Sicily brought the Bubonic Plague to Europe. The “Black
Death” was already wiping out the populations of China, India
and the Middle East; inits first European rage, continuing until
1351, it would kill almost one third of Europe’s population -
in fact 25 million souls were lost.

Some modern scholars speculated that the plague
was particularly deadly because the general population had
already been weakened by years of bad weather, bad harvests,
reduced supplies of wood (and therefore ashes) and famine.

Atthe time, most residents of Europe viewed the plague
as evidence of the wrath of God! After careful consideration,
the medical staff and faculty of the prestigious University of
Paris determined that the plague was caused by “an unusual
alignment of the planets that had occurred on March 20,
1345.”

In England, the plague would break out again and
again and again, for at least three additional charges before
1400, and Europe’s population would continue to be ravaged
by disease until 1500. By 1500, only 3 million people lived in
England, about half the number that had lived there before
the plague’s assault. As deserted and abandoned farms and
lands were reclaimed by the forests and trees became large
enough to provide for the needs of the decimated population,
coal sales went into the tank - the coal market literally dried
up, and wood ashes were back!

In the late 1500s, the English suffered an energy crisis
again, when their population grew and again, because of an
increased demand for and consumption of wood, the forests
shrank.

===== Screenshot (150) =====
 


 

The English learned to tolerate what had been thought
of as intolerable - they became the first western culture to mine
and burn coal on a ‘massive preferential national scale. The
coal burning public and businesses filled the air of London
and other cities with the most polluted and foul air in the
history of humanity.

‘The English went on to power an Industrial Revolution
with coal and transform the world of industry, agriculture,
human health and the fate of the planet Earth itself - the
energy produced from coal was a double edged sword and
had devastating unintended consequences.

The nobles led popular demonstrations with the
populous denouncing the use of coal in the city - King Edward
I banned the use of coal for political expediency. The original
bans were ignored, so more desperate laws were passed to
punish first offenders with “great fines and ransoms.” Second
offenders had their furnaces destroyed.

In 1661, John Graunt, a little known London draper,
theorized that by making tables out of London mortality data
and analyzing it, he could create more useful information. His
resulting observations, published in 1662, resulted in Charles
Il, recommending Graunt’s appointment to the prestigious
and newly formed Royal Society - adding that, “If they found
any more such tradesmen, they should be sure to admit them
all without further ado.”

Graunt's analysis confirmed that infant and childhood
mortality was higher than earlier believed, and that thirty per
cent of London’s deaths were children under the age of four
or five years old. The records also showed that lung related
deaths reached between 20% and 25% of the total deaths.

Graunt recorded that, “Seasoned residents lived nearly
as long in London as elsewhere, yet new-comers, and children
do not, for the Smoaks, Stinks, and close Air are less healthful
than that of the Country; otherwise why do sickly Persons
remove to the Country Air?”


 

Benjamin Franklin

Benjamin Franklin, as a result of the unintended
consequences produced by some of his scientific endeavors,
would actually shorten the lives of Americans and individuals
in other industrialized nations.

Franklin was born in Boston on Sunday, January 17,
1706. Boston was by then an incorporated town for 76 years.
Boston was no longer a simple, isolated religious outpost - it
was a bustling, growing port city.

Boston boasted more than 1,000 permanent houses,
a thousand seagoing ships registered at its port and 7,000
permanent residents - the population would double every
twenty years.

Even as a child, Franklin was a creative, yet practical
inventor. One of his first inventions was a set of flippers for
his hands and feet to improve his swimming speed. He made
two oval palettes, with holes for his thumbs, and “T also fitted
to the soles of my feet a kind of sandals.”

Early in life Franklin became infatuated with kites. While
flying his kites, Franklin would strip, wade into a pond or lake,
floated on his back, and used the kite and wind power to pull
him across the body of water. “Having engaged another boy to
carry my clothes round the pond,” he recalled, “I began to cross
the pond with my kite, which carried me quite over without
the least fatigue and with the greatest pleasure imaginable.”

Franklin was originally slated by his father to be
a minister, and to that end he was sent to the Boston Latin
School at the age of eight to prepare for entry into Harvard.

Franklin did well at Harvard, moving up from the
middle of his class to the number one slot, which resulted in
his being advanced to a higher grade level. Because Franklin’s
father couldn’t sustain the tuition cost, Franklin was forced to
drop out of school.

===== Screenshot (151) =====
 

Oo

Young Franklin was described as skeptical, puckish,
curious and irreverent, “Dr. Franklin, when a child, found
the long graces used by his father before and after meals very
tedious,” his grandson stated. “One day after the winter's
provisions had been salted, ‘I think, Father,’ said Benjamin,
‘if you were to say Grace over the whole cask - once for all - it
would be a vast saving of time.”

At age ten, with only two years of schooling, Franklin
went to work full time in his father’s candle and soap shop.
It was not the most fun job that a ten year old kid could have;
he spent his working hours skimming rendered tallow from
at; trimming wicks, adding wood

boiling tubs of “stinking” f
ashes to the soap and filling candle molds was considered
a mindless repetitive activity by young Franklin. His father
took him on long walks to visit various tradesmen to find a
trade or art that might better interest him.

As it turned out, his knowledge of many crafts and
skills allowed him to become a first rate tinker and inventor.
Franklin at age twelve years went on to sign on for nine
years of indentured service as an apprentice and to acquire
the training and the experiences of a publisher, printer and
newspaperman.

Franklin was directed to produce poetry based on
news stories, the stories were well received, to which Franklin
wrote, “(the praise) flattered my vanity.” Herman Melville
would write that, “Franklin was everything but a poet.”

Benjamin Franklin’s four year old son died of smallpox
in 1736, causing him to become an advocate of vaccination for
smallpox.

The print industry was a natural course of life for
Franklin, “From a child I was fond of reading,” he recalled,
“and all the little money that came into my hands was
ever laid out in books.” Books became the most important
influence in his life. The love and respect of books was one
of the common traits shared by the Puritanism of Mather and
the Enlightenment of Locke, worlds that would blend in the


 

character and personality of Benjamin Franklin. “Often I sat
up inmy room reading the greatest part of the night, when the
book was borrowed in the evening and to be returned early
in the morning, lest it should be missed or wanted,” admitted
Franklin.

In 1743, Franklin proposed the formation of the
American Philosophical Society, which was to be an American
version of the Royal Society think tank of England. It would
be housed in Philadelphia, and its members would include
illustrious scientists and thinkers from other cities.

The members would exchange their studies by post,
and abstracts would be distributed to the members quarterly.
The subjects to be investigated were to be practical rather
than theoretical and included “newly discovered plants,
herbs, trees, roots, their virtues, uses, etc....improvements
of vegetable juices, such as ciders, wines, etc.; new methods
of curing or preventing diseases, ....improvements in any
branch of mathematics...new arts, trades, and manufactures...
surveys, maps and charts...methods of improving the breeds
of animals... and all philosophical experiments that let light
into the nature of things.”

In 1747 Franklin proposed a volunteer military force
that would be independent of Pennsylvania’s colonial
government - the Pennsylvania Militia was born.

Franklin didn’t realize it, but his charter of the Militia
Association, in its spirit as well as wording, conceived and
preceded the writing of the Declaration of Independence
three decades later:

“Being thus unprotected by the government under
which we live, we do hereby, for our mutual defense and
security, and for the security of our wives, children and
estates...form ourselves into an Association.”

In 1748, at age 42, Franklin retired and sold his printing
business to David Hall, his trusted foreman; the agreement

===== Screenshot (152) =====
 


provided him with half the businesses profits for eighteen
years, which amounted to 650 British pounds per year (the
average colonial income at the time was 25 pounds per year).
Now, he calculated, he would have “leisure to read, study,
make experiments, and converse at large with such ingenious
and worthy men as are pleased to honor me with their
friendship.”

Franklin gave up his print shop to focus and engage
his energies on science, then politics, then diplomacy and
statecraft. As Poor Richard (one of Franklin’s pen names)
declared in his almanac that year, “Lost time is never found
again.” His intellectual curiosity and infatuation with the
orderliness of the greater universe drew him to science.

Franklin didn’t have any formal academic training in
science or math to be considered a great theorist of the likes of
Einstein; his interests of what he fondly called his “scientific
amusements” caused some educated contemporaries to
dismiss him as a mere tinker. However, even during his
life he was held up as the most famous scientist alive - as
Harvard professor, Dudley Herschbach stated, “His work on
electricity was recognized as ushering ina scientific revolution
comparable to those written by Newton in the previous
century or Watson and Crick in ours.”

An example of Franklin’s practical application of
science was his 1740s invention of an iron wood-burning
stove that could be built into fireplaces to maximize heat
output into the house while at the same time minimizing, fuel
requirements, smoke and drafts - using his understanding of
convection currents and heat transfer, he produced an original
and unique stove design.

The resulting byproduct of the daily use of the
“Pennsylvania Stove” - wood ash (plant minerals), were still
available to mix with food as “culinary ashes,” cut salt at the
rate of ten parts ash to one part salt as a condiment and the
balance mixed into homemade lye soap and into the compost
to fertilize the garden.


 

During a visit to Boston in the summer of 1743, Franklin
and others were entertained one evening by a traveling
scientific showman from Scotland called Archibald Spencer.
Spencer featured amazing demonstrations of science that were
designed to amuse the host’s guests. He revealed Newton’s
theories of light and exhibited a device that measured blood
flow - both were interests of Franklin’s.

Dr. Spencer performed electricity parlor tricks, such as
creating static electricity by rubbing a glass tube and drawing
sparks from the feet of a boy hanging by silk cords from the
ceiling. “Being (entertained and educated) on a subject quite
new to me,” Franklin recalled, “They equally surprised and
pleased me.”

One hundred years earlier, Galileo and Newton had
described, explained and demystified a force knownas gravity,
however, another awesome force of the universe, electricity,
was no more understood than it had been by the prehistoric
people who had tamed fire. There were individuals, like Dr.
Spencer, who used the wonders of electricity as a parlor trick
to entertain admiring audiences; however, no one as yet, had
put the power of electricity to the task of real work.

The Abbe’ Nollet, court scientist to France’s King Louis
XV, connected 180 soldiers and then 700 monks and “made
them jump as if one of the court’s entertainment by sending
a pulse of static electricity through them.” Franklin however,
was the ideal person to convert electricity from the status of
a simple parlor trick into a functional, practical and working
science.

Franklin’s initial experiments involved collecting an
electric charge and then studying its various properties. He
employed his friends to draw charges from a spinning glass
tube and touch each other to see if sparks flew. These early
experiments resulted in the discovery that electricity was
“not created by the friction of rubbing the tube, but collected
only.”

Many of Franklin’s originally coined terms such as

===== Screenshot (153) =====
 


 

battery, charged, neutral, condense and conductor are still
used today in the fields of electricity and the greater subject
of energy. “I fe has written equally for the uninitiated as well
as the philosopher,” the 19th century English chemist, Sir
Humphrey Davy noted, “and he has rendered his details as
amusing as well as perspicuous.”

Franklin’s discovery that the creation of a positive
charge was accompanied by the generation of an equal
negative charge became known as the “conservation of
charge” and the “single-fluid theory” of electricity. The
concepts were easily formulated in Franklin’s “book keeper”
mind. His observations were a scientific breakthrough and
discovery of historic greatness - “As a broad generalization
that has withstood the test of 200 years of fruitful application,”
Harvard professor I. Bernard Cohen declared, “ Franklin’s law
of conservation of charge must be considered to be of the same
fundamental importance to physical science as Newton’s law
of conservation of momentum.”

In a practical progression of his electrical storage
experiments, Franklin lined up a series of parallel glass plates
separated by metal - charged them up, wired them together,
and discovered a new electrical device - “what we call an
electrical battery.”

Franklin was able to use the “electrical battery” to turn
the iron fence surrounding his house into the first electrified
security fence; he created an electrical toy spider that would
hop like a live one; he electrocuted turkeys with his electrical
battery for the entertainment of his guests before the birds
were roasted for dinner with electricity, thus inventing the
first electrical execution device and first electrical cooking
device.

This early Franklin experiment, in which he killed
and cooked turkeys with electrical devices, was perhaps
the very first close call humanity had with an epidemic
of obesity, type If diabetes, the metabolic syndrome,
arthritis, osteoporosis and many other chronic degenerative

4


 

diseases, for with the use of electricity there were no wood
ash byproducts that had been produced for millennia by
the burning of wood. No wood ashes to mix with salt, no
culinary ashes - no plant minerals with which to nourish
our bodies or to fertilize the garden.

For millennia, lightening was universally considered to
be a supernatural phenomenon or an expression of God’s
wrath. During one 35 year period of time during the 1700s, in
Germany alone, “386 churches were struck by lightening and
an excess of 100 bell ringers were killed by heavenly bolts.
In Venice, 3,000 people were killed when tons of gunpowder
stored in a church was struck.”

Franklin noted that, “The lightening seems to strike
steeples of choice and at the very time the bells are ringing;
yet they continue to bless the new bells and jangle the old
ones whenever it thunders. One would think it was now time
to try some other trick.”

Franklin concluded that the “water vapors in a cloud
can be electrically charged and when these electrically charged
clouds pass over a high tree, lofty towers, spires, masts of ships
...draw the electrical fire and the whole cloud discharges.” His
proposal led to some practical advice - “Dangerous therefore
it is to take shelter under a tree during a thunder gust.”

Before he even tried his lightening experiments himself,
Franklin detailed his thoughts in two famous letters to
Collinson in 1750, which were presented to the Royal Society
in London and then widely published. The basic concept was
to use a tall metal rod to draw some of the electrical charge
froma cloud, just as he had done with a needle to draw off the
charge of an iron ball in his workshop.

Franklin correctly surmised that the “lightening rod”
could, render safe one of the greatest natural dangers faced by
humans. “Houses, ships and even towns and churches may
be effectively secured from the stroke of lightening by their
means,” he predicted.

Franklin had been waiting for the steeple of

===== Screenshot (154) =====
 


 

Philadelphia’s Christ Church to be finished to perform his first
lightening rod experiments; because of construction delays,
he became impatient and in his typical zeal, he developed the
idea of using instead a kite. To that end he enlisted the efforts
of his son William to help fly the silk kite. A sharp wire was
attached to the top of the kite, and a brass key was attached
near the base of the wet string, so that a wire could be brought
near it in an effort to draw sparks.

Initially clouds passed over the kite to no effect. Franklin
almost fell into despair until he saw several of the strands of
the string straighten out and stiffen. He put his knuckle to the
key and drew an electrical spark.

Franklin was literally moments away from discovering
a commercially viable source of clean household energy -
electrical appliances and the end of the dusty ash byproduct
of heating and cooking fires! However, the onset of the
American Revolution and the birth of a new nation siphoned
off his creative juices and energies.

It would take an additional 150 years before Edison’s
work produced the practical generation of electrical power
and the flurry of invention of patented electrical appliances
that would replace wood fueled heating and cooking
fires and eliminate once and for all the health and life
supporting kitchen and heating byproducts of wood ash -
plant minerals!


 

Chapter 10

“Every basket is power and civilization. For coal
is a portable climate. It carries the heat of the tropics
to Labrador and the polar circle; and it is the means of
transporting itself whithersoever it is wanted. Watt and
Stephenson whispered in the ear of mankind their secret,
that a half-ounce of coal will draw two tons a mile, and
coal carries coal, by rail and by boat, to make Canada
as warm as Calcutta; and with its comfort brings its
industrial power.”

Ralph Waldo Emerson, 1850

The Fuel Revolution

After having tamed fire 1,500,000 years earlier, humans
had created many unique ways to use the power of fire to
change natural materials into everything from metals, pottery,
bricks, and glass, to salt, soap and ale. However, in all those
hectic and creative millennia of baking, boiling, melting and
smelting, it didn’t quickly come to them that fire held another
power - the power of movement and motion.

For the average human, the back-breaking work of
transporting loads from one farm to another, or from one
market to another was performed only with the power of
muscle, water and wind.

===== Screenshot (155) =====
 

 

a

This fact of work powered only by humans, animals,
water or wind limited the production and growth that a culture
could expect to accomplish in each century. There was only so
much mechanical lifting, turning and pulling that humans and
animals could do and only so many hours in the day.

In the 1700s, a machine was invented that allowed
humans to grow beyond the historical constraints and limits
by turning “solar income” fuels into enormous amounts of
mechanical torque, lift and forward motion!

This invention was the steam engine and the fuel was
coal. The steam engine would soon become the center of
the Industrial Revolution, causing it to be called, “The most
important invention in the creation of the modern world.”

Coal, “motivated” the steam engine and its inventors,
who were searching for a solution to the problem of coal mine
flooding as the mine shafts were driven below the water table.
Wood had become scarce, and as a result, coal had become a
necessity of life and the problem of mine flooding had to be
solved.

To keep the mines dry, operators employed gravity
by digging tunnels or “watergates” from the valleys up into
the mine; others used various means of power including
windmills, windlass, chain of buckets and simple buckets
carried on the backs of diggers. The greatest amounts of water
in the largest mines was raised by horse power, however, the
cost of maintaining 50 to 60 horses and their teamsters was
too burdensome for even the largest mines.

By 1700, coal production was ten times greater than
it had been in 1550. England was now mining five times
more coal than all other nations combined, she was now
totally dependent on coal as a fuel for home use and for
powering industry. Even if England optimized her wood
fuel production from her forests, ceased the use of wood
for building and manufacturing, the forests could no longer
replace the dependency on coal - wood fuels were once and
for all gone in England.


 

The flooding of the mines remained a threat to
England's very existence as a world power - asa result, in 1660
Charles II created the Royal Society. The Royal Society was an
independent group commissioned to study and promote the
theory and application of science.

The original members of the Royal Society were the great
philosophers, thinkers and scientists of the day, including Sir
Issac Newton, Gottfried Leibnitz, Christopher Wren, Samuel
Pepys, John Evelyn and Robert Boyle - “Religion, nationality,
profession - all were subordinated to a degree perhaps never
before found in history.”

One of the more important and practical concerns of
the illustrious group was coal, yet coal was still a mystery to
them. In the 17th century, people still believed that coal was a
living organism and still alive much like peat moss.

The Royal Society members, although initially confused
about the origin of coal, they agreed for certain to one thing -
England’s coal supply was threatened by mine flooding. They
focused all of their attention on pumping technology, and on
atmospheric pressure.

Scientists had only recently begun to appreciate that
the atmosphere had weight and volume and it fills vacuums
with a powerful force. They learned and appreciated the
fact that the force of air opposing a vacuum could be put to
work, however, producing and controlling the vacuum was
problematic.

Denis Papin, a French Huguenot living in England,
found the solution - steam! Papin had previously proven his
knowledge of steam by demonstrating to the members of the
Royal Society his steam pressure cooker which he named
the “Digester” or Engine for softening bone so they could be
completely eaten.

Between 1690 and 1695, Papin invented a small
contraption consisting of a moveable piston within a brass
cylinder. When he heated water that filled one end on the
cylinder, the resulting steam vigorously drove the piston

===== Screenshot (156) =====
 


 

upward. When he removed the flame, the steam condensed
and produced a vacuum - the piston was then vigorously
pushed back toward the vacuum by the outside atmospheric
pressure.

Papin didn’t believe his first steam piston could be
built large enough to be of practical use. The inventor who
actually put the steam piston to work, was not a member of
the Royal Society or London’s scientific elite, rather it was
Thomas Newcomen, a simple ironmonger or “tinker” froma
small rural town.

The members of the Royal Society tried to downplay
the importance of Newcomen’s steam engine, leading many
historians to believe that Newcomen’s lack of credentials
was the problem. One historian went on to say, “to the small
scientific world of London, whose center was the Royal
Society, it seemed inconceivable that such a man could excel
the brightest intellects of the day in the power of invention.”

Newcomen’s steam driven device was similar to the
Papin piston, however, it was much larger and sported a
separate boiler in which to generate the steam. After the
steam was let into the bottom of the cylinder, cold water was
added to condense the steam; the resulting vacuum forced
the piston ina downward stroke. As the piston moved up and
down, it rocked an overhead beam. The opposite end of the
beam moved the rod of a vacuum pump ina similar fashion
to those driven by horse power.

The first practical Newcomen steam engine was
“quietly and without ceremony” installed at a coal mine in
1712. The engine was called a “fire engine,” and the men who
fed coal into the engine’s boiler and kept the engine’s fire alive
were called “firemen.”

Newcomen’s steam engine was an immediate success
and much revered by mine operators - one engine did the
work of pumping more efficiently and more economically
than 50 horses!

Those historians who know Newcomen’s story, write


 

of him and his work with an almost religious reverence,
calling his steam engine, “the most wonderful invention which
human ingenuity had yet produced,” and credited him with
giving “to the whole world ...The art of converting fuel into
useful power for the benefit and convenience of humanity.”

By the 1760s, hundreds of Newcomen engines (because
they used atmospheric pressure rather than steam pressure
were often called atmospheric engines) were pumping water
from mine shafts throughout England and Scotland; some of
Newcomen’s engines were built to gigantic proportions for
the day, reaching cylinder diameters of more than six feet and
lengths of ten feet.

Initially the Newcomen engine was used only by coal
mine operators because it was a fuel hog, and coal was for all
practical purposes free at the mouth of the shaft.

In 1736, James Watt, the son of a Sottish carpenter was
born. He was the only one of five children born to his parents
to survive to manhood; however, Watt was very frail and
suffered from continuous migraines that kept him in bed for
weeks at a time.

According to a 19th century biographer, Watt's
fragile health left him with “an almost feminine delicacy and
sensitiveness, which made him shrink from the rough play of
robust children”; as a result, he was home schooled during his
early years, he spent his time playing with his father’s carpentry
tools and intently watching steam pouring from tea kettles.

Watt excelled inmathand studied the art of constructing
mathematical instruments. It was as an instrument maker,
only 20 years old, that he was asked to fix a small working
model of a Newcomen engine that had been reverently kept
at Glasgow University.

The Newcomen model, like the full sized engine, was
extremely wasteful, requiring enormous amounts of coal to
keep it going. Watt observed that as steam was injected into
the system and then cooled with water, heat was wasted in
the cyclical process of reheating and cooling the cylinder.

===== Screenshot (157) =====
 

iste eet yee ee

Watt revolutionized the Newcomen steam engine
by installing a separate condenser; the cylinder would be
attached to another container that was surrounded with cool
water, after the cylinder filled with steam, the steam passed
into the cooler container where it condensed. In this way, the
cylinder itself would always remain hot and ready for the
next injection of steam!

According to Dr. Joseph Black, a good friend, of Watt's,
“This capital improvement flashed upon his mind at once,
and filled him with rapture.”

Watt, after suffering many challenges (“of all things in
life, there is nothing more foolish than inventing”), eventually
found the perfect partner, the Birmingham industrialist,
Mathew Boulton, and one of the most successful partnerships
in history was started.

Boulton was a manufacturer and an_ industrial
visionary. Together, Boulton and Watt formed the Lunar
“- members included Joseph Priestly who had
discovered oxygen, sulfur dioxide and carbon monoxide;
Joseph Wedgewood, the pottery giant; and Erasmus Darwin,
inventor, poet and grandfather of Charles Darwin.

Boulton built his financial empire with buckles, rather
than coal, iron or textiles. He employed 1,000 workers in his
Soho manufactory, where they made precision crafted goods,
with a range from buckles, buttons and filigree to classic
instruments including clocks, thermometers and
Boulton’s manufactory became so famous that it

Society

scientific
telescopes.
drew tourists, including Catherine the Great.

Boulton had a serious interest in steam engines because
the water wheels that powered the Soho facility could not be
depended upon for a continuous source of power. He had
unsuccessfully tried his hand at inventing and designing
steam engines, he even exchanged letters on the subject of
engine design with his good friend Benjamin Franklin.

In 1773, Watt moved to Birmingham and began
working on improvements on his steam engine at Soho.


 

Boulton provided skilled artisans, financial backing and best
of all emotional encouragement to Watt.

The all important cylinder was produced by John
Wilkinson, the pioneering iron founder. In 1776, while the
American Revolution was in full swing, James Watt installed
his first two engines, one to pump water out of a mine shaft
and the other to inflate and drive the bellows in Wilkinson’s
iron foundry.

While Watt improved the steam engine in many ways,
his finest moment was in getting the Newcomen engine to
extract four times the work force or “horse power” out of a
lump of coal. The steam engine’s new fuel efficiency moved
it out of the darkness of the coal mine and into the business
world to find a home in the world’s factories.

England’s coal industry had become relatively large by
the 1700s just to meet the domestic and non-iron industrial
requirements of the nation; it would expand again tenfold by
1830; and double again by 1854. Between 1842 and 1856 the
number of English coal mines quadrupled again.

The steam driven Industrial Revolution’s impact on
human endeavor has been compared to the discovery and
taming of fire, the invention of the wheel, and to the tasting of
the apple from the Tree of Knowledge.

Not until the 1800s would it be scientifically established,
and commonly accepted, that coal itself, not only the plant
fossils held within, was plant material and prehistoric.

y 1790, “potash fever” swept through the young,
American country; trees were burned by the tens of thousands
simply for the ash which was exported to Great Britain for
fertilizer and culinary ashes. The first U.S. Patent, Patent
Number 1, was awarded for “the production of wood ash and
the use of wood ash as fertilizer.”

Coal was not just another simple fuel, and “no mere
article of commerce.” Coal represented man’s victory over the
elements of nature - it became the foundation of civilization
itself:

For centuries, Britain led the world in coal production,

In the United States, coal transformed an empty

Without coal we would have wallowed longer in the poverty

ignorance of the pre- industrial world; however, given more

Michael Faraday, the scientist, acclaimed by all the
world, had little formal education and had taught himself
everything. Since the 1820s his had beena career of “marvelous
discoveries in many different fields, that of induced electricity

Faraday published a two volume collection of his

Faraday’s written works on electricity and formed his views
on electricity based on the work of Franklin and Faraday.

Faraday’ s “dynamo” seemed to provide the answer to

the problem of a cheap supply of electric current. This early

dynamo, powered by steam engines, was gradually improved

over time by many inventors and tinkers - by the 1860s light

demonstrated before

In 1876, at the Philadelphia Exposition, Moses G.
Farmer exhibited three arc lights powered by his privately built
dynamo. In 1877, a newspaper reported that Paul Jablochkoff,
a former Russian officer of engineers had invented “electric

candles” which were introduced as street lamps in Paris.

Coal, steam and electricity now had a toe hold in the
daily life of human culture - it was clear that wood as a fuel
for the hearth and industry was no longer viable, because
the maximum rate of re-growth of trees could no longer
keep up with the growing demand for fuel and power.

Unfortunately at this point in history, territorial
expansion, financial empires, industrial growth and mass
transportation (i.e. - roads, canals, railroad, trans- Atlantic
ships, etc.) were the driving force in all industrialized nations
- the race was on! As a result of all the excitement generated
by new sources of power and business growth, no one paid
any attention to the human price that would be paid over the
horizon of time.

Wood, the source of the seemingly endless flow of
wood ashes (plant mineral nutrition) that had supported
human development in the fields of agriculture, nutrition,
intellect, art, industry, health, longevity and science had
begun to be pinched off - we had moved from wood to coal
as the common fuel, we had moved from wind, water and
muscle power to steam and electricity to run our machines,
farms and industries in almost a single breath.

The Tibetans & Chinese

Since before written history, wood ashes (plant derived minerals) were the most universally
available mineral nutritional supplement and mineral based fertilizer. Wood ashes were
predictable; they were a fine particle-sized mineral dust and an immediately available
source of plant food (fertilizer) and mineral supplement that was produced everyday in the
human’s daily life as a simple byproduct of cooking and warming our homes.
Wood ash was an unintended, passively produced by product, a cottage industry in even the
most humble of dwellings. Wood ash was produced and employed in their daily lives by
Neanderthals 200,000 years ago.
The systematic cultivation and fertilization of land to commercially produce food was first
written about in 4000 B.C. in China. Chinese farmers used “organic material, wood ashes and
silt reclaimed from streams and rivers that had been eroded from the hills and mountains to
fertilize gardens and field crops.”
Wood ashes were universally available and contained a wide variety of “plant derived
minerals” that were easily available to cultivated garden plants and directly available to
humans as a source of nutritional minerals when mixed with table salt and applied to our
food as a condiment and mixed with our food as “culinary ashes.”
“culinary ashes” were of great and absolute importance to human survival, health, fertility
and longevity. Wood, peat and kelp ashes were added to human and animal food since before
written history.
The “munchies,” pica and cravings didn’t begin with the first potato chip or Hershey bar -
the munchies began with the first man as a “salt craving.” From the first man to the modern
man, we were and still are dependent on plant source minerals.
Food preparation, in its earliest and simplest form, was raw meat or meat crudely “cooked”
in fire. Food was immediately eaten or preserved by being rolled in wood ash or “salted.”

About 5,000 years ago, one of the world’s early’
civilizations began along the banks of the Yellow River, as:
the Chinese stopped their hunting and gathering, drove andi
dragged their Neolithic tools into the virgin soil of the flood!
plains - the hunters became farmers.

Over the millennia, the Chinese extended farming as:
far north as the rain fall permitted. Beyond the rain fall line:
only barbarian nomads who herded goats and camels could:
survive. The northern boundary of Chinese civilization was
in fact determined by the Chinese deferring to the climate;
the Great Wall was China’s way to codify the natural border
between wet and dry, settled and unsettled.

Climate not only determined the borders of Chinese
civilization, but also stimulated the formation of one of its
most important identifying features - a powerful, centralized
government, designed, in a manner like that of the Roman
Empire.

Even south of the Great Wall, rainfall is so unpredictable
that China has always been squeezed between the double-edged disasters of drought and flood, and many millions
have starved or drowned.

Chinese mythology describes the first dynasty as being
founded about 2200 B.C. by Yu the Great, who provided some
relief from the natural disasters. Yu the Great was “part god
part hydraulic engineer” - he brought an equilibrium betweer

the earth and the waters and made cultivation predictable by
constructing dams, canals and irrigation ditches, and also a:
legend has it, “by dancing a special flood control dance.”

For thousands of years, China’s emperors, had the

responsibility, of constructing, and maintaining enormous
water projects. To this end the emperors mobilized huge
armies; the stakes were high, failure to control flooding and
droughts raised doubts as to whether the emperor's heavenly
appointment to rule the people had ended.

Success resulted in long dynasties, the crops flourished
and taxes flowed to the emperor's coffers. The emperors
succeeded more than they failed, so China grew to such
impressive levels that their degree of modern civilization
would shock the West.

Marco Polo wrote and published his famous China
travel book in 1300. The book described the amazing feats
and wonders of wealth, food, farming, canals, huge bustling
cities and successful government in an attempt to educate and
entertain his fellow Europeans. Polo’s descriptions seemed so
outrageous to the Western mind he was condemned as a liar.

One of the unusual practices in China was described
by Polo as:

“Throughout this province (of northern China) there is found
a sort of black stone, which they dig out of the mountains, where it
runs in veins. When lighted it burns like charcoal, it retains fire
much better than wood; insomuch that it may be preserved during
the night, and in the morning be found still burning. These stones
do not flame, excepting a little when first lighted, but during their
ignition give out a considerable heat.

It is true there is no scarcity of wood in the country, but the
multitude of inhabitants is so immense, and their stoves and baths,
which they are continually heating, so numerous, that the quantity
(of wood) could not supply the demand. There is no person who does
not frequent a warm bath at least three times in a week, and during
the winter daily, if it is in their power. Every man of rank or wealth
has one in his house for his own use; and the stock of wood must
soon prove inadequate to such consumption; whereas these stones
may be had in abundance, and at a cheap rate.”

===== Screenshot (162) =====
 


The Tibetans were the inspiration for the Pulitzer Prize
winning book, by James Hilton, The Lost Horizon. Tibet's “Old
People” are found on Chang Tang or the northern plateau,
elevation 15,000 feet, which consists of salt lakes fed by glaciers
from the eastern slopes of the Himalayan Mountains.

This mountain culture has about 500,000 people who
are devout Buddhists. The Tibetans are a mix of nomadic
herdsmen, merchants of salt, farmers and great cavalrymen
on camel or horse.

Tibet makes up almost 10% of China’s land mass and
was founded in the 7th century by King Songtsan Gambo.
The Chinese government sent an army into Tibet in 1950
and established a military governor to prevent Tibet from

establishing an independent nation. Since 1965 the region has:
been administered and known as the Tibetan Autonomous

Region (T.A.R.) of China.

The herdsmen build their corrals, huts and roads out
of salt bricks carefully cut from dried salt lakes on top of the
plateau; they hunt Marco Polo sheep, antelope, gazelle, wild
asses and yak.

The yak, which translates to “wealth,” provides meat,
sausage, milk, cheese, yogurt, butter, wool, skins and animal
power for plowing, riding and to transport freight.

The staple diet of the Tibetan consists of “tsampa,” a
smelly hand-mixed paste of lightly toasted barley flour, yak
butter, salt and black tea. The tsampa mainstay is supplemented
with turnips, cabbage, potatoes, trout, omelets and beans.

Tibetans routinely drink 30 to 40 medium sized cups
of black or green tea daily because the high elevation has an
extremely low humidity, requiring a high fluid intake. Each
cup of tea is flavored with a chunk of rock salt the size of a
Concord grape and two pats of yak butter.

The Tibetan capital city of Lhasa (translates to “Place of
the Gods”) was the inspiration for James Hilton’s legendary
city of Shangri-La. The city is found at an elevation of 12,000
feet, where temperatures can fluctuate as much as 80 to 10¢

degrees F each day.

In the center of old Lhasa is the holiest shrine in Tibet -
the Jokhang built in 650 A.D. The Jokhang is the “Mecca” to the
Tibetan Buddhists - many of the faithful would trek through
the mountains for years to come and pray at this holy site.

In the mountains west of Lhasa is Drepung, a gigantic
monastery that houses 10,000 monks, 25,000serfs and assistants
working 185 estates and tending over 200 pastures.

The Potala, “high heavenly realm,” is a sprawling
mountaintop palace of the Dalai Lama (translates to “ocean of
wisdom’ and represents the living prophet of the Buddhist faith
and the inspiration for the ancient living sage in James Hilton’s,
The Lost Horizon) built into the mountain 700 feet above Lhasa.

The Potala is famous for its 1,000 rooms, 10,000 altars,
20,000 statues of Buddha and the eight gold gilded tombs of
past Dalai Lamas.

South of Lhasa, there are tens of thousands of acres
of terraces fed and irrigated by mineral rich “Glacial Milk”
which originates from the Himalayan glaciers and wood
ash (from heating and cooking fires) enriched compost. The
vegetables, fruit and grains that are grown in this mineral rich
essentially manufactured soil take up these plant minerals
and are consumed by the Tibetans.

The Yao People of Bama

In 2008, China is being challenged by what they call
the “Gray Tide.” The annual growth rate for the numbers
of Chinese seniors over age 80 years is approximately five

The octogenarians in Bama have the dubious pleasure
of “looking up to their elders, the nonagenarians, who in turn
defer to those centenarians aged a hundred and over.”

Wangang typically has large numbers of centenarians
that number 34 per 100,000. It is not uncommon for households
to contain five generations of the same family.

The Yao typically buy their own coffin at age 61 and
use it as a sitting bench and grain storage locker until the need
arises to use it for their own burial.

Yao centenarians typically participate in their share of
chores by climbing the mountain paths to work the fields and
terraces and actively engage in social activities, particularly
folk singing.

The Yao people of Bama irrigate their terraces with the
mineral laden waters of the Panyang River, and they fertilize
their gardens with compost consisting of kitchen waste, wood
ash, plant debris and animal manure.

The Yao people have an extremely low incidence of
chronic disease such as coronary artery disease, cancer and
diabetes. There are no sudden increases in the
diseases after the age of 40.

Only 90 years of age

Diet, is regarded by gerontologists, to be the most

The Yao people consume large quantities of lima beans,

The people of Bama regularly drink “snake wine”

Mahjong, chess, calligraphy and folk singing are

Li-Ching-Yun

Li-Ching-Yun reportedly lived to the age of 256 years,
he outlived 23 wives and had 180 descendents! Li of Kaihsien,
in the Province of Szechwan, China, was born in 1677; he sold
herbs for 100 years; and died in 1933 at the age of 256 years
(and was the inspiration for the 200 year old leader of Hilton's
Shangri-La).

Professor Wu-Chung-Chien, the Dean of the
Department of Education, of the Minkuo University reported
that he had found records showing that Li was in fact born
in 1677 (the Chinese have the most extensive and the most
accurate of all census records in the history of man), and that
on Li’s 150th birthday (1827), he was congratulated by the
Chinese Imperial Government. Fifty year later, in 1877, Li was
sent a second official birthday congratulations, for his 200th
birthday. Fifty years later, at the age of 250 years, Li lectured
to 1,000 medical students in Beijing on the art of living a long
and healthy life.

Li’s advice for a long and healthful life was to, “Keep
a quiet heart, sit like a tortoise, sleep like a dog.” The “Sit Still
and Do Nothing” approach to health has many proponents,
and there is a time-tested practice that has been found over
and over to reduce the risk of illnesses, retain beauty and
youth and to engender longevity - meditation and prayer.

Meditation has been shown to lower blood pressure,
relax one’s muscles, raise the circulating levels of DHEA (the
“youth hormone”), lower age-inducing cortisol levels, and
generally support the rejuvenating processes in the body.

According to several studies, meditation reduces one’s
risk of cancer by 55% and heart disease by 80%. In a study
of more than 2,000 people who meditate, there was a 50%
decrease in the use of medical facilities, for those over 40 years
of age the reduction was 70% (this included visits for heart


 

disease, respiratory disease and gastrointestinal problems).

Being religious definitely increases one’s longevity. In
a meta-analysis of 42 studies which examined 125,826 people
(American Psychological Association in the year 2,000), it was
demonstrated that regular attendance at a place of worship
(church, synagogue, mosque or temple) added eight years to
the average lifespan and improved general health.

London Times, May 8, 1933

Telegrams in Brief - A telegram from Chungking in
the Province of Szechwan, China states that Li-Ching-Yun,
reputed to be the oldest man in China, and presumably the
world, has died at Kiah-Sien, at the alleged age of 256. -
Reuters

Sardinians

Dr. Paolo Francalacci, an evolutionary anthropologist
from Stanford University, said, “Eighteen thousand years
ago, during the period called the Glacial Maximum, humans
could survive in Europe only in two refuges, one in Iberia and
one in the Balkans.”

As the glaciers receded and the climate began to warm,
humans began to re-enter Europe. They traveled westward
from the Balkans and eastward from the Iberian Peninsula.
Sardinia, itself was populated almost entirely by a genetically
homogeneous Iberian peoples. They originally survived by
hunting, gathering and fishing.

Farming showed up in Sardinia 6,000 to 7,000 years
ago, brought by Neolithic bands of peoples from the Levant,
where farming had been recorded 3,000 years earlier.

About 14,000 years ago, Earth was thawing from an Ice
Age. As the ice melted, a small group of “genetically related”
people in Iberia migrated from the Pyrenees Mountains
to the Mediterranean Sea. They traveled east along the sea
coast, passing through what would become the “French
Riviera,” Tuscany, then crossing the sea to Corsica and finally
established villages in the Sardinian coastal foothills.

The Nuraghic peoples, tribal bands from the Bronze
Age appeared in Sardinia - the Nuraghi people were named
for the stone towers commonly found throughout Sardinia.
The Phoenicians and Romans appeared with their armies and
occupied the Sardinian coasts and the southern lowlands.
Sardinians, who had originally settled the entire island of
Sardinia, were squeezed into the central hills.

Gianni Pes, a medical statistician, traced the personal
histories of 1,000 Sardinian centenarians. He personally did
physical examinations of 200 individuals.

While examining his data, he noticed an unusual

===== Screenshot (167) =====
 

concentration of male centenarians in mountainous Barbagia,
Sardinia in the administrative district of Ogliastra.

The word Barbagia, comes from the Latin “ Barbaria,”
land of the Barbarians. They had little or no interest in the
“Bvenifancient Sardinians knew of farming
“They carried on

“developed an intense wariness and distain for visitors.
Newcomers had always meant subjugation, exploitation, and
taxes. So they turned inward, developing an intense dedication
to their families and community and earning a reputation for
toughness. One Barbagia prov erb said it all about foreigners:
Furat chie benit dae su mare (He who comes from the sea is

centenarians out of a population of 2,500 - one out of 357!

Dr. Michael Poulain, a Belgian demographer created
the Extreme Longevity Index, an index that codified the birth
and death data of all centenarians born between 1880 and
1900. He focused on areas that had the greatest numbers of
documented centenarians and circled them on maps with
blue ink which resulted in the use of the term “Blue Zone” to
indicate longevity sites throughout the world.

The Blue Zone sites in particular showed extreme
longevity in men. Typically women centenarians are recorded
rate 400% than men, in Barbagia,
Sardinia, the rate was one to one!

Statistically, male longevity is the story in Sardinia! In
the Sardinian Blue Zone, 47 men and 44 women have made
it past their 100th year out of 17,865 born between 1880 and
1900 - a rate of 100 year olds that surpasses America’s rate by
a factor of 30!

he found seven

at a greater however,

As time passed in Sardinia, the native Sardinians
developed their own unique culture and environment. Many
villages retained their pre-Roman names. “In this region,”

says Francalacci, “the names of the Sardinian villages such as
Ollolai, Illorai, Irgoli, Ittiri, Orune, to name a few, sound very
exotic to a continental Italian ear like mine.”

Nurra,a region north of Alghero, is thought by linguists
to be derived from the word nur, which translates to “a heap
of stones” in the Nuraghian language. The Bronze Age towers
that are commonly found scattered throughout Sardinia are
referred to as nuraghi.

The early Sardinians, did not hold on to their ancient
Nuraghic dialect, because the Romans had occupied them
over sufficient time, they learned to speak Latin. “It suggests
that the Sardinians’ lifestyle in the Blue Zone hasn’t changed
much since the time of Christ,” Francalacci said.

Francalacci continued, “The laws of evolution dictate
that a species will not evolve in a comfortable, isolated
environment where reproduction is easy. By contrast, a
species will evolve quickly in a tough environment where
individuals of different backgrounds and conditions interact.
Similarly, in a place like the Sardinian Blue Zone, there is less
pressure to adapt (and change). The people there maintained
not only their pure genetic features, but also their economic
isolation and traditional social values, such as the respect for
elders as a source of experience, the importance of the family
clan, and the presence of unwritten laws - all of which proved
to be effective means for avoiding foreign domination over
the centuries.”

Prior to 1950, “Sardinia had looked more like a
backwater than a centenarian’s Shangri-La. Poor hygiene,
poor water quality, and a scarcity of water led to rampant
infectious diseases. Dysentery, plague, tuberculosis, malaria,
and diarrhea killed many young Sardinians. As English
traveler William Henry Smyth wrote in his 1828 Sketch of
the Present State of the Island of Sardinia, ‘it is surprising that
with such inconvenient residences, and uncleanly habits, the
natives should remain so generally healthy as they do.”

As D. H. Lawrence traveled through Sardinia in 1921

===== Screenshot (168) =====
 

A

in search of a simple lifestyle, he embraced Barbagia. “Here,
since endless centuries, man has tamed the impossible
mountainside into terraces, he has quarried the rock, he has
fed his sheep among the thin woods, he has cut his boughs and
burnt his charcoal, he has been half domesticated even among
the wild fastnesses. This is what is so attractive...Life is so
primitive, so pagan, so strangely heathen and half savage.”

Dr. Gianni, Blue Zone demographer, said, “Consider,
for instance, the genes of inflammation. We studied several
tens of gene variants related to inflammation but we didn’t
find any evidence of their role in survival of Sardinians.
The same for genes related to cancer, and those related to
cardiovascular disease. I suspect that the characteristics of
the environment, the lifestyle, and the food are by far more
important for a healthy life.”

Villages in the center of Sardinia’s Blue Zone (Fonni,
Gavoi, Villagrande Strisali, Talana, and Arzana) around the
Gennargentu Mountains have progressed over the centuries
from small collections of shepherd huts into modern
communities that are home to thousands of people.

The traditional Sardinian diet consists of fava beans,
eggs, pecorino cheese, sheep and goat cheese, vegetables
potatoes,
eggplant, etc.), bread, pasta, almonds, raisins, minestrone
soup, olive and mastic oil and boiled meat (goat meat, roast
lamb, ham, chicken, etc.) - adults drink a liter of Sardinian
flavonoid and resveratrol rich red Cannonau wine every day.

(squash, zucchini, onions, olives, tomatoes,

Fish is not commonly found in their diet. The Sardinians cook
and heat their homes with wood stoves, the resultant wood
ashes are then mixed with plant debris, kitchen waste and
animal manure to create a compost, that is then mixed into
the gardens and terraces as a fertilizer.


Campodimele

Campodimele is known as Europe's “village of Eternal
Youth.” Campodimele, translates to “meadow of honey,” is
an isolated hilltop village that sits halfway between Rome
and Naples in the south of Italy.

It is said that it is a rare thing for a villager to die before
their 85th birthday, many reach their nineties, and is not
unusual for them to pass the age of 100 years without ever
having to visit a doctor.

The 1995 census revealed that there was forty-eight 90
year-olds, three 99-year-olds and a centenarian of 104 in the
population of 840.

The village is a rat warren of cobblestone streets and
stone houses surrounded by plantings of grapes, herbs, chili
peppers and protected by a medieval wall. Outside the walls
are planted with olive groves, orchards and groves of fruit
and nut trees and terraces dense with vegetables and grain.
The meadows beyond are used to graze cattle, pigs, goats and
sheep. Red and white wines are daily staples.

Meals are based on whole grain breads and pasta, beans,
fruit, olive oil, garlic, onions, tomatoes, Porcini mushrooms,
eggplant, meat, eggs, snails, goat cheese, butter, arugula,
lettuce and wine.

Symi

The classical Mediterranean diet is legendary for its
health benefits and the Greek island of Symi is a well known
success story - “may you live to be one hundred and more.”
According to Sally Beare, “Even when they are old, Symiots
are young - men in their seventies and eighties, are well toned
and vigorous, flirt openly with any woman who catches their
eye, which is hardly surprising , as the women on Symi are
also well toned and vigorous, with beautiful skin and hair.”

Up into their nineties, men and women are still working
their herds and flocks, collecting herbs, fermenting yogurt and
working their gardens. The population on the island of Symi
is only 2,700. Epidemiologists have recorded that Symi has
the best health record and highest longevity for the region.
Experts believe that the most important piece of the Symiot
health and longeviy secret is their diet and their very unique
cooking style.

The Symiot diet consists of fish, olive oil, tomato sauces,
goat's cheese, Greek yogurt, red meat, vegetables, salads, fruit
and red wine.

Vegetables include artichokes, green beans, stuffed
grape leaves, potatoes and Greek salads (add olive oil, lemon,

===== Screenshot (171) =====
 

herbs and garlic). Symiot fruit include figs, pomegranate and
grapes.

The Symiots eat garlic with just about everything -
tsatsiki (a yogurt dish), fish, Greek salads, meat dishes, and
vegetables are all impregnanted with the “stinking rose.”

Capers are a classical ingredient in the Symiot version
of the Greek salad. The zingy taste of capers adds zest to the
salads, rabbit and fish dishes. Capers, eaten with the stalk and
leaves are believed to encourage longevity.

Agapios Monachos, a Cretan monk wrote of the
caper in the 15th century, “It cures the spleen, destroys the
vermin, heals the hemorrhoids, increases the vitality of the
sperm, cures liver ailments and cramps, mobilizes the urinary
bladder, facilitates menstruation, and prevents rheumatism,”
and he recommended starting each meal with a single caper.

Thyme bushes are scattered all over the hillsides of
Symi; sage basil, peppermint and spearmint are grown as
potted plants at the front door and windows. Thyme is used
with ham and as a preservative for fruit.

Marjoram goes into Greek salads and marinades;
rosemary is steeped in wine, olive oil and mint and is used to
flavor cheese and lamb dishes.

Fresh whole grain wheat bread is a dietary staple, it is
typically dipped in olive oil and is eaten with goat cheese, soups,
stews, fish, and salads or eaten by itself as a breakfast dish.

Symiots prepare soups with beans, tomatoes, garlic,
herbs, and lamb. Organ meats, rabbit, goat and lamb are
commonly used in stews or roasted with garlic and herbs
including rosemary.

Feta cheese produced from ewe’s milk and live yogurt
are commonly eaten in Symi.

Meals in Symi are laced with red or white wines, 0uzo,
raki and retsina. Retsina is flavored with pine resin to create a
zesty and hardy dinner wine.

Rustic Bread with Olive Oil and Sesame Seeds*

Approximately 1 Tblsp yeast

4 fluid oz tepid water

5 cups whole-wheat flour (or other whole grain flour)
2 tsp sea salt

2 Tblsp extra-virgin olive oil

1-2 tsp sesame seeds

1. Dissolve the yeast in 1 cup of tepid water

2. Mix the flour and salt together and put ina pile on a clean,

dry surface. Make a well in the middle and add the dissolved
yeast water, a small quantity at a time. Each time the yeast
water is added, swirl the yeast into the flour, using circular
movements until the yeast is totally mixed in.

3. Keep adding water and mixing together until you have a
oO J

moist dough. Knead the dough for five minutes until it
is springy - the Symiots claim that, when done, it should
feel “like a woman’s breast.” If the dough is too dry or too
sticky, you can add more water or flour as required.

4. While kneading the dough, add the olive oil one tsp at a

time.

5. Shape the dough into a round shape and place on a baking

sheet. Score the top with a knife - this helps it to rise.
Cover with a dishtowel and set aside in a warm place for
40 minutes to 1 hour, until it is approximately double its
original side.

===== Screenshot (172) =====
 


 

6. Remove the loaf from the sheet, punch the air out of it, and
knead for a few minutes. Reshape into a loaf and allow it to
rise a second time for 20-30 minutes.

Sprinkle the top of the loaf with sesame seeds, and bake
at 435 degrees F for about 25 minutes until brown on the

NI

outside.

8 Serve warm with a small bowl of extra-virgin olive oil for
dipping.

The Georgians

The Georgians, as well as the Abkhazians, Azerbaijanis
and Armanians are found at the timber line of the Caucasus
Mountains which have peaks of 12,434 to 13,274 feet above
sea level.

They live in simple stone houses without electricity,
their blood pressure is typically 104/72 at age 100; women
continue to have children after age 52, they typically drink
an eight ounce glass of vodka or “applejack” with breakfast
and have a large glass of red wine with lunch and dinner.
Almost all of the “old people” are from rural backgrounds or
occupations such as farmers, shepherds and/or hunters.

This group of centenarians, from the Caucasus
Mountains, are found between the coasts of the Black Sea
and the Caspian Sea, and mountain villages at or above 4,500
feet above sea level. This region supports over 500,000 people
4,500 to 5,000 of whom are reported to be over 100 years of age.
They make up more than 25% of the regions centenarians.

The oldest known person from the Caucasus region
was identified in 1973 as Shirali Mislimov - at age 167 he still
worked in the village tea plantation in the small Azerbaijani
village of Barzavu on the Iranian border.

Those in the Caucasus region believe that those
individuals under 80 years of age are still considered a youth;
from 80 to 100 years one is considered middle age and only
those over 100 years of age can be considered a senior.

The strength and vigor of the peoples of the Caucasus
region are so legendary, that in the mid-eighteenth century
Johann Blumenbach used their region’s name to represent all
of the “white” peoples of Europe - Caucasians.

Many married couples claim to be married for over 100
years, although the Guiness World book of records says that
80 years of marriage is the official record.

===== Screenshot (174) =====

 

In a study that looked at 15,000 individuals over the
age of 100, only the married individuals attained advanced
age and still have an active sex life after the age of 100 years.

Mejid Agayev celebrated his 140th birthday February
12,1975. Agayev was the oldest living citizen in the Azerbaijani
village of Tikyaband that bragged of having 54 people over
the age of 100 years.

Work delegated to centenarians included weeding
fields, feeding livestock, shepherds, picking tea, washing
laundry, house work, cooking and baby sitting.

Women over 100 years of age had an average of 4to 15
children during their child bearing years.

The staple diet of the Caucasus region includes chicken,
mutton, beef, goatmilk, cheese, butter, yogurt, bread, tomatoes,
cucumbers, green onions, garlic, fruit, pita bread, boiled corn
meal mush (abusta), red pepper, tea, red wine and salt. The
total caloric intake per person ranges from 1,500 to 1,900 - 70%
from vegetables and dairy products, a small daily portion of
meat and most food is eaten raw or boiled - nothing is fried.

The terraced fields and gardens have been irrigated
with a highly mineralized “Glacial Milk” and fertilized witha
wood ash (from heating and cooking fires) enriched compost
for over 2,500 years.

The Indians of North America,
Mexico, Central & South America

The Indians of North America, Mexico, South America
and Central America share a common history and similar
cultural evolutions.

They had trade routes reaching from what would
become Canada, the United States, Mexico, Central America
and South America. They traded salt, cacao, dried fish, meat,

N
oO


 

grain, quetzal feathers, pyrite (for fire starters), tools, jewelry,
obsidian and jade.

Central to this transcontinental trade was a pre-Columbian city state known as Takalik Abaj. Takalik Abaj,
located near the western coast, of what is now Guatemala,
was a hub for trade, information and goods.

Archeological evidence points to Takalik Abaj being
ruled by the Olmec culture (known as the “rubber” people
and the traders of cacao) and then the last Mayan King.

In almost every Indian culture, there was and in many
cases, there still are, clusters of long lived people living as
they did hundreds or even thousands of years ago.

Like all long lived people, these seemingly primitive
tribes, through the accident of choices of where to live and
what and how to eat produced healthful centenarians.

In all cases these cultures have daily practices that
provide Calorie Restricted diets without malnutrition and
have discovered some form of highly efficient antioxidants.

“Culinary” ashes were used by pre-Columbian Indians.
In the 13th century, the use of culinary ashes was identified
from the analysis of the contents of garbage dumps in North
America and reported by anthropologists.

In 1588, Thomas Harriat (1560-1621), tutor to Sir Walter
Raleigh, was appointed “geographer” for his second Virginia
expedition - he soon published in Hakluyt’s Voyages on North
Carolina Indians and how they raised corn. He described
mountain spinach five foot high and how the Indians made
a “fine broth from the seeds, and from the stalks of the herb,
they produced a kind of “salt” by burning it to ashes, they then
season their broth with it. This is the only “salt” they know,
and they enrich their fields by burning weeds after drying in
the sun. This they (unknowingly) do to save them selves the
labor of carrying it away, rather to enrich better the ground.”

The Navajo

The diets of the Navajo Indians have changed
significantly since the pre-Columbian times. Archeological
data suggests that their early diet, was the standard fare of
hunter gatherers and consisted only of indigenous plants and
wild game until A.D. 1200, when a northern Athabaskan tribe
of Navajos migrated to the southwest and learned farming
from the Pueblo Indians.

Corn, laced with “culinary” ashes, became a staple
in the Navajo diet. Two centuries later, the Spanish brought
sheep and cattle, and the Navajo became shepherds.

Today the Navajo Nation numbers more than 151,000
and is the largest Native American tribe. In recent decades,
many processed foods have been introduced into the Navajo
diet as a result of government rationing, food distribution
programs and the growth of trading posts and food stores,
yet the tradition of “culinary” ashes being added to their food
remains in the more rural areas.

Juniper ash proves to bea good source of dietary calcium,
iron and a moderate source of magnesium. One teaspoon of
juniper ash provides the equivalent calcium levels found in one
cup of milk; it supplies 28% of the RDA for iron and nine percent
of the RDA for magnesium. The woody branches contain more
calcium and silica, whereas the twigs and needles (the growth
part of the tree) contained the highest levels of trace minerals.

Typical Navajo recipe that includes “culinary ashes”:

Shaped Corn Griddle Cakes

 

Mix one cup juniper ashes with water and strain
through a grass brush into basin of corn flour, using about
three and one half cups of the liquid. Add one half cup clear


 

water to this greenish dough and knead until thick. Shape
with moist hands into round cakes less than an inch thick and
four inches in diameter. Bake on a fry griddle, rubbing it (the
griddle) with a cloth between bakings, and moisten cakes on
both sides when done.

Even today, the corn based foods of the Navajo contain
“culinary” ash, which is mixed with boiling water and added
after straining, to the blue corn flour “to make it blue.” Blue corn
contains an anthocyanin pigment that turns a distinct greenish-blue color in the alkaline medium created by the addition of
wood ash. The wood ash was also used daily to make a “tea”
and was used medicinally for stomach and intestinal ailments.

Of the three major types of plants used to make
culinary ashes listed in the Navajo culture, juniper (Juniperus
monosperma), commonly called cedar is used most frequently.
The small branches and needles are most favorably used as
they produce the finest particle ash (minerals that are left
when the wood and needles are burned).

Green juniper branches are gathered, placed on a grill
or screen and set alight. The ash is colleted in a pan placed
underneath the grill. Juniper being absent, ash was also made
from dried tumbleweed or greasewood.

The three sources of “culinary” wood ashes were all
good sources of essential minerals. Iron concentration was
higher in the greasewood. Wood ash in general, although
variable in analysis, tends to be rich in potassium, calcium,
iron, copper, zinc, trace minerals and rare earths.

When wood ash was not available, a special traditional
clay wasmixed withsaltand/ or the Navajo food. Unfortunately
in the 1940s, urban Navajo cooks used electricity and gas to
cook and heat with and so began replacing “culinary” ashes
with the more available baking soda - thus began the transition
from the slim wood ash using Navajo shepherds to the obese
and diabetic baking soda using urban Navajo.

The Pima

The Pima Indians are a band of Native Americans who
live on the invisible border dividing the state of Arizona and
Mexico. The Pima band on both sides of the border are from
the identical genetic pool; yet the Pima Indians on the Mexican
side of the border have no almost no type II diabetes and have
only a ten percent obesity rate compared with the rate of 22%
diabetes and the 70% obesity in the Arizona Pima!

With a small population of 12,000 individuals, the
Arizona Pima ran up a $7 million annual Federal cost for
pharmaceutical treatments; additionally $3 million was spent
annually for dialysis charges for diabetic Pimas. As a result of
the epidemic rate of diabetes and obesity, the Arizona Pima
Indian's life span is shorter than the average American life
span by 15 to 19 years.

The Pima Indians from Arizona live in government
provided trailer homes that use natural gas or propane,
electric stoves and microwave ovens for cooking and heating
- they use automobiles, pickup trucks and tractors; and they
buy food and government Native American “commodities”
from the grocery store like every body else.

The Arizona Pima’ stransitionfromsubsistence farming,
wood stoves and their traditional diet to the American lifestyle
began in the 1940s, and by 1960, the epidemic of obesity and
type II diabetes, seemingly targeting the Arizona Pima had
started in earnest.

By contrast the Pima Indians living south of the Arizona
border, in Mexico, are still subsistence farmers, they still cook
and heat their traditional homes with wood and they are still
putting their wood ash byproduct of daily living into their
gardens and their corn tortillas.
