Tarahumara

Another “Blue Zone” in the Western Hemisphere that is
considered to have a reputable longevity record is the hot, arid
Sierra Madre Mountains of northern Mexico. The areas most
famous residents are the Tarahumara Indians, iconic as the
world’s only “jogging nation.” Their tribal name, Tarahumara,
roughly translates to “foot runners,” and jogging is their main
mode of travel - “Run, don’t walk,” is their national motto.

The Tarahumara warriors and hunters are legendary
for trailing deer for two or three days until the deer collapses
from exhaustion or blindly falls over the edge of a cliff.

Foot races are the national pastime of the Tarahumara;
in 1928 two Tarahumara men, Jose Torres and Aurelio
Terrazas, participated in the Olympic marathon competition
held in Amsterdam - they lost first place by two minutes,
claiming they would have run faster if they had known the
race was so short.

Another Tarahumara sport is a kickball game that is
played on fields ranging in length from two to 12 miles and
can last from six hours to days.

The hostile climate and poor soil fertility make the
existence of the Tarahumara culture an “ecological marvel.”
Their main crops are corn, pumpkins and beans, plus goat
meat provide only a 1,200 to 1,800 calorie diet - they did
not speak Spanish; in 1970 their total population was only
40,000.

The Tarahumara love to celebrate and are famous also
for drinking and eating feasts called tutuguri. These events
are described by the National Geographic as “a cacophony of
chanting, fiddles, rattles, guitars, stomping dances, shouts,
raucous laughter, and howling dogs. Both men and women
dropped out of the dancing lines to drink corn beer (rarely
drank anything stronger) and soon were staggering about in


 

a stupor.” It is estimated that a minimum of 200 pounds of
corn are required by each family for the annual party.

Following the Jim Fixx running craze in America,
legends of the Tarahumara health and longevity prospered,
although, reliable records are few and far between.

The Vilcabamba

Ecuador's star-shaped “Sacred Valley of Longevity”
(Vilcabamba) is actually five valleys that converge and sit
between two Andean Mountains at 12,434 feet above sea
level. The western skyline of Vilcabamba is dominated by the
summit of Mandango, the tallest mountain whose glaciers
supply the mineral rich “Glacial Milk” that is used to irrigate
the terraced fields.

The “Glacial Milk” originating from the high peaks of
Podocarpus National Park pours into the Rio Yambala which
converges with the Chamba River, the waters of which are
also used for irrigation.

In 1971, a census revealed nine people over 100 years of
age for every 819 people or an astounding one centenarian per
100 people - this gave Vilcabamba a centenarian population
almost 400 times that of the United States. Vilcabamba has a
historical record of good health and longevity dating back to
the Incas, these claims are backed by religious (Roman Catholic
churches) and civil records dating back to the 18th century.

Popular interest in Vilcabamba was ignited in 1956
during a press conference in New York City when Javier
Pereira, age 167, was introduced by the syndicated newspaper
feature, Believe It or Not.

Miguel Carpio, at age 123, was the oldest living
Vilcabamban found at the census - he still smoked, drank red
wine and “chased women.”

===== Screenshot (179) =====

ae ED

The staple diet of the Vilcabamba Indian includes
corn, beans, goat meat, chicken, eggs, milk, cheese and a
soup, known as repe that is made from bananas, beans, white
cheese, salt and lard. Their average total calorie intake is 1,200
to 1,800 calories per day.

“Dr. Weston A. Price, (while working in the high
Andes of Peru) chanced to notice that each individual
carried a little bag, of which all seemed to be particularly
careful. Out of curiosity he looked into the bags and
discovered that they contained kelp. When he asked where
they had got kelp so far from the ocean, they said calmly
that they got it from the ocean. This surprised him since
it took a month to make the trip to and from the coast.
What did they use the kelp for? ‘To guard the heart, they
explained to him.””

D.C. Jarvis
Folk Medicine

The Titicaca

Gucilaso de la Vega, whose father was Spanish and
whose mother was an Inca princess, recorded (1609) the
legend of the beginnings of the Titicaca peoples:

“Our Father, the Sun, seeing that men lived like
wild animals, took pity on them, and sent to earth a son
and daughter of his, in order that they might teach man the
knowledge of our Father, the Sun, and that they might know
how to cultivate plants and grains and make use of the fruits

of the earth like men and not beasts. With these orders and
mandate, our Father the Sun placed his son and daughter in
Lake Titicaca.”

Thus as legend holds, the first Incas came to Earth on
an island in Lake Titicaca, to start what was to become the
most advanced civilization in pre-Columbian America.

Titicaca is found in the Andean highlands of Peru at
12,506 feet above seas level. The main city of Titicaca is Puno
(population 32,000); the people of the Altiplano (high Plains)
are divided into the pure Indians and the “mestizos” who are
a mix of Indian and Spanish.

The treeless hills that form the Altiplano surrounding
Lake Titicaca are covered with “pata pata,” the stone terraces
built by the Incas to provide level farming areas, and as
catchments for the mineral rich “Glacial Milk” from the great
Andean Mountain, Mt. Cardillera Real with which they
irrigated their crops.

Because of the extreme elevation of the Altiplano,
maize or Indian corn wouldn’t grow in the Titicaca region,
instead the Incas grew tubers never before seen by the Spanish;
“They supplement the lack of bread with some roots they
call “papas” (potatoes) and which produce under the earth,”
noted Father Acosta. When introduced into Europe by the
Spanish, the potato became more valuable than all the gold in
the world. The Inca also invented a freeze drying technique to
save harvested potatoes for the winter, described in 1609 by a
Spanish Friar:

“To keep potatoes from rotting, they leave them out
for many months to freeze. After they have been repeatedly
frozen, they tread gently on them, to press out the moisture
they contain. When they have been well squeezed, they
are placed in the sun until they are thoroughly dry. In this
manner, the potato keeps for a long time, and it changes its
name to chunci.”

===== Screenshot (180) =====

 

The original Indians of the Lake Titicaca region are
the Aymara; the Quechua, who still speak the language
of the Incas, were descendants of Inca slaves after the Inca
occupation in the 12th century.

The grinding of the massive glaciers of the Cordillera
Real, the towering Andean Mountain that reaches over 20,000
feet produces a mineral rich “Glacial Milk” that is used to
irrigate the terraced fields that the Titicaca grow their grains
and vegetables in and the wind up in Lake Titicaca itself.

When Francisco Pizarro arrived in 1532, the Inca
Empire was in the midst of a great civil war, making it easy
for a small band of Spaniards to conquer a kingdom of over
3,000 miles in length, reaching from Ecuador to Chile. In the
early days of the Inca culture, the word Inca referred only to
the king and not to a race or nation.

The ancient ancestors of the Titicaca worshipped many
gods; today they have settled on a patchwork of ancient gods
and Catholicism that was injected by the Spanish.

Lake Titicaca itself is over 3,200 square miles in surface
area, 122 miles long and 47 miles wide - when viewed from a
plane the outline of the lake looks like a jaguar ready to pounce
ona rabbit, thus the name Titicaca (translates to “Rock of the
Puma” or Jaguar). The effluent or Rio Desaquadero from Lake
Titicaca flows from the “head of the rabbit.”

The Incas used irrigation ditches to channel water
from the lake itself as well as from the “Glacial Milk” to water
and remineralize, along with the wood and animal manure
ash (plant minerals) enriched compost, their well manicured
terraces.

Like all Alpine habitats, the Lake Titicaca area has very
little variety when it comes to wildlife. Father Acosta noted in
his early writings that the lake held only two kinds of fish, a
large catfish and a minnow.

The catfish made up a major source of animal protein
for the local Indian tribes. In 1939, trout were released into
the lake and to no one’s great surprise, the trout flourished.


 

Within two years the Indians began landing lake trout
weighing more than 25 pounds - the highly mineralized
waters of Lake Titicaca produced a bounty beyond anyone’s
wildest dreams.

The Isla de Sol, the mythical birthplace of the Incas is
located 40 miles into Lake Titicaca. Manco Capac and Mama
Ocllo appeared approximately 1,100 A.D.

The Incas, much like the Romans, were at the same
time conquerors and lawmakers, engineers and very firm
taskmasters - “their totalitarian state was a benevolent
dictatorship.” Whenever the Incas conquered a culture, they
basically left it intact, requiring only a tax in the form of salt
and that the conquered people worship the Sun god. No one
went hungry or naked but nothing was left to free will.

When the first Aymaras came to Lake Titicaca, they
found other Indians, the Urus, already living on floating
islands made of Tartara reeds. “Whole villages of Urus, were
to be found on the lake living on their rafts of Tartara,” wrote
the Jesuit Father Jose’ de Acosta in 1590. The Uru still live long
healthy lives today and still refuse to give up their Tartara
islands.

The quinoa, a grain of Titicaca begins to ripen in late
April or May, it is used as Europeans use wheat; barley is also
grown. At 15,400 feet, the barley never ripens to seed heads so
the Indians use it for cattle feed. The Titicaca shepherds live
and work at 17,000 feet.

The Inca “Cattle” were in fact llamas or New World
camels that feed on moss and lichens.

“There is nothing in Peru of greater value and utility
than the cattle of the land, which our people call “sheep of the
Indies, but the Indian in their tongue call llama,” wrote Father
Acosta.

When the Spanish discovered the Maya in the 1500s,
they noted that everyone drank liquid chocolate.

The Incasalso built 10,000 miles of stone roads to provide
rapid communication around Lake Titicaca. Messengers were

===== Screenshot (181) =====

stationed every 1.5 miles and ran messages in relay, much like
a human pony express - these warriors could relay messages
over 150 miles per day. Inca nobles also used the roads to
reach the Isla del Sol to worship the Sun god.

The Costa Rican Nicoya

A Costa Rican man at age 60 has twice the chance of
reaching age 90 as does a man living in the United States,
France or even Japan. It is also known that when a Costa
Rican man reaches 90 years of age, he can expect with high
probability that he will live another 4.4 years.

Vital information of every person born in Costa Rica
has been collected since 1888, so conformation of age is quite
easy!

Costa Rica spends only 15% of what the United States
does on health care, yet its citizens live considerably longer
- longer and healthier lives than people in any other country
on Earth! Specifically the greatest concentration of Costa
Rican centenarians was found in a cluster of villages scattered
around the Nicoya Peninsula.

The Nicoya have a higher fertility rate than other
centenarian groups, they are taller, weighed less and had
slightly higher blood pressures, yet most smoke.

An examination of the life expectancy and mortality
rates showed that one region in the northwest of the country,
including the 80 mile long Nicoya Peninsula that was
significantly different from any other (in that area, people die
of cancer - particularly stomach cancer - at a rate 23% lower
than in the rest of Costa Rica).

Specifically the oldest of the old in the Nicoya Peninsula
were found only in the middle segment of the peninsula - they
were not found in any significant numbers in the southern or


 

northern segments or along the Pacific Coast.

The Nicoyan diet consists of corn, beans, cheese, pork,
eggs, lard, garden vegetables, yams, garlic, onions, nanpi’ anda
plethora of fruit (papaya, mango, chico zapote, oranges, lemon,
banana, jobo (Spondias), guayabo (Psidium guajava), caimito
(Chrysophyllum cainito), papaturro (Coccoloba), maranon and
anona), much of it grown in and around their yards.

The Chorotega Indians, the original inhabitants of the
Nicoya, thrived before the Spanish arrived in 1522. They lived
in simple thatched-hut communities ringed by vegetable
gardens, fruit trees, which then are ringed by milpas or fields
of corn and beans.

The Chorotega were subsistence farmers who
employed slash-and-burn agriculture, they grew maize and
many varieties of beans.

The corn is dried, then, soaked in water with wood ash
and lime; the corn is then ground, made into dough, patted
into tortillas and served with beans and squash.

A University of California Press report - Nicoya: A
Cultural Geography, dated 1958 and written by Philip Wagner,
an anthropologist, read:

“The day of the country people begins before sunrise,
when the women rise to prepare coffee. The family meets
about dawn to take its cup of black coffee, or coffee with milk,
heavily sweetened, and perhaps to eat a cold tortilla. The time
from dawn to eight o’clock is for chores and beginning the
day’s work. At eight there may be a complete breakfast with
rice and beans and eggs. In seasons of heavy work the men
take with them to the fields tortillas with gallo pinto (rice and
beans cooked in pork fat). Work may end on very hot days at
twelve, or at two in the afternoon. The workers come home
from the fields or the woods and wait about an hour for their
meal.

The midday meal often begins with a pot of soup
in which there are a few bits of meat, fat, boiled plantains,

===== Screenshot (182) =====

 

tisquisque or yucca, and perhaps a few greens. After the
soup come rice and beans, usually accompanied by fried
eggs (cooked in lard). On occasion there may also be some
vegetables: pipidin or ayote (Cucurbita moschata) or calabaza
(Lagenaria), cabbage, the flower of pinuela (Bromelia penguin)
or some other wild product. Meat sometimes appears on
even the poorest table, and there is usually cuajada, a milk
curd. Tortillas come with this meal and afterward the men sip
heavily sweetened black coffee, made from local berries or
from the mashed seeds of nanju (Hibiscus esculentus).

The evening meal is simpler, since the custom is to
spend the afternoon in idleness, and appetites are less hearty.
Rice and beans, tortillas, and perhaps eggs are served just at
dusk.”

The Costa Ricans take daily “sun-bathes” for 15 to 30
minutes each day by exposing their faces, arms and legs.

The Nicoyan water supply percolated up through the
Nicoya limestone bedrock and contains high levels of calcium,
magnesium and other minerals. “The water is so hard and
has such high levels of calcium and magnesium that it had
to be diluted by 50% with distilled water just so it could be
tested.”

Nicoyans typically cook on a wood-burning fogon, the
traditional Chorotega clay oven. The wood ashes are saved to
fertilize the garden and to add “culinary ashes” to the soaking
corn kernels to make tortillas.


Ne eee

“The Hunza soil is almost a manufactured soil.
Every solitary thing that can serve as food for vegetables,
field crops and fruit trees are diligently collected, stored
and distributed in rationed equality for every square
foot of the hundreds of terraces. Sunken compost pits are
conveniently located, and into them go ashes from cooking
and heating fires, inedible parts of vegetables, pulverized
animal bones, dead leaves, rotten wood and the collected
manure of animals.”

Dr. Allen E. Banik and
Renee Taylor
Hunza Land

drinking and irrigation purposes (the fields are flooded,
when the water soaks into the soil, a thick layer of mineral
silt or ‘rock flour’ is left on top of the soil-this silt is plowed
into the soil before it ts planted).”

John H. Tobe

HUNZA

“Land of milk and honey.”

Doctors practicing in Hunza today have concluded that
the main cause of premature death amongst the Hunzakut
people is from accidents, from landslides or falls from the shear
rock walls. Infant mortality from infectious diseases have been
reported; however, the average individual surviving past one
year of age lives beyond the age of eighty, in exc ellent health
and significant numbers live into their nineties and past 100
years.

Alexander Leaf, “The Hunzakuts
eat an average of 1,900 calories daily, which are composed
36 grams of fat and 354 grams of

According to Dr.

of 50 grams of protein,
carbohydrates.”

Adding to their already Calorie Restricted diet was
the fact that by late spring of each year the Hunzakut’s
food reserves ran low, producing what they call “Starvation
Springtime.” Meals are reduced to a frequency of two to three
meals a week.

The original research into the secrets of the Hunza


 

health and longevity was done by Dr. Robert McCarrison,
personal physician to the King of England. His extensive
work was published in 1927.

During the 1890s, Robert McCarrison completed his
medical studies at Oxford University. In 1901 at the age of
23, he traveled to India, and became a regimental officer in
the Foreign Department in 1902. He was recognized for
identifying the sand fly as the carrier of the Three Day Fever

 

of Citral. In 1904, McCarrison was appointed to the post of
army surgeon to the nine villages of the Gilgit Agency, where
he served until 1911.

Soon after his appointment, McCarrison began to do
research on the endemic goiter that was common throughout
his post. Finding that eight villages had a goiter problem and
one did not, he was soon able to discover the relationship of

| goiter to an iodine deficiency.

McCarrisonrelated the remarkable healthand longevity
to the quality of the Hunza diet. A summary review of Hunza
was published in Studies in Deficiency Diseases (1921):

Myown experience provides an example ofa race unsurpassed
in perfection of physique and in freedom from disease in general,
whose sole food consists to this day of grains, vegetables and fruit
with a certain amount of milk, and butter, and goat's meat only on
feast days. I refer to the people of the state of Hunza, situated in the
extreme northernmost point of India...When the severe nature of
the winter in that part of the Himalayas (Karakorum) is considered,
and the fact that their housing accommodations and conservancy
arrangements are of the most primitive, it becomes obvious that the
enforced restriction to the unsophisticated food stuffs of nature is
compatible with long life, continued vigor, and perfect physique.

In his classic nutritional study McCarrison used 1,189
laboratory weanling rats which he divided into three diet
groups:

===== Screenshot (184) =====
 


 

Group 1 - These rats were fed a typical Hunza diet
of whole-grain chapattis, butter, legumes, raw carrots, raw
cabbage, whole milk, eggs, meat, water and they were exposed
to sunlight daily.

When the rats reached the age of 27 months (the
equivalent of 55 years in humans), they were killed and
examined: the rats were free of overt disease, there had not
been any early mortality; the rats were alert yet lived together
without aggression.

Group 2 - These rats were fed a typical “Bengali”
diet of white rice, legumes, vegetables, and spices. These
rats soon developed diseases of the lungs, nose, ears, eyes,
gastrointestinal tract, urinary system, reproductive system,
skin, blood, lymphatic system, nervous system and heart.
The Bengali fed rats also suffered from hair loss, muscular
weakness, ulcers, boils, tooth loss and they became agitated
and aggressive.

Group 3 - McCarrison fed his third group of rat pups a
typical working class English diet of white bread, margarine,
sugared tea, boiled vegetables, canned meat and cheap jams.
These rats developed “diseases of the lungs, diseases of the
stomach and intestines, and diseases of the nerves - diseases
from which one in every three sick persons among the
insured classes in England and Wales suffers. Additionally
these rats were nervous and apt to bite their attendants; they
lived unhappily together, and by the sixteenth day of the
experiment they began to kill and eat the weaker ones among
them.”

The story of the Hunza began 2,300 years ago with
Alexander the Great. Alexander was the first born son of King
Philip of Masedonia. Legend has said that “the night before
the consummation of Philip’s marriage with Olympias, she
dreamed that a thunderbolt fell upon her body and kindled


 

a great fire whose divided flames dispersed themselves all
about and were then extinguished.”

Philip dreamed “that he sealed up his wife’s body with
a seal whose impression was the figure of a lion.” Some of
the alchemists and diviners interpreted Philip’s dream as a
warning to “look narrowly to his wife.;” however, Aristander
of Telnessus assured Philip that the meaning of his dream
was “that the queen was with child of a boy, who would one
day prove as stout and courageous as a lion.”

Alexander was born on the sixth of Hecatombaeon,
which is called Lous by the Masedonians - the same day that
the Temple of Diana at Ephesus was burned to the ground.
The temple caught fire and was destroyed while its priestess
was assisting at the birth of Alexander. All of the diviners
in Ephesus ran to and fro beating their faces and predicting

\“that this day had brought forth something that would prove

destructive and fatal to all Asia.”
Following Philip’s military victory and occupation of
Potidea, “he received three messages simultaneously:

1) His commander Parmenio had overthrown the
Illyrians in a Great battle;

2) his race horse had won the course at the Olympic
Games;

3) and his wife Olympia had given birth to a son -
Alexander!”

Philip was overjoyed and he was assured by the
diviners “that a son whose birth coincided with three such
successes could not fail to be invincible.”

“Alexander was fair and of light color, passing into
ruddiness in his face and upon his breast.” From his early
childhood Alexander was energetic and eager, he displayed
a love of glory and pursued glory with an endless spirit. As a

===== Screenshot (185) =====

 

teenager, Alexander was very bold; when he was asked if he
would compete in the Olympic Games, he replied, “1 will if I
might have kings to run against!”

While still a boy, Alexander entertained visiting
rovalty in the stead of his father. The visitors included the
ambassadors of the king of Persia. Alexander asked, “What
is the nature of the road to inner Asia, the character of your
king, and how does he carry himself to his enemies? What
forces is he able to bring into the field?” His forwardness and
lofty purpose appeared early in his life and the visiting royalty
“looked upon Philip’s famed ability as nothing compared to
that of his son Alexander.”

Whenever Alexander heard that father had
conquered a town of importance or had won some notable
victory, instead of being happy, Alexander would tell his
companions, “father would anticipate everything and leave
him and them no opportunities of performing illustrious
actions.”

A Thessalian horse trader brought King Philip a
fiery war horse called Bucephalus, offering him at a price of
thirteen talents. When Philip and his party went into the field
to try him out, they found him so vicious and unmanageable
that he reared up when they mounted him and would not
so much as endure the voice of any of Philip’s grooms. They

his

were just about to lead Bucephalus away as totally useless
and intractable, when Alexander said, “I could manage this
horse, better than others do!”

“Do you reproach those who are older than yourself,
as if you knew more and were better able to manage him than
they? And what if you do not,” said Philip, “what will you
forfeit for your rashness?”

“1 will pay the whole price of the horse,”
Alexander.

replied

At this boy’s answer, the king’s entire company
began to roar with laughter. As soon as wagers were settled,
Alexander ran to Bucephalus, took hold of the bridle, and

 


 

turned him directly into the sun (Alexander had observed the
horse was shying from his own shadow).

Alexander let the horse move forward a little, still
keeping the reins in hand, he stroked him gently; when the
horse grew eager and fiery, “he let fall his upper garment
softly and with one nimble leap securely mounted him; when
he was seated, little by little he drew in the bridle and curbed
him without either striking or spurring him.

Presently, when he found him free from all
rebelliousness and only impatient for the course, he let him
go at full speed, inciting him with a commanding voice and
also urging with his heel.”

King Philip and his court looked on, first in silence,
then nervousness until, seeing him turn at the end of the
course and return rejoicing and waving in triumph - they all
burst into cheers and applause.

Philip, shedding tears of joy and pride, kissed
Alexander as the boy dismounted and said, “Oh, my son, look
for a kingdom equal to and worthy of thyself, for Macedonia
is too little for thee!”

Philip now looked upon the instruction of Alexander
to be of greater importance than to be entrusted to ordinary
masters of music, poetry and typical school subjects, and
to require as Sophocles stated, “The bridle and the rudder,
too.”

Philip sent for Aristotle, the most learned and most
celebrated Greek philosopher and scholar of his time. Philip
rewarded Aristotle with “a munificence proportionate with
the private tutelage of his son, prince Alexander.”

Aristotle taught Alexander his doctrine of morals and
politics, herbal medicine, alchemy and abstract and profound
theories only shared by philosophers amongst themselves.
Alexander as a student had a feverish inclination to practice
medicine; for whenever his friends were sick, he would
“prescribe healing diets and medication (herbs and minerals)
proper to their disease.”

===== Screenshot (186) =====

 

Alexander loved Aristotle no less than if he were his
own father, “as he had received life from one, so the other had
taught him to live well.”

While in Asia, Alexander heard that Aristotle had
publicly published several of the oral treatises normally
reserved for communications between great thinkers and he
was disturbed enough to write him a letter:

“ Alexander to Aristotle, greeting,

You have not done well to publish

your books of oral doctrines; for what is

there now that we excel in, if those

things which we have been particularly
instructed in be laid open to all? For

my part, I assure you, I had rather excel

in the knowledge, of what is excellent,

than in the extent of my power and dominion,

Farewell!”

Later, while King Philip was absent on his extended
expedition against the Byzantines, he left Alexander, now
only 16 years old, “as his lieutenant in Macedonia committing
the charge of his seal to him.”

Alexander refused to sit idling away the time, so he
declared war on the Maedi and reduced them to slaves after
conquering their capital city by storm - not too surprising,
Alexander renamed the conquered city Alexandropolis!

During the battle of Chaeronea, which Philip fought
against the Greeks, Alexander was the first man in the charge
against the fierce and sacred Thebams guards near the
Cephisus River.

Despite King Philip’s pride and love for his brave son,

 


 

they soon became enemies. The straw that broke the camel’s
back was the marriage of King Philip to Cleopatra, the child
queen of Egypt. When Cleopatra’s uncle Attalus, toasted the
marriage and asked the gods for “a lawful successor to the
kingdom,” Alexander became furious and threw a cup at him
shouting, “you villain; what am I then a bastard?” Philip,
took to Attalus’ defense, rose up from his bride’s side and
would have run Alexander through with a sword but in his
drunken rage he slipped and fell to the floor. Being the young
cock, Alexander couldn’t resist mocking his father, “see there,
the man who makes preparations to pass out of Europe into
Asia, overturned in passing from one seat to another.” Philip
and Alexander were soon reconciled by Demaratus, a family
friend.

The marriage between Philip and Cleopatra was not
an affair of the heart or the product of lust for a flat-chested
gangly pre-teenage girl, but rather a political treaty between
Macedonia and Egypt - the Macedonians wanted and got
exclusive rights to the highly mineralized wheat from the Nile
flood plains, wheat that was legendary in providing strength
and stamina to the armies that ate it - Macedonia needed this
Egyptian wheat to support its armies for the coming invasion
of Asia! The Egyptians wanted and got a peace treaty from
the Macedonians exempting them from participating in the
war with Asia and protection by the Macedonians from the
Asian hordes. It was a marriage made at the bargain table.

However, the father and son reconciliation was short-lived. Pixodorus, viceroy of Caria, sent an emissary to make a
matchbetweenhisoldestdaughterand Philip’sson Arrhidaeus.
Alexander’s contemporaries and friends convinced him that
Philip, by a political marriage and important alliance was
going to transfer the Macedonian Kingdom to his brother
Arrhidaeus.

Alexander, out of alarm, sent Thessalus as his own
representative into Caria to urge Pixodorus to change his mind
and offer his daughter into marriage to Alexander himself, so

===== Screenshot (187) =====
that Alexander might be his son-in-law. Pixodorus responded
that Alexander’s offer was much more favorable to him.

Philip, enraged at Alexander's offer, “chastised
Alexander bitterly” for being unworthy of the power he was
to inherit, as to marry a low Carian, who at best was a slave of
a barbarous prince. Philip further wrote to the Corinthians to
send Thessalus (Alexander’s messenger) to him in chains; he
also banished Alexander’s closest friends for their part in the
intrigue.

Soon after these terrible events, a Macedonian named
Pausanias was stripped of his post and properties in Egypt
by Cleopatra and her uncle Attalus. Pausanias, unable to get
the decision overturned and himself reinstated by Philip,
became enraged and assassinated Philip. The guilt for the
murder of Philip was laid at the feet of Alexander's mother,
Olympias, who was reported to have incited young Pausanias
to revenge.

Some accusing fingers also pointed to Alexander,
however, these suspicions were dispelled after he captured
Pausanias and his conspirators and had them summarily
executed. Alexander was now 20 years old.

Alexander inherited a troubled Macedonia, besieged
on all sides with restless and dangerous enemies. The
barbarians that had been conquered by Philip’s armies were
anxious to be governed by their own princes. Philip had also
been victorious over the Greeks, however, he hadn’t had time
to consolidate his battlefield gains and win them over to the
Macedonian law and culture all he had conquered before
being assassinated.

Alexander’s response was to declare war and he
immediately invaded the heart of the barbarian territory as
far east as the Danube River, conquering and overthrowing
Syrmus, the king of the Tribillians.

The Tribillians revolted and became allies with the
Athenians against Alexander. Alexander ordered his armies
into a forced march through the pass of Thermopylae; he sent

the following message to the Greek Demosthenes, “You called
me a child when I was in the country of the Tribillians and a
youth when I was in Thessaly - I will appear as a man before
the walls of Athens.”

When Alexander arrived at the walls of Thebes he only
demanded the authors of the rebellion - Phoenix and Prothytes
and declared a general amnesty to all who would submit to
Macedonian law. The Thebans responded by demanding
Philotas Antipater be surrendered to them and invited the
Macedonian army to become Greeks. Alexander and his
army immediately fell on Athens, sacked the city and burned
it to the ground; then to terrify the people of Greece into
submission he publicly executed 6,000 of the Theban leaders
and sold 30,000 of the lesser members of the Athenian Court
into slavery - only the priests and those who had historically

| been friends of the Macedonians were spared.

The ambassadors of Greece assembled at the Isthmus
and voted to join their armies with Alexander’s in his march
against the Persians and to prove their allegiance, they voted
Alexander their general of the allied armies.

Alexander’s armies were actually the smaller of the
forces in the alliance - he commanded 30,000 foot soldiers and
4,000 cavalry. After gaining a positive “You will be victorious”
from the head priests of Athens, Alexander marched northeast
towards Persia (Iran).

Darius, the great warlord king of Persia, had his
armies camped at the eastern banks of the Granicus River -
the Gateway to Asia. It seemed that Alexander would have
to fight at the very entrance to Asia when he reached the
western banks of the river in the evening. Parmenio, military
advisor to Alexander urged him to wait until morning as dark
was but moments away. Alexander immediately spurred his
horse into the river and called his army to him:

“They advanced against whole showers of darts thrown
from atop the almost sheer eastern bank. Despite his lack of

===== Screenshot (188) =====

 

prudence Alexander prevailed, he and 13 troops of cavalry
gained the top of the bank where they engaged in hand to
hand combat.

Alexander, easily recognized, was attacked from all
sides, his cuirass was pierced by ajavelin. Two Persion officers,
Rhoesaces and Spithridates set on him at once, he dodged them
and broke his spear striking at Rhoesaces so that he was forced
to defend himself with a dagger; while engaged in this hand-to-hand combat, the other Persian officer, Spithridates rode
his horse full tilt at Alexander and struck him such a blow on
the helmet with his battle axe that he sheared off the plumed
crest and the edge of the blade clipped off a large shock of
blond hair. As Spithridates raised up in his stirrups to deliver
a second and probably fatal blow, Alexander’s lieutenant - the
Black Clitus, pierced the Persian’s body through and through
with his spear at the same time as Alexander killed Rhoesaces
with his dagger.”

While the advance cavalry were engaged in the
dangerous hand to hand combat, the Macedonian foot soldiers
crossed the river and forming a great pincher advanced into
the battle. When the Persian advance force turned and fled,
they left 20,000 foot soldiers and 2,500 cavalry of their own
dead on the river bank. Alexander had lost only 34, of whom
five were foot soldiers and the balance cavalry.

The overwhelming victory at the Granicus River
resulted in the surrender of Sardis, the capital of the Persian
Maritime province as well as hundreds of other small towns
and villages. Only the two cities of Halicarniossus and Miletus
held out; Alexander destroyed them by force.

While Alexander was deciding what route to take into:

the heart of Persia, an ancient copper plate was washed up
on the flooded banks of a spring near the city of Xanthus in
Lycia. The inscription on the plate’s edge stated, “the time
will come when the Persian Empire would be destroyed by
the Greecians.” Enthused by this finding, Alexander took his


 

armies to destroy Cilicia and Phoenicia and then along the
seacoast to Pamphyia; normally this coastline was rugged and
pounded by great waves and surf, but an unusually low low-tide allowed Alexander and his armies a speedy unhampered
passage.

Alexander conquered the Pisidians and the Phrygians
at their capital of Gordium, the home of the ancient Midas.
Alexander came across the fabled chariot fastened to a pillar
by braided cords made from a cherry tree. The legend stated,
“Whosoever should untie this, for him was reserved the
empire of the world.” Alexander, unable to untie the knot, cut
it through with his sword. He then advanced to Pophiagornia
and Cappadocia, both of which he destroyed.

When Darius, king of Persia, heard that Memnon, his
finest officer along with thousands of cavalry was killed in the
battles, he decided to carry on the war with the Macedonians
in the upper mountainous provinces of Asia. Darius
marched from Susa very confident, number one because of
his superiority of numbers which totaled 600,000 men and
number two, in a dream he saw the Macedonian army on fire
and Alexander waiting for him clad as the child he saw when
he visited Philip’s court ten years earlier.

Darius’ confidence grew stronger as Alexander and
his armies remained encamped in Cilicia so Alexander could
recover from an illness - Darius mistook this delay as a sign of
cowardice in Alexander.

A Macedonian general named Amyntas and two other
generals married to Persian women were in contact with
the Persian king Darius, giving him Alexander’s proposed
battle plans. When Amyntas learned that Darius was going to
ambush Alexander in the mountain passes he advised the king
to keep his larger army in the open plain, where his superior
numbers could be brought to advantage. Darius, instead of
heeding this advice, said he was afraid that Alexander would
flee and escape to fight another day; however, Amyntas
stated:

“That fear is needless, for assure yourself that far from

Amyntas’ advice to Darius was to no avail, as Darius
immediately marched into Cilicia at the same time as
Alexander advanced into Syria to confront him. Passing one
another in the night, they turned their armies around towards
each other; Alexander took up position in the passes using
the sea, the mountains and the Pinarus River to force Darius
to split his forces. Not to allow himself to be outflanked by
such a superior number, Alexander strung out his right flank
much wider than his left flank and fought there himself in the
forward where he was wounded in the thigh by an arrow.
After putting the Persian army to flight, Alexander counted
110,000 dead of Darius’ army - unfortunately Darius was not
found amongst the dead.

Learning that Amyntas and his two fellow generals
had Persian wives and had most probably given council to
Darius regarding this last battle, Alexander sent members of
his personal body guard to execute the three generals and
their wives.

The generals in turn learned of the execution orders
and thus forewarned were able to fight their way free and
with their three wives, fled northeast following the Indus
River and eventually crossing through the pass of Babusar
and across the 3,000 foot deep chasm of the Hunza River.

The pass into Hunza is 13,700 foot high and is only
passable during the three summer months. This was part
of the trade route taken by Marco Polo on his return from
Cathay in 1326; over the 3,000 foot deep Hunza River gorge
via a suspended bridge made of goat hair.

The three generals and their wives formed the nucleus
of a warrior tribe that initially flourished by preying on and
raiding the trade routes and caravans that flowed from China
to India and back again.

The Hunzas were not always known as Hunzas; up
until the turn of the 20th century, the valley was known as the
Kanjut and the people known as the Kanjuts. When Indian
Girkis tried to invade Kanjut they gave the valley and the
people in the name Hunza, because all of the people in the
valley were allied and united as arrows in a quiver. In the
Burushaski language (a mix of Masedonian and Persian) the
name Hunza translates to “arrow.”

The indigenous plant life of the Hunza Valley was
rather limited. Ninety-nine percent of the valley was bare rock
and the growing season limited to 45 days. Later, cultivated
plants included barley, millet, wheat, buckwheat, potatoes,
turnips, carrots, beans, peas, beans, pumpkins, tomatoes,
melons, onions, garlic, cabbage, spinach, cauliflower, apricots,
mulberries, walnuts, apples, plums, peaches, cherries and

pears. Pomegranate trees are scattered throughout the

valley.

The Hunzas consume milk, buttermilk, yogurt and
butter (which they put into their tea and use as a cooking
shortening). Hunza children are breast-fed until two years of
age.

A large variety of indigenous wildlife (i-e.- ibex, or
“Markhors,” Marco Polo sheep, geese, ducks, pheasants and
partridge) provided the early Hunza with meat (the Hunza
do not eat pork because they are muslim).

The Hunza salt supply is mined from hills near the
Shimshal and Muztagh Rivers and used in their tea and for
cooking in its raw brown state (the color originates from the
trace minerals included in the salt deposit when the seas dried

up).

' Maps refer to this Karakorum Range at the junction of
many countries as the Pamirs - the Afghanistan Pamir; the
Russian Pamir; the Chinese Pamir, etc.

Pamir translated from the Persian language becomes
“Roof of the World. “Roof of the World,”
however, Lhasa is at an elevation of only 13,000 feet and is
Saaunded by summits only 18,000 feet high or less.

A few miles above the junction of the pearl gray
waters of the Hunza River and the Gilgit River in a village
called Secundersbad; it is here that the Hunza Valley begins;
the Hunza Valley follows the Hunza River up to its sources
beyond Misgar, almost on the Chinese border where it springs
from glacial torrents spewing from under the 5,000 square
mile Ultar Glacier.

The Hunza Valley is barely 200 miles long. When the
original generals from Alexander's ar my arrived in the Hunza

Valley, there were no naturally fertile v valleys in Hunza as
compared with the Willamette Valley in Oregon. The Hunza
Valley was originally bare rock, the manufactured “soil” only
being carried basket by basket up the 3,000 foot gorge walls
and placed in hand crafted stone terraces that were designed
to level and increase the growing surfaces.

This man made soil is continuously replaced again by
hand from the mineral laden silt dredged up by hand from
the bed of the Hunza River 3,000 feet below, irrigation with
Glacial Milk (laden with a suspended colloidal mineral silt)and
fertilized with wood ash (plant mineral) containing compost.

Then there was the problem of water in the barren “safe
haven.” Fortunately, one of the Macedonian generals was a
military engineer. He was able to locate a year round source of
water roaring from under the Ultar G lacier 50 miles S away.

The Ultar Glacier originates on the 25,500 foot high
Mount Rakaposhi. He was able to design and construct a

” Tibet is often called


 

gravity propelled aquaduct which carried water for drinking
and irrigation.

The water originating from under the millions of tons
of ice grinding on the parent rock of Mount Rakaposhi was so
rich with minerals that it was bluish white, so white that the
original people called it “Glacial Milk.” For generation after
generation, crop after crop and year after year for more than
2,300 years the Hunza people have drunk and irrigated their
terraced fields with “Glacial Milk,” unwittingly assuring their
people of an optimal intake of the more than 60 minerals in
the “Glacial Milk” of the Ultar Glacier truely the “land of milk
and honey”!

Except for the polar glaciers, the Karakorum Range
has the largest collection of glaciers in the world. Millions of
tons of glacial ice (in some places hundreds or thousands of
feet thick) grind four inches of living mountain rock into a
fine “rock flour” each year, which is carried in suspension as
“Glacial Milk.” No rock, no mineral, no metal, no alchemy
is resistant enough to be exempt from the grinding forces of
these millions of tons of ice!

The “Glacial Milk” ejaculates from under the glaciers
in great white water “nullahs.” Thousands of glacial nullahs
merge and join the Hunza River, very quickly the Hunza
River joins the Gilget River; the two great glacial rivers now
married join the Indus River - this now thundering river
carries a colloidal suspension of all of the mineral building
blocks of the Karakorum Range.

All of the minerals known are found in these mountains.
“Tis this wealth of solvent minerals in liquid form that is
guided by means of conduits to the stone-terraced fields of
the valleys of Hunza and allowed to soak and penetrate into
their soil.”

There are sister mountain ranges paralleling the
Karakorums - the Hindukush (translates to “the Hindu
killers”) and the Himalayas have most of the worlds tallest
mountains; yet their “Glacial Milks” are not as complete or

===== Screenshot (192) =====

 

as dense with minerals as the “Glacial Milk” from the Ultar
Glacier that feeds and nourishes the Hunza Valley.

Glacial Milk is a mixed liquid, a solution of ionically
dissolved elements and a suspension of finely ground rock
dust (rock flour) shaved and ground from the living parent
rock of the mountain by glacial friction. The suspended
minerals in the Glacial Milk are referred to as “metallic
colloidal minerals.” The average particle size of these colloid
particles is 7,000 times smaller than a red blood cell - so small,
that they can only be visualized by an electron microscope.

The individual elements in “Glacial Milk” are not
found in their separate pure atomic form, but rather they are
found in aggregations or “mini-alloys” to form suspended
solute particles.

The presence of these aggregate particles (ranging in
size from one hundred thousandth to one ten millionth of a
centimeter in diameter) are only detected by chemical analysis
or the electron microscope.

The raw rock inorganic colloids cannot passively
commute through semi-permeable membranes and therefore
produce little or no osmotic pressure, depression of the
freezing point of water (unlike a salt solution) or elevation of
the boiling point. These molecular mini-alloys or aggregates
carry a uniform negative charge.

Plants - wild forms and crops suchas grains, vegetables,
fruits and nuts actively take up these inorganic metallic
colloids and convert them into intracellular (within the cell)
organic plant colloids - these organic plant colloids are the
form of minerals found in and used by all living cells of plants,
animals and humans.

Organic colloidal minerals derived from plants are up
to 98% available to humans as compared to as little as 8 to
12% availability of metallic minerals.

It is the eating of the plants rich in organic colloidal
minerals, that is the secret of health and longevity of the
cultures we have called the “age beaters!!!”


 

The serendipitous irrigation of their terraced fields with
the common denominator of a highly mineralized “Glacial
Milk” and the fertilization of their fields with an organic
compost rich with the recycled colloidal plant minerals found
in wood ashes from their heating and cooking fires is the
secret of the Hunzas’s long and fruitful lives.

Hunza Recipes*

The Hunzakuts use apricot oil for cooking; extra-virgin olive
can be used as a substitute.

 

Chapattis -

Hunzakuts eat these flat breads with meals, especially stew,
spinach and dahl.

5 cups whole-grain flour (wheat, rye, millet,
buckwheat)

Water at room temperature.

Olive oil or apricot oil

1. Mix the flour and a small amount of water (enough so that
the flour mixes with the water without becoming sticky) to
make dough, and knead for 5 minutes.

Okinawa

Originally, Okinawa was the Kingdom of the Ryukyus;
the 161 island archipelago is now a prefecture, or state of
Japan, 1,000 miles from Tokyo. In the United States Okinawa
is principally known for its part in WWII.

The Battle of Okinawa, the “typhoon of steel,” was
one of the longest and bloodiest of the WWII Pacific Theater
where some 70,000 U.S. marines, sailors and soldiers were
killed or wounded and 150,000 Okinawan civilians died - it
was the only battle fought on what was considered Japanese
home territory.

===== Screenshot (195) =====

 

As of 1996, Okinawans (the Hawaiians of Japan),
according to the WHO, were t the longest lived people of the
industrialized world. The centenarians of Okinawa have been
studied more intensely than any other long lived peoples on
Earth.

A 25 year The Okinawa Program,
launched by the identical twin Willcox brothers. Bradley
J. Willcox, who received his M.D. from Harvard School of
Medicine and his twin brother, D. Craig Willcox,
his Ph.D. in pharmacy along with Makoto Suzuki, M.D.,
PhD., a cardiologist from Okinawa International University
conducted the Okinawa Centenarian Study.

In Okinawa, individuals generally have a very high life
expectancy of 78 years for men and 86 years for women and
what is considered to be the greatest number of disability-free

long study, was

received

years of 72.3 men and 77.7 years for women.

Okinawans have a documented rate of centenarians at
5/10,000.

The Okinawan lifestyle (and diet), not genes is the
primary reason for their extraordinary health and longevity.
The Okinawan diet contains an average of 1,500 calories and
consists of boiled pork, chicken, eggs, dried minnows, miso
and fish soup, tofu, many types of garden and sea vegetables,
sea weed, kelp, onions, garlic, daikon, greens, purple sweet
potatoes, bitter melon and tubers grown in their own gardens;
antioxidant rich tumeric tea, green tea and awamori (sake -
rice wine) are the daily drinks.

Teaism

According to Kakuzo Okakura:

The historical isolation of Japan from the rest of the world, so
conducive tointrospection, has been highly favorable to the development
of “Teaism.” Our home and habits, costume and cuisine, porcelain,
lacquer, painting - our very literature - all have been subject to its
influence. No student of Japanese (or Okinawan) culture could ever
ignore its presence. It has permeated the elegance of nonle boudoirs,
and entered the abode of the humble. Our peasants have learned to
arrange flowers, our meanest labourrer to offer his salutation to the
rocks and waters. In our common parlance we speak of the man “with
no tea” in him, when he is insusceptible to the seriocomic interests of
the personal drama. Again we stigmatise the untamed asthete who,
regardless of the mundane tragedy, runs riot in the springtide of
emancipated emotions, as one “with too much tea” in him.

The Okinawan centenarians show a “greater appetite”
for mineral rich garden vegetables and dairy products than
the general population.

Seaweed

Sea water is a complex-water; universally around
the world analysis of sea water reveal that it contains 3.5%
dissolved inorganic salts and minerals. The oceans are located
in the lowest levels of the earth’s surface and are therefore the
catch basin into which mineral substances of every kind have
been drained by the many moving forces of nature.

The various elements and minerals, carried by wind
and water, as soon as they arrive in the oceans, join in the

===== Screenshot (196) =====
 


great milling of chemical changes, reacting, dissolving,
precipitating, re-dissolving, being integrated into living
organisms (plants and animals), travel and move through the
seasonal life cycles and then are set free again.

It is not a secret that every known reaction occurs in
the oceans, as they are stirred and mixed by wind, perennial
currents, frozen and thawed, exposed to light including entire
spectrums of intensities and colors.

The current chemical composition of the ocean is the
accumulation of millions of years of time and natural forces
- all things found in the earth ultimately finds their way into
the sea.

Chemists have realized, for centuries that, in the great
oceans there are dissolved all of the naturally occurring
elements and minerals. It is estimated that each cubic mile
of ocean contains 200,000,000 tons of elemental and mineral
compounds.

Kelp and algae growing in the sea takes up these
inorganic salts and converts them into useable organic forms.
When used as fertilizer (either as dried kelp or kelp ash) or
added directly to the diet as a vegetable, kelp is a significant
source of dietary plant minerals.

Seaweed, a staple Okinawan sea vegetable, is a generic
term for more than 2,500 varieties of aquatic plants that grow
in bodies of fresh and salt water. In common terms “sea weed”
is placed in groups according to color - i.e. - brown, red, green,
and blue-green.

Kombu is a form of kelp, it is classified as a brown
seaweed that is found inabundance in the cold sea surrounding
the northern Japanese island of Hokkaido. Kombu has been
very popular in Okinawa since the early 1800s, when it was
first gathered and brought to southern China. Kelp is very
popular with Okinawans and they eat approximately three
times the quantities that the Japanese eat. It tastes like pasta
mixed with seafood.


Noriisclassified as red seaweed, however, it looks black
when dried. This seaweed is typically used to wrap sushi and
rice balls, and as a seasoning for salads, soups and noodles.
Okinawans eat nori with breakfast as flat strips mixed with
rice. The Irish and Welsh eat nori as “laver” which is used to
make flat cakes.

Hijiki is a form of black seaweed that is served in short
bitter-tasting strips. Okinawans typically eat hijiki simmered
with vegetables and soybeans.

Wakame is a form of kelp that tastes like spinach

lasagna. It is commonly put in soups, salads and buckwheat
noodles (soba).

Se

Bitter melon, or Goya chample (Momordica charantia),
is a staple in the Okinawan diet which we ate a lot of while
visiting Okinawa. Goya or bitter melon, which looks like a
cucumber with warts, is thought to have been brought to
the Ryukyu Kingdom from old China during the Ming and
Manchu dy nasties. It is thought to have been used as a food
staple from around 1100 A.D. and as an added benefit it is
known to support healthy blood sugar levels in diabetics.

In 2003, a small group of our associates went to
Okinawa with us to determine the Okinawan’s health and
longevity secrets and where these exceedingly long lived
people obtained their nutritional minerals.

We first went to the ets of Agriculture and
learned that the soil of the entire 800 mile long Okinawa
archipelago of 161 islands was made up of red, yellow or black
clay - very similar to the red sandy clay that is characteristic of
the American state of Georgia.

In 1605, Sokan Noguni, an Okinawan returned home
from a visit to China with a sweet potato. It was the perfect
tuber for Okinawa’s rocky soil. Soon the sweet potato was
grown all over the island and by 1902, it provided 80% of the
total caloric intake of the people.

The Okinawan’s eventually erected a statue of Noguni,
who is respectfully referred to as Lord Sokan, the Imo (sweet
potato) King.

Many large studies, including the 25 year long Okinawa
project, were conducted in an attempt to determine their
secret of longevity. Because of the detailed completeness of
the questions in these studies, we felt we didn’t need to again
ask questions on diet, religion, exercise and professions that
had been asked over and over again - we took the information
in these studies to be correct. We decided that we were going
to ask only one question - “what did you do with the wood
ashes from your heating and cooking fires?”

We visited many of the Okinawa “old ones,” and shared

traditional meals of boiled pork, steamed sea vegetables, bitter

melon, sweet potatoes and squash with them. Drink included
green tea and rice wine.

We asked old men in their 90s and many over the age
of 100 years, “what did you do with the wood ashes from
your cooking and heating fires?” The old men replied, “I don’t
know.” We asked the same question of Okinawa women over
the age of 90 and 100 years of age, and they replied, “Everyone
imnows that, we put the wood ashes into our gardens and rice
paddies to fertilize our rice, vegetables and fruit.”

And then the lady centenarians shared something we
hadn’t thought of, “We also added rice straw ashes to our
noodle dough and rice as we cooked them because it added
a wonderful yellow color and enhanced the flavor.” Culinary
ashes (plant minerals) were part of the Okinawa secret!

The last thing we learned from the Okinawa old ones
and centenarians was that, “Each morning, an ash merchant
would appear and buy our excess ashes to sell to restaurants
and farmers.”

During the Edo period (1603 - 1867) of Japan, rice straw
was used to make a variety of products including sandals,
hats, mats and rope. The manufacture of products from rice
straw was a common cottage industry that was developed to
supplement the meager Japanese cash income.

The old and used straw items were burned as fuel in the
kitchen ovens and heating stoves - farmers and “ash traders”
purchased or traded for the ash which was then resold for
fertilizer. In this way, almost all of the 10 million tons of the
annual production of rice straw ash was returned to the soil.

A recent comparison of the mineral content of the
bones from the modern Japanese and the 3,000 year old bones
from ancient Japanese monks showed that the ancient bones
contained a wider variety of minerals and a greater amount of
each mineral - a testimony to the value of wood and rice straw
ashes for fertilizer and for “culinary ashes.”

The Common Threads of Anti-Aging

The aging process starts the moment you are conceived and continues unabated,
progressing forward at varying speeds, and degrees at an hourly, daily, weekly and
annual rate until the ultimate end of the process - death.

There is an accumulated knowledge, however, that will allow each of us to prevent and
slow the aging process so that we can successfully reach beyond 100 years of age in a
beautiful, healthful and functional way.

While there are genetic upper limits to human longevity, all humans certainly and
predictably have the genetic potential, the genetic equipment and the genetic capacity
to live well beyond 100! The only reason that all humans don't achieve or reach their
genetic potential for longevity beyond 100 years is that: 1) consciously or
unconsciously, they omit essential pieces of the anti-aging circuitry in their daily
lives necessary to fulfill this potential; and/or 2) they ignorantly or proactively take
pleasure in consciously stepping on land mines.

While the percentage of hundred year olds currently varies from country to country
(Blue Zones), all humans can easily reach 100 years of age by simply following certain
known and predictable physiological laws.

These physiological laws for longevity are as unforgiving and predictable as gravity.
If you step off of the roof of a third story house without a ladder, the law of gravity
will surely take you suddenly and rudely to the ground. If you are unaware of, or simply
ignore the physiological requirements to live to be 100, you will surely not make it to
100 years of age even if you have "good genes."

On the other hand, if everyone in your family died at 35 years of age and you accurately
fulfill your physiological requirements for longevity and you avoid the land mines, you
can easily make it healthfully to 100.

Antioxidants:

Daily consumption of antioxidant rich food and drink is a universal feature of those
living in Blue Zones and in particular centenarians. These potent carbon compounds
quench or neutralize free radicals that attack our cells, cell walls, mitochondria and
physiological processes and increase our risk of cardiovascular disease, cancer,
Alzheimer's disease and other dementias, cataracts, diabetes and aggressively speed up
the process and damage of aging itself.

A universally agreed upon method of predicting the antioxidant power and free radical
neutralizing value of foods, drink and supplements is the Oxygen Radical Absorbance
Capacity or ORAC value - based on current studies, we believe that the optimal total
daily intake of ORAC points to reach the age of 100 years is 20,000 to 25,000 per day.

In 2007, scientists with the USDA published an updated list of ORAC values for 277
foods commonly consumed by the U.S. population (fruits, vegetables, nuts, seeds,
spices, grains, drinks, etc.).

ORAC is a method of measuring antioxidant capacities of various foods. It was developed
by scientists at the National Institute on Aging, a division of the National Institute
of Health (NIH) in Baltimore, Maryland and Tufts University, Boston, Massachusetts.

The ORAC analysis, which utilizes Florescein as the florescent probe, provides a
measure of the scavenging capacity of antioxidants against the peroxyl radical, which
is one of the most common reactive oxygen species (ROS) found in the body.

In 2008, the U. S. Department of Agriculture (USDA) listed a database of the ORAC
values. Correlation between the high antioxidant capacity of fruits and vegetables, and
the positive impact of diets high in fruits and vegetables, is well accepted and
universally known to play an important role in the Free-radical theory of aging.

In many countries, of North America, Europe, Asia, Central and South America and their
Blue Zones, the daily consumption of a wide variety of vegetables, fruit, dark berries,
chocolate, wine, coffee and tea are the universal rich cultural sources of antioxidants.

In the "Blue Zones" of the world, large quantities of vitamin C, Beta carotene,
flavanols, lycopenes, leutenes, proanthocyanins, resveratrols, catechens (EGCGs) and
polyphenols are found in dark fruits and berries, yellow and red vegetables, cocoa, red
wine, green tea or coffee - these are the antioxidants that have universally been found
to be consumed daily in the Blue Zones and are known to protect the centenarians.

Wine & Beer

“Alcohol has existed longer than all human
memory. It has outlived generations, nations, epochs and
ages. It is part of us, and that is fortunate indeed. For
although alcohol will always be the master of some, for
most of us it will continue to be the servant of man.”

National Institute on Alcohol Abuse and Alcoholism

Wine is simply fermented grape (Vitis vinifera) juice,
with an alcohol content of 5.5 to 14 percent (grape spirits can
be added to wines in order to raise the alcohol content to 15
to 22 percent). The first evidence for the consumption and
production of wine goes back more than 10,000 years ago.
Wine frequently appears as a finished product in Egyptian
pictographs circa 4,000 B.C.

Brewing as an art, dates from the beginning of
civilization in ancient Egypt, many Egyptian gods were gods of
a family or were local diaties, however, Osiris, the god of wine
was worshiped throughout Egypt. The Egyptians believed
that Osiris in addition to creating wine also invented beer. The
Egyptians considered beer as a necessity of life and as a result
was brewed in every home “on an everyday basis.”

Both beer and wine were deified and offered to the gods
whose hieroglyph was a winepress. The ancient Egyptians
brewed at least 17 varieties of beer and 24 varieties of wine.

The Egyptians used alcoholic beverages for pleasure,
nutrition, medicine, ritual, renumeration and funerary
purposes. The wine and beer placed in tombs were to be used
by the dead during their journey into the “after-life.”

The earliest wines are thought to have been made from
fermented berries or honey in the wild grape regions of the
Middle East.

Recorded in the Old Testament (Genesis 9:20), Noah
planted a vineyard on Mt. Ararat in what is now eastern Turkey.

===== Screenshot (206) =====

abundantly available in their new land.

With the advent of Christianity and its gradual
displacement of the previously predominant religions,
the drinking habits of Europe were directly affected by the
content of the New Testament. Jesus used wine and approved
of its moderate use and consumption. Paul considered wine
to be a creation of God and therefore inherently good, he
recommended its use for medicinal purposes, while at the
same time condemned drunkenness.

The spread of Christianity and viticulture in Western
Europe occurred simultaneously. St. Martin of Tours was
actively involved in preaching the gospel and planting
vineyards.

The period between the fall of Rome and the beginning
of the High Renaissance, the Middle Ages (1500 A.D.),is known
for rapid development of human culture. In the Middle Ages,
mead, rustic beers, and wild fruit wines became commonly
available and popular, particularly among the Celtics, Anglo-Saxons, Germans and Scandinavians, while wines remained
the favorite beverage in the Romance countries (Italy, Spain
and France).

After Roman Empire collapsed, monistaries
became the centers of brewing beer and winemaking. Rustic
beers continued to be brewed in the common home, whereas
the professional and commercial brewing techniques where
developed and closely guarded by the monks.

The monistaries kept the art of viticulture alive. With
their greater resources, security and stability, they were able to
develop and improve the quality of their vineyards. Throughout
the time of the Middle Ages, the finest vineyards were owned
and managed by the monistaries, and “vinum theologium” or
wine of the church was considered to be the finest of all wines.
In addition to fermenting the quantity of wine necessary to
celebrate the mass, the monasteries also produced large volumes
of wine for commercial trade to support the maintenance and
expansion of the monastic movement.

the


 

Common Wines of the World

 

Wine Grape Variety

 

Cabernet Sauvignon Black grape

This is the most well known black grape variety in
the world. It is the most important Bordeaux variety and has
also been successful in the New World. The grapes are high
in tannins, so these wines age well, especially if aged in oak
barrels. At their best, these wines are rich in color, aroma and
depth and have a long lasting flavor of black currants.

Chardonnay White grapes

Without a doubt, the most popular white wine/ white
grape variety. It is the variety used to produce the classic white
Burgundy and one of the three varieties of white grapes used
to make Champagne. Wines from this grape are typically dry,
and light with a crisp apple-like flavor to a full-bodied buttery
flavor depending upon the method of production.

Chenin Blanc White grapes

This grape is characteristically a high acid variety that
requires a high level of sunlight to ripen, otherwise the wines
become tart. The main growing regions for Chenin Blanc
include Loire, New Zealand and South Africa. The wines
range from very dry to sweet and sparkling; the successful dry
wines are fresh and fruity and sweet wines are well-balanced
and honeyed.

===== Screenshot (208) =====

Gamay Red grapes

Beaujolais wine, fermented from the Gamay grape, 1s
of enormous commercial value in Burgundy, where it is the
grape that accounts for more than half of all wine produced.
Characteristically, these wines are uncomplicated, light, fruity
reds intended for quaffing when young and fresh. These
wines have a distinctive intense pear aroma and raspberry
flavor, which is the result of the production methods. Most
high grade Beaujolais can be aged.

Gewurztraminer Pale pink grape

The very aromatic wines fermented from these spicey
pink grapes are full-bodied white wines that can be dry or
sweet. These wines have a low acidity and a high alcohol
content (greater than 13%). The best of this category of white
wines come from Alsace. Because of its exotic perfume and
intense litchi flavor, Gewurztraminer is one of the few wines
that fits well with spicy foods.

Grenache Black grape

When fermented by itself, the Grenache black grape
produces a warm, fruity flavored wine with a high alcohol
content and an aroma of freshly ground black pepper. These
grapes are low in tannins, and asa result they are often blended
with high tannic acid grapes such as Cabernet sauvignon or
Syrah. The primary growing areas of the Grenache grape are
southern France, Australia, California and Spain.


 

Merlot Black grape

The Merlot grape is the most commonly planted black
grape variety of Bordeaux, where it is frequently blended
with Cabernet sauvignon. Unblended Merlot are usually soft
and easy-drinking wines, with flavors ranging from plums
to fruitcake. Cabernet sauvignon lends “backbone” to Merlot,
which is lower in tannins and acidity, yet brings a richness
and smoothness to the Merlot which would otherwise be
perceived as harsh.

 

Pinot Noir Red grape

This is the grape that is used to make red Burgundy
wines. Yields are not high, which makes it challenging and
expensive to produce. Pinot noir grapes make a pale-colored,
light to medium-bodied red wine that has a strawberry or
raspberry aroma. The highest quality Pinot noir are made in
Burgundy and they have the reputation of being “the most
sumptuous reds in the world.” New Zealand and Oregon
in the U.S. produce good Pinot noir wines. The Pinot noir
is also the main grape variety used to produce high quality
champagne.

 

Riesling White grapes

This classic German grape variety makes many of
the finest white wines in the world - they range from very
dry to very sweet. The Riesling wines are light in body and
low in alcohol, yet strongly flavored and “very long-lived.”
Fantastic, very sweet white wines are typically made from
Riesling grapes that have been subjected to “noble rot.”

===== Screenshot (209) =====
 


(“Noble rot” - an overgrowth of the fungus, Botrytis cinerea,
causes a shriveling and dehydration of the grapes, however,
this condition causes an increase in sweetness. The finest
examples of very sweet dessert wines made from botrytized
grapes are produced in Bordeaux and Germany. “Noble rot”
affects Riesling, Sauvignon blanc, Chenin blanc, Semillon,
and Gewurztraminer grapes in a positive way.)

 

Sauvignon Blanc White grape

Wines produced from this grape are typically very
dry, fresh white wines that should be consumed young.
They are easily recognized by their intense taste and aromas
that are those of green grass and gooseberries. Sancerre and
Pouilly Fume’, from the Loire, are the best known Sauvignon
blancs, however, New Zealand is known to produce a good
Sauvignon blanc.

Semillon White grape

This very utilitarian white grape makes a wide range
of white wines, from dry to very sweet - good Semillon wines
are made in Australia and Bordeaux. It is typically blended,
usually with Sauvignon blane and Chardonnay. Dry and
blended Semillon wines are best consumed when they are
young, however, many of the sweeter wines will improve
with age. Semillon is at its best when infested with “noble
rot,” which produces some of the most well known dessert
wines such as Sauternes and Barsac.

 


 

Syrah (Shiraz) Red grape

Referred to as Shiraz, in Australia (sparkling version)
and South Africa the Syrah grape produces a dark, full-bodied,
heavy, red wine with great potential longevity, especially
when oak aged. They contain a considerable level of tannic
acid and are best consumed when three years old with food.
Mature Shiraz wines have aromas of black currant, cedar and
mixed spice. Syrah is commonly grown in the Rhone Valley.

Zinfandel Black grape

Thought of as California’s own grape variety, Zinfandel
produces wines that range from light and elegant (white or
rose) to massive tannic reds - the grape’s berry-like aroma and
taste always comes through. The best Zinfandels are those
that are rich and darkly colored with a short oaking - these
wines are at their peak when they are five years old.

Distillation of alcohol was one of the more important
discoveries during the Middle Ages. Albertus Magnus (1193-
1280 A.D.) was the first individual to clearly describe the
process for the distillation of spirits. Details of the new art soon
spread amongst the monks, physicians and alchemists, who
were looking to distilled alcohol as a cure for human ailments.
Distilled beverages were originally called aqua vitae or “water
of life” - later they were called brandy. The term brandy was
derived from the Dutch word “brandewijn” which translates
to burnt or distilled wine.

The Bubonic Plague which began in the 14th century
reduced European village populations by as much as 82%.
Reaction to the unstoppable varied from “processions of
flagellants who mobbed city and village streets, hoping, by
the pains they inflicted on themselves and upon each other,
to take the edge off the plagues they attributed to God’s wrath
over human folly,” to a dramatic increase in the consumption
of alcohol, in hopes of protecting themselves.

In Bavaria, beer consumption is believed to have risen
to 300 liters per capita per year (compared with the 150 liters
consumed today) and in Florence, wine consumption reached
ten barrels per capita per year.

The consumption of distilled spirits which was
originally used exclusively for medicinal purposes increased
significantly.

With the end of the Middle Ages, the popularity of beer
spread to England, France and Scotland. Beer brewers were soon
officially recognized as a guild in England, and the adulteration
of beer and wine became a crime punishable by death.

The early Modern Period was a time of increasing
prosperity and wealth. Towns and cities sprouted up all
over Europe, grew in size and numbers, new territories were
“discovered” and colonized and world trade developed.

In the 16th century, the consumption of alcoholic
beverages reached 100 liters per person per year in Valladoid,
Spain; Polish peasants consumed about three liters of beer
per day. In Coventry, the average weekly consumption of
beer and ale was 17 pints per person per week (three pints
per week today), Swedish beer consumption reached what is
thought to be 40 times what it is today, English sailors were
issued one gallon of beer per day, while English soldiers were
issued two thirds of a gallon, in Denmark adult laborers and
sailors consumed one gallon per day.

In 1668, Dom Perignon, a wine-master from a French
abbey, created asparkling champagne. He employed extra-thick

glass bottles to contain the pressure, created a new and efficient

===== Screenshot (211) =====

cork and created the art of sparkling champagne. It would take
another 100 years before the drink became popular.

Distilled Spirits

The original grain spirit, “whiskey,” was first distilled
in Ireland, whiskey was widely consumed in Scotland. It
was in the 17th century that Franciscus Sylvius (aka Fraz de
la Boe), a professor of medicine at the University of Leyden,
distilled spirits from grain.

Distilled spirits were typically flavored with juniper
berries. The flavored beverage was known as junever, a Dutch
word which translates to “juniper.” The French changed the
name to genievre, which the English changed to “geneva,”
which was then shortened to “gin.”

Gin originally used for medicinal purposes,
however, in 1690, the English passed “An Act for Encouraging
the Distillation of Brandy and Spirits from corn,” and within
four years the annual production of distilled spirits, the majority
of which was gin, reached more than one million gallons.

The 17th century colonialists from Virginia, carried on
the European belief that alcoholic drink was a “natural food”
and was good when consumed in moderation. Beer came with
the original colonists, who believed “it was essential to their
well being.”

The

was

Puritan minister Increase Mather preached in
favor of alcohol and also preached against its abuse: “Drink
is in itself a good creature of God, and to to be received with
thankfulness, but the abuse of drink is from Satan; the wine is
from God, but the Drunkard is from the Devil.” At that time the
first distillery was established in the colonies on what is now
Staten Island, the production of hops began in Massachusetts,
and both brewing and distilling were supported by legislation


 

in Maryland.

Rum, originally produced in the West Indies by
Europeans, is produced by distilling fermented molasses; by
1657, a rum distillery was operating in Boston. The Boston
distillery became so successful that in only one generation the
production of rum became colonial New England’s biggest
and most successful industry.

The English government began to actively promote
gin distillation to use surplus grain and raise revenue through
taxation. Suported by public policy, cheap gin began to flood
the market as there was no negativity attached to excessive
drinking and at the same time the growing numbers of poor
in London sought relief from the harsh realities of big city
life by drinking large quantities of gin - the result was the
appearance of the “Gin Epidemic.”

Resveratrol (phytoalexin)

Another important function of alcoholic beverages has
been for therapeutic and medicinal purposes. In 1928, Johanna
Brandt, a South African healer wrote a book entitled the Grape
Cure, a collection of diseases, including cancer, heart disease
and diabetes, that would benefit from the consumption of
dark red grapes - there was something in those red grapes!
Brandt, however, was considered a charleton by local medical
associations and they chased her out of the United States.

The French consume more fat per person than any other
culture in the world, yet they rank third oneveryone’s charts on
health and longevity out of 193 nations. American physicians
call this amazing phenomina the “French Paradox.”

It is generally agreed throughout the scientific world
that resveratrol, the anti-aging substance found in red wines
are the basis for the “Grape Cure” and the “French Paradox.”
