Modernresearchreveals that themoderateconsumption
of red wine is preferable to complete abstenence. Red wine
is known to reduce vascular inflammation and the risk of
coronary heart disease and cancer, a plethora of degenerative
diseases and to significantly increase longevity in yeast,
worms, flies, fish, small mammalian vertebrates (including
primates) and fish.

A 16-year study of 13,000 people in Denmark revealed
that a daily glass of red wine could significantly reduce the
risk of thrombotic stroke by 32%.

Resveratrol (trans-3, 4’, 5-trihydroxystilbene), is a fat
soluble, powerful and naturally occurring polyphenol
antioxidant, and it is found in varying amounts in wine. In
general red wines contain more resveratrol than white wines do.

Grapes and wine grown in humid environments
produce more resveratrol than wines grown in more arid
environments. The theory for this phenomena is that grapes
grown in humid environments produce more resveratrol in
order to repell damaging fungi.

Resveratrol appears to function in animals and humans
by activating a family of enzymes called sirtuins (Sir-2) -
these proteins are involved in the same anti-aging effects as a
Calorie Restricted diet.

===== Screenshot (213) =====
ee

Resveratrol can be found in the skins, seeds; notable
grape varieties that contain high levels of resveratrol are
muscadine grapes, Pinot noir grapes and Concord grapes.

Resveratrol is also found in the Japanese Knotweed
(Polygonum cuspidatum), blueberries and peanuts.

In a study, which appeared in the November 11,
2005, issue of the Journal of Biological Chemistry, investigators
demonstrated that resveratrol significantly lowers the levels
of secreted and intracellular amyloid-beta (Abeta) peptides
produced from different cell lines.

Several epidemiological studies indicate that the
moderate consumption of wine is associated with a lower
incidence of Alzheimer’s disease.

The October 30, 2006 edition of the Wall Street Journal
reported that studies on the red wine compound resveratrol
extend animal lifespans by as much as 40%. Resveratrol was
also considered for its potential life extending properties in
an article related to Calorie Restricted diets in the October
31, 2006 edition of The New York Times - the scientific journal
Nature published the original article that was quoted by both
newspapers.

The study conducted on mice by Harvard Medical
School and the National Institute of Aging researchers, finds
that resveratrol negates the negative effects of a high-fat, high-calorie diet, a finding which experts are calling remarkable. In
the study, one-year-old mice were randomly split into three
groups, a control group fed a standard mouse pellet diet, a
group fed a high-fat (60% fat), high calorie diet, and a group
fed the same high-fat, high-calorie diet plus resveratrol. Within
six months both groups of mice on he experimental diets had
become obese. However, after a year, the mice which were
also receiving the resveratrol were healthier, they did not
develop the insulin resistance, heart disease or liver damage
that their counterparts did. In addition the mice getting the
resveratrol had a 31% lower risk of death.


 

Coffee

Coffee (Coffea arabica) has been consumed for more
than 1,000 years as a health drink, central nervous system
stimulant, physical stimulant, as an energizer and to increase
one’s metabolic rate. Today, approximately 60% of Americans
drink a significant quantity of coffee daily.

Different types of coffee are preferred in different parts
of the world. Arabica coffee is produced mostly in South and
Central America, particularly in Brazil, Columbia, Mexico
and Guatemala, while “robusta” coffee is produced mostly in
African countries including the Ivory Coast, Uganda, Angola,
etc. In the United States, Columbian and Central American
coffees are preferred over Brazilian and African coffees. The
March 1981 issue of National Geographic concluded that the
world’s annual coffee bean harvest would make 3,644,000,000
cubic feet of liquid coffee, a volume equal to the Mississippi's
outflow for one and one half hours.

History of Coffee

There are several accounts that describe the discovery of
the benefits of coffee. A favorite account tells of the legendary
Sufi mystic Shaikh ash-Shadhili traveling through Ethiopia
and that he noticed goats with great energy, whereupon he
tried eating the berries that the goats were eating and he
personally experienced the same increase in energy.

The second legend involves Kaldi, a goat herder from
Caffa, Ethiopia, who told his friends about a strange “dancing”
behavior in his goats after they consumed a particular plant.
He noticed that his goats became hyperactive and jumpy (a
behavior he described as dancing) after eating the red cherries

===== Screenshot (214) =====

 

from a shiny green plant when they changed pastures. He
tried a few of the berries himself and was soon as hyperactive
as his goats.

The story goes on that a local monk happened by and
chastised Kaldi for “ partaking of the devil's fruit.” The monk
soon realized, however, that the berries from the shiny green
plant could help him stay awake longer and that he could
then pray for several more hours each day.

Omar, an Arabian, and his followers were banished
from a town for petty crimes and directed out to the desert
where it was predicted that they would die of thirst and
starvation. As a last resort, Omar and his friends boiled and
ate the berries from the shiny green plant.

Omar and his band of friends not only survived, but
they became folk legends and their survival was considered a
religious sign by the local residents of the town called Mocha.
Locally the previously humble plant was named after the
town and became “ mocha!”

The coffee plant is a native of the African nation of
Ethiopia, however, through merchants and their use of
traditional trade routes coffee was transplanted to Arabia
where it became a significant and initially monopolized trade
good. It is believed that the name coffee comes specifically
from the Kingdom of Kaffa in Ethiopia.

Prior to 1000 A.D., the people of the Galla tribe of
Ethiopia traditionally ground up the coffee berry, wrapped
the ground up coffee bean in animal fat and used it as a source
of energy on long trips or raids on neighboring tribes.

Arab merchants brought the energy laden beans back
to their home countries and started coffee plantations about
1000 A.D. They called the boiled drink, “qahwa” which
translates to “that which prevents sleep” or “qahhwat al-bun”
which translates to “wine of the bean.”

The first scientific writings on coffee appear as
“Bunchum” in the writings of the 10th century Persian
physician Razi. The most important of the early writers on


 

coffee was Abd al-Qadir al-Jazin, who in 1857 compiled a
history on coffee, including the legal controversies of coffee
entitled Umdat al Safwa fi hill al-qahwa.” He reported that
Sheikh Jamal-al-Din al-Dhabhani, mufti of Aden, was the first
to adopt the use of coffee (circa 1454).

The Turks were the first people to use coffee as a
drink and that is why Turkish coffee is so highly prized by
coffee experts yet today. The Turks often added spices such
as cloves, cinnamon, cardamom and anise to the beverage to
create unique flavors.

The export of the coffee plant outside of the Muslim
nations was deemed to beacrime by governments and the world
wide distribution of coffee was actually started as an illegal
enterprise. An Arab by the name of Baba Budan smuggled the
beans to Mysore, India and started a coffee plantation.

Coffee was introduced to Constantinople in 1453 by the
Ottoman Turks who called the brew “kahve.” The world’s first
“coffee shop” called Kiva Han opened there in 1475. At the
same time Turkish law allowed that a woman could divorce
her husband if he failed to provide her with her daily coffee.

In 1511, Khair Beg, a corrupt governor of Mecca,
attempted to ban coffee because he feared that, “The brew’s
influence would kindle opposition to his power.” Orthodox
imams at a theological court in Mecca also banned the drink.
The popularity of coffee, however, resulted in the bans being
overturned by an order of the local Ottoman Turkish sultan
Selim 1. He declared that, “Coffee and its use was sacred” and
had the governor executed in 1524.

According to Leonhard Rauwolf’s account, coffee
became available in England in 1583, largely through the
efforts of the British East India Company and the Dutch East
India Company.

The first English coffee house was opened in St
Michael's Alley in Cornhill. The proprietor was Pasqua
Rose’e, a servant of Daniel Ewards. Edwards was a trader in
Turkish goods who imported the coffee and financed Rose’e

===== Screenshot (215) =====

to set up the coffee house. The popularity of coffee houses
spread rapidly throughout Europe and America and by 1675,
there were more than 3,000 coffee houses in England alone!

In 1598, the word “coffee” entered the English language
from the Italian word caffe’.

In 1600, coffee was introduced to the western world by
Italian merchants.

In the early days of the introduction of coffee to the
Christian world, the drink was believed to be “the devil's
drink” and there were calls to ban the Muslim brew. Pope
Clement VIII heard this complaint and decided to taste the
coffee brew before he declared coffee drinking a sin. He
enjoyed the coffee so much that in 1600 he baptized the drink
and declared, “Coffee is so delicious it would be a pity to let
the infidels have exclusive use of the drink.”

In 1607, Captain John Smith introduces coffee to North
America during the founding of the colony of Virginia at the
village of Jamestown.

In 1645, the first coffee house opens in Itay. The busy
trade between the merchants of Venice and the Muslims of
North Africa, Egypt and East Africa introduced a broad variety
of African goods. The merchants of Venice then decided to
sell coffee to the wealthy of the city because they could charge
a high price for the exotic drink.

In 1652, the first coffee house opens in England. Coffee
houses quickly multiplied and became popular gathering
places for people of all ranks of life, where discussions and
the great debates of the day took place. The price of a cup of
coffee was a penny, as a result coffee houses became known
as “penny universities.”

Antoine Galland (1646-1715) had translated the 1587
writings of Abd al-Qadir al-Jazin on the history of coffee
for the French Royal Library and described the Muslim
connection with coffee, tea and chocolate. He said, “We are
indebted to the great (Arab) physicians for introducing coffee
to the modern world through their writings, as well as sugar,

tea and chocolate.”

By 1668 coffee replaced beer as the universal breakfast
drink in New York. In the same year Edward Lloyd's coffee
house opens in England and is frequented by merchants
and maritime insurance agents. The coffee house ultimately
becomes Lloyd’s of London, the most well known insurance
company in the world.

In 1669, Soleiman Agha, Ambassador from Sultan
Mehmed IV arrived in Paris with his entourage bringing with
him a large supply of coffee beans. They treated their high
ranking guests with coffee to drink and “by May of 1670,
drinking coffee in side walk cafes was a custom in Paris.”

In 1672, the first coffee house opened in Paris.

In 1675, Turkish forces surrounded the city of Vienna
and threatened Austria itself. Franz Georg Kolschitzky, a
Viennese citizen, of Polish extract (Franciszek Jerzy Kulczycki),
who lived in Turkey, broke through the Turkish lines with a
relief force and saved the city. In their haste, the fleeing Turks
left behind many things including sacks of “dry black fodder”
which Kolschitzky recognized as coffee. He claimed the
coffee as his and promptly establishes central Europe’s first
coffee house in 1683. He invented the habit of filtering out the
“srounds,” sweetening the coffee with sugar and adding milk
to the warm drink.

In 1690, the Dutch merchants smuggle live coffee
plants out of the port city of Mocha to Ceylon and their East
Indian colony of Java (the source of the drink’s nickname)
and they become the first people in the west to cultivate coffee
commercially.

In 1713, the Dutch mistakenly provide Louis XIV of
France with a live coffee bush. In 1723, Gabriel Mathieu do
Clieu, a French naval officer, steels a coffee bush seedling and
delivers it to Martinique. Within 50 years an official survey
lists 19 million coffee trees in Martinique and over time 90%
of the world’s coffee production comes from the descendants
of the single stolen plant!

In 1727 the Brazillian coffee industry starts up when Lt.
Colonel Francisco del Melo Palheta is dispatched by Brazil to
arbitrate a border dispute between the French and the Dutch
colonies in Guiana. He settles the dispute and he establishes a
secret relationship with the wife of the French Governor.

Officially the French government closely held the
cultivation secrets of coffee to prevent other nations from
establishing competing coffee exports, however, when his
lady bid him farewell, she sent Palheta off with a bouquet i in
which she had hid live plants and beans of coffee.

In 1732 Johann Sebastian Bach composes his famous
Kaffe-Kantate. He wrote the piece partly as an ode to coffee
and partly as a slap at the movement in Germany to keep
women from drinking coffee. The cantata includes the aria,
Ah! How sweet coffee tastes! Lovelier than a thousand kisses,
than muscatel wine! I must have my coffee.”

In December 1773, the “Boston Tea Party” makes
drinking coffee a patriotic duty in America and countries that
supported the fledgling nation.

In 1775, Frederick the Great of Prussia, attempted to
block the import of green coffee, however, as Prussia’s wealth
was drained as citizens continued to buy the foreign beverage he
relented and finally allowed the importation of green coffee.

By the 1800s, Brazil’s coffee harvests changed coffee
from an indulgence of the elite to a drink of the masses. Brazil
relied heavily on slave labor from Africa to operate their coffee
plantations until the abolition of Brazilian slavery in 1888. Brazil
continued to be the largest coffee producer until the 19th and
early 20th centuries when Columbia, Guatemala, Indonesia
and Vietnam jumped into the coffee market. In the 21st century
Vietnam ranked second only to Brazil in coffee production! i

In 1886, Joel Cheek, originally a wholesale grocer, named
his particular coffee blend “Maxwell House” after his hotel in
Nashville, TN where his brew was featured as a hot drink.

In the early 1900s, the afternoon coffee gatherings of
women in Germany become known as “KaffeeKlatsch” to
describe the gossip sessions - the term was later used to describe
gatherings of relaxed conversation where coffee was served.

Int 1900 the Hills brothers begin packing and selling
roast coffee in vacuum cans which resulted i in the end of local
roasting shops and the coffee mills.

In 1901 the first soluble “instant” coffee is invented and
marketed by Satori Kato, a Japanese-American chemist from
Chicago.

In 1903 Ludwig Roselius, a German coffee importer,
donated a supply of damaged coffee beans to inventors who
perfected the process of removing caffeine from the coffee
beans without damaging the flavor. Roselius marketed the
“decaffeinated” coffee under the brand name of “Sanka.”
The brand name of Sanka was not introduced into the United
States until 1923.

In 1906 an English chemist by the name of George
Constant Washington, notices a fine dust forming on the
spout of his silver coffee carafe. After some time he perfects
an instant coffee, which he mass-produces and markets under
the brand name of “Red E Coffee.”

By 1907, in less than a century, Brazil produces 97% of
the world’s coffee harvest!

In 1920 prohibition is voted into law, causing coffee
sales to skyrocket!

In 1938, Brazil engages the Nestle Company to come
up with a way to economically use their coffee surpluses.
Nestle scientists create freeze-dried coffee and they market it
in Switzerland as Nescafe.

By 1940 the United States imports 70% of the world’s
coffee crop!

In 1942 American Gls are issued Maxwell House

In 1946, Achilles Gaggia, an Italian, perfects the
first espresso machine. Cappuccino is named for its color’s
resemblance to the color of the robes of the monks of the
Capuchin order.

In 1969, one week before the Woodstock concert, the
“Manson Family” murders coffee heiress Abigail Folger as
she visits with her friend Sharon Tate at the home of the movie
producer Roman Polanski.

In 1971, Starbucks (named for the first mate in Melvile’s
Moby Dick) opens its first coffee bean store in Seattle’s Pike
Place public market. In 1983, Howard Schultz, who started
as a coffee bean salesman for Starbucks, visited Milan, Italy
and fell in love with the environment of the “espresso bar.”
“It was about conversation. About community. About human
connection. And fine coffee was the link. I thought. You know,
we could do this in Seattle,” says Schultz.

In April of 1984, Schultz set up a small espresso bar
in the rear corner of the coffee bean store and offered “caffe
latte” which created a marketing frenzy over the fresh-roasted
whole bean coffee. Within days there were long lines on the
sidewalk outside of the coffee bean store! He left Starbucks
and opened his own espresso bar, which he called Il Giornale
(The Daily). Two years later he bought out Starbucks and by
2008 he opened 8,500 stores world wide.

In mid-2008, Schultz was forced to close 600 Starbucks
stores in the U.S. and two thirds of his Australian stores
because of a widespread financial crisis - job transfers out of
the U.S. to China, India and Mexico and a resultant housing
mortgage collapse.

It was about 200 years ago that we learned that the
“buzz” we got from coffee and tea was produced by the same
phytochemical substance. The buzz was in fact produced by
an alkaloid (caffeine) that is found universally in the leaves,
seeds and the fruit of coffee, tea, cacao, kola and as many as 60
other plant species. This “wonder drug” was prescribed for
human energy enhancement as far back as 500 B.C. by Lao-


 

tzu, the spiritual leader who created Taoism.

In 1820 coffee shops flourished throughout Western
Europe. At this time, Friedlieb Ferdinand Runge, a German
chemist isolated and purified the active ingredient of coffee
- caffeine or “something found in coffee.” In 1838, plant
chemists discovered that the buzz ingredient in tea was the
same phytochemical as Runge’s caffeine. Before the end of the
19th century caffeine would be found in kola nuts and cacao.

It was not an accident that coffee and tea became
popular in Europe just as the Industrial Revolution was
launched. The universal use of caffeinated drinks replaced the
popular consumption of beer and paved the way for humans
to move from the subsistence farm to the factory!

The act of boiling water to make coffee or tea reduced
the incidence of water borne disease in the factory’s employees
and the caffeine prevented accidents by keeping them from
falling asleep and falling into the machinery. Truly, coffee
and tea greased the rails into the Industrial Revolution.

Caffeine, like other “drugs” has a significant impact
on mental and physical performance. Numerous studies
have shown that caffeine is analeptic (it stimulates the central
nervous system) and ergogenic (improves energy levels and
physical performance); it improves reaction speed, combats
fatigue, improves performance in driving, flying, solving
simple math problems and improves data entry accuracy.

In 2006 a significant study was released that showed
the high antioxidant content of coffee (i.e. polyphenols,
catechins, epicatechins, etc.) could reduce the risk of diabetes,
heart disease and rheumatoid arthritis, and more specifically
the caffeine in coffee was linked to relieving post workout
pain and facilitating healthy fat metabolism.

Twenty-five years ago, lattes and cappuccinos were
typically found in the coffee houses of New York, San
Francisco and Seattle - now they are being offered in the
humble fastfood environment including McDonald’s, Jack in
the Box and Dunkin’ Donuts.

Tea - “The Elixir of Immortality”

The tea plant (Camella sinensis or Thea sinensis), a native
of southern China, early times to
Chinese botanists and as a medicine to Traditional Chinese
Medicine. It is written of by various names including Tou,
Tseh, Chung, Kha and Ming, and was highly revered for its
ability to relieve fatigue, delighting the soul, strengthening
the will and repairing eyesight.

was known from very

Tea could be administered as a drink internally, but
as frequently it was applied topically as a paste to alleviate
arthritis pains. The Taoists claimed it as an important
ingredient of the “Elixir of Immortality.” The Budhists used
tea extensively to prevent drowsiness during their long hours
of meditation.

By the 4thand 5th centuries, Tea becamea favorite drink
of the people of the Yangtse-Kiang Valley. It was during this


 

time that the modern name, Cha was coined as a corruption
of the more classic Tou.

Emperors rewarded ministers with rare blends of
tea leaves for special services rendered. The leaves were
steamed, crushed in a mortar, made into a cake, and boiled
together with rice, ginger, salt, orange peel, spices, milk and
sometimes onions. These customs of tea preparation remain
today in Tibet and Mongolia. The use of lemon slices with tea
was introduced by the Russians.

Luwuh, a poet, recognized in the Tea-service the “same
harmony and order which reigned through all things.” In his
classic writings, the “Chaking” (The Holy Scripture of Tea),
he formulated the Code of Tea. Since his writings, Luwuh
continues to be “worshipped as the tutelary god of the Chinese
tea merchants.”

The “Chaking” is made up of three volumes and ten
chapters. In the first chapter Luwuh discuses the nature of
the tea plant, in the second he describes the tools required for
the gathering of the leaves, in the third he talks about how
to select the finest tea leaves. According to Luwuh, the best
quality leaves must have “creases like the leathern boot of the
Tartar horsemen, curl like the dew-lap of a mighty bullock,
unfold like a mist rising out of a ravine, gleams like a lake
touched by a zephyr, and be wet and soft like fine earth newly
swept by rain.”

The fourth chapter is devoted to the enumeration and
description of the 24 members of the tea tools and equipment,
starting with the tripod brazier and concluding with the
bamboo cabinet for stowing the utensils.

Luwuh favored Taoist symbolism whichalso influenced
the art of Chinese porcelain making. The Celestial porcelain
had its beginnings in an attempt to produce the exotic and
the exquisite shade of jade, resulting, in the Tang dynasty, in
the blue glaze of the south, and the white glaze of the north.
Luwuh believed the blue as the ideal color for the tea-cup,
as it lent additional greenness to the tea; whereas the white

“a

===== Screenshot (219) =====
Soe

imparted a pinkish and “ distasteful result;” the result was not
good because he used cake-tea.

Sometime later the tea-masters of Sung favored the
powdered tea, they preferred heavy bowls of blue-black and
dark brown colors. The Mings used steeped tea and preffered
the lighter bowls of white porcelain.

In the fifth chapter Luwuh reveals the methods of
making tea. He eliminated all ingredients except salt. He
devoted much space to the choice of water and to the degree
the water was boiled. Luwuh preferred mountain spring
water, with river and spring water coming in second and
third. He described 3 stages of boiling:

The first boil is when the little bubbles like the eye of
fishes swim on the surface;

the second boil is when the bubbles are like crystal beads
rolling in a fountain;

the third boil is when the billows surge wildly in the kettle.

The cake-tea is typically roasted before the fire until it
becomes soft “like a baby’s arm” and is shredded into powder
between two pieces of paper. Salt is added to the first boil, the
tea in the second. At the third boil, a dipperful of cold water
is added to the kettle “to settle the tea and revive the youth of
the water.” The tea was then served and consumed. —

The tea-room does not pretend to be other than a mere
cottage - The simplicity and purism of the tea-room - Symbolism
in the construction of the tea-room - The system of its decoration
- A sanctuary from the vexations of the outer world.

The tea-room (the Sukiya) does not pretend to be other
thana mere cottage - a straw hut, asit is usually referred to. The
original characters, for the Sukiya, ment - the” Abode of Fancy.”
It is an Abode of Fancy in that it is built to welcome a creative
impulse. It is an Abode of Vacancy as it is without decoration.


 

It is an Abode of the Unsymmetrical as it is designed for the
witnessing of the Imperfect, leaving something unfinished on
purpose to invite mental activity to create the completion. To
the western eye the extreme Japanese simplistic interior of the
tea-room appears barren.

The first free standing tea-room was the brain-child
of Senno-Soyeki, generally known as Rikiu, the greatest and
most revered of all tea-masters created and perfected the
formalities of the Tea-ceremony. The dimensions of the tea-room had been determined earlier by Jowo, a highly regarded
15th century tea-master. The original tea-rooms consisted of a
small portion of the drawing-room partitioned off by screens
for the purpose of the tea-gathering. The portion partitioned
off was referred to as the Kakoi or enclosure, a name that is
still used to describe tea-rooms that are built into a private
home. The Sukiya refers to the tea-room itself, it classically is
designed to comfortably seat five or less people, symbolizing
the thought “more than Graces and less than the Muses,” an
anteroom (midsuya) where the tea utensils are washed and
arranged before being brought into the tea-room, a portico
(machiai) in which the guests congregate until they are invited
to enter the room, and a garden path (roji) which connects
the machiai with the tea-room. The tea-room is in itself
unimpressive in appearance. It is smaller than the smallest
of Japanese houses, and the materials used in its construction
are purposely intended to give the idea of “refined poverty.”
A good tea-room will cost more than a typical mansion!

Green tea (Camella sinensis or Thea sinensis) and black
tea both originate from the same plant and differ only in the
preparation. Green tea is dried for a short period of time and
heated sooner than black tea to prevent fermentation (China
and Japan). Black tea comes from India, Sri Lanka, Kenya,
Indonesia, Turkey, Pakistan, Malawi and Argentina. In the
21st century tea is a $750 million cash crop in India’s eastern
state of Assam alone. Historically there is thought to be three
stages in the evolution of tea.

art of life.

spare beauty of a hand-molded brown cup, matter as much as
the tea itself.” The earliest record of tea in European writings
is said to be found in the statement of an Arabian traveler,
that after the year 879 A.D. the main sources of revenue in

the deposition of a Chinese minister of finance in 1285 A.D.
for his arbitrary augmentation of the tea-taxes. It was at the
period of the Great Discoveries that the European people
began to know more about the extreme Orient.

Almeida (1576), Maffeno (1588), Tareira (1610), also mentioned
tea. In the last-named year, ships of the Dutch East India
Company brought the first tea into Europe. It was known in
France in 1636, and reached Russia in 1638. England welcomed
tea in 1650 and generally spoke of it as “That excellent and by
all physicians approved China drink, called by the Chineans
Tcha, and by other nations Tay, alias Tee.”

Like many good things found in the world, the mystique
and marketing of tea generated opposition. Henry Saville
(1678) denounced drinking tea as a “filthy custom.” Jonas
Hanway (Essay on Tea, 1756) said that, “men seemed to lose
their stature and comeliness, women their beauty through the
use of tea.” Its cost in its first introduction to the west (15 or 16
shillings a pound) prevented popular consumption and made
it “regalia for high treatments and entertainments, presents
being made thereof to princes and grandees.”

The coffee houses of London in the second half of the
18th century became tea-houses, the haunts of writers and
artists such as Addison and Steele who “beguiled themselves
over their dish of tea.” Tea quickly became a necessity of life
and as a result - taxable.

In 1711, the Spectator, stated, “I would therefore in a
particular manner recommend these my speculations to all
well-regulated families that set apart an hour every morning
for tea, bread and butter; and would earnestly advise them
for their good to order this paper to be punctually served up
and to be looked upon as a part of the tea-equipage.”

Samuel Johnson described himself as “a hardened and
shameless tea-drinker, who for twenty years diluted his meals
with only the infusion of the fascinating plant; who, with tea,
amused the evening, with tea solaced the midnight, and with
tea welcomed the morning.”

Colonial America accepted the King of England’s
oppression until he imposed a 3% tax on tea. The American
Revolution against England began with Samuel Adams and
a loyal band of revolutionaries (Sons of Liberty) dressing as

===== Screenshot (221) =====
 


 

Indians and throwing imported English tea into the Boston
Harbor as a protest.

The “Boston Tea Party” began when the British
Parliament passed the Tea Act in 1773 in hopes of forcing the
colonies to purchase tea directly from Great Britain’s failing
East India Company rather than from colonial importers, who
often sold smuggled tea purchased from Holland through the
Dutch East India Company.

The new law was seen by Americans as an attempt by
Great Britain to undercut local merchants in order to reward a
London monopoly, and a tea boycott soon spread throughout
the colonies. However, a boycott seemed too passive a strategy
for Boston’s Samuel Adams.

On December 16, 1773 Adams led a wild meeting that
was sponsored by the Sons of Liberty at Boston’s Old South
Church. A crowd of more than 8,000 people attended and
filled the hall and the crowd soon spilled out onto the street.
Adams, who was a charismatic speaker, whipped the crowd
into hysteria, and urged the crowd to defy the British by
throwing the officially approved tea into the Boston Harbor.

At a signal from Adams, dozens of members of the
Sons of Liberty appeared from the back of the church made
up as Mohawk warriors. They led the screaming crowd to the
wharf, where H.M.S. Dartmouth, Eleanor and Beaver were
tied up. They then boarded the vessels and proceded to throw
342 large bales of British tea overboard!

Adams wascommited to havea political victory without
causing a bloody response from the British. He convinced the
rebels to restrain themselves, “No violence or you'll hurt the
cause!” As one patriot wrote, “We were surrounded by British
armed ships, but no attempt was made to resist us.” .

The Boston Tea Party energized and galvanized the
entire population of the American Colonies. Parliament
countered by passing five laws that the Americans quickly
labeled the “Intolerable Acts,” which closed Boston Harbor,
established martial law, and effectively abolished local


 

and state government in Boston and the entire state of
Massachusetts.

Though the Intolerable Acts only directly affected
Massachusetts, the severity of these five laws united the
colonials everywhere, and caused them to quickly organize a
Continental Congress.

At that moment the colonials moved from the cry, “No
taxation without representation,” to “Give me liberty or give
me death!”

After hearing of the events in Boston, King George
III predicted, “The die is now cast. The Colonies must either
submit or triumph.”

Chocolate

The Spanish first found the Mayans drinking a beverage
considerably different from what we usually think of as hot
chocolate.

The Maya preferably consumed hot chocolate thick
and foamy. While standing, the Maya poured the chocolate
beverage froma spouted pot into another vessel on the ground.
The act of pouring over a distance, combined with the fatty
cacao butter, produced a thick head of rich, dark, chocolate
foam - the most revered part of the drink. The scientific name
for the cacao tree is, Theobroma cacao, or “food of the gods.”

In 1981, a team of archaeologists from the University of
Texas at Austin, discovered 14 spouted drinking vessels used
by the ancient Mayan civilization dating back from about 900
B.C. to A.D. 1300. The origin of chocolate use goes back to
the Olmec civilization which preceded the Maya. The Olmec,
or “rubber people,” were the first culture in the Western
Hemisphere to relish chocolate.

In modern times, chocolate is thought of as an

===== Screenshot (222) =====
 


indulgence, as something that overweight and obese people
gorge themselves on - yet, chocolate can be one of the great
natural herbal weight control products ever known to man.

Studies published in the Journal of the American College
of Nutrition, reported that chocolate does not raise cholesterol
or increase LDL.

In a 2003 issue of the journal, Hypertension, it was
reported that the flavanoids in cacao could help increase
blood flow to the heart, brain, hands and legs.

Anarticle in the Journal of Agriculture and Food Chemistry,
reported that cacao has a higher antioxidant rating than tea or
red wine.

When people crave chocolate, they are really mineral
deficient (pica, munchies, etc.). In fact, bitter, dark unsweetened
chocolate has a very low glycemic index because up to 70% of
the chocolate’s calories come from fat, and fat does not raise
blood sugar.

Cacao butter, the fat in chocolate, is comprised of
stearic acid (34%), palmitic acid (20%) and oleic acid (30%).
Both stearic and palmitic acids are saturated fats; oleic acid is
a mono-saturated fat that is also found in olive oil.

Cacao powder is made when the fat, or “Cocoa butter,”
is pressed out of the “liquor” - most cocoa powders have little
or no fat.

Chemistry of Chocolate

Chocolate is a rich source of cocoagin, caffeine,
theobromine, flavanol and flavonoids. These compounds
thermogenic (energy generating) properties and
antioxidant properties that make bitter chocolate a great
energy generator, a great weight loss product and a super free
radical neutralizing herb.

have


 

Dozens of scientific studies have found that chocolate
is rich with a broad range of phytonutrients. The class of
phytonutrients, known as polyphenols, are one of dark
chocolate’s most important chemical constituents. These
phytochemicals include some of the most potent antioxidant
and anti-inflammatory compounds that occur in nature.
Polyphenols occur as flavonoids or bioflavanoids, phenolic
acids, simple phenols, phenylpropanoids, quinines, stilbenes
and xanthones.

Flavanoids make up two-thirds of the polyphenol
family of phytochemicals. Flavanoids typically cause the the
plant (cocoa, green tea, etc.) to have a bitter taste - “bitter”
chocolate. Chocolate, with a dark color and bitter taste, has a
significant level of clinically useful antioxidants.

Anthocyanins are an extremely valuable subgroup
of flavonoids that provide extra nutritional benefits.
Anthocyanins provide anti-inflammatory and antioxidant
features and also supports the body’s ability to metabolize
sugar. Supporting healthy metabolism and healthy blood
sugar levels lowers the risks of developing type II diabetes
and obesity. Anthocyanins are able to support healthy blood
sugar levels and efficiently neutralize free radicals.

Anthocyanins are unusual in that they prevent and
repair free radical damage to small blood vessels. Damage
to blood vessel’s walls (inflammation) is the direct cause of
atherosclerosis, coronary thrombosisand stroke. Anthocyanins
can block histamine response, reduce the affects of allergic
reactions, inflammation and irritation. Several studies showed
that anthocyanins can stop the inflammation associated with
arthritis, gastric ulcers, diabetes and cardiovascular disease.

Catechins and epicatechins are phytonutrients that
add to chocolate’s disease relieving arsenal. Studies show
that catechins and epicatechins prevent the creation of
carcinogens, and they initiate and support the body’s inherent
“detoxification” and defense processes. Many investigators
believe that epicatechins should be listed as a third category

===== Screenshot (223) =====
 


 

of vitamins: i. e. - 1) fat soluble; 2) water soluble; 3) flavanols/
catechins.

Many additional categories of polyphenols and
phytonutrients are found in chocolate. Dozens of significant
and well respected studies have shown that the antioxidant
nutrients found in chocolate can lead to a longer, more
energetic and fuller life!

An important phytochemical found in chocolate is
phenylethylamine (PEA), this neurotransmitter stimulates the
body’s pleasure centers. Large q uantities of PEA are associated
with feelings of excitement, attraction and sexual pleasure. It
is the affects of the PEA in chocolate that has caused chocolate
to be given as a traditional seductive gift for Valentine’s Day,
anniversaries and to get out of the “dog house.”

Anandaminde_ is found
in chocolate. This phytochemical targets the same brain
structures that are targeted by the active ingredient in
marijuana - THC.

The cardiovascular

another neurotransmitter

benefits of dark chocolate are
considerably higher than those attributed to green tea, red
wine and other whole food antioxidants.

A team of researchers from the Harvard School of
Public Health reviewed 136 studies on the relationship of
chocolate and cocoa to cardiovascular health. The study
included research ranging from lab tests to human studies.
The research team concluded that chocolate is a major source
of flavanoids (epicatechins, catechins and procyanidins) and
determined that “the primary fat in chocolate (stearic acid)
did not have adverse effects on blood vessels, cholesterol
or overall cardiovascular health” because it is metabolized
differently than other types of saturated fats.

The Harvard study also concluded that regular
chocolate intake can lower blood pressure, decrease vascular
inflammation, rates of thrombosis, increased
levels of HDL and lowered levels of LDL, improved vascular
endothelial function and _ health,

decreased

increased nitric acid

production and increased activity of prostacyclins, which
support the relaxation of blood vessels.

Research performed jointly by a team from the
University of California, Davis and Tufts University, and
published in 2005 in The American Journal of Clinical Nutrition,
demonstrated that the proanthocyanin and flavanol content
of cocoa demonstrated significant antioxidant properties and
the ability to protect the heart and vascular systems. According
to Dr. Carl Keen, “These nutrients have been shown to affect
numerous intracellular signaling cascades, and to influence
the cardiovascular system by enhancing vascular function
and decreasing platelet activity.”

A 2006 study completed by a team of researchers
from the University of California-Davis and the University
of Dusseldorf, Germany determined that the compound
epicatechin found in cocoa is directly linked to improved
circulation and other hallmarks of healthy cardiovascular
function. The researchers stated that, “The results of the study
provide direct proof that epicatechin is responsible for the
beneficial vascular effects observed after the consumption of
certain flavanol-rich cocoa (foods).”

Crucial to this study were volunteers from the
Panamanian tribe of Indians called the Kuna. High blood
pressure and other negative signs of cardiovascular disease
are rare amongst the island-dwelling Kuna, who are also
known to consume large amounts (3 to 4 cups per day) of
chocolate in the form of a traditional beverage.

Further studies showed that the Kuna who migrated to
urban environments, and consequently consume less cocoa,
do not enjoy the same level of cardiovascular health.

When the Kuna returned again to a high consumption
level of cocoa, the researchers found that the risk factors for
cardiovascular disease were reduced significantly.

The well respected investigators Mary and Marguerite
Engler completed a study that elucidated the mechanism of
how chocolate improves the health of the endothelial lining

===== Screenshot (224) =====
 


of blood vessels. In their studies, 21 subjects were given either
a high-flavanoid dark chocolate or a low-flavanoid dark
chocolate every day for two weeks. The results indicated that
those individuals eating the high-flavanoid dark chocolate had
a significantly higher endothelial (vascular lining) function
than those consuming the low-flavanoid chocolate.

Chocolate Tasting

There are many ways to evaluate chocolate; one is by the
percentage of chocolate in the product; the country of origin;
another is by the ORAC value; and yet another is by taste.

Evaluating and choosing chocolate by taste is done
much in the same manner as evaluating and choosing a good
wine. Taste is defined by categories: “Chocolatey,” “Fruity,”
“Nutty,” “Earthy,” “Floral,” and “Citrus.”

Calorie Restricted Diets

Calorie Restricted diets (without malnutrition),
universally known to increase the life span of all laboratory,
pet and farm animals, is a common thread found amongst
centenarians in history and currently amongst centenarians
in all Blue Zones.

In most cases the caloric levels fall between 1,250 and
1,800 calories per day, with those performing more physical
labor consuming the higher calorie levels.


Mineral-rich Diets

Mineral rich diets, found universally in all Blue Zones,
are an absolute requirement to reach the centenarian club. In
all cases, the Blue Zones have multiple sources rich in a wide
spectrum of minerals.

A high protein, low carbohydrate diet is relatively
forgiving of a low vitamin intake, but unforgiving of a low
mineral diet.

Religious Dietary Practices

All human cultures have developed dietary laws,
many of which are attached to their religious beliefs. It is of
interest to note that just about all gerontologists highlight a
deep abiding religious faith as a common feature of people
who live beyond 100 years of age.
taboos from ancient times that
prevent the consumption of certain foods that are perceived
as dangerous or sinful. Across the global spectrum of man’s
history is his observation that he is dependant upon nature
and therefore God for food and life.

In some religions, a prohibition on the consumption of
stimulating drinks (i.e. coffee, tea, cocoa, alcohol, etc.), certain

long periods of time to be beneficial for reducing the risk of
degenerative diseases and for extending life spans.

In other cases some religions, such as the Seventh-day Adventists and Mormons, have proactively encouraged
the consumption of fruit and vegetables (i.e. antioxidants,
etc.), a practice which has been shown to reduce the risk of
degenerative diseases and extend life spans.

Many religions require their members to avoid gluttony
(one of the seven “deadly sins”) and eat sparingly, eating only
for their minimal requirements and then in addition require
fasting, all of such practices encourage a Calorie Restricted
diet.

There are religions that espouse some form of
meat restriction (eat meat “sparingly”) or some form of
“vegetarianism” - some are as old as Hinduism and Jainism
and some as new as Seventh-day Adventism and Mormonism.
There are the strict orthodox dietary laws of Judaism and
a spin-off of those dietary laws which became an integral
practice of Mohammedanism.

There are dietary laws that are professed by early
religious practice that separate people from people and caste
from caste.

In the collection and diversity of Christian faiths there
are the days of feasting and the days of fasting (abstinence and
self-denial) and the Sacrament of the Lord’s Supper. There is
Christ's direct instruction to his disciples to mention bread in
their prayers.

It is therefore not too far out of line to say that “in the

“And the Lord God commanded the man, saying, Of

But of the tree of knowledge of good and evil, thou

The Concept of Forbidden Fruit

The forbidden fruit, as it is called, is commonly
romanticized as a sexual prohibition, however, early Bible
interpretation and some modern religious groups, pa rticularly
the Mormons, believe that the prohibition was just as the
Bible says it was - a food unsuited for man’s body in its newly
created and immortal state. And, when the first man and
woman, who were made in the image of God, first ate “the
forbidden fruit,” their bodies fell vulnerable to disease, decay
and death.

It was, therefore, through diet, that humanity, gained
the knowledge of good and evil and of the dire consequences
of bad choices, of cause and effect.

This direct interpretation of the Book of Genesis has
been part of Judaic and Christian law for centuries and is
thought to be the basis of the German proverb, “man ist was
man isst!” - “man is what he eats.”

The concept that, certain foods are unclean and
dangerous, thus making the body an unfit abode for the spirit
has long been a touchstone of religious belief that persists
even yet today.

The religious concept that grains, vegetables and fruit
are good is widespread even today. In the Sinai Desert, where
the Israelites wandered for 40 years, manna filled a huge gap
in the subsistence diet of the Children of Israel. There are two
forms of manna, one that “rained from heaven” and one that
“sprang from the ground.”

The manna that sprang from the heavens appears to
have been a mix of flower and tree pollen, the manna that
sprang from the ground is a lichen of the genus Lecanora spp.
that grew on the ground and rocks in large patches and is said
to taste like honey ona cracker.


 

Fertility and Food

Fertility ceremonies and myth related to the harvest
season and the Earth’s bounty were an integral part of the
ancient religion and mythology of Sumer. According to the Epic
of Gilgamesh, which is believed to be the oldest writing existing
in recorded history, it seems sprang full-grown upon the earth,
like Athena from the head of Zeus, around 3500 B.C).

Therefore, even before the story of the Creation was
written down in Genesis, the abundance of the earth was
celebrated in legend and interwoven with worship - and food
and diet as part of religion has been in the historical record
ever since.

Fasting and Health

Along with the gratitude to God and the Earth for
abundance, there has always been the consideration and
practice of asceticism (one who led an austere life of self
denial) to prove to God that the practitioner loved Him.

The practice of fasting or severely restricting one’s diet
in order to “subdue the flesh and strengthen the soul” is also
as old as religion. There were sects among the Jews who made
abstinence part of their doctrine -the Essenes.

Before Christ began his ministry he retired to the desert
wilderness, fasted and prayed for 40 days and overcame his
temptations that were offered by the devil.

Paul, in his first epistle to the Corinthians, drew a
parallel between the athlete’s life and the Christian life. The
word ascetic is derived from the Greek word for exercise,
and was intended to transmit the concept of the strict health
regimen and diet of the athlete. Asceticism has been espoused

===== Screenshot (228) =====
 


as a tool to fight the war against the deadly sins of gluttony,
envy, pride, sloth, covetousness, anger and lust.

The early Anchorite Christians purposely wandered in
the deserts of the Middle East and Africa looking for a pure
life that would be pleasing to God and they ate only what the
desert provided (a very severely Calorie Restricted diet).

Catholics observe the rules of abstinence during Lent,
the Muslims fast during Ramadan, the Mormons fast the first
Sunday of each month.

In China, the diet and fasting of Taoism play a role,
with certain foods and herbs supplying the vital energy for
specific organs. According to the Tao, the heart is related to
fire, the lungs to metal, the kidneys to water and foods that
nourish these organs include poultry, vegetables and fish.

In Confucianism, dietary laws were connected to
ancestor worship, and when one was making a sacrifice to
their ancestor's spirits they took great care to ensure that their
diet was ritually proper.

The Hindus and Jainists of India proscribed meat
because of their belief of the transmigration (reincarnation)
of souls. No living creature could be killed for meat as the
soul inhabiting the creature might have once been or might
eventually be the soul of a man.

The Jainists were so strict that they carefully inspected
fruit and vegetables before they would eat them to ensure that
they did not have any insects or grubs on them - some went to
the extreme to wear masks to prevent the inhalation of small
flying insects.

Buddhism, which beganin India and spread throughout
Asia, had strict laws prohibiting the killing of animals but held
no prohibition on eating them. The only time that Buddhist’s
prohibit the consumption of meat is during ritual feasts and
celebratory fasting.


Strong or Stimulant Drinks

The prohibition of alcoholic beverages is one of the five
precepts of the Middle View in Buddhism and the concept
of the Middle View led to the ethic of moderation in eating
(Calorie Restricted diet) and to the ethic that is against suicide
through fasting.

In Islam, “strong drink” (distilled liquor) is
unequivocally forbidden, however, wine is reluctantly
permitted - there is “sinfulness in wine but also some utility.”
The Koran concludes that the sinfulness of wine outweighs
the usefulness of wine.

The Children of Israel were more liberal regarding
strong drink. In their early scriptures describing the Promised
Land “flowing with milk (Glacial Milk) and honey (verdant
fields and abundant orchards),” they were appreciative for
the bounty of nature, however, they were also strict regarding
their dietary laws.

The dietary strictures in Leviticus and Deuteronomy
are simple and direct: No meat of predators, either birds or
beasts; no animals that do not have a cloven hoof, and even
if they do have a cloven hoof they must also chew the cud.
No seafood that does not have fins and scales. No reptiles.
Only certain insects could be consumed; and absolutely
no consumption of blood. Later, came the Talmud and the
“hedge about the Law” which became the most strict and the
most complex of all dietary laws in history.

Amish

The Amish (Pennsylvania Dutch) are members of
the original Dutch Reform Church, a conservative Christian

===== Screenshot (229) =====
 


sect who have opted out of the American Social Security
System, Medicare and private insurance and have since their
beginnings i in the 15th century looked to themselves for health
care and self insurance.

The Amish operate today as 18th century farmers and
have rejected electricity and other modern conveniences and
still use wood to heat their homes and cook their meals. Wood
ashes (plant minerals) are an important byproduct of daily
living for the Amish; they use the wood ash (plant minerals)
added to compost as fertilizer in their gardens.

The Amish employ horses to get around in buggies and
wagons; they use horses to pull and power farm implements.

The Amish are born in their homes and die in their
homes. The use herbs, nutritional supplements, homeopathic
remedies, acupuncture, chiropractors, naturopathic physicians
and midwives as health professionals - they have for the most
part opted out of the modern medical system and they take
care of their elderly in their own homes.

Mormon

The state of Utah is 70% Mormon and is the healthiest
state and the longest lived peoples in America. According to the
National Center for Health Statistics, Utah has the lowest death
rate from virtually all the common degenerative diseases.

Joseph Smith was killed by a mob in 1844, not long
before the Mormons escaped Nauvoo; however, by that time
Smith had compiled his book of his visions and revelations
and this book became the Doctrine and the Covenants of the
Mormon faith.

Literally driven by mobs from the Illinois city of
Nauvoo, the Mormons made their epic migration west so
they could live isolated from the rest of the world.


 

The Mormon religion (Latter Day Saints of Jesus
Christ) is a relatively new American religion. Their beliefs
say the Mormon religion began in 1820 near the small town
of Manchester, New York - a boy of 14 named Joseph Smith
sought help in prayer because he was bewildered by the
religious upheavals and rivalries of the times. “God the Father
and Jesus Christ appeared to him and told him not to become
involved with any existing denomination.”

Joseph waited three years for further enlightenment
and then according to the Book of Mormon, “A messenger from
God appeared, a man named Moroni who had once lived
on the North American continent and whose ancestors had
escaped from Jerusalem before the Babylonian captivity.

Moroni related the story of a historical record to Joseph,
that Moroni’s father, a man named Mormon, had given to him
to hide before he and his people were killed by their bitter
enemy from the same branch of Isreal - the American Indians.

Moroni told Joseph Smith where the record was hidden
and how it was to be translated. In modern times, the Book of
Mormon and the Bible, together, form the core scriptures of
the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints.”

In 1800, a bounty was placed on the heads of Mormons
by the Governor of Missouri; this event precipitated a legendary
mass migration of Mormons from Nauvoo, Illinois, east of the
Mississippi River westward to Salt Lake City, Utah.

The Mormons live an average of 10 years longer than
the average American. A common and consistent thread that
is the touchstone of the Mormon religion is their dietary laws.
The 89th Section of the Doctrine and Covenants, the Word
of Wisdom was revealed on February 27, 1833 - this section
gives Mormons their basic laws of diet and health:

“For, behold, the beasts of the field and the fowls of
the air, and that which cometh of the earth, is ordained for the
use of man for food and for raiment, and that he might have
in abundance.”

The Mormons are very faithful in avoiding “stimulant
drinks” which includes alcoholic drinks, caffeine (coffee, tea,
soft drinks, energy drinks, energy herbs, stimulating herbs,
etc.), they restrict calories and limit meat intake to a secondary
position behind the consumption of grains and vegetables.

As a group the Mormons tend to live a smoke free
lifestyle that is based on strong parenting beliefs.

According to Brigham Young, Smith’s successor as
president of the Mormon Church, Smith was conducting a
class for members of the priesthood. This class was held ina
small room above the kitchen of Smith’s home and the room
was frequently clouded with large volumes of tobacco smoke.
To Smith, the atmosphere in the room was unseemly, so one
day he left the room, “inquired of the Lord” and received the
Word of Wisdom.

Essentially, the instructions, which were given “not by
commandment or constraint, but by revelation and the word
of wisdom,” were in keeping with the traditional Christian
attitude, that all foods are for man. The prohibitions are for
non-foods - tobacco, alcohol and “hot drinks” which were
interpreted to mean drinks containing harmful drugs, such as
“soft” drinks and chocolate.

The remainder of the revelation was a proactive
statement regarding nutrition and the prudent consumption
and storage of food:

tea, coffee, caffeine containing

Every herb in its season and every fruit in its season to
be used with prudence and thanksgiving.

Flesh of beasts and fowl to be used sparingly. “And
it is pleasing unto me that they should not be used, only in
times of winter, or of cold, or famine.” :

All grain is good for man and all plant food “whether


 

in the ground or above the ground” - wheat for man; corn for
the ox; oats for the horse; rye for the fowls and swine; and
barley for all useful animals and for mild drinks.

It is a precept of the Mormons to be prepared. Every
church or “ward,” has a “bishop’s storehouse” from which
members can draw on in time of need.

Mormons eat for the sake of their physical and spiritual
health and they fast for the same reason. It is traditional for
the Mormon to fast on the first Sunday of each month and
give the price of two Sunday meals to help the needy.

Mormon Health and Longevity

The average lifespan of Mormons is 10 years longer
than that of the average American. Dr. James E. Enstrom, of the
School of Public Health, University of California at Los Angeles,
examined a 1965 study done by the California State Department
of Health that showed Mormons in Alameda County, across the
bay from San Francisco, have a total mortality rate of only 55%
of the adult population of the county as a whole. The original
sample was small, 111 Mormons in a total of 6,928 people,
however, the data reinforced statistics from other sources.

When Dr. Enstrom reviewed data which the U.S.
Cancer Institute had gleaned from the U.S. Census and state
mortality records, he learned that areas of Utah that are
heavily Mormon have lower death rates than areas with a
lower percentage of Mormons.

In one area in particular - Utah County, just south of
Salt Lake City, where Provo is, there is a Mormon population
of 85% compared to 70% for all of Utah. And Utah County’s
cancer-death rate is lower than the state’s as a whole and only
65% as high as the U.S. as a whole.

2D eons
Enstrom then examined the cancer death rate

among California’s 400,000 Mormons using membership
information and names of those who had died, and mortality
records, showing the cause of death (supplied by the state of
California); the data revealed that California Mormons have a
cancer death rate of only slightly more than half (55%) of the
U.S. cancer death rate.

Seventh Day Adventist

The Seventh Day Adventist, are a relatively young
American religion, who pursue a strict vegetarian diet.
No religious movement, ancient or modern, has put greater
emphasis on diet and nutrition than the Seventh-day Adventist
church - on the average the Adventists live eight to ten years
longer than the average American.

Long before nutrition reached the level of a science,
Ellen G. White was teaching Seventh-day Adventists in the
basic concepts of healthy living. In addition to denouncing
the use of alcohol and addictive drugs (i.e. tobacco, alcohol,

‘

pharmaceuticals, street drugs, etc.) she insisted on a “well-balanced diet” before the phrase was coined.

More than 70 years ago White stated that tobacco is
“a slow, insidious, but most malignant poison.” She also
emphasized the avoidance of preserved meats and refined
foods, particularly white flour and sugar - she was one of the
first natural food enthusiasts.

In the early part of the 1800s, in numerous parts of the
Christian world, there was a sudden awareness of the second
coming of Christ - the leaders of many established churches
became caught up in the new Reformation.

One of the believers of imminent second coming of
Christ (Adventists) was William Miller. Afteraserious study of


biblical prophecy, Miller concluded that Christ would return to
earth on October 22, 1844. Despite their disappointment when
the appointed day came and went, many of the “Millerites”
remained faithful and continued to meet in small groups and
study the Bible and pray.

Among these loyal Millerites was a young woman
named Ellen Harmon. In December of 1844 at an Adventist
meeting in Portland, Maine Harmon had her first vision.

Ellen Harmon (became Mrs. James White) became
the guiding force of the Seventh-day Adventist Church - it
was only a few weeks later that she saw the famous vision of
the last days of the world that showed her “the only hope of
mankind - obedience to the commandments of God.”

During her vision there was a special emphasis on
the Fourth Commandment, to keep the Sabbath day holy,
and it was shown to the young woman that the Sabbath was
the seventh day, not the first, so in 1861 the church formally
adopted these two primary beliefs into its name. Her books
have sold in the tens of millions, making her one of the best-selling and most prolific authors of all time. Just one book,
Steps to Christ, has sold more than 10 million copies and has
been translated into 80 languages.

White inspired and directed the begining of a vast
network of missions, schools and colleges, hospitals, clinics
and dispensaries in all parts of the globe, including the well
known school of medicine at Loma Linda University, 60 miles
east of Los Angeles.

In 1863, Ellen White saw the vision that still forms the
core of the Adventist diet and health programs. The event is
often referred to as the “Otsego vision” because it came in the
home of some Adventists whom she was visiting in Otsego,
Michigan:

“It is wrong to eat merely to gratify the appetite, but
no indifference should be manifested regarding the quality of
the food, or the manner of its preparation. If the food eaten is

===== Screenshot (232) =====
 

i aa

not relished, the body will not be so well nourished. The food
should be carefully chosen and prepared with intelligence
and skill.”

“Here is a suggestion for all whose work is sedentary
or chiefly mental; let those who have sufficient moral courage
and self control try it: At each meal take only two or three
kinds of simple food, and eat no more than is required to
satisfy hunger. Take active exercise every day, and see if you
do not receive benefit.”

“For use in bread making, the superfine flour is not
the best. Its use is neither healthful nor economical. Fine-flour
bread is lacking nutritive elements to be found in bread made
from the whole wheat. It is a frequent cause of constipation
and other unhealthful conditions.”

“Those who eat flesh are but eating grains and
vegetables at second hand; for the animal receives from these
things the nutrition that produces growth. The life that was
in the grains and vegetables passes into the eater. We receive
it by eating the flesh of the animal. How much better to get it
direct, by eating the food that God provided for our use!”

In the same year of 1863, the headquarters of the church
was established in Battle Creek, Michigan, and it was there that
the first Adventist medical institution was established. At first
it was called the Western Health Reform Institute, and then
simply the Battle Creek Sanitarium and then affectionately
the “San.”

Basically the Adventists follow the instructions in
Leviticus concerning cleanand unclean food. Porkis forbidden,
as is shellfish, and the flesh of predators. Adventists also
abstain totally from the use of alcohol, tobacco and harmful
drugs, including coffee and tea - many believers do not eat
meat and those who do eat it only “sparingly.”

White became suspicious of the widespread and
careless use of “poisonous drugs” by the doctors of the day -
at the Battle Creek Sanitarium the “true remedies” were ” pure
air, sunlight, abstentiousness, rest, exercise, proper diet, pure


water and faith in the healing power of God.”

By 1903, when the church headquarters was moved
to Washington, D.C., the sanitarium, was the largest and
best equipped health institution in the world. Its medical
superintendent was Dr. John Harvey Kellogg, and it was he
and his brother, W. K. Kellogg, who gave the world cornflakes -
W. K. Kellogg started the prepared breakfast cereal industry.


The answer, the secrets of health, longevity and even

to translate all of the different “dialects of immortality” into a
